### The Fourth Journey — On the soul, from its beginning in bodily matter through its final stages and return to its highest ends

This journey discusses the soul’s stages and its return to its highest ends. It is divided into books.[^V08-1][^V08-2]

### Book One — General questions about the soul

#### Chapter 1 — Defining the soul

Know that God’s care gives every possible thing existence[^V08-3] through His most sacred outpouring, each level in order from the noblest down to the least noble.[^V08-4] His care does not stop at some point[^V08-5] and refuse to give more.[^V08-6] So there remain infinitely many things that can move from possibility into existence. Though bodily matter is dark, dense, and cold, it can still be brought to a fuller state by an active source. The sun and other stars, for example, warm and refine it.[^V08-7] In this way it ripens and becomes balanced, fit to nourish living things and to serve as the material from which new beings can form. It becomes ready to receive growth and life,[^V08-8] taking on forms whose effects show God’s wisdom and care, as in plants and animals. Nature first carries out the tasks of the compounds and powers that came before them. We have already explained why what is lower can receive what is higher: possible things were not made in vain, but so they could return to their proper end. The elements were made to receive life and spirit.

The first kinds of life matter receives are nourishment, growth, and reproduction.[^V08-9] Next come sensation and movement, and then knowledge and the ability to distinguish things. Each kind of life has a form that completes it and gives matter the powers it needs. This form is called a soul: the plant soul is the lowest, the animal soul comes after it, and the rational soul is the highest. These three share one basic meaning and one broad definition. In this chapter I will prove that souls exist in general and explain what a soul is. A simple thing may have no definition,[^V08-10] and there may be no proof of it from its own simple essence. But we can define it and prove that it exists by looking at what it does and what happens to it. The same is true of souls and forms.

To prove that souls exist, we observe bodies that produce many different effects, not in one fixed way and without choosing to do so: sensing, moving, taking in nourishment, growing, and reproducing. These effects do not come from basic matter, because matter only receives and has no active power of its own. Nor do they come from the bodily form shared by all bodies. Some bodies lack these effects, while others produce them. So living bodies must have powers beyond their shared bodily nature. These powers cannot themselves be bodies, or we would have to look for another source of their effects, and then another without end. They are powers joined to bodies. As we have learned in discussing possibility and actuality, we call any active power that produces different effects in this way a soul. The word names the power as the source of these actions, not in its bare, simple essence. That is why understanding the soul is part of understanding nature.

To define the soul, we follow the philosophers. The soul can be considered in several ways, and so it is given several names: power, perfection, and form. Because it can act by moving the body, and can receive the forms of things through perception and thought,[^V08-11] it is called a power. In relation to the matter it inhabits, from which a plant or animal substance is formed, it is called a form. And because a general kind is incomplete until its distinguishing feature is added, the soul completes the kind and makes it a full species. For this reason, philosophers say it is better to define the soul as a perfection than as a form. First, “perfection” has a wider meaning.[^V08-12] Most people use “form” for something impressed in matter, but some souls are not impressed in matter. Such a soul is not the body’s form, but it completes the body, as a king completes a city or a pilot a ship.

Second,[^V08-13] “completion” describes how a thing becomes the particular kind of thing it is, while “form” relates it to the matter from which it comes. Form points to the matter from which a substance is made, to what exists only as a possibility, and to something that is not truly the source of actions: the kind of thing itself. Third, when we speak of matter, we are already speaking about a species, but speaking of a species does not always refer back to matter.[^V08-14] So defining the soul as a perfection says more about it and includes all kinds of soul, including souls that exist apart from bodies.

“Perfection” is also better than “power.” First,[^V08-15] the soul has a power to perceive, which receives what it perceives, and a power to move, which acts. Neither meaning should be given more weight than the other. If we use “power” to mean only one of them,[^V08-16] the definition has the same problem: it covers just one side of the soul, not the soul as a whole. Logic teaches that a definition should not describe a thing from only one of its sides.

And “power” has two separate meanings here, since acting belongs to one basic category and being acted upon to another. A definition should avoid relying on one word with two unrelated meanings. “Completion,” by contrast, applies to both without changing its meaning.[^V08-17] In its power to perceive, the soul completes the animal; in its power to act, it also completes the animal.

Second,[^V08-18] “power” describes the soul as a source of action, while “perfection” also describes how it completes a kind. A definition that explains a thing from every side is better than one that explains only part of it. So “perfection” should be used as the broad kind in the soul’s definition.

Someone may object that even if we know the soul is a perfection of this or that body, we still do not know its true nature. We know it only through a relation. As explained in the discussion of relations, something that exists only as a relation has no independent existence. We know the soul only through what follows from its limited relation to the body,[^V08-19] because the word “soul” names this relation of governing and acting upon a body. It does not name the inner substance of the soul in either its general or specific sense. A relation exists by reference to something else, and here it is measured by the body. That is why the body belongs in the definition of the soul’s dependent, relational existence, just as building belongs in the definition of a builder. It does not belong in the definition of a human being. Definitions match the ways things exist.[^V08-20] A human being exists as a substance and has a definition of its own. “Human being” does not belong in the definition of “human being” or of any substance that is one of its kinds: substance and relation are entirely different categories.

So looking at the soul only as a soul means looking at it in relation to the body. That is why the study of the soul is part of natural philosophy, which examines matter and its changes. Anyone who wants to know the soul’s own nature apart from this relation must approach it from another starting point and in another science.[^V08-21] If this definition already told us the soul’s full nature, it would not be difficult to know which category it belongs to.[^V08-22]

There is a subtle Eastern insight here.[^V08-23] It shows how a substance can grow stronger in its very way of existing, and how human nature can become more complete in its own being. It also shows that this definition of the soul is not merely a definition of a name, as “builder,” “father,” and “son” are.[^V08-24] A builder’s nature exists apart from his relation to building; a person is not a builder simply by being human. The soul is different: being a soul is its own way of existing.[^V08-25] Its nature has no separate existence in which it is not yet a soul and only becomes one after some later change in its essence. The soul’s own being develops and changes from within.

The soul’s relation to the body is not a feature that happens to it later, like a planet’s motion or a father’s relation to his child. If, as many philosophers claim,[^V08-26] the soul first existed as a complete, separate substance among the intelligences and only later acquired a reason to join the body, it would have to leave the sacred realm and take part in the material world. But this is impossible. What belongs to a thing by its nature cannot be taken away. And a separate substance cannot acquire a state it did not have in itself, because new states arise only in bodily matter and things joined to it.[^V08-27] Also, the soul completes the body. Together with bodily matter, it makes a complete bodily kind. But something immaterial and something material cannot form one material natural kind. So the first claim is false: the soul’s joining to and acting through the body belong to its own particular existence. Its relation to the body helps make it what it is.[^V08-28]

This does not mean that the soul belongs to the category of relations or ceases to be a substance. It means only that it is not a separate intellect. Its relation is like matter’s capacity to receive, form’s relation to matter, God’s relation as creator, knower, and powerful, and an accident’s relation to its subject.[^V08-29] Each has meanings beyond the relation itself. I do not mean that it has an existence separate from that relation. For example, black has its own nature and belongs to the category of quality, but its actual existence is in a subject: its being in a subject belongs to what it is as an individual, not to its definition. The same is true of matter, form, nature, and soul: each has a substantial nature beyond its relation, but its particular existence involves a relation. This also shows[^V08-30] that existence is something beyond a thing’s definition. Since the soul is a form for matter, its relation to the body follows, just as every form relates to matter. But this does not make it a mere relation or an accident. The soul completes and brings its subject to fullness; an accident simply depends on a subject.

As long as the soul has not developed beyond bodily existence to the fully developed life of a being that understands without a body, it is a form tied to matter. But it can be nearer to or farther from its intellectual beginning, according to how strong, weak, complete, or incomplete its existence is. Existence itself can grow stronger or weaker, as we have shown in the general discussion of being and possibility.[^V08-31]

Let us return to the definition of the soul. It is a first perfection of a body. Some perfections are first: they make a kind what it is, as a sword’s shape makes it a sword or a chair’s form makes it a chair.

Other perfections are second: they follow from a thing’s kind and its actions or experiences. Cutting is a second perfection of a sword. For a human being, these include distinguishing things, thinking, sensing, and moving by choice.[^V08-32] A kind does not need to be doing these things in order to be fully the kind it is. It is enough to have the source of those actions, so that it can do them when the chance arises. An animal is an animal even when it is not moving by choice or sensing anything. A geometer is a geometer even when not measuring[^V08-33], and a doctor is a doctor even when not treating anyone.

The soul is a first perfection.[^V08-34]

And what is a first perfection of one thing may be a second perfection of another.[^V08-35] So it is no objection that the human soul is not a perfection of an animal considered only as an animal, or that the soul in general is not a perfection of a body considered only as a body. As we have seen, a nature exists when any one of its individuals exists, and it ceases only when all of them cease. The soul is a first perfection of one thing, though it can be a second perfection of something else. The thing it first perfects must be a body, but “body” here means body as a general kind,[^V08-36] not matter as a subject,[^V08-37] as explained in logic.

Nor is this body every natural or made body, such as a bed, chair, ship, or city. If we suppose[^V08-38] that a pilot’s soul completes a ship or a king’s soul completes a city, their souls do not make those things living beings.[^V08-39] And if we treat the ship and its soul as one thing, that whole would not be a body, a substance, or anything belonging to the recognized categories. What a thing is depends on how it exists, and existence always involves unity. A thing with no unity has no existence. So the soul is a first perfection of a natural body—not every natural body,[^V08-40] since a soul does not complete fire or earth, but a natural body whose second perfections are carried out through tools and powers. These are the activities of life, such as sensing and moving by choice.

This definition also tells us something about the soul’s nature. Some forms act on matter directly, without using another power. Their existence is joined to matter, as with the basic elemental and mineral forms: fire, air, ruby, gold, and others. They are almost entirely material and are divided when their matter is divided. Other forms act through powers they use as tools. Those powers must be lower than the form itself, because they serve it. So the form stands above the level of matter. This is true of the soul: it has some share in the higher, world beyond physical matter.[^V08-41]

So when we say that a soul is the first perfection of a natural body with tools, “tools” does not mean only body parts. It also means lower powers that the soul uses, such as nourishment, growth, and reproduction in a plant soul, or imagination, sense, and desire in an animal soul. This broad meaning of “tool” lets the definition include all souls. The soul of a heavenly sphere also fits it, since proof shows that the sphere has a soul that knows and also has a natural power that directly moves it.[^V08-42] It has a power of sense too, but not like the sense of animals, which depends on an organ receiving an impression. Because a heavenly sphere is simple, it does not have different powers in different parts of its body. The whole sphere is imagination, the whole is a natural power that moves it in its place, and the whole is sense. Its power of sense is lower than its power of imagination and its understanding of universal things.[^V08-43]

This answer resolves a problem that later philosophers found difficult: how can one definition cover the plant, animal, and heavenly souls? If we call every power that produces any effect a soul, then every natural power would count as a soul, which goes against the earlier philosophers. But if we limit “soul” to a power that acts by choice, we leave out the plant soul. If we define it as a power that produces different kinds of action, we leave out the heavenly soul. Add any further condition and the definition becomes narrower still. Each attempt to include the animal and heavenly souls leaves out the plant soul, or includes the plant and animal souls but leaves out the heavenly soul.

Do not be misled by the different directions in which the heavenly spheres move. They are not opposites: each movement comes from one source and has one aim, which does not change. This is the problem later philosophers raised. You have already seen how to solve it by understanding “tool” in a wider sense. There is no need to rely on a theory about the heavens that even its supporters say is uncertain.[^V08-44] One view is that each sphere containing a star, together with its smaller spheres, forms an animal body governed by a soul whose actions make those spheres its tools. But this account does not work for every sphere. Another view is that each sphere has its own separate life. Those who accept this view must say that “soul” means something different when applied to heavenly spheres and plants.

Now consider the proposal to include “living” in the definition of the soul. This would leave out the plant soul, but it does not follow, as Avicenna claims, that “life” and “intellect” mean something different for the heavens and animals.[^V08-45] The “intellect” of a heavenly being is actual understanding; in an animal it is only a capacity. These are different stages of intellect, not unrelated meanings.[^V08-46] Likewise, animal perception here means receiving images of sensed things and being affected by them. The heavenly sphere does not sense in this way.[^V08-47]

If “living”[^V08-48] means having the source of perception and movement, however, the definition includes both the heavenly sphere and the animal. Perception is wider than sensation: it includes sensation, imagination, and understanding.

If perception in the definition of life means sensation alone, the heavenly sphere could still be included. Sensation does not require a bodily organ to be affected. If a particular image were present to a sense power without affecting a bodily organ, sensation would still truly exist. We see something similar when a vivid image appears to the common sense in a dream or while awake, as in delirium: the soul sees the image even though no sense organ is being affected. So sensation is the presence of a particular image, not an effect on a bodily organ or an image impressed on it. We also hold that sight occurs when the soul produces an image like the external thing; that image is not in a physical subject. The heavenly spheres, then, may have sense through an active power, without the kind of passivity found in bodily senses.

Someone might still ask why we need to posit a soul at all. Why not say that life itself is the perfection, the source of the actions attributed to the soul?

Our proof already showed that when some bodies produce these effects and others do not, the effects must come from particular powers beyond the bodily nature shared by all. “Life” could mean one of three things: those powers themselves; the fact that a body has those powers; or the body’s being able to produce such effects.

The first answer simply agrees with what we have been saying. If someone wants to call the power we call a soul “life,” we have no disagreement beyond the word used. The second is wrong, because a body’s having a source is not the same as the source itself. The third is also wrong, because a body’s being able to produce these effects is not the same as a soul or a living thing. The ability of a ship to be useful depends on a pilot who gives it that ability; this ability may require an earlier condition.[^V08-49] But a first perfection cannot itself depend on an earlier perfection, or it would not be first.[^V08-50] Think of a body that can burn:[^V08-51] its capacity to burn and the heat in it are one thing[^V08-52], but the soul and a body’s capacity to produce the acts of life are not one and the same.

A body’s ability to produce the actions of life can come about in two ways.[^V08-53] For living bodies, this ability must be preceded by another condition. If a body’s existence were simply identical with being able to produce the acts of life, every body would be alive. But living bodies have this ability because of something more than their being bodies.[^V08-54] You cannot say that this ability is what first makes the body into a body, because the body’s life would then be the ability that comes after the condition that supposedly makes it alive.[^V08-55] Whether “body” means matter or a general kind, each is prior to the life that is being discussed. But something that is not a body may have an existence identical with its being alive. Indeed, for many nonbodily beings, such as the First Being, the intellects, and souls, their existence is their life.[^V08-56]

Life is not something that first makes a living thing alive. It is impossible for a thing to become alive by first having the very condition of being alive. In the same way, existence is not something that first makes an existent thing exist. As we learned in discussing existence, relations, place, and intellect, some things are identical with their own relation or state: a relation is the relation itself; a place does not need another place; an intellectual form’s existence is the same as its being understood.

#### Chapter 2 — What the soul is in general

Now that we have finished defining the soul in the broad sense of its relationship to the body, we should ask what the soul itself is. Is it anything beyond being a perfection of the body? Calling it a perfection does not yet tell us whether it is a substance or an accident. A perfection is something whose presence makes a kind of thing what it is. The soul makes an animal an animal and a plant a plant. But many perfections exist in a subject, like blackness or writing. They are first perfections of a whole made up of themselves and a subject: blackness completes something as black, and writing completes someone as a writer.

Someone may object:[^V08-57] is not a part of a compound present in the compound, while the compound itself does not exist in a subject? In that case, the part exists in something that depends on it.[^V08-58]

We reply that being a part of a whole that is not itself in a subject does not prevent that part from existing in a subject, if we consider it apart from the other parts. Nor does being a part that exists within a whole, rather than as something placed in a subject, make it a substance. As we explained when discussing substance, a thing’s substantiality does not change with what it is compared to. It cannot be a substance in relation to the whole of which it is a part, but an accident in relation to what it appears in. That is like the difference between what belongs to a thing by its nature and what belongs to it accidentally.[^V08-59] Not everything substantial is a substance, and not everything accidental is an accident.[^V08-60] A substance is a substance in itself and in relation to anything else; likewise, an accident is an accident in itself and in relation to anything else. Comparing a thing with other things does not change whether it is a substance or an accident, though it can change whether we call it substantial or accidental.

Avicenna says that when we consider a thing in itself, if it has no subject at all, then it is something that exists in its own right. But if it exists in something—not as a part of it, but in the way an accident exists in a subject—then it is an accident in itself. Still, the fact that it is not an accident in one thing does not mean it must be a substance in that thing. It may be neither an accident nor a substance in relation to it,[^V08-61] just as something may be neither one nor many in a particular thing, while being one or many in itself. “Substantial” and “substance” are not the same; nor is “accidental” in the logical classification of kinds the same as an accident in the category of qualities.[^V08-62] We have explained these points in logic.

So the fact that the soul is a perfection of the body, that the body is a substance, and that the soul is a part of a compound rather than an accident inhering in the compound does not prove that the soul is a substance. It may be a soul that exists in a subject while also being part of a compound; or it may exist in no subject and so be a substance.

The idea of the soul as a perfection has therefore not yet shown whether it is a a being in its own right or a feature that depends on something else. We must examine what the soul is. But we can prove that the soul is a substance. It is not enough, for this purpose, to prove only that our knowing self is a substance, as later philosophers did when they set out by a special route to show that the rational soul is separate from the body. That would not prove that souls in general are substances. It is better to begin by showing that the soul, simply as soul, is a substance before showing that some kinds of soul are immaterial. And we should first show that human souls are separate from elemental bodies and material bodies before showing that some human souls—the ones that have reached the level of intellect and actual understanding—are separate from both worlds.

Here is the proof that the soul in general is a substance. We have already seen that some bodies, by their own nature, carry out the actions of life. In animals, life belongs essentially to the body’s nature—not body as a subject or matter, but body as a general kind.[^V08-63] The source of that life is part of the body in the sense in which the body is matter, as we explained earlier. Many later philosophers, including the author of *The Wisdom of Illumination*, failed to distinguish these two meanings of “body.” They claimed that animal life is an accidental feature of the body and that no body is alive in itself: every body, they said, is dead and dark by nature.

That is not so. Every animal is a body by its very nature. Body is its general kind, and animal is a particular kind of body. In other words, some bodies, considered as bodies, are animals.[^V08-64] Every animal is alive in itself, so some bodies are alive in themselves. This contradicts the claim that no body is alive by its own nature. Only body considered with all further features excluded is not alive.

Once we accept that animal is a real kind of thing within the broader kind of body, and that life belongs to it by its nature, we can see that animal life does not come from the general nature of bodyhood. If it did, every body would be an animal, since all bodies share that nature. Animal life instead comes from something that completes this particular kind of body. Whatever completes a basic kind of substance must itself be a substance. So the source of life in an animal is a substantial form.[^V08-65]

The same applies to a plant body. Growth and nourishment belong by nature to this particular kind of body, not to bodyhood shared by all bodies. A plant body must therefore have a specific form that completes the nature of natural body in a particular way, gives form to its matter, and produces its distinctive effects. Since this form completes, distinguishes, and constitutes a substance, it has a stronger claim to be called a substance than the general, indeterminate nature of body itself or the material body that can be acted on by it. This is the plant soul. We have already given a principle in our discussion of specific forms that shows why such sources of life are substances; readers who still have doubts may return to it.
### Book Two — The nature of the animal soul, its substantiality, and its partial independence from matter

#### Chapter 1 — The animal soul is a substance

What we have said so far is enough, but let us make the point clearer. Some people claim that the soul is the body’s mixture, or temperament. Several proofs show that this is wrong.

First, the body is a substance made from elements that naturally resist one another and tend to separate. Something other than the mixture itself must hold them together and bring about their balanced state[^V08-66], whether the elements retain their own specific forms, as is commonly held, or do not.[^V08-67] The qualities of a mixture are accidents and follow the forms in which they occur. The soul, whether it is joined to complex matter or to simple matter,[^V08-68] cannot be identical with the temperament. The source and preserver of a thing cannot be the thing itself. Nor can the soul be one of the elemental forms: each element has effects of its own that are not effects of the soul. So the soul is neither the mixture’s quality nor the elemental form of any one element.

But someone might object that the elements in an animal body are simply forced to stay together and form a mixture. Perhaps nothing joins them or preserves the mixture.[^V08-69] Our reply is that forced mixtures remain together only in certain cases: either when something blocks their separation, as when fire and air are trapped beneath the earth and later break out in earthquakes, or while they are in the process of separating, as when oil is stirred into water. But the fiery and airy parts in semen are not so minute that they cannot escape the mixture, and there is no hard barrier to stop these lighter substances from leaving it.

There are many airy and fiery spirits in semen. Something other than the semen’s bodily mass must hold them together with the earthy and watery parts. We can see this because, once semen leaves the womb and is exposed to cold—which should make it set—it quickly thins. The same happens if it is exposed to heat or comes from a womb with an illness.

Someone may grant that no external force holds the mixture together, but suggest that it is the small size of the particles and how tightly they cling to one another. We answer that small particles do not resist separation when they are not immersed in a large amount of fluid.[^V08-70] This is what happens to semen when the mouth of the womb does not draw it in: it loses its thickness.[^V08-71]

Another objection: if the fiery and airy parts of semen outweighed the watery and earthy parts, semen would naturally rise, but it does not. So the fiery and airy parts cannot predominate. If the heavier, watery and earthy parts predominate, they could hold the others down by force.[^V08-72]

We reply that if the heavier parts held the lighter ones down, the fiery and airy parts should remain in the semen after it leaves the womb and is exposed to cold. You might say that the fiery and airy parts are not freed but are destroyed by the watery parts as the semen cools.[^V08-73] But then the semen should keep roughly the same volume for some time, especially when no outside force—such as the warmth of the surrounding air—is quickly breaking it down. It does not.

Someone might also say that water and earth come together by drying, after which fire attaches to them as it does to wood. But drying occurs only when air takes the place of water, which has left because there is no room for it and nothing to replace it.

Suppose the two heavier elements do come together by chance, without anything outside them joining them.[^V08-74] What, then, brings together the fiery and airy parts? And fire does not attach to wood in the way people unfamiliar with these natural principles imagine.[^V08-75] Fire develops in wood bit by bit and continuously, then gradually turns into air. There is not one fixed fire attached to the wood. The fire in a wick is more like a stream of water, always flowing and renewing itself.

Another suggestion is that the parents’ movement or the womb’s temperament brings the elements together, and that this forced state then lasts for a time. We answer that the parents’ movement may bring together the elements already present in semen. But something must also join to the semen the material added later, so that the animal’s organs can take shape. And something must preserve that union. This answer also deals with the previous two objections.[^V08-76]

If there were no agent in semen that shaped its mixture and organs, the first organ formed should be the one that appears first. Bodies act through their physical mass, by contact and connection, and the nearer parts should form before the more distant ones. But observation shows otherwise: the heart is the first organ to form. Also, many animals do not arise through parentage.

Another objection is that the elements may not need anything to keep them together. A dead body remains joined together for a while after the soul has left it. If the soul were what held the parts together, they should come apart at death.

We answer that the body left behind contains some dense elements and retains a shape.[^V08-77] It does not need a soul to keep it together any more than a building needs something beyond the dryness of its material to keep its parts in place. The body the soul had been preserving is not the same as the remains after death. It was a different amount of matter and had a different temperament. Death occurs only when that living mixture has changed and broken down. The soul is not the immediate cause of the color and shape that remain in a corpse. It is more like a distant cause: through a series of movements, it brings about the form and color, as a builder does through building. A different cause can preserve these features for a time, in a body or in other things. During that time, the elements can complete their motions of separation and decay. They may separate quickly if the body is little submerged, or slowly if it is more deeply immersed. The fiery and airy elements tend to separate first; the earthy and watery elements follow later.[^V08-78] Some time must pass for this separation. The fact that a corpse remains intact for a short time does not prove that its parts were never held together by anything. And, on closer examination, there is no instant when a living person leaves life while remaining exactly as they were a moment before.

One more objection: the soul comes into being only when matter is ready to receive it. Matter becomes ready only when it has developed the right temperament. Temperament is one of the preparatory causes of the soul. Though such a cause is only incidental, it is naturally prior to what it prepares. So if the soul is what joins the elements, how could it exist before the union that must naturally come first?

The answer given is that the parents’ souls first join the parts of the semen. The mixture then remains under the care of the mother’s soul until it is ready to receive the child’s soul. Once it comes into being, the child’s soul preserves the mixture and brings in the other parts of the body through nourishment.

Later philosophers disagree about whether the power that joins the body’s parts is the same one that preserves them and their temperament, and whether it belongs to the child or to its parents. Fakhr al-Dīn al-Rāzī held that the parents’ souls join the parts before the child’s soul comes to be; afterwards, the child’s soul preserves and builds the body through nourishment. He also reports a letter in which Bahmanyār asked Avicenna for proof that the power which joins the elements in a human body is also the power that preserves them. Avicenna replied that no such proof was needed: the parents’ souls join the parts of the embryo’s body, while the formative power first preserves this union, and the child’s rational soul does so later. The formative power is not one and the same throughout. Different powers take over as the embryo’s matter becomes ready for each stage. In short, the formative power directs the matter until it is fully ready to receive the rational soul.

The first objection to this account is that philosophers treat the formative and generative powers as powers of the soul and instruments it uses. Yet, according to Aristotle and those who followed him, the soul comes into being only after the temperament and the bodily organs are complete.[^V08-79] To say that the formative power shapes the organs and preserves the temperament would mean that the instrument exists before the one who uses it, and acts without anyone using it. That is impossible. The proposed replies do not help: that the soul may be eternal,[^V08-80] that it may exist before the body, that the formative power belongs to a plant soul essentially distinct from the child’s soul,[^V08-81] or that it belongs to the mother’s soul.[^V08-82] None of these answers solves the problem.

The second objection is the one raised by the commentator on Avicenna’s *Pointers and Reminders*. The letter’s account conflicts with what Avicenna says in *The Healing*: “The soul of every animal gathers the elements of its body, combines them, and arranges them so they can serve as its body. It preserves that body in the order it needs.” The commentator also argues that it makes no sense, in natural processes, for one power to hand over the work of governing the body to another after a time. Such a handover occurs only between agents that are not part of nature and act by making fresh choices.

He then answers the original objection this way. The parents’ souls use the power of attraction to gather nourishing material. The generative power turns it into the bodily fluids and separates from them the material of semen. It prepares that material to receive a power that will shape it and make it a human being in potential. This power is a form that preserves the semen’s temperament, as mineral forms do. It grows more complete in the womb as the matter becomes ready, until it can receive a more complete soul. This soul, while preserving the matter, carries out the plant’s functions. The matter then develops until it can receive a still more complete soul, which carries out the animal functions along with the earlier ones. Finally, when the body is ready, it receives the rational soul, which carries out reason along with all the functions that came before it and governs the body until death.[^V08-83]

The successive powers have been compared to heat that develops in a piece of coal beside a burning fire. First, heat arises in the coal; then it grows stronger until the coal glows; finally, it catches fire. The first warmth resembles the form that preserves the mixture; its growth resembles the beginning of plant functions; the coal’s glow resembles the beginning of animal functions; and its bursting into flame resembles the rational soul. The powers are one unfolding process, moving from a less complete state to a more complete one. The name “soul” applies to its last three stages. On this account, the parents’ souls gather the nourishing parts that enter the semen, but they do not preserve them. The child’s soul gathers the parts added later and preserves the body’s temperament. Avicenna’s claim that the two powers are one holds in this sense; his claim that the one that joins the parts differs from the one that preserves them holds in the earlier sense.

This is a strong account, but a difficulty remains. It can be resolved only by our proof that a thing can become more complete in its very being. The account may be read in three ways.[^V08-84] First, all these actions—preserving the mixture, growth, voluntary movement, and reason—might come from one numerically single source with several powers that appear in succession. That would mean the rational soul comes into being with the semen and remains inactive except for lifeless functions. This conflicts with the established principles of philosophy, and no philosopher has held it. Second, the actions might come from several sources, each with a different nature and degree of completeness. But then one natural agent would hand the body’s care over to another, which is the very idea the account rejects. Third, the earlier form might be destroyed, and a later form created as the matter becomes more complete.[^V08-85] But the earlier power’s work is to preserve, shape, and complete its matter. A natural power that exists to complete matter cannot be what destroys it[^V08-86] or prepares it to receive something that will destroy it. That would be like saying that plant, animal, and rational powers oppose one another. They do not. They differ in how complete their existence is. Each earlier power is directed toward the later one as toward its own completion. The end of a thing completes it; it does not destroy it.

Since all three readings fail, the substantial form must itself grow stronger in its existence, become more complete in its own substance, and pass through successive stages: natural, plant, animal, and human, and beyond as God wills. As the earlier discussion showed, a thing’s own actions are directed toward what is good and complete. In its natural development, nothing moves toward what opposes it unless some outside force compels it or blocks its way. Reason and direct insight both show that all things seek the highest good and the highest light, whose glory is beyond measure.

The second proof that the soul differs from temperament is this: plants and animals move themselves toward their own completion, in both size and quality. Their temperament also changes during these movements, since temperament follows the elements that make up the body. So the temperament changes, while the mover remains. The mover cannot be the temperament. Also, a body whose temperament has been disturbed by illness can return to health. The power that restores it is neither the healthy temperament that is gone nor the unhealthy one that is present. So this restoring power is not the temperament. It is within the animal’s body; if it were separate, it could act only through a bodily power. And if it were within the body, it could not act by position or through the bodily nature shared by all bodies.[^V08-87] This is clear on reflection:[^V08-88] nourishment, growth, and reproduction are not forced on the animal by an outside cause. They come from a power within it.

The third proof: if temperament were the mover, fatigue would not occur. Fatigue comes from a movement imposed on the body against its natural tendency. But temperament is what determines that tendency. If the mover were temperament, it could not cause fatigue or set up the conflict between the soul’s tendency and the body’s natural tendency that appears in trembling.

In *Pointers and Reminders*, Avicenna says that an animal moves by something other than its temperament, which often resists it during movement, in the direction of movement, and in the movement itself. Earlier commentators explain “during movement” as resistance to speed, as when a tired person tries to lift a foot but cannot move quickly. “In the direction of movement” means that the soul moves the body upwards while its temperament, because of the weight of the heavier elements, pulls it down; trembling is a combination of these movements. “In the movement itself” means that fatigue may become so severe that the soul can no longer move the body at all.

A later commentator understands “during movement” to mean the time when movement takes place. Temperament may resist the direction of movement when a person climbs a hill: the person wants to move upwards, while the weight of the body pulls down. “In the movement itself” means that the body’s temperament tends toward rest on the ground because of its weight, while the soul may want to move it along the ground. He rejects the earlier explanation of trembling: it is not made up of only these two movements. It also includes every movement in the direction the soul intends, and every opposing movement caused by a limb’s resistance to the soul. When a mover gives an object a tendency in one direction and something blocks it, the obstruction produces a tendency in the opposite direction.

The fourth proof: in this world, sense perception occurs only when a sense organ is affected by a sensible quality. Touch, for example, requires a change in the temperament of the organ that touches. If the temperament perceived that quality, it would have to be either the temperament that had already been destroyed or a new one. The first is impossible because it no longer exists; the second is impossible because the body’s original healthy temperament cannot perceive itself, so it cannot perceive a new, foreign temperament either. In short, sensation requires something to be affected, and a thing cannot be affected by itself. Something must remain through the two states in order to notice the change. Temperament does not remain the same.

The fifth proof: an animal’s temperament can grow weaker or stronger. What changes is not the temperament itself.[^V08-89]

The sixth proof, given in *The Throne Book*: perception means that what is perceived exists in the one who perceives it. If the soul were the temperament, then an accident would exist in itself. That is impossible. A quality such as temperament exists in a subject, not on its own. So temperament is not what perceives it. Nor can the perceiver be the body in general, or an elemental body; otherwise every body, or every elemental body, would perceive, which is plainly not so. The power that perceives temperament is therefore something other than temperament and other than the body in which temperament exists.[^V08-90]

The seventh proof: we will show that the animal soul and the imaginative power are immaterial, and immateriality is a higher state than substantiality. So the soul cannot be temperament.

Some have tried to disprove that temperament is the soul in a different way. The temperament of a simple organ resembles that of its parts. If the power that shapes it were its temperament, the whole organ and each part would have the same shape. But that is false. A critic objects that the shaper, in this account, is the formative power, which spreads through its subject and is the same in kind in each part as in the whole. So the same problem would apply to the formative power.[^V08-91] It would also mean that a part of the celestial sphere had the same shape as the whole sphere.[^V08-92]

I reply that, although the formative power spreads through the organ, it does not shape it independently. It serves the soul, shaping the organs according to the soul’s needs and aims. As for the simple powers that make spheres, a single power acting in a single material body calls for one particular size and gives it a spherical shape. Once the whole is spherical, its continuity prevents a part from having a different shape.[^V08-93]

It has also been argued that if the mover were a power of temperament, it would move in only one direction, since one temperament has one natural tendency. The same critic replies that plant powers also come from a single source and yet produce many different actions.

I answer as before: the plant powers carry out different actions in keeping with the soul’s aims. These nine proofs[^V08-94] show that the animal soul is not a power of temperament,[^V08-95] like a mineral form. In fact, the effects and properties of minerals also come from substantial forms other than their temperaments.

#### Chapter 2 — Proofs that the animal soul is not wholly dependent on physical matter

The first proof is that an animal’s body can gain or lose parts as it breaks down,[^V08-96] yet the animal remains the same individual through these changes. Every animal body is affected by its inborn heat, heat from the elements within it, the heat of movement, and the surrounding air—especially in the height of summer. The animal remains the same individual through all these conditions. So its identity is not the same as the bodily structure we can sense.

Also, animals are aware of themselves. They flee what hurts them and seek what feels pleasant. They are not fleeing pain in general—for it is commonly held that animals do not grasp universal ideas; if they did, the proof would be even stronger. Nor do they flee another animal’s pain, even though it too is pain. They flee their own pain. This shows that they know themselves, and that self-knowledge points to a kind of immateriality in two ways.[^V08-97]

First, knowledge requires what is known to be present to the knower. A form that exists in matter exists for the matter, not for itself. Anything that exists in itself rather than in a subject is immaterial. So what knows itself is immaterial in this sense.

Second, an animal’s awareness of itself is constant; it is not learned through the senses. Its awareness of its visible and hidden organs is different.[^V08-98] To establish the point, we must show that the animal does not learn of itself through either sense or reasoning. If self-awareness came through the senses, the animal would have to sense itself. But a sense cannot sense itself, much less what serves as its instrument.[^V08-99] Also, an animal may know itself even when it senses nothing at all.[^V08-100]

Could the animal know itself by reasoning? Reasoning requires a proof, and an argument requires a step connecting its starting points to its conclusion. That middle term would have to be either the cause of the soul or one of its effects. The cause cannot be the middle term, because the cause of souls is too exalted for an animal to know. Most people know themselves without ever thinking about what caused them.[^V08-101] Nor can an effect serve as the middle term. If it is an effect in general, the proof would establish only some agent, not the individual animal itself. If it is an effect belonging to that particular animal, then knowing the effect already depends on knowing the animal to which it belongs. The reasoning would go in a circle. So an animal’s knowledge of itself does not come from sense or proof.

The larger point is that a human being knows the visible organs only through the senses and the hidden organs only through dissection. An animal, too, knows its organs only by one of these routes. Its awareness of itself is not the same as its awareness of its organs.

Here is another proof that an animal is not merely its perceptible bodily structure. Imagine an animal created all at once, fully formed, but unable to sense anything outside itself. Suppose it were suspended in open air, where the air offers no resistance and it feels no qualities at all. Its limbs are separated so they do not touch one another. In this state, it would still be aware of itself, while unaware of any of its visible or hidden organs. It would affirm that it exists, but would not affirm that it has size, length, width, or any direction. If it imagined a position, direction, or limb, it would not imagine it as part of itself. What it is aware of is plainly different from what it is unaware of. Its identity is therefore different from all its organs.[^V08-102]

#### Chapter 3 — Replies to doubts about the soul’s being a substance

Some people argue that plant powers, and the animal soul too, are states of the body: they perceive particular things and carry out particular actions.[^V08-103] A separate substance,[^V08-104] they say, cannot perceive or act in these particular ways.[^V08-105][^V08-106] So the soul must be a bodily power that causes the whole made up of itself and the body.[^V08-107] The plant and animal powers would then exist in a subject that depends on them, making them substantial forms. Those who allow that one thing can be both a substance and an accident in different respects say that the soul is a substance as a part of a plant or animal, but an accident because it exists in and depends on the body. Those who deny that something present in a subject can also be a substance reject the soul’s substantiality altogether.[^V08-108] We have already shown that both views are mistaken.[^V08-109] Still, those who call the soul an accident offer several arguments for their position.

First, a state cannot be the cause of the subject in which it exists, since that would make each depend on the other. So the soul cannot be a substance. Second, even if they grant that a state can help constitute its subject, they say the soul cannot do so, because it comes into being only after the right temperament has formed. What comes later cannot cause what came earlier. So the soul cannot be what brings about the temperament. Third, if the soul were a substance, substance would be essential to it, since substance is the general kind under which specific kinds fall. We would then know immediately, without learning or proof, that the soul is a substance. But we do not. So the soul cannot be a substance.

We have already answered the first objection. As for the second, the temperament that prepares matter to receive a soul or a lifeless form is not the same temperament that the soul or form creates and preserves. That removes the supposed circle.

As for the third objection, Avicenna says that all we know about the soul is that it is something that governs the body; what that thing is in itself remains unknown.[^V08-110] Substance belongs to the soul’s true nature, not to the general idea of “something that governs the body.” What is constituted by substance is unknown to us, while what we do know is not constituted by substance. So there is no contradiction.

A critic replies that my knowledge of myself is not learned, as has been proved. Either I know myself only through my relation to my body, or I know my true nature. The first option is false, because we have already shown that self-knowledge is not just knowledge of a relation to the body. My knowledge of myself is the same as my self, and it is always present. I am truly a substance, not a relation to something else. The critic is astonished that anyone could hold both claims without seeing their contradiction. He then offers the right answer, in his view: substantiality is not an essential feature, so it can remain unknown, as we explained earlier.

I say that we have proved that substance is essential to kinds of things that are substances. But this critic misses another way the self can be known immediately and yet its substantiality remain unknown. Substance is essential to what things are when their nature is made up of a general kind—the idea of substance—and a distinguishing feature. But substance is not the general kind of individual existences, because existence, as we have shown, has no kind or distinguishing feature.

When we know something by forming an additional image of it in the mind, we know it through a representation. If that knowledge is exact knowledge of what the thing is, it must include a definition with its general kind and distinguishing feature. But when we know ourselves directly, we know our own particular existence and identity. Existence is something added to what a thing is, and what a thing is does not form part of its own existence. Knowledge through representation gives us general ideas; direct awareness gives us a particular identity. So when a person turns inward and becomes aware of who they are, they may be unaware of all general ideas—even the ideas “substance,” “individual,” or “governing the body.” I find in myself only a particular existence that is aware of itself.[^V08-111] Everything other than this particular identity that I point to by saying “I”—including the ideas of “I,” “existence,” “perceiver,” “governing the body,” and “soul”—is something general that I can point to as “it.” Not knowing or noticing the soul’s substantiality does not mean it is not essential to what a human being or animal is.

Avicenna’s words can be understood in this way, which removes the apparent contradiction. The argument has shown that the plant soul is not a body, and that some souls can exist apart from the body. These are the souls shown to have actual imaginative perception or direct awareness of themselves. There can be no doubt about the substantiality of human beings and of animals with fully developed senses and inner powers of perception, such as estimation, memory, and imagination. But how do we establish the substantiality of animals that have only touch and related senses, and of plants?

As we said earlier, the matter directly informed by these souls has a special temperament and structure.[^V08-112] It keeps that temperament only as long as the soul remains, because the soul gives it that temperament. The soul must therefore be the cause of the plant or animal body’s particular temperament and of its generation and growth. The immediate subject of the soul cannot already be fully actual without it, formed by some other cause and then joined to the soul as an added feature, as with an accidental quality. That is what some people—indeed, most—claim when they say that semen first becomes a mineral body and only then receives a soul. This is false. The soul gives its immediate subject its actual form and constitution.[^V08-113]

When the soul leaves, its immediate subject falls apart and the powers that branch out from the soul disappear. The more distant matter remains in another state. Either the kind and substance that made it matter for the soul cease to exist, or another form takes the soul’s place and keeps the matter in its natural condition.[^V08-114] It is difficult to explain how this replacement happens. If the new form came from the soul, it should disappear when the soul does. If it did not come from the soul, and the matter was already made actual by some form other than the soul, then the soul would not be a substantial form. Perhaps we have already explained how such a form can take the soul’s place, as in bone, hair, nails, and horns—remains of plant and animal souls. I will not pursue that question here.

The more pressing problem is this. Suppose the plant soul causes the constitution of its immediate matter by giving it a temperament and joining its parts.[^V08-115] The animal soul, however, joins the body only after the plant soul has already constituted the matter with a substantial form. It would then seem that the animal soul is present in matter already constituted by the plant soul, and so exists as an accident in a subject.[^V08-116]

We answer that “plant soul” may mean one of three things. It may mean the specific soul that belongs to plants and not animals. Or it may mean the general principle shared by plant, animal, and human souls: the source of nourishment, growth, and reproduction. Or it may mean the soul’s particular power that produces these three plant functions.

If it means the first, then the specifically plant soul does not exist as an actual, separate kind in an animal. This is like saying that a weak shade of black exists as a separate actual thing inside a deeper black.[^V08-117]

If it means the second, then a general principle produces only a general effect, just as writing in general comes from a writer in general. Plant matter is not made fully actual by a separate, general plant soul. A natural kind or general feature cannot exist by itself apart from the conditions of its existence. If matter is not made fully actual by a general plant principle, then the animal soul does not arrive in something already constituted in its own right.

If it means the third, then the matter is not as those unfamiliar with theoretical philosophy imagine: the power of growth first produces an animal body, and then the animal soul arrives and takes control of it, making the growing power one of its tools. That is like a craftsperson deliberately making a tool and then using it. The plant power is one power of a soul that has other powers too. It cannot exist before that soul; otherwise the soul’s instrument would be in use before the soul that uses it.[^V08-118] The plant power is a branch of the animal soul and cannot exist fully apart from it. In the same way, the animal soul is not fully actual in a being with a rational soul except through that rational soul. As we shall see, each of us has one soul, and all the other powers are effects of it and branches that spread through the organs. This is the view widely held by the leading philosophers of later times.

My settled view is that the soul is all the powers together: their single, unified source and their beginning and end.[^V08-119] The same is true of every higher power in relation to the lower powers it uses, whether it uses them in sequence or together. The powers come one after another in time; what comes earlier in time is later in rank and dignity. The soul in us—or in any animal—gathers the elements of the body, combines them, and shapes them into a body fit to be its own. It nourishes and grows that body, makes it an individual through nourishment and a member of its kind through reproduction, preserves its health, wards off illness, restores the right temperament when it has been disturbed, and keeps the body in its proper order. While the soul is present, outside changes do not overcome the body.

If the soul were not also the source of the plant and natural functions in the body, as it is of perception and animal actions,[^V08-120] the body would not keep its health. It would fall apart under the pressure of outside conditions. This is also why feelings and beliefs that please or hurt us affect the body and strengthen or weaken the powers of growth. It is not belief by itself that changes the body. It must be accompanied by a change in the subtle materials that run through the body, brought about by joy or grief. These, too, are states of the soul. They first change the state and temperament of the vital spirit, then, through the powers, the state and temperament of the denser body. Rational joy makes bodily powers—especially the power of growth—stronger and more active. Rational grief weakens them, leaving them feeble and sluggish until their activity is impaired and the body’s temperament is damaged. This strongly shows that the soul’s action and governance extend through the body’s furthest and densest material layers, even though its own essence belongs to a subtler and higher level.

The animal soul—and likewise the human soul—gathers the perceptive and plant powers, together with their immediate and more distant subjects. It is therefore a perfection of a subject that cannot become actual without it. It also completes and brings about a kind of thing. Things that differ in their souls differ in their essential kind, not just in temporary features.[^V08-121]

The plant powers found in plants differ in nature and kind from the plant powers found in animals. In neither case are these powers accidents, as some have supposed. They are substances: actual in plants, but potential in animals. “Potential” here does not mean that they have only a preparatory possibility. Their potentiality is like the way intelligible forms are present in the simple intellect as a unified, active capacity.

#### Chapter 4 — The powers of the soul that branch out through the body

In *The Healing*, Avicenna says that the powers of the soul[^V08-122] fall first into three kinds. The first is the plant soul: the first perfection of a natural body with organs, insofar as it grows, develops, and reproduces.[^V08-123] The second is the animal soul: the first perfection of a natural body with organs, insofar as it perceives particular things and moves by choice. The third is the human soul: the first perfection of a natural body with organs, insofar as it understands universal things and acts through reflective choice and judgment. Each kind has degrees, from greater to lesser perfection. The plant soul has three powers.[^V08-124] The nutritive power changes another body into something like the body it belongs to, attaches it to that body, and makes it similar to itself, replacing what has worn away.

The growth power increases the body by adding material that has been made like it, in proportion across its length, width, and depth, until the body reaches its full growth. The reproductive power takes from the body a material that is potentially like it. With the help of other bodies, it shapes and forms this material until it becomes actually like the body that produced it.[^V08-125]

By its first division, the animal soul has two powers: a power that moves and a power that perceives. The moving power is either a motive power, which urges the animal to move, or an active power, which carries out the movement. The motive power is a final cause[^V08-126]—the noblest of the four kinds of cause, as you know. It is the power of desire, which is connected to the imaginative power discussed below. When an image of something wanted or avoided takes shape in imagination, desire prompts the other moving power to act. Desire has two branches. One is appetite, which urges movement towards something imagined as necessary or useful for preserving the animal’s life, in pursuit of pleasure. The other is anger, which urges movement to repel something imagined as harmful or destructive, in pursuit of dominance.

The active moving power is a force that arises in the nerves and muscles. It contracts the muscles, drawing the tendons and ligaments attached to the limbs towards their point of origin; or it relaxes them or stretches them, moving the tendons and ligaments away from that point.

The perceptive power is divided into two kinds: one perceives from outside, the other from within. The external powers are the five senses—or eight, if touch is divided into its parts.[^V08-127] Avicenna then defines each of the external senses,[^V08-128] and when he comes to touch he says that it is a power located in the nerves of the skin and flesh throughout the body. It perceives what comes into contact with it and affects it by opposition—either changing its temperament or disrupting the way its parts are arranged.[^V08-129]

Some people seem to treat touch not as one kind of sense, but as a general power containing four, or even more, powers spread through the skin. One detects the opposition between hot and cold; another, between wet and dry; a third, between hard and soft; and a fourth, between rough and smooth. Because all four work through one instrument, they may seem to be a single power in their own right.

Avicenna then explains the inner powers in a summary that makes his account easier to follow. The perceptive power either perceives particular things or understands universal ones. Particular things are perceived either by the external senses, already described, or by the inner senses. An inner sense may only perceive, or it may both perceive and work with what it perceives.

If it only perceives, it may perceive particular images or particular meanings. By particular images I mean, for example, the image formed of Zayd or ʿAmr. By particular meanings I mean perceiving that one person is a friend and another an enemy. The power that perceives particular images is called the common sense: all the images received from the external senses come together in it. The power that perceives particular meanings is called estimation. Each of these two powers has a storehouse. The common sense’s storehouse is imagination; estimation’s is memory. These are four powers in all.

The power that works with what it perceives[^V08-130] combines and separates the images stored in those two storehouses. It might combine a human shape with a bird’s, or imagine a mountain made of emerald or a sea of quicksilver. When the animal’s power of estimation uses this faculty, it is called imagination; when the rational power uses it, it is called thought.

Avicenna and his followers say that the common sense and imagination are located in the front part of the brain. The power that combines and works with images is located in the middle part, as is estimation. Memory is located in the rear part of the brain. They do not mention the location of imagination, since it can be inferred from the fact that it is a storehouse.[^V08-131]

Some philosophers treat the animal soul as the whole set of these powers. Others identify the soul with estimation and treat all the other powers as subordinate to it. Each view has something right in it, as you will see. This is a brief account of the animal powers and their divisions. They are all carried by the vital spirit, whose substance resembles that of the heavens. The details of the rational soul’s powers will be discussed elsewhere. For now, I will set out the proofs for these powers, consider the objections, and answer them. First, however, I will explain how many kinds of difference there are among the soul’s actions. May God grant success.

#### Chapter 5 — A principle for working out how many powers there are

People commonly think that five kinds of difference explain why there are different powers and sources of action.[^V08-132] First, things may differ in meaning. For example, “animal” includes the meanings of growing and sensing. Some therefore say that an animal is made up of matter and form: a plant-like body and a sensing soul. In the same way, they distinguish the nature of a plant by its bodily aspect, which comes from matter, and its plant life, which comes from form. They apply this pattern to everything made of matter and form. Second, actions may differ by being present or absent: movement and rest, knowledge and ignorance, certainty and doubt. Third, they may differ in strength: certainty and opinion in belief; greater or lesser desire in character; or larger and smaller bodies.

Fourth, actions may differ in speed, as when someone grasps an idea at once rather than working it out step by step. Fifth, effects may differ in kind. They may share a close general kind, as when sight perceives black and white or taste perceives sweet and bitter; they may differ within more distant kinds, as when we perceive colors and sounds; or they may differ even more widely, as perception and movement do.[^V08-133]

With these distinctions in place, we can say that the first kind does not require several powers or several existences. A single, simple power can express several meanings without being divided either in itself or in its way of existing. For example, a separate intellect is both the one that understands and the one that is understood. Likewise, knowledge, power, will, and life—and all the other divine attributes—are truly said of the Necessary Being, exalted is He.[^V08-134]

The second kind does not require two powers or several sources of action either. An action may be present because its power exists, while its absence is due not to the loss of that power[^V08-135] but to the absence[^V08-136] of some condition needed for it to act. So the fact that an action sometimes occurs and sometimes does not is no proof that there must be more than one power.

The third kind does not require several powers either. Otherwise, there would have to be a different power for every degree of greater or lesser strength, and so an infinite succession of distinct powers at successive moments. That is impossible. Here is why. An increase in strength is a continuous change in quality. It has an unbroken course in which we can imagine infinitely many points, each reached at some imagined instant and each having its own degree of strength. If each degree required a separate power, there would be infinitely many actual, distinct powers. Time and distance would then be made up of indivisible parts; an infinite series would be bounded by two limits; and a bodily power would be the source of infinitely many actions. All of these conclusions are impossible. The reason is more likely that one and the same power becomes stronger or weaker as the causes acting on it change, as its recipient or instrument changes, or as the instrument is sometimes impaired or tired and at other times sound and vigorous.[^V08-137] The same account applies[^V08-138] to differences in speed.

As for the fifth kind of difference,[^V08-139] some people claim that one power cannot independently produce effects that differ in kind, whether the kinds are close or far apart.[^V08-140] On this view, a single power cannot both perceive and move; nor can one power perceive both inwardly and through the outer senses; nor can one power perceive colors, tastes, and smells. Each kind of effect must have its own power. This is the view Avicenna and his followers chose. But once you see that none of the differences listed above is, by itself, the source of the many powers, you can see that their explanation needs more care.[^V08-141][^V08-142]

Later commentators commonly say that philosophers such as Avicenna distinguish powers because each power is simple, and a simple thing can directly produce only one action. So, they say, one power may produce only one action as its primary effect, though it may produce others indirectly or by coincidence. For example, sight is one power that perceives color.[^V08-143] The color it perceives may happen to be black or white. Imagination preserves an image stripped of matter, though not to the degree required for intellectual understanding; that image may happen to be a color, taste, smell, sound, heat, or something else. The intellect perceives things free of matter and its ties; what it perceives may be a heavenly sphere, an animal, a lifeless thing, a quality, a quantity, a position, a place, or any other category, general kind, or particular kind.

This is not what Avicenna’s argument shows. The principle applies only to what is truly one in every respect. The powers are not like that: they have several possible aspects.[^V08-144] Also, the argument that one thing of a given kind can produce only one effect of that kind would equally imply that one individual power can produce only one individual effect. That would mean the power of sight by which we perceive one patch of black is different from the power by which we perceive another patch of black. But if one individual power can be the source of many effects of the same kind, why could it not be the source of effects that differ in kind?[^V08-145]

The sound basis for distinguishing powers is not that famous principle. There are two proofs.[^V08-146] The first[^V08-147] is that one power can exist without another, which shows that they are distinct. For example, the power of growth can fail[^V08-148] while the nutritive power remains, both in animals and in plants. So the growth power[^V08-149] differs from the nutritive power. The reproductive power, too, may be absent while growth continues. The second proof is that two positive actions can oppose one another, as attraction opposes repulsion, or receiving something opposes holding on to it. Merely[^V08-150] producing effects that differ in nature or kind does not prove that there are several powers. One power may act in different ways through its different aspects. The First Intellect, for example, gives rise to another intellect through the fullness and strength of its existence, and to a heavenly sphere through the limitation of its possible nature. Yet these active aspects cannot explain effects that differ only as separate individuals of the same kind do. Such individual differences require matter that can receive changing states brought about by accidental causes, such as motion—states not fixed in the thing from the beginning.

Once these points are clear, no one can object:[^V08-151] “Why not say that the soul itself performs all these actions, without having to suppose all these separate animal and other powers exist?” Even if we accept that the rational soul differs from the animal powers because it can reason without depending on bodily organs, while the animal powers act through the body, why not say that the animal power is one, and that perception and movement come from that same power? Even if we accept that they differ, why not say that the moving power is one, and that appetite and anger are one power that responds one way when it encounters pleasure and another when it encounters pain? Why not say, too, that one power perceives both external and internal things, and that a single external sense produces different actions through different organs?

And why should the plant power differ from the animal power? Even if they differ, why not treat nourishment, growth, and reproduction as one power? At the beginning of an individual’s life, it supplies more material than is lost, so the body grows until it reaches its full size. Once it can no longer do that, it sends food to the moving body’s organs to nourish them. Any surplus no longer needed for nourishment and not used for growth is then put to another needed use: reproduction.[^V08-152] The power continues to replace what is lost until it can no longer do so, and death follows. What we have already said answers these and similar objections. The case for several powers does not rest on the famous principle, as some have assumed, so the familiar counterexamples do not refute it.

One counterexample is that the common sense perceives everything received by the external senses. If these perceptions differ, then the original argument has failed; if they do not, why could they not all come from one power? Another is that sight does not perceive just one kind of thing: it perceives black, white, and all the colors between them. If one power can perceive two kinds under one close general kind, why could it not perceive things that differ but fall under a more general kind? Also, a single power perceives shape and size, even if it does so by perceiving color first. So one power can perceive things that differ in kind, as well as things that differ within one kind.

Another counterexample is that imagination perceives things that differ in kind, and the intellect perceives all things. So, the objectors conclude, the claim that one thing can produce only one effect is false, along with all the other objections of this sort.

But, as you have seen, that principle applies only to a simple agent free of conditions, relations, and instruments. If an agent acts only when certain conditions are present, one and the same thing can produce many effects as different conditions join it. For example, nature is a simple power: it moves a body when the body is outside its natural place, and brings it to rest when it is in that place. Even the Active Intellect, which they say governs this elemental world, is a simple substance, yet it is the source of the events that occur here. The conditions and causes that prepare the matter differ.

These objections and counterexamples come from a misunderstanding among later writers such as Abū al-Barakāt al-Baghdādī, Imam al-Rāzī, the author of *al-Mawāqif*, its commentator al-Sayyid al-Jurjānī, the commentator on *Sharḥ al-Maqāṣid*, al-Taftāzānī, and others who debate theology without probing deeply into rational inquiry or immersing themselves in philosophy.[^V08-153]

One of the arguments the author of *al-Mulakhkhaṣ* takes pride in, as though it had demolished the philosophers’ principle about the number of powers and the orderly arrangement of existence—where a natural whole gives rise to different effects and therefore requires different sources, all grounded in the unity of one soul—is this. He says: “We have a true and certain premise that no rational person doubts: whoever judges two things must perceive them. For judgment means affirming that something belongs to something, or denying it. That cannot happen without conceiving both sides. So the person making the judgment must conceive the two things being related, in order to judge one of them by the other.”

“Now suppose we perceive a particular person and know that this individual belongs to the general kind “human being” but does not belong to the general kind “horse.” Whoever judges that this particular person is a human being rather than a horse must perceive both the individual being judged and the general description applied to it. So the power that perceives individual things must be the same power that understands general kinds.”

He calls this a decisive insight that anyone with even a little understanding must accept, and wonders how earlier thinkers, despite their skill, could have missed it. I say: How can anyone who has searched so widely, written so much, and thought so deeply still have strayed so far from the truth and become so blind to what is plainly before him? He has failed to notice what the soul itself does. Some of its actions involve thought, some the senses, and some movement. The soul is the one that understands, imagines, smells, tastes, walks, grows, feeds itself, desires, feels anger, and more. It performs these many actions through different organs.
