It also acts and is affected without an organ: it forms an image of itself, understands first principles, and uses its organs. No organ lies between the soul and its use of an organ. What problem, then, is there in saying that the soul perceives or acts in some cases by itself and in others through an organ? It perceives this particular human being through sense, and understands what human beings have in common directly.

It then judges what it perceived through sense by what it understood directly, saying, “This particular person is a human being.” Perhaps, after so long in a high religious office and such a wide reach of authority, the critic came to think that perceiving something through an organ means that the organ itself is the perceiver, while the soul knows nothing of what the organ perceives. That is a very poor assumption. It would mean that a single human being contains a crowd of separate perceivers, each limited to one kind of perception: one hears, another sees, a third tastes, a fourth smells, a fifth touches, a sixth imagines, a seventh estimates. The same would hold for movement: one power handles things, another attracts, another repels, another digests, another desires, and another feels anger.[^V08-154]

The same would be true of all the other powers. On this view, the soul would know nothing of these actions and reactions except its own nature and the qualities that belong to it. That is contrary to what we experience and what reason proves. In experience, each of us knows that we are the one who understands, imagines, smells, tastes, writes, moves, and sits. The proof is that the soul chooses to see, hear, and touch, and uses its particular organs to achieve general or particular aims. A particular aim is clear: someone may move in order to see a certain person. A general aim would be to go to a teacher to learn intellectual sciences. Whoever chooses to use a particular organ must perceive that organ.

Reason also shows that the soul, not the sense or its organ, perceives the particular images received by the senses. Whoever perceives something must also be aware of itself in the act of perceiving, as we have already explained.[^V08-155] A body, or a power that exists in a body, cannot perceive itself. Anything that returns to itself in its perception[^V08-156] is therefore spiritual. These and similar arguments show that the human rational soul perceives both particulars and universals. It can judge a particular by a universal, just as it can judge one particular by another.

The philosophers also use the first of the two proofs just mentioned to show that a natural power differs from an animal power. They say that we find a healthy organ[^V08-157] carrying out natural functions while its sensory functions are impaired. The loss of sensation must be due either to the absence of a sensory power or to the organ’s inability to be affected by that power. If the first is true, the animal power must be something other than the natural power spread through the organ: the natural power is present even when the sensory power is not, so the two must differ. The second option is false, they say, because[^V08-158] these bodies[^V08-159] can receive heat and cold, tastes and smells. If the sensory power were present in an organ and these sensible qualities reached it, perception would follow: the perceiving power would be there, along with the form that produces sensation. The same kind of argument can show that the powers of hearing, sight, smell, and the other senses differ.

For example, if the power of taste were the same as the power of smell, each should perceive what the other perceives, since the organ in each case can receive both taste and smell. But neither perceives what the other does, even when the relevant quality is present. So the two powers must differ in their nature.

The author of *al-Mabāḥith* objects that perception does not necessarily follow just because the perceiving power and the thing perceived are both present in one place. Perception may depend on a condition that is missing. For example, the organ of touch may contain both the power of touch and the quality being touched, yet that power does not perceive the quality in the organ. Likewise, the power of sight exists in the visual spirit, which has some color and shape; still, sight does not perceive the shape, color, or appearance of its own organ. So the meeting of a perceiving power and a perceptible form does not always produce perception. The fact that one organ fails to perform a particular act of perception, while another can perform it, therefore does not prove that the powers differ.

I answer that conditions and impediments are either essential and internal, or incidental and external.[^V08-160] A difference of the first kind shows that the powers themselves differ. External, incidental conditions do not last forever and can be removed. The divine philosophy has established that the characteristic action of every natural kind occurs either always or for the most part. No natural power can be prevented from acting according to its nature for all time. If a mineral power were by nature able to perceive or imagine but never did so because a condition was missing or an impediment was present, its nature would be left without any effect. Yet reason proves that nothing in existence is wholly without effect.[^V08-161] The same would be true if sight could perceive smells and smell could perceive visible things, but never did so because an external condition was missing or an external obstacle was present. This would make the powers and natures useless, which is impossible.

As for the claim that touch and sight do not perceive the organs in which they are found, or the qualities present in those organs, that is not an essential impossibility, as the objector assumes. It is not simply something that could happen if the right condition were supplied. You already know that perception cannot be attached to a material form as it exists in matter. Every perceived form must be immaterial in some way, since matter and material things are not present to the perceiving power, as we explained earlier.

Perception is not, as this researcher and those who follow him claim, a relation between the perceiving power and the existence of an external thing. Nor is sensation the presence of a sensible image in the organ of perception. Nor is it the organ’s being affected by the thing sensed outside it. That reaction only prepares the conditions for sensory perception. A living organ is affected in a particular position relative to matter carrying a certain quality. This leads the soul to take on an image resembling the qualities in that matter—the qualities we call sensible. In short, perception occurs only when the image of a thing exists in the perceiver itself, not in matter or in an organ.[^V08-162]

What shows that the powers in the sensory organs differ is this: each organ can be affected by some quality that also affects the others, yet one organ cannot bring about the kind of perception the others do. This proves that the powers the soul uses through them have different natures.[^V08-163]

The philosophers use the same line of thought to show that the animal power—that is, the power of sense and voluntary movement—differs from the plant power. If the animal power were identical with the plant power, plants would move by choice. Their bodies can move, and the power that moves by choice would be present in them. When the agent and the thing it can act on are both present, the action should follow. Plants would therefore move by choice, since there are motives in this world to seek benefits and avoid harms for bodies that can undergo opposite states. But they do not move by choice. So the plant power in us differs from the animal power.

An objection is raised here: according to the philosophers, the nutritive power in one organ differs in kind from the nutritive power in another. The nutritive power in an animal must therefore differ in kind from the nutritive power in a plant. So from the fact that a tree’s nutritive power cannot produce voluntary movement, it does not follow that an animal’s nutritive power cannot produce it. What is absent from one thing need not be absent from something of a different kind.

I answer that although the nutritive powers in plants and animals differ in kind, they share a general kind. Calling both “nutritive power” is not merely using one word for two unrelated things.[^V08-164] We identify powers by their actions. If their actions have something in common, the powers do too. So the animal’s nutritive soul shares something with the plant’s, while also going beyond it through sensation and voluntary movement. It includes a further, more complete power that gives it the distinct feature separating it from plant life. Whether this general power and its distinctive feature can exist as one existence, or must exist as several, is a separate question that will be discussed later.

My true view is that the soul perceives every kind of thing and produces every kind of perception; it also moves the body through every kind of natural and voluntary motion. This does not mean that we can do without the many distinct powers that mediate these actions. A higher power does not perform a lower action except through a lower power. For instance, what produces movement from place to place or a change of position must be a power spread through the body, unlike what produces movement through desire. Thought is higher than bodily extension. Any power that directly perceives must be immaterial to some degree, and the degree of immateriality matches the kind of perception: intellectual powers stand highest, while the senses—and touch above all—are lower and more deeply tied to matter.

The different effects therefore come either from distinct powers with different natures or from different levels of one and the same being. Each level has a nature and effects that agree with it, whether it is separate from other levels or joined to them. Nutritive powers of different kinds share one reality, whether each is a soul in its own right, a power of a soul, or part of a soul. Touch, too, has one nature in all its forms, whether it exists independently—as in worms, whose animal soul is touch itself—or as an existential power or meaningful part of an animal or human soul. The same is true of all the powers of perception and movement.

Sight, for example, may differ in kind from one animal to another, yet it has one shared reality. So do hearing, smell, taste, nourishment, growth, and the other animal powers, whether they are gathered in one power or spread among several. An example of powers gathered in one is the common sense, which brings together the five external senses. In Greek it is called *phantasia*. The human rational soul, though simple, likewise gathers all the powers of perception and movement. This does not mean that the organs themselves are the true sources of perception and movement, apart from the soul, except as instruments through which the soul acts. The soul itself is the sense behind all the senses and the source of intellectual, natural, and voluntary movement, because it has three levels and worlds: intellect, imagination, and sense. This will become clearer later.

No one can object that if nourishment and growth were actions of the soul, then the soul would be aware of the changes and digestion it brings about. It would have to know, in detail and without learning, every stage through which food changes and every organ that food becomes part of. But that is false. So, the objector concludes, these actions must be done by a power that is unaware of what it does.[^V08-165]

The answer is not that the soul may be aware of these things but forgets them because they change so quickly—like someone who hears a long string of words spoken in rapid succession and remembers none of them. That would lead to absurdity. If we could be aware of every change in food and every bodily material into which food is transformed, without finding any awareness of it within ourselves, one might as well say that an ordinary person knows every subtle detail, even though they have no sense of knowing it.

Instead, I will answer by first explaining several points. First, knowledge can be active or receptive. Active knowledge may cause its effect, or it may be identical with the effect itself.[^V08-166] In that case, the effect’s existence is itself the cause’s knowledge of it. In general, existence is awareness, both in the cause and in the effect. This is one principle.

Second, as you have learned, existence comes in degrees of strength and weakness. Its weakest forms are matter, motion, size, and number. We call something knowledge only when its existence is like form,[^V08-167] not when it is like matter, overwhelmed and buried beneath matter’s veil. You have also learned that bodies and spatial or temporal extension mix existence with nonexistence, unity with multiplicity, and wholeness with division. This mixture prevents unified awareness and is the source of what remains unknown. That is a second principle.

Third, something may be knowledge without being what is known, or it may be what is known without being knowledge. In other words, knowledge can be less complete than what it knows, which may be stronger and clearer than our knowledge of it; or the reverse may be true. When existence is supremely noble and great, it cannot be understood through some additional knowledge beyond itself, though it is knowledge of itself and known to itself. This is the case with God and the most direct intellects. Here, existence is knowledge, but it is not known by anything other than itself. By contrast, when existence is extremely low and imperfect, its image can be present to a knower even though the thing itself cannot. In this case, the image is stronger than the thing known as far as knowledge is concerned.[^V08-168] The image that arises is knowledge; what it represents is not.[^V08-169] In the first case, the known thing is itself knowledge, while our knowledge of it is not the thing itself but only one of its aspects.[^V08-170]

With these principles in place, we can say that even if our soul is the source of all animal and plant perceptions and movements—including attraction, transformation, and expulsion—it does not follow that it consciously knows the natural actions it carries out through matter and nature. The claim that knowing a cause entails knowing its effect is true.[^V08-171] But when the cause’s knowledge is identical with its existence, then its knowledge of the effect is also identical with the effect’s existence.[^V08-172]

If the effect’s existence is so low and imperfect that it is almost like nonexistence, then knowledge of it is almost like not knowing it. This is what the early philosopher meant when he said that the First Intellect is ignorant of some things in a way that is nobler than knowing them.[^V08-173][^V08-174]

Not all the soul’s actions are like writing, walking, eating, drinking, and other voluntary acts, which require knowledge and a separate act of will. Such actions do not issue from the soul unless it first imagines them and judges their benefit to be real, whether with certainty, probability, imagination, or mistaken belief. Bodily actions, by contrast, come from the soul through its awareness of itself—which is identical with its own being—and through its love of its own perfection. This gives rise to its natural longing for what belongs to it, a longing that follows its longing for its original source. Though these points can be proved, they can be understood only by the light of inner insight and direct vision.[^V08-175] That is why they have eluded most thinkers, and even more so those who rely on debate or imitation.

One sign that the soul’s power extends through every organ, acting in ways suited to its animal, plant, and natural levels, is this: if the soul did not attend to balancing the body’s temperament and keeping its parts joined, then the slightest change in temperament—from heat, cold, movement, fatigue, or a disturbing wind—would not cause pain. Nor would events that affect the mind, such as frightening or hopeful news and warnings, affect the body. Wounds and the separation of bodily parts should cause no immediate pain either. All pain and harm would instead come only from fear of future consequences, financial loss, or punishment in the afterlife. But these conclusions are false. Illness and bodily injury hurt us at once. So the soul itself must be the subject of these sensations and reactions: its power reaches into the seat of bodily nature, making the soul itself subject to injury, pain, hardship, and illness.

Even death comes upon the soul because, by God’s decree, it has entered this world. Death does not come upon the place of faith and knowledge of God and the Last Day; it comes upon the place of ignorance, darkness, motion, and change.

Another sign that the soul itself, rather than an unconscious natural power, performs actions such as attraction, expulsion, and the like is that when a person urgently needs food to be transformed, digested, and expelled—as happens to a sick person at the crisis of an illness—the person finds their other perceptive powers weakened.[^V08-176] This is because the soul is occupied and absorbed in those bodily actions, so its other actions are interrupted. Once that work is done, the soul turns again to the level where its perceptive actions take place. God knows best.

### Book Three — The plant powers, their actions, and their states

#### Chapter 1 — The main kinds of plant power

Plant powers may be powers that are served, powers that serve, or powers with a role between the two. The powers that serve the soul act on food either for the sake of the individual or for the sake of the species. If they serve the individual, they do so either to keep it alive or to bring its own growth to completion.

The nutritive power alone serves the individual’s survival.[^V08-177] It changes food into something like the substance of the body it nourishes, replacing what has worn away. The growth power serves the individual’s completion.[^V08-178] It increases the body’s dimensions in a natural proportion until it reaches full growth. It does this by adding more than what is needed to replace what has been lost. The nutritive power serves the growth power by supplying this extra food, beyond what is needed simply to stay alive.

But not every increase in a body’s size is growth. Getting fatter after the age when growth stops[^V08-179] is not growth, and losing weight during the years of growth is not shrinking. Growth is a proportional increase that brings the body to its full development, as in youth. Its opposite is decline: a proportional decrease, as in old age.

You may ask: what power causes fatness and thinness? Every action or change must have an agent that brings it about. We answer that the same power causes these changes, but under pressure from an external force. Both are forced changes. As we have already said, even in forced movement the agent is the nature and power of the thing being moved. But we use the name “nature” for a power only when it moves something by itself, not when an outside force compels it. In the same way, we call this plant power “the growth power” only when it naturally increases the body’s dimensions, not when an excess of moisture causes fatness or too much heat causes thinness.

The nutritive power’s action has three stages. First, it produces a mixture that is almost ready to act and resembles the organ. Second, it makes that mixture part of the organ. Third, it makes the mixture resemble the organ in color and texture, as blood does. The process can fail at the first stage, as when food is lacking; at the second, as in dropsy of the flesh; or at the third, as in leprosy and similar conditions.[^V08-180]

The two powers that preserve the species are reproduction and formation. The reproductive power separates a portion of the final digestive surplus in the body it nourishes and gives it a power of its own kind. The formative power gives semen, after it has changed, a shape that prepares it to receive its powers and qualities by God’s permission.[^V08-181]

The powers that serve the nutritive power are four: attraction, retention, digestion, and expulsion. We can see the power of attraction at work in some organs, such as the stomach and womb; in others, we establish it by reasoning.

For example, someone who is upside down, with their head below and feet above, can still drink water and swallow food. Since water and food are heavy, their movement upwards is not natural but forced. Forced movement comes either from something attracting or something pushing. In this position, pushing cannot explain how food and drink move upwards; so the stomach must draw them upward by a power of attraction within it.

We also find that the oesophagus and stomach draw food in from the mouth when the body needs it. When food tastes especially good, they draw it in quickly; even the liver may draw it from the stomach because it is pleasant and akin to its nature. If someone eats one food and then something sweet, and then vomits, the sweet food comes out last. The stomach has drawn it down to its lower part. The womb behaves in the same way when it draws in semen. So does the urethra when it is empty and a woman’s desire for intercourse is strong. For this reason, some philosophers called the womb an animal that longs for semen, because it draws it in so strongly.

We can show the same power of attraction in other organs. When blood forms in the liver, it contains the other three bodily fluids: yellow bile, black bile, and phlegm. Each is then separated from the others and flows[^V08-182] to a particular organ. Unless each[^V08-183] of these organs had a power that attracted its particular kind of fluid, the fluids could not separate by themselves, nor could each organ be supplied, for the most part, with one particular fluid.[^V08-184] This proves that the power of attraction is present throughout the body.

We can observe the power of retention in the stomach and womb through dissection. If we feed an animal wet or dry food and then open its abdomen, we find the food held tightly inside the stomach, which surrounds it on all sides. When the womb draws in semen, it grips it firmly from every direction and closes its opening so tightly that even the tip of a probe cannot enter. If you cut open a pregnant animal from below the navel towards the vulva and expose the womb, you will find it as described.

We can prove that other organs also have a retaining power. Food is heavy because it contains a great deal of earth and water. It must be held in place while it is digested—a transformative process that follows attraction. This requires a retaining power distinct from the nature of food and drink, which, because they are fluid and thin, would not naturally stay in these slippery places.

The digestive power changes what the power of attraction has drawn in and the power of retention has held, making it suitable for the next transformative process and bringing it to a temperament that can become part of an organ. According to those who examine the matter carefully, this power differs from the nutritive power.

Here is one account of the difference. Digestion begins when attraction has finished and retention begins. When an organ’s attracting power draws in some blood and its retaining power holds it, the blood has its own specific form. Once it becomes like the organ, that form disappears and another form takes its place. The blood’s form comes to an end, and the organ’s form comes into being. This coming-to-be and passing-away occurs through cooking: the matter gradually becomes less ready to take the form of blood and more ready to take the form of the organ. The first readiness decreases while the second increases, until the blood’s form disappears and the organ’s form appears. The first stage, which increases the matter’s readiness to receive the organ’s form, belongs to the digestive power. The second, when the organ’s form is present, belongs to the nutritive power. This is the usual account of how digestion differs from nourishment in each organ.

Two objections are raised. First, the digestive power changes the food’s qualities until it takes on a form like that of the organ. Whatever moves something towards an end also brings it to that end. So the digestive power brings food to the form of the organ. The larger premise is clear: what a thing moves towards is the end of that movement, and the end is what the movement is primarily for. It follows that one power produces both actions.

Second, the digestive power in each organ gives the matter greater readiness to receive the organ’s form through cooking and ripening. This readiness has degrees of strength, and there is no reason to assign one degree to digestion rather than another. When the readiness is complete, the giver of forms brings the form into being. Once these actions are complete, nourishment is complete too. So there is no difference between the digestive and nutritive powers.

I answer the first objection as follows.[^V08-185] Every action of the soul has two causes: an active cause and a cause that prompts it to act. It also has two ends: the point the movement reaches, and the purpose for which it takes place. These are different ends, so they require different powers.

The digestive power acts like fire: it cooks and ripens. Cooking and ripening by themselves do not mean that the process is aimed at preparing matter to receive a particular form, namely the form of an organ. The power that directs matter towards a particular purpose is different from the power that acts without such a purpose. Consider voluntary movement in an animal. One power directly moves the muscle and limb in a direction, without aiming at any particular goal. A higher power prompts movement towards something desired or away from something harmful. The first is the immediate cause of movement; its end is simply the point the movement reaches. The second is the motive power, whose end is something beyond that point: the purpose for which the movement is made, and for which the animal reaches that place. Although the purpose cannot be separated from reaching the place, it is not the same thing as reaching it.

The same applies here. The power that cooks the food is different from the power that directs it towards the form of the organ, as a means of completing the individual or preserving its kind.

As for the second objection, the power that gives forms to all things that come into being is indeed another source, higher than these active and motive powers. But because it is above these changes and renewals, every bodily action must also have an agent that carries it out directly. A stable agent, untouched by change, cannot directly produce these changing, renewed actions. If the recipient’s readiness alone were enough to produce movement and form, without an agent acting on it, we would not need to posit natural powers and souls in fire’s heat, water’s coldness, earth’s dryness, minerals’ stability, plant growth, and animal movement. The reactions of matter alone would suffice. But even[^V08-186] every reaction and readiness in matter depends on the action of an agent,[^V08-187] as we explained earlier: every ability to change depends on something already existing and able to bring about that change.

Just as many different reactions cannot come from one receiver without several different aspects of receptivity, many different actions cannot come from one agent without several different aspects of activity. We have already shown in our discussion of cause and effect that a general action requires a general agent, and a particular action a particular agent. This piece of writing comes from this particular writer; this image comes from this particular artist. An observable action that we can point to must be traced to an observable agent that we can point to. An intellectual agent is not directly the agent of anything except an intelligible action. May God guide us.

#### Chapter 2 — Digestion, waste, and the powers that expel it

We now turn to the expelling power, distinguish these four powers, and identify the organs that serve as their instruments. As for digestion, remember that food contains two substances: one can be made like the body it nourishes, and the other cannot. The digestive power acts on each in a different way. Its action on the first is as described above. The second may be coarse, thin, or sticky. The digestive power thins what is coarse, thickens what is thin, and breaks up what is sticky. One might object: the thinner something is, the easier it should be to expel; why, then, do you count thickening as one of the changes that makes expulsion easier? We answer that a thin substance may be absorbed into the stomach wall and remain there, while a thicker substance is not absorbed and can therefore be expelled completely.

The existence of an expelling power is shown by two kinds of evidence: particular examples[^V08-188] and a general argument. First, when someone vomits, the stomach seems to be pulled upwards from its place, moving much of the surrounding organs with it. Something similar happens during a bowel movement, as the surrounding organs help expel what they contain. This is especially clear in painful straining: the force of expulsion may even pull the rectum out of place. Second, blood reaches the other organs mixed with the three bodily fluids. Each organ takes what suits it. If it did not expel what is unsuitable, that material would remain there, and no organ would be free of the diseased, harmful fluids. But that is not what happens. So an expelling power exists.

Once we have established these four actions in the body—attraction, retention, transformation, and expulsion—we cannot say that they all come from one power that is merely described in different ways: attracting food as it is swallowed, holding it after swallowing, changing it while it is held, and expelling what is no longer needed. The reason is not simply the principle that one thing can produce only one effect; that principle does not apply to what is not one in every respect. Rather, these actions either oppose one another, as attraction and expulsion do, or can occur separately. An organ may be weak in one of the four and strong in another. If their sources were not distinct, that would be impossible.[^V08-189]

As for the instruments of these powers, attraction uses lengthwise fibres, retention uses oblique fibres, and expulsion uses transverse fibres. What the philosophers mean, apparently, is that the main forms of these actions require these kinds of nerve fibres. Each organ’s power of attraction, retention, or expulsion needs a nerve instrument; they do not mean that every tiny part of an organ must have its own lengthwise or transverse fibre.

This answers several objections. First, the liver’s flesh has no fibres, yet the liver has powers of attraction, retention, and expulsion. Second, the eye’s crystalline humour draws in food, holds it, and digests it, even though it plainly has no fibres. Third, if every fibre were made of still smaller fibres, there would be an endless regress, yet these powers are found in them. Fourth, a transverse fibre has no lengthwise fibre, even though it attracts; and a lengthwise fibre has no transverse fibre, even though it expels waste. The reason is that these instruments are needed for the main actions of attraction, retention, digestion, and expulsion, not for every fine detail or part contained within those larger actions. The three kinds of major power act through the three kinds of fibres in the veins. Each acts directly through its own instrument, and its action extends to what lies beside that instrument, even if only indirectly. For example, when a vein draws food through its lengthwise fibres, the food passes from the vein into the substance of the liver. The same applies in the other places mentioned.

You may ask: does the blood pass from the lengthwise fibres into the flesh of the liver, which is itself attracting the blood, or does it pass into the liver without the liver flesh attracting it? If the first, attraction does not depend on the fibres; if the second, the organ has no nutritive power. We choose the first. This does not mean attraction fails to depend on the fibres: it depends on them whether directly or indirectly. So every organ’s power of attraction requires fibres within it or close by, as in the liver and the eye’s crystalline humour.

#### Chapter 3 — How some organs have doubled powers, and what food is

Some natural philosophers may be right that the stomach has two sets of these four powers. The powers that draw food from outside the body into the stomach are not the same as those that draw from the stomach what is suitable for the stomach itself. Likewise, the powers that hold food in the stomach and change it into a substance suitable to become blood—the chyle—are not the same as those that send it to the liver. Other powers hold in the substance of the stomach the blood drawn from the liver, change it into food that nourishes the stomach, and expel its waste. The same applies to the liver: changing food into blood is different from changing it into the substance of the liver, just as turning food into liquid is different from making it part of the stomach.[^V08-190]

The more precise account[^V08-191] is that all organs, despite their different substances, have the four powers that directly nourish them and initially produce their food. The stomach and liver have four additional powers like these, which prepare food but do not directly produce it in the first instance. Such preparatory powers may also exist in places other than the stomach and liver, such as the mouth, oesophagus, and intestines. The veins, spleen, testes, and gall bladder are among the organs that serve these functions.[^V08-192]

Know that food, in its true sense, is what replaces what the body loses by changing into the body’s own kind. Food may be potential, as wheat and bread are; or actual, when it needs no further change beyond being joined to the body. Some call it food only once it has become part of the nourished body and is actually like it.[^V08-193]

In the first sense, food must be a particular physical substance. It must be a substance, because an accident cannot become a substance or part of one. It must be physical, because what is immaterial cannot turn into a body. And it must be particular, because a universal body does not exist in the external world. So the food of every body is a particular physical substance. We observe that animals do not feed on simple bodies but only on compounds, since a simple body is too unlike them, while a compound is more like them. Plants do not disprove this, as some suppose.[^V08-194]

The truth calls for a different kind of explanation, one that goes beyond the level of discussion people are used to. Here is a hint. Anything that exists in its own right, whether a natural thing or a soul, is one existing being. Yet one being may contain the capacities for several different things. Its activity, too, has one source while it can take several forms.

Every plant or animal body is one existing thing. The elements are present in it as capacities, not as separately existing elements. So, in our view, nourishment does not mean that a body called “food” enters the body it nourishes to replace what has worn away. Nor does growth mean that the body’s size increases because more external matter is added to it. Rather, suitable moist matter next to a plant or animal prepares the soul’s power to flow into the body,[^V08-195] preserving its size or bringing it to the degree of growth needed at each moment. As we explained earlier, the body’s change in size is a continuous flow of magnitude from an active power into receptive matter.

If nourishment meant that a foreign body entered the nourished body, the body would not remain one individual at every moment.[^V08-196] Likewise, if growth meant that the quantity added were greater than what was lost, there would not be one individual subject undergoing growth. The same applies to decline. The truth is as we have indicated.

You must also understand that the way food completes the body it nourishes is like the way knowledge completes a learner, or an idea that is actually understood completes a mind that could understand it but has not yet done so. Something may be capable of being known before it is actually perceived or understood. What completes a sense is something actually sensed, such as an image present in the sense—not colors, sounds, tastes, or smells as they exist in external matter. In the same way, what completes the intellect is something actually understood: an intelligible form present in an intellect that understands it, not an external thing such as a heavenly sphere, an animal, a plant, or the earth.

We have shown by proof that things actually sensed are joined in their being with the perceiving subject, and that things actually understood must likewise be joined in their being with the intellect. What completes a thing is identical with what it completes:[^V08-197] the perceiver is what it perceives, and the knower is what it knows. Likewise, the nourished body and its food become one when the food has actually become part of that body, not when it is merely capable of nourishing it. Wheat, for example, has only a remote potential to nourish. the partially digested fluid formed from it in the stomach is closer to becoming food; the material formed in the liver is closer still; and blood in the veins is closer to being food than all these earlier stages. What reaches an organ and becomes flesh, bone, or another organ is food in actuality—and it is that very organ being nourished.

Food and the body it nourishes are one and the same existing thing once nourishment has taken place. They differ only when the mind analyses the process: it may consider the body or organ on its own, before the new completion reaches it, or consider it as complete, with the perfection it has gained through the earlier actions and changes that prepared it. This is like the way the soul is completed by acquiring forms of knowledge. One of the subtle points that can bring insight here[^V08-198] is that the stages of digestion correspond to the stages of abstraction in understanding.

#### Chapter 4 — The Four Stages of Digestion

Know that animals, and human beings too, need food as long as they remain in this world and have a bodily nature. Their food must resemble the body it nourishes, in both form and matter, because these bodies are constantly changing and wearing away. Each organ needs its own share of food, suited to it and like it. The body's nutritive power matures and transforms the food, then strips away its husks and waste. This power stands to the body as the intellect stands to the soul.

When food enters the body and comes under the soul's nutritive power, that power works on it through subordinate powers. It changes the food through stages of digestion, using their natural craft like alchemy to make it pure—free of adulteration, waste, and husk. Digestion and transformation take place in four stages. The first is in the stomach, though it begins in the mouth. We know this because chewed wheat can bring boils and abscesses to a head in a way that wheat boiled in water cannot.[^V08-199] The first digestion is complete when the food reaches the stomach and is cooked by its blazing heat, which, when asked, “Are you full?” replies, “Is there any more?”[^V08-200]

The body's powers, placed in nature's service, purge the food of the sins of its waste. They refine what has strayed from obedience to God and from the balanced unity that is nature's straight path for governing bodies according to divine wisdom.[^V08-201] The food becomes like a thick, milky liquid called chyle. The second stage begins when these powers have finished their work in the body's lower region and the chyle rises from that dark place to another: the liver. There, other powers and keepers take over and cook and dissolve it again. Some of its remaining impurities and waste fall away, and it becomes the four humours, partly distinct and partly mixed—“they have mixed a good deed with a bad.”

Of these four, the best is the substance called blood. Some of it passes along the vessels called mesenteries toward the heart.[^V08-202] There it follows the soul's command and undergoes training, exertion, and purification for a time, until it is ready to receive the soul's form. The rest travels through the veins to the organs, where it is digested once more. After its remaining waste is removed, it takes on the likeness of the organs. Each portion of blood becomes like a particular organ, joins it, and is drawn to it, just as the portion fit for the heart is drawn there. Each part is drawn to what resembles it.

The waste products correspond to the four stages of digestion. Waste from the first stage, in the stomach, is excreted as stool through the intestines. Most waste from the second stage leaves as urine through the bladder; the rest is carried off by the spleen and gall bladder. Waste from the third stage leaves in sweat and through other visible openings, such as the nose, ears, and eyes—as tears—or through openings we cannot see, such as the pores. Some waste from the fourth stage leaves as semen. The rest leaves through what grows beyond the body's needs—hair, nails, dirt, and other matter expelled by the body's nature, such as burst abscesses and the like.

#### Chapter 5 — What the Powers of Nourishment and Growth Really Are

You have learned from our approach that a definition can explain what a thing is or explain its existence. We have already distinguished these questions. We may explain what a thing is by naming the broader kind it belongs to and what distinguishes it from other members of that kind. But existence itself has no parts, so it cannot be explained in that way. If an existence is complete, it is defined through its agent and its end; if it is incomplete, through its action. As we indicated earlier, the definition of an existence and the proof that it exists are one and the same. A proof that explains why something exists is stronger than one that shows only that it exists.[^V08-203]

On this basis, every power is a simple instance of existence, and powers can be identified by what they do. We identify the nutritive power by its effect: it changes food into something like the body it nourishes, replacing what the body has lost. Every power is a source of change in this world. Each change has a form and a material, and each action has an end. Here, the form is food's change into something like the nourished body; the material is the food; and the end is replacing what has worn away. In effect, we have said that the nutritive power performs this action, in this place, for this end.

The growth power, by contrast, is defined as the power that increases a natural body's dimensions, in natural proportion, until it reaches its full growth, by adding to its original parts. “Increasing a body's dimensions” rules out artificial changes: if a craftsperson adds to a candle's length and width, its depth must shrink, and the reverse is also true. “In natural proportion” rules out abnormal swelling, such as dropsy and other tumours. “Until it reaches its full growth” rules out getting fat. And “adding to its original parts” points to the difference between growth and fattening.[^V08-204]

A clarification about the real reason for this last difference: growth is a movement in the very substance of the organs, so the power that moves them nourishes and increases their substance. Fattening, however, comes from foreign matter entering the organs and adhering to them. Nourishment and growth each involve three things: first, obtaining food that is potentially like the body being nourished or grown; second, joining it to that body, as is commonly thought; and third, making it actually like the body. If the amount added to the original substance of the organs equals what has worn away, that is the work of the nutritive power. If it is greater, that is the work of the growth power.

At this point, someone may object: nourishment and growth are really one power. The only difference is that the nutritive power adds as much as has worn away, while the growth power adds more. The difference between them is therefore only a matter of greater or lesser strength. If a power is strong enough to do something, it is strong enough to do the same thing again. So if the nutritive power can restore the original part, it should also be able to add another part like it. When this power is especially strong, it should both replace what was lost and add to it. At the start of life, it is strong enough to do both; later, it weakens and replaces only what was lost.

The fact that the nutritive power supplies less than what is lost in old age, but supplied as much as was lost in middle age, seems to support this objection. Its supply is greater in middle age than in old age, so one power may supply different amounts at different times. This is a strong objection.

I answer that different natural ends show that the powers themselves differ.[^V08-205] The end of nourishment is to repair what has been lost through bodily change. We see this when an animal does not seek food until hunger's heat has taken hold in the stomach and the body has been depleted. Then it desires food. The end of growth is to extend the organs in particular proportions. Adding more food than the body loses does not always produce growth—or even growth at all.[^V08-206] Sometimes the body takes in more food but grows less; at other times, the reverse is true. We often see boys and girls grow rapidly during puberty even while a serious illness has weakened their nutritive power and prevented it from doing its work. So if these two powers sometimes produce opposite results, they must differ in their very nature, not merely in strength. If the difference were only greater or lesser strength, they could differ only in degree. But they can differ in opposite ways: growth may increase while nourishment decreases, or nourishment may increase while growth decreases. They differ in what they do and in what each is for. That establishes our point.

Here is a doubt, and a closer look at it. Some people claim that the nutritive power is fire. They argue: “The nutritive power nourishes, and fire nourishes, so the nutritive power is fire.”[^V08-207] Apart from the faulty form of the argument—which draws a conclusion from two affirmative statements in a pattern that does not allow it—the claim that fire nourishes is false. Fire does not nourish; it arises and rises by its nature. Once it rises, cold air takes hold of it and destroys it. There is no single fire that is being nourished. Still, the work of the nutritive power resembles fire's work in maturing and transforming things.

Others say that there are empty spaces in the organs which the growth power fills. This is false, as we have already indicated: filling a gap does not make an organ grow. Nor do we accept the account Avicenna gives in *The Healing*, and some scholars approve: that the growth power separates the parts of an organ, then food particles enter the gaps between them. The same explanation is also offered for nourishment.

When parts are separated, the result is inherently painful. It is impossible that nature or the soul should carry out a natural action that is inherently at odds with the body it inhabits—one that would cause constant pain if there were a power that sensed it and the sensing were actually taking place. As we have already discussed, separation of parts is inherently painful. A single power cannot cause or require two opposing effects.

The truth, as we have established, is that growth is a continuous movement in quantity. Change means that an ability which has not yet been realized gradually becomes real. Here, the process is gradual because, at every moment, the thing that moves has a different instance of quantity. In growth, there is no fixed amount to which another amount is added, whether by matter entering the organ or by any other means.

#### Chapter 6 — Why the Powers of Nourishment and Growth Stop, and Why Death Is Necessary

First, let us present the arguments people have made on this subject. Then we will set out a general principle from divine metaphysics and philosophy as a whole, which God has taught us and which will clarify this question and others like it.

The usual explanation for why growth stops is this: bodies are formed from blood and semen, so every newborn is moist, as we see in children before birth. Gradually, dryness takes hold. You have learned that growth is thought to happen only when the organs stretch, as food passes through their pores. This is possible only while they remain soft. Once they harden and dry out, growth stops. So growth continues from birth until the organs have hardened and dried; then the growth power stops. That is the usual explanation.

As for why the nutritive power stops and death follows, several arguments have been offered. The first is that the nutritive power is bodily, so its actions must be limited. But this is contradicted by the heavenly souls, which are bodily powers—especially according to Aristotle and his followers, who say that these souls are present in their bodies—yet their actions are supposedly unlimited. Avicenna's reply was that, although they are bodily, the light of a separate intellect shines on them,[^V08-208] enabling them to act without limit. His student Bahmanyār wrote to ask: if you allow this, why could bodily powers not also receive the ability to act without limit from that separate intellect? Avicenna answered that this is impossible because the body is made up of opposing elements. From his answer we can see that merely insisting that bodily powers must have limited action does not settle the matter. We must examine what follows from the body being made up of the basic elements. The second argument is this:[^V08-209] in *The Canon*, Avicenna says that after the age when growth stops, the body's natural moisture must keep diminishing until it disappears altogether. Heat gradually consumes this moisture; once it is gone, the body's natural heat must also die out. That is when death occurs.

We say that this moisture must diminish for three reasons. First, the surrounding air acts on it with its heat and dries it out. Second, the body's natural heat helps to dry it from within. Third, the movements of the body and soul add to the effect. Someone may ask: why can the nutritive power not replace the moisture as it wears away? We answer: suppose that in old age this power supplies as much as it did in youth. Even so, more moisture is consumed in old age than in youth. So what the nutritive power supplies is less than what is lost. The body must then keep losing moisture, which leads to decay and death. The older a person becomes, the stronger the effect of these three drying forces: dryness increases, heat decreases, and the nutritive power weakens further. If this continues, it eventually stops altogether, and death follows.

The objector may reply that the amount consumed was once equal to the amount supplied in youth. If supply and loss are no longer equal in old age, either the consuming forces have grown stronger, so that more is consumed, or the nutritive power has weakened, so that it supplies less. The first option is false, since there are only the three causes just mentioned: internal heat, external heat, and bodily and psychic activity. These causes were present in youth just as they are in old age. If the consuming forces have not increased, then the amount consumed cannot have increased either. The second option is also false, since the nutritive power weakens only when heat decreases, and heat decreases only when moisture diminishes. If we then say that the moisture diminishes because the nutritive power has weakened, we reason in a circle, which is impossible.[^V08-210]

One might reply that the consuming forces in old age are the same three forces that were present in youth, but they act for longer. A weaker force may have a stronger effect than a stronger one if it acts for longer. So the drying forces may act longer in an old person than in a young one and cause more dryness. This argument has no weight.[^V08-211] The nutritive power also works for longer, just as the forces that cause dryness do. If a drying force increases dryness by acting for longer, the nutritive power should likewise increase the body's nourishing moisture by acting for longer.

A view close to this one is attributed to Socrates. He said that the natural heat in semen, once it reaches the womb, acts like the heat of an oven on a loaf that sticks to it. The oven first works on the surface until a kind of crust forms. Then its heat enters through the crust and works the inside into shape until the loaf is cooked. In the same way, the heat in semen first forms a crust. It then spreads through the growing body in proportion to its length, width, and depth. If the body's substance has little moisture, the formative power completes its shape in six months. If it has abundant moisture, the body takes longer to form, sometimes as long as 304 days. A newborn is still dominated by moisture, which is why it cannot stand upright or move about. The natural heat that the Creator has placed within it gradually dries the moisture in its organs. First it becomes ready to sit; then it can move without standing; then it can stand; then it can walk. These differences, too, depend on the gradual loss of moisture, which is why children begin to walk at different ages.

The natural heat continues this work in an animal's body until its moisture is completely used up. The heat then goes out because it has lost what sustained it, and death follows.[^V08-212] The cause of death is thus the same as the cause of life. Life would not occur if heat did not prevail over moisture; yet as heat prevails, the moisture is depleted, and when it is gone, the heat also disappears. God has measured out the heat so that it overcomes moisture, first bringing life and later bringing death. This is what is reported in the commentary on *The Canon*. We have already discussed the weaknesses in this view. It deserves careful attention, though: when examined fully, it proves sound because it leads back to the principle we will establish.

The third argument, found in the works of careful investigators, is this: the cause of death in all animals is that the replacement supplied by the nutritive power, though enough to replace what is lost and even more than enough in quantity, is not enough in quality.

The explanation is that the body's original, innate moisture first matured in the vessels that hold food, then in the vessels that hold semen, and finally in the womb. The replacement supplied by the nutritive power matures only in the first of these, not in the other two. It is therefore not fully mixed or matured, and does not reach the quality of what it replaces. It cannot fully take its place. Its strength is less than that of the original moisture, like replacing oil in a lamp with water. As long as the original quality prevails over the acquired quality in the mixture, natural heat grows stronger and supplies more than is lost, so the mixture grows. But once the second quality appears and weakens the first, the heat stops and can no longer supply more than what is lost.

When the acquired quality prevails, the mixture declines and the heat weakens, until nothing remains of the original quality. Then death necessarily follows. This shows that the original, innate moisture begins to decline in quality from the moment it first forms, and that this is what eventually spoils the mixture. It also shows that even if the nutritive power were unlimited and supplied replacement for what was lost without end—not in a fixed amount, but in proportion to what was lost—the replacement still could not match the original in quality, even if it matched it in quantity.

I say that this argument, too, is weak and persuasive rather than conclusive, for several reasons.[^V08-213] First, we do not accept that semen has a stronger and finer temperament than the animal materials formed later. If its temperament were more balanced and finer, the soul's powers would flow into it from the beginning, and it would be alive and able to speak. That is plainly false. Second, the material of semen is not present as separate parts in the animal's body,[^V08-214] some with the temperament of semen and others with the temperament of the replacement food, as this argument assumes. So one cannot say that semen's temperament is stronger while the replacement is weaker.

Third, we do not accept that proper maturation and fermentation, or a strong temperament, depend on their having occurred in those particular vessels.[^V08-215] A temperament may arise elsewhere and be firmer and stronger than one formed in those vessels. We see this in animals that do not come from parents but arise in other ways; some formed this way have a stronger temperament than those produced by reproduction. So the temperament of the replacement supplied by the nutritive power could be stronger and firmer than the temperament of what is lost. This argument therefore fails to prove that death is necessary.

These are the three arguments people have offered for the necessity of death, along with the objections to them. Avicenna points to all three in the general discussion of *The Canon*. He says that after growth stops, heat must begin to decrease because the surrounding air draws moisture from its source; the body's natural heat helps from within, and bodily and psychic movement—necessary for life—helps as well. Nature cannot resist this forever, because all bodily powers are limited and therefore cannot keep supplying replacement without end. If this power were unlimited, it could supply what is lost continuously, not in one fixed amount. But loss does not stay constant; it increases day by day. The replacement cannot keep pace, and the loss consumes the moisture. Moreover, another cause helps extinguish the heat: foreign moisture, which keeps arising when digestion fails. It puts the heat out in two ways: it smothers it, and it gives the body a cold, phlegmatic quality. This is natural death.

From “We must understand” through “bodily and psychic movement necessary for life,” Avicenna points to the second argument. From “Nature cannot resist this” through “cannot keep supplying replacement without end,” he points to the first. From “If this power were unlimited” through the end, he points to the third.[^V08-216]

The fourth argument is that if individual human beings lived forever, those who came before us would have used up the matter from which we are formed, leaving none for us. Even if matter remained, there would be no room or provision for us. If we and those who come after us remained forever nonexistent while earlier people alone continued to exist, that would go against wisdom: they have no greater claim to eternal existence than we do. Justice requires that everyone have a share in existence. So those who exist first must die, allowing those who come later to exist. This argument, too, is merely persuasive and weak: it does not show that every individual must die.

The fifth argument is that if death were not necessary, an unjust tyrant could remain in the world forever, continuing to do evil and spread corruption, while those he wronged would have no hope of justice. That would surely lead to disorder. This argument is also weak: it does not show that everyone in the world must die. A tyrant's existence may have some purpose. And just as a whole class of tyrants may continue to exist in this world—as we see and know—so may one tyrant or several. The wronged can receive justice in ways other than the tyrant's death.

The sixth argument is that if death and the afterlife were not necessary, the devout and good would be the most miserable of people, since they would have given up worldly pleasures without receiving anything in return. That would encourage people to sin, pursue pleasure, and turn away from everything else, bringing evil and disorder. This argument, like the two before it, treats death in terms of incidental ends. None of these is a primary cause or an essential end. We are looking for an essential cause that can serve as a premise in a proof. It must be either a proximate efficient cause, as in natural philosophy, or an essential end, or a remote efficient cause, as in divine metaphysics. The previous three arguments are neither natural efficient causes of death nor essential final causes. They are only incidental consequences, which cannot serve as the basis of a proof—only of persuasive speech and the like.

These matters follow from death's true end; they are not that end itself. Death's true end is the existence of the everlasting world and the arrival of souls at their own stations, whether levels of happiness or depths of misery. Death does not happen primarily so the earth's matter will remain for those who come later, or so the wronged may take revenge on the tyrant, or so the righteous may be repaid for their pain and hardship in this world. Those are consequences of death's true end.

Now that you have seen the weakness of these arguments, let us offer one that proves the necessity of death. From the standpoint of reason, possible beings are either originated or generated—in another wording, complete or incomplete.[^V08-217] An originated being remains, because both the cause that brings it into being and its ultimate goal remain. A generated being, however, must perish. Its complete cause includes motion and time, and every motion must come to an end. When the cause, or even one part of it, ends, the effect must also cease. So everything generated must perish.[^V08-218] Animal bodies are plainly generated and continually renewed; they must therefore perish and die.

Complete beings remain because they were not made to serve as matter or as a means for another being. For this reason, each kind of them consists of a single individual. Incomplete beings, by contrast, naturally give rise to another being. They are prepared causes for other beings. If they remained forever, they would not be incomplete but complete—and that is a contradiction.

Understand that death is natural,[^V08-219] not because bodies cannot last forever. As you have learned, they may continue through change and an influx from above.[^V08-220] Nor is natural death caused by the nutritive power's inability to supply food forever in the right amount or quality; we have already shown the weakness of such claims and others like them. Death is natural because powers and souls are always turning toward, and moving from, one level of existence to another. Divine metaphysics has shown that natural motions and actions have ends.

In our discussion of causes, we proved that the true goal of an action must bring the agent itself to a fuller state of existence. In truth, the end belongs to the agent. An incidental end is a benefit that follows from the true end. For example, sexual desire results in children, and the heavenly powers bring about new beings; these are incidental ends.

The true end of the plant powers is to become food for animals; the true end of animal powers is to provide food and other things for human beings. As for human powers, the body's motive power aims to obtain material for nourishment and growth, while the perceptive power aims to provide material for the intellect or the soul's powers: imagined forms, estimated meanings, and the first principles of reason. Once the human soul has developed the powers that were previously only possibilities—whether toward angelic intellectual happiness or toward satanic, predatory, or animal misery—it naturally leaves this world for another.[^V08-221]

When the soul leaves the body, death occurs. This is the natural term of life referred to in scripture: “Every soul will taste death.” It is different from sudden death caused by chance events or forced disruptions. In general, death is natural for the body: the soul parts from it by nature and stops using it because its life has reached a new, fuller state. It may become happy, joyful, and content in itself, like the angels; miserable, consumed by the fire of God that reaches the heart, like devils and evildoers; or forgiven, cleansed of its inner aims and veils. Other animal souls are consumed by the fires of nature for as long as the heavens and the earth endure, except those God wills to save from suffering and punishment.[^V08-222]

The world is like a farm, and women's wombs are like fields. “Your women are a field for you.” Semen in the wombs is like seed in a field; birth is like a plant emerging; the days of youth are like growth; middle age is like ripening; and old age is like drying. After these stages comes the harvest: death. The grave and the path are like the threshing floor.

On the threshing floor, crops of every kind are gathered, trodden, and sifted. The husks, straw, and leaves are separated from the fruit and grain; some are made fodder for animals or fuel for fires, while some are kept as sound grain. In the same way, all peoples, from the first to the last, will be gathered in the afterlife: “The first and the last will all be brought together at the appointed time of a known day.” Secrets will be laid bare, and God will separate what is vile from what is good. He will pile the vile together and cast it into Hell; He will rescue those who were mindful of Him, so that no harm will touch them and they will not grieve. All this is drawn from several passages in God's noble Book.

The Prophet also said, “This world is the field of the next.” This is the wisdom of death, shown by proof and by the Qur'an. Death is a mercy from God to His friends and a gift to the believer. That is why God's friends long for death. God rebukes those who falsely claim to be among them: “Say: ‘You who are Jews, if you claim that you alone are God's friends, apart from all other people, then wish for death, if you are truthful.’”

Human souls, and other souls too, naturally long for death. They are like craftsmen, while bodies and limbs are like workshops, and the body's powers are like tools. A craftsman works in a workshop for a purpose. When the work is complete and he has reached his aim, he leaves the workshop, sets down his tools, and rests. Likewise, when souls have completed what was required of them in union with the body, and each has developed its capacities in the way it was made to, they turn to themselves. The body then becomes a burden and keeps them from returning to their own homes and sources. Souls have their own sources, like mines of gold and silver, as the hadith says. Death, then, is wisdom and mercy from God, as He says: “Then He causes you to die, then gives you life; then to Him you will return.” The father of the prophets, Abraham, peace be upon him, said: “He is the One who causes me to die, then brings me to life.”

#### Chapter 7 — What We Need to Know About the Formative Power

Let us first present what has been said about this power, then explain what we take to be true. One of the leading later philosophers says: “In my view, the formative power is unreal, because these actions cannot come from a power that has no awareness.” We have already explained how powers are aware of themselves and of what they do. Another thinker before him said that these actions, and similar ones usually assigned to plant powers, come from angels.[^V08-223] Once you understand the kinds of angels God has and how they act by His permission, you will see that the highest ranks are too exalted to carry out such lowly actions directly, without the help of servants and assistants. We have also shown that powers which directly perform changing physical actions must themselves be bodily, while the powers that understand truths applying to many things, wise purposes, and general benefits exist apart from bodies. So the question returns to the powers that act directly: how do they form and shape things? Most theologians, such as the Ashʿarites, assign all these acts to God alone, without a mediator or a specific cause, since they allow an act to be preferred by the will without any reason for that choice. You have already seen the problem with this view.

Others, such as the Muʿtazilites, say that one act produces another.[^V08-224] This, too, conflicts with the unity of all action under God. Here is the real difficulty.[^V08-225] It seems impossible that the formative power could produce the organs—their shapes, sizes, positions, roughness, smoothness, hardness, and softness—because semen is uniform in its parts. The power in it must spread through every part. But one power working on one uniform material can produce only one uniform effect. The shape it produces should therefore be a sphere. If there is one formative power in the animal, the whole animal should be one sphere. If there are several powers, each acting on a different part of the uniform material, the animal should be a collection of spheres. This objection has been strengthened by the following disagreement.

Aristotle and his followers say that semen is uniform in all its parts because it comes only from the testes, not from the whole body: each small portion of it has the same name and definition as the whole. Hippocrates and his followers say that semen is not uniform. They say it comes from the whole body: from flesh comes something like flesh, from bone something like bone, and so on for every part. On this account, the organs in semen are unlike one another because they come from organs that differ in kind. They are also unlike the whole, as is plain if this account is accepted. The whole would not be uniform throughout, but would be a mixture of different parts that the senses cannot distinguish, though they remain distinct in reality.

Explaining the animal's different organs by saying that the parts of semen differ does not help solve the problem. It brings back the very difficulty this view was meant to avoid, and adds another impossibility: that semen is already a complete animal with all its organs.[^V08-226] If the parts of semen differ because they come from different organs, and those organs differ because their parts differ, then the difference among the animal's organs is explained by the difference among the animal's organs. This leads either to circular reasoning[^V08-227] or to an endless chain of causes.[^V08-228] Both are impossible.[^V08-229]

Know that it is a poor argument to explain different shapes and forms by matter alone, or by the material part of a thing.[^V08-230] Matter is indeterminate in every case. It has no substance of its own apart from the form it receives. So saying that the semen causes the different shapes of an animal's organs is just as impossible as saying that matter, considered before it has any particular form, causes all the different forms in the world. Matter in that state can only receive forms; it cannot make something actual, single it out, or make it necessary.

Some people have tried to prove that semen is not uniform by using the same arguments as Hippocrates and his followers, as Avicenna reports in the eleventh chapter of the eleventh treatise of *The Healing*. He says they relied on three points. First, pleasure is felt throughout the body during ejaculation; if semen did not come from the whole body, the pleasure would be confined to the organ from which it came.[^V08-231] Second, resemblance between parent and child is general: if every organ did not send a portion, the child would resemble the parent only in one organ. Third, a child sometimes resembles a parent in a damaged or unusual organ, or has an extra part. They say that if semen is made of different kinds of matter, what comes from flesh forms flesh, and what comes from bone forms bone. The animal should not have to be a sphere.

There are three answers. First, as we have already indicated, this brings back circular reasoning or an endless chain of causes. Second, as we have also said, matter is what a thing can become. In itself it is indeterminate and unclear; it only becomes actual through something that singles it out. The question is precisely what causes the animal's matter to take on the different forms of its organs. Saying that the parts of semen differ does not answer this. If we suppose that semen is made of different parts, we must still ask what caused them to differ. And any compound made of unlike parts must contain simple parts that are alike. Each of these parts must have one simple power working on simple matter. Such a power can produce only one uniform effect. It must therefore produce a sphere, so that the animal formed from semen would be several spheres joined together.[^V08-232]

Third, Aristotle questioned whether these arguments are sound and whether semen really comes from all the organs. He gave ten arguments for his view in *The Healing*; they are also quoted in the commentary on the general parts of *The Canon*. First, a child can resemble a parent in the nails, though no semen comes from the nails.[^V08-233] Second, a child may resemble a distant ancestor, though no seed from that ancestor remains in the family. It is said that a dark-skinned woman gave birth to a white girl, who later gave birth to a dark-skinned child.

Third, semen is not sent out by the body's compound organs as organs, even though it is those organs that resemble one another.[^V08-234] Fourth, if semen were as described, it would already be an animal, because it would contain part of every organ. If these parts were arranged in their proper places, semen would be a tiny human being.[^V08-235] If they were not arranged, what arranged them?

Fifth, if a woman releases semen when the man does, then two kinds of semen enter the womb; that would mean two human beings are formed there.[^V08-236] Sixth, if a woman's semen already contained separately formed organs, what would prevent her from conceiving when she released semen without a man? The answer to this point also answers the fifth. Seventh, a person may first have male children and later have female children. This is due to a change in temperament, not because the semen once carried male organs and later carried female organs.[^V08-237] If temperament can account for the difference between male and female, it can account for other bodily differences too, without the parts being transferred from each organ.

Eighth, many animals are produced from a different species.[^V08-238] Ninth, one mating may produce several young, sometimes both male and female. Tenth, a branch taken from a tree that has not yet borne fruit can be planted and later bear fruit. It should not be able to do so if it came from a tree that had not borne fruit and if every part of the branch were already a matching part of that tree's fruit. The supporters of the mixture theory might answer that the parts of the future fruit are mixed into the branch. If so, the same could be true of animals. One would not need seed from every part of the body; a single part would suffice, since it contains all the parts.

These arguments show that resemblance is not caused by different forms in the material. It comes from the formative power, which works according to the readiness of the material prepared by the changing power. These are the arguments Avicenna reports from Aristotle for the view that semen is uniform in its parts. There is room to question most of them, as the commentator al-Shirazi does in his commentary on the general parts. Perhaps Aristotle did not mean them as proofs. He may have wanted to answer the arguments for semen being unlike in its parts with other arguments based on the same kinds of evidence, supporting the view that it is uniform.

I say that the truth, established by proof, goes further than Aristotle's view. Semen is not, by itself, a fully formed substance of its own kind, like a mineral or another complete natural substance.[^V08-239] It cannot remain as it is for even an hour, as other waste fluids can. Its condition is like that of first matter: its substance is incomplete until forms are added to it, and through them it becomes a particular kind of thing and is changed into that thing.[^V08-240]

Fakhr al-Din al-Razi argues in *The Investigations* that semen has different parts. He says it is the waste product of the last stage of digestion. That stage occurs when the blood has matured and is fully ready to become the substance of the organs. This is why losing semen weakens a person more than losing ten times as much blood: it draws on the original substances of the organs. If so, semen must consist of parts, each close to becoming a particular organ. This would mean its mixture is uniform, but its parts are not.[^V08-241]

This argument, too, is weak for several reasons. First, as you have learned, the food that actually nourishes an animal is not the food that enters the body from outside, nor is it the food that has merely changed through digestion. In truth, an animal is nourished by what comes from within itself and is strengthened by its own inner power. What people call foods and medicines[^V08-242] are only things that prepare the body's matter to be acted on by its own source, in a way suited to it. This is like the difference between what is potentially understood and what is actually understood. What is called potentially understood never becomes an actual object of thought on its own; it only prepares the soul to become actually thinking and to have something actually understood. Likewise, flesh and bone do not become what they are simply because food has entered the body.

A ligament, too, becomes a ligament according to the soul's patterns, qualities, parts, and powers. These forms pass from the world of spiritual meanings into the world of bodily shapes through the movements and changes that prepare the food and other matter.

Second, even if semen is the product of the fourth digestion, why must waste be the same as, or close to, what it replaces? Is animal dung like the animal? Is human sweat like a human being?[^V08-243]

Third, suppose semen, while still joined to the organ-forming power, was like the organs or nearly ready to become them. Once it leaves those organs and moves to another place, we cannot assume it keeps the same arrangement. What preserves its parts in their order, position, and quality?[^V08-244] If the parents' souls preserve them, how can one soul naturally govern and preserve more than one body? A soul does not act throughout any body other than its own. If it acted in the semen, the semen would no longer be semen; it would be an animal with a soul. Nor does weakness after ejaculation show that semen has different parts.
