The weakness has a psychic cause:[^V08-245] bodily pleasure affects the soul, and the resulting weakness spreads through the body, as fear and other states of the soul can disturb it and weaken its powers and main organs. In short, semen is matter that can receive the organs. A thing that can receive something must be free of that thing and its opposite; otherwise it would already have what it is meant to receive, or it would contain opposites at once. Both are impossible.

Semen is also a flowing moisture. Such a fluid cannot contain different arrangements and qualities in separate parts, clearly enough to make it a means for producing different forms and effects. We have already seen that differences in readiness and lack of readiness are possibilities and relations; they do not exist on their own, but only depend on other things.

Before we prove that plants and animals have a formative power, you must understand one more point. What we ascribe to matter and its readiness can only concern particular details: specific times, places, and positions. But regular features shared by every member of a kind must come from causes capable of producing them across that whole kind.

With that established, we can see that the different shapes, structures, sizes, and positions of organs—and the particular shape each organ has for the benefit of human beings and animals—cannot be explained by the material of semen, for the reasons already given. This is especially clear in the organs of the head: the eye and its layers, nose, mouth, teeth, tongue, lips, and all their uses. Some are needed for survival; others bring ease, excellence, or beauty, such as eyelashes, dark eyebrows, and the arch of the foot.

Nor can these features come from a lower power such as bodily nature. Nature acts in one pattern, toward one or at most two ends. It cannot account for the many purposes, motives, and aims found in a body. These forms must come from powers of the soul, guided by higher purposes that lead to the world of intellect and the divine mystery.

Let us put the whole question in general terms. The active cause of an animal's body must be either something without knowledge or awareness, such as bodily nature and similar powers,[^V08-246] or something that has knowledge and awareness. The first option is impossible: every sound human nature knows that the maker of this wonderful order and firm arrangement cannot be a power with no awareness. The second option is either God acting directly, without a mediator, or some other cause.

The first is impossible, as shown by decisive arguments and by revealed teachings. God's essence is too exalted to perform particular, changing, perishable acts. Anyone who attributes such changes and actions to Him has not understood the Lord's true nature or what it means to be divine; they have not given God the reverence due to Him. They have also misunderstood what it means to act and cause. Every effect exists through its nearest cause, as light exists through what gives light and speech through a speaker—not as a building exists through a builder.

Anyone deeply versed in knowledge of God, which reaches beyond the study of physical nature, knows that all beings come from God, without time or place, through powers, souls, and natural processes that He has set to work. God gives life, causes death, provides for creation, guides some and misleads others. But the angel who directly brings life is named Israfil; the angel who brings death is named Azrael, taking souls from bodies, bodies from food, and food from the earth. The angel who provides sustenance is Mika'il, who knows the measure and amount of food. The angel of guidance is Gabriel. The agent of misguidance is not an angel but a devilish being named Azazil. Each of these angels has helpers and hosts of powers placed under God's command, as do His other acts. If God were the direct agent of every lowly act, He would have created the means that carry out His commands without purpose. Exalted is He above creating anything useless or leaving it inactive; this is what those who deny Him suppose.[^V08-247]

If the cause is not God acting directly, it must be either a being that knows and exists without matter—one of the higher intellects or exalted angels—or a soul. The first is impossible for the reason already given: their actions do not change or multiply into separate instances across time. These beings are not separate in their essence from the First Reality. They are rays of His sun and lights of His light; their being is lost in His being, and their lights are gathered into His light.

As for the soul as the cause of the body, it either acts by choice, after a purpose, will, and knowledge beyond its own being, or it does not. The first is false. Even now, with all our knowledge, we do not know the shapes, sizes, positions, and structures of our organs until we study anatomy. How could we have known and chosen these things at the very beginning of our existence? It remains that the soul is the cause of the body and its organs. But the soul either acts independently or in obedience to a command from God and His unseen realm, as a servant. It cannot act independently, because it does not exist independently. How much less could it create? When the organs are taking shape and their forms are first appearing, the human soul is at its weakest and least developed. At that time, a person has no control over their own benefit or harm, life or death, or resurrection.

The cause that shapes these bodies, organs, and arrangements must be a wise maker and an all-knowing guide. So the only possibility left is that the one who creates, shapes, and forms these bodies, nourishes and grows them, and brings them forth is a command from God, using the soul and its powers in obedience to God's will and wisdom. By God's command, the soul passes through different stages. At one stage, as in the womb, it gives suitable forms to matter and bodies. At another, it gives the powers of sensation forms that suit the senses and the things they sense, carrying images of the world of light into the world of darkness. At another stage, it gives inner powers images of imagination and thought.

At a later stage, the soul gives beings the forms of divine truths, meanings, and knowledge. This is how we should understand the account of some wise philosophers about how an embryo forms.[^V08-248] Matter is prepared for one thing: the soul.[^V08-249] But the soul has different organs, needs, and powers that are joined in a certain way. The matter must therefore have different potential capacities,[^V08-250] which unite in a special way: through the body's temperament.[^V08-251]

The powers that arise in it must also have structures that allow them to act. Through these structures, one organ divides into many; the different arrangements of the powers produce the different positions of the organs. It is like the way what comes after the first intellect and active intellect comes from the forms and patterns already present in them. Just as these forms are present in the intellects, the shape of a human being is present in the nutritive power, with the help of matter. This is how the philosopher explains it.

His purpose is to show that these acts can come from the soul through its powers and states, without being impossible or surprising. Every being whose existence proceeds from God, who depends on nothing else contains, within its unity, many qualities, states, and meanings suited to its level and rank. In this, it reflects the First Cause: one, yet containing all divine qualities and names. You have already learned that God is all things without being many.[^V08-252] The closer a being is to Him, the more unified it is and the more meanings it gathers. Each being is, in itself, a world that reflects the world of Lordship.

The human soul gathers within itself all the powers and organs that are spread throughout the body. The different shapes of the organs are shadows of the different powers of perception and movement spread through them, just as the spirit that comes from the heart spreads through the veins and arteries.[^V08-253] Though these powers differ and branch out, they are gathered in the soul and joined with it, as the intellect is joined with what it understands.

In short, lower powers branch out and spread when they flow from higher powers. They then act in different ways to serve the purposes of the powers above them, even if they do not know those purposes. The powers that draw, digest, and expel food act for the soul's purposes as it governs the body, though the powers of drawing and digestion do not know those purposes on their own. They act by following and serving the soul.

The same is true of powers of perception. Hearing and sight serve the soul, and what they perceive is the soul's own perception. The soul itself has hearing, sight, smell, taste, and touch.[^V08-254] That is why it can perceive these things in dreams when the outer senses are inactive.[^V08-255] Each sense, when it acts, serves the soul by its very nature. The formative power works in the same way.

It shapes the organs according to the soul's purposes, needs, and aims.

No one should object that the formative power is bodily and has no awareness of these aims, and ask how it can produce actions that serve them, as though it had been created for these benefits and goals. The answer is that a higher power—the soul—uses it. Someone may reply: “But we know that our souls are not aware of these purposes, benefits, and advantages.”

We answer: while your soul’s abilities are still undeveloped, it is like the formative power, which acts directly in service to a power above it. If your soul developed those abilities fully, the light of insight would show you that it uses all the other powers, its own soldiers and servants, for wise purposes and benefits that protect the individual or the kind, whether in this life or another. When the philosopher said that matter is prepared for one thing, the soul, he meant that matter is semen and that its readiness is remote. Through many changes, this readiness can gradually become near. The only thing that first flows into it from the soul is a form that preserves its structure from decay. But this form can grow stronger and more complete until it reaches a higher level. The same is true at each level.

The philosopher's claim that the soul has different organs, needs, and powers joined in a certain way refers to the soul's different levels and stations. Everything that becomes one of the soul's powers or instruments acts before the user of that power comes into being. Its action prepares it to receive the flow of another form, which will use and rule it, without rebellion. This continues through every stage of the soul. Each power is made by nature to act in a way that does not oppose the aim of what is above it, but moves toward that aim and follows its path.

This answers the objection that, if the formative power belongs to the soul, the instrument would act before the user—the soul—came into being. Before the soul flows into it, the power is not yet an instrument of the soul. It is a form or first completion, like other animal or plant powers that arise before the human soul. When the animal form, or even the earlier plant form, appears in the body's matter, there is no doubt that it acts on its own, as a ruler, not as an instrument.

Later, as it becomes more complete and its being grows stronger, it reaches the rational level and joins with it. Its own being then becomes subject to, and obedient to, the command of God. It performs the same actions it performed before, now in service to this higher being. This is what it means for the powers to be joined in a certain way. The human soul itself understands general kinds and individual things, senses and moves, nourishes, grows, reproduces, and keeps the body's temperament. In short, it itself does all that the inanimate form, plant soul, and animal soul did. By being one, it is all these substances, each of which exists outside it as a separate, complete kind.

When the philosopher says that matter has different potential capacities that unite with it in a certain way—namely, the body's temperament—he does not mean that semen is ready from the beginning for all the powers, organs, and features of the soul. Someone might object that a seed cannot receive the rational soul and its powers until it has become a human body, but it cannot become a human body until those powers and organs already exist.[^V08-256] As we have explained many times, near readiness always follows from forms and qualities that already exist.[^V08-257] What makes a thing ready is not the readiness itself, nor is it the thing that can be received. It must be suited to what it is ready for and lie on the path toward it, as each earlier stage in a movement prepares the next.

The philosopher means that semen is a substance which, through a series of changes, can receive successive stages of readiness.[^V08-258] These changes end in a human body with different organs, all connected to one organ, the heart, whose relation to the other organs is like the soul's relation to its powers.[^V08-259] The readinesses that exist as many and varied powers in the heart and organs all began, while still potential, from one readiness that could receive and give rise to them. This is the temperament of semen. It must be balanced: no one side of its qualities should dominate, and no quality should be fully active at the expense of another. Then it can receive a complete form that gives rise to all the opposing powers—attraction and repulsion, desire and anger, love and dislike, and actions that belong to nature, sensation, or reason. If the matter already had one of these natures or forms in actuality, it could not receive them all. That is why human semen is so weak in its own substance.

When the philosopher says that what came after the first intellect and the active intellect was already present in them, he means that each active power contains, in its complete unity, the realities that flow from it later in detail—just as the sea contains raindrops and a mountain contains small stones.[^V08-260] When he says that the shapes are “imprinted” in the intellects, and so the shape of a human being is “imprinted” in the nutritive power, with the help of matter, he does not mean that the organs are already pictured inside that power. Philosophers agree that it has no awareness. Even the soul, which is stronger, does not know the organs' shapes and arrangements until it studies anatomy. He means that the power contains aspects and directions suited to those shapes, because it serves as an intermediary in the flow of forms from their higher sources.

Do not think that we mean God or the active intellects are places in which forms grasped by the mind are pictured. Our view, which some ancient philosophers also held, is that each actual knower is joined with what it knows. We have explained this carefully, leaving no room for doubt.[^V08-261] Every higher power therefore contains whatever is below it in a more complete and simpler way. Philosophers agree that the creator of all is one being, wholly simple and perfectly one. Yet they also agree that this one being creates bodies and animal organs. See what they say about divine providence, and read Galen's *On the Use of the Parts*, as well as the works of Hippocrates, Plato, and others on anatomy. They all agree that God creates bodies and organs. This does not prevent them from accepting intellectual intermediaries, powers of the soul, and natural instruments, each acting according to God's decree. A wise person does not personally sweep, carry away waste, or work in a rubbish heap. Their own work is to judge, command, guide, decree, send down, and send forth—not to perform every movement and bodily change. Anyone who denies that the intermediaries act according to their nature has not understood God's greatness, as scripture says: “They have not measured God with the measure He deserves,” when they say, “God has sent nothing down to any human being.”

A Further Clarification

Know that the particular forms of physical things have higher realities, shared by many individual things, in the world of God's command and decree. God says: “There is nothing whose stores are not with Us, and We send it down only in a known measure.” These stores are higher realities grasped by the intellect. Each has particular, limited forms that appear through particular causes: specific matter, position, time, and place. Those higher realities exist with God in their own right, while their sensory particulars exist through dependence and inclusion.[^V08-262] These realities, as they exist on that higher level, do not move from their place. Their partial forms descend and take shape in bodies, since they are connected to, and present at, every level. This is like the angels descending to creation at God's command.[^V08-263] A partial form is the same reality in a weaker degree; the difference is one of strength and weakness, completeness and lack.

Consider Azrael: his higher reality stands before God, bears one of the supports of the greatest Throne, and remains in its high station. It does not move from its place. Yet his partial forms and hosts, through the reach and descent of his power into the levels below him, are present within created beings. For every soul that dies, one of these particular forms of his higher reality directly takes that soul. They also carry out tasks that match his work, such as drawing nourishment, freeing knowledge from its sensory forms, and freeing sensed things from their particular matter. That higher reality remains in its original station before God, with one concern never distracting it from another.[^V08-264]

The same is true of Gabriel's higher reality in the world of God's command. It stands before God. Only those who are pure and free of matter can be honored by this closeness to Him. Gabriel receives from his Lord what he sends down through his partial forms, which appear to the prophets and messengers whose inner selves are joined to him. He brings them clear revelation, or teaches and inspires an heir or saint through different forms and instructions. God says: “It is not for a human being that God should speak to him except through revelation, from behind a veil, or by sending a messenger.” And He says: “He was taught by one mighty in power.” “Mighty in power” refers to Gabriel; his powers are the partial forms that appear to the prophets, such as when he appeared to the Prophet as Dihya al-Kalbi.[^V08-265]

The same is true of Israfil, great in his existence before God, bearing the Throne on his shoulders and breathing into the spirit that embraces the whole heavenly sphere, which contains the detailed spirits of all creatures. He gives each particular spirit its detailed spiritual breath through partial forms connected to individual spirits. Yet he remains on his highest horizon before his Lord, never distracted by one concern from another, because the source in him that embraces all these individual forms is above time, change, and material renewal.

Likewise, Mika'il stands before his Lord and receives what he must do: send down rain and provide sustenance at every time. The forms that provide different kinds of sustenance descend from one level to another until they appear through the powers assigned to this work, including human beings and others, shaping the body's different kinds of food. His particular forms carry out these tasks, while his higher reality remains in its place, unchanged and undistracted. The same applies to all the powers of perception and movement. Each kind has different levels, higher and lower, nobler and less noble: some are purely intellectual, some imaginative, some sensory, and some purely bodily.[^V08-266]

Most people fall into serious mistakes because they ignore the rights of each level and look at things with only one eye. Consider the disagreement about the formative power. One group assigns these acts to an unaware bodily power. They deny the rights of God and His closest angels, and their right eye goes blind: they see the world of creation but not the world of God's command. Another group assigns the actions of the formative power—shaping and creating things that change, decay, and come into being—to God Himself. They deny the rights of God's servants and remove from action those He appointed to carry it out. Their left eye goes blind: they try to see God without seeing creation. In the end, they see neither God nor creation, because creation is the path to God. How can someone reach the end after denying the path and leaving the straight way? They will be raised blind in both eyes, though they once had sight and the ability to see, because they did not look at God's signs and wisdom or reflect on His work and care. Their state on the Day of Resurrection will be as God describes: “We will raise him blind on the Day of Resurrection.” He will say, “My Lord, why have You raised me blind when I could see?” God will say, “Our signs came to you, but you forgot them; so today you will be forgotten.”

#### Chapter 8 — A Classification of the Soul's Powers and the Powers Below Them

Classification here means that the powers are not different kinds, each separated from the others by an essential feature, as plant species differ from animal species. They differ instead through features connected with human life: some arise before others, some are used before others. Before they flow out from the human soul and spread through the body, they are like parts of that soul. Each then occupies the place suited to it. The soul first divides into three parts, which are like three separate souls elsewhere: a plant soul, located in the liver; an animal soul, located in the heart; and a human soul, whose rule is in the brain. These souls arise one after another. The plant part comes first, then the animal, then the human. The first two disappear when the body's balance is lost and its readiness ends. The human part remains in the next world. It does not arise from the body's readiness or balanced temperament. It is a divine matter, a sacred secret; its relation to God is like light to its source. The spirit remains through God, as we will explain in its proper place.[^V08-267]

The aim here is to show how the powers are divided and arranged. The human power is served by all the other powers,[^V08-268] and in turn serves the higher intellects: the Active Intellect, the intellect before it, and the intellect after it, called the Acquired Intellect; next comes the Actual Intellect, then the intellect known as the Intellect in Readiness. The animal powers serve the human, and the plant powers serve the animal. The plant soul has three powers, as we have seen: nourishment, growth, and reproduction.

The animal soul also has two powers: one that moves and one that perceives. The moving power is either the one that sets desire in motion or the one that carries out movement. The first is called the appetitive power. It has two branches: desire and anger. The power that carries out movement serves them. It uses the tendons and ligaments attached to the organs. In serving desire, it pulls the tendons toward their source, the heart. In serving anger, it relaxes and stretches them in the opposite direction to push something away.

The animal's perceptive power has two parts: one perceives what is outside, and one perceives inwardly. Inner perception is subtler and nobler; the outer senses are like its husk and bran. The inner power that perceives images includes the power that gathers the outer senses together, imagination, the power that forms images, and the power that combines and separates them. In animals, the last is called the imaginative power; in human beings, it is called the thinking power. The inner power that perceives meanings includes estimation, memory, recall, and recollection.

The human soul has a practical and a knowing power. The practical power manages the body. It is complete when it rules the other animal powers instead of being ruled by them, and directs them according to the judgment of the theoretical power. The knowing power is the theoretical intellect. Through it, the soul connects with beings free of bodies, receives knowledge and truths from them, and is changed by them. The practical power should not be ruled by the body's powers; the knowing power should always receive from what is separate from matter and return to the world of wise remembrance. This gives it a lasting ability to connect with the Active Intellect. Its connection with the Active Intellect and with forms that are fully free of matter begins as a mere possibility. It then reaches readiness when it gains first principles; after that, it understands them in actuality, though not all the time. It can call them to mind when it wishes, without having to learn them again.

When the soul becomes those forms, remains joined to them, and continually sees them in itself or in the source that sustains it, it is called the Acquired Intellect. At this point, the animal kind reaches its full human perfection, attaining every level and form of completion. The Acquired Intellect is the head and master of all the powers. It is served by the Actual Intellect, which in turn is served by the two receptive intellects: the Intellect in Readiness and the Material Intellect. The practical intellect serves all of them, since the theoretical intellect develops after it and is used and completed through it. The practical intellect is served by estimation, which is served by memory. Everything ranked after estimation and, in time, before it—that is, the powers closest to the body—serves memory and imagination.

Imagination itself is served by two powers, one that moves and one that perceives. The appetitive power obeys and serves it; the imaginative power serves it by presenting images stored in the formative power, ready to be combined and separated. The power that gathers the outer senses serves all these powers. It receives what the external senses bring and presents it to the power that combines and separates, so that it can put images together or apart, and to estimation, so that it can consider them and understand their meanings. That shared power serves the external senses by bringing together what each one perceives. Desire serves the appetitive power, while desire and anger use the motor power in the muscles. This is the last level of animal powers, since it is found only in animals.

The plant powers serve the animal powers. Reproduction comes first; nourishment and growth serve reproduction, and nourishment serves both of them. The natural powers—that is, the powers that draw in, retain, digest, and expel—serve the plant powers. Retention serves digestion, while attraction and expulsion serve digestion as well; expulsion serves all the powers.

The four qualities serve all the powers just mentioned. Cold serves heat, either by preparing the material for it or by preserving what heat has produced through change. Moisture and dryness serve all four qualities. These are the lowest powers. Below them is the receptive matter that can receive all forms. This bodily receptive power first supports the extended bodily form, then takes on other forms until it reaches the highest level of completion, like the Active Intellect. Existence begins at the lowest level, flowing down from the highest, then gradually rises again toward the level from which it came. Existence is like a circle that turns back on itself, with its end joining its beginning, as God says: “As He began your creation, so you will return,” and “He is the One who begins creation, then brings it back again.”

When the soul begins to rise and develop, it becomes one with every form it takes on. As these powers rise, they join one body, with one substance and one soul-identity. This continues until the human being becomes a great world containing all existing things, so that a person can see all things as parts of their own being.[^V08-269] You will understand this more clearly if you look at yourself, apart from everything else. You will find that you understand general and particular things, imagine, sense, hear, see, desire, feel anger, love, feel joy, and will—along with countless other qualities. You have learned that forms may be simple or compound, and matter may be simple or compound. But whatever exists as a compound must be joined in some way: what has no unity does not exist.

Multiplicity depends on unity, which is the source, root, return, and end of multiplicity. The heavens and the earth stand through divine unity; the animal body stands through the soul's unity. We came to know the soul and its powers in action much as we came to establish the forms of the elements in first matter and the humours in the body. We saw qualities and quantities in first matter and knew that their forms were other than matter. We saw different effects in the body and inferred that different humours were present. In the same way, we saw psychic actions in certain bodies and inferred that they had principles other than bodies and their qualities. From the links among these principles, and their need for one another, we came to see that they have one unifying source. In truth, this source produces all their actions and effects. It is the root of what they are, the form of their forms, the soul of them all, and their shared identity.

Once we saw that it understands intelligible things, is present to itself, and knows its own identity, it became clear that this is a sacred substance that cannot die or decay.[^V08-270] It remains with God and will be gathered with the angels and prophets, peace be upon them, when its bond with them has grown firm. “How excellent those companions are.”

#### Chapter 9 — Each Body Has One Soul, and All Its Powers Arise from It

Rather, the powers we have listed are details of the soul's own being and expressions of its identity. Know that some people of limited insight saw that human beings have effects like those of natural powers: heat and cold, attraction and repulsion, transformation and ripening, and other actions also produced by the forms of the elements. They saw other effects like those of plants: nourishment, growth, and reproduction. They saw still others like those of animals: sensation, imagination, desire, and anger. Then they saw in human beings speech, understanding, and reasoning. They thought that a human being is made up of a natural form and three other souls: plant, animal, and human.

A slightly more advanced group thought that a human being is the rational soul alone, and that all the other levels are temporary features that belong to it from the start of its life to the end. On this view, the body and its powers are like tools used by a craftsperson. They do not form part of the person's nature or way of existing; they only help complete its actions. This is not the truth.

The truth is that a human being has one identity with different stages and levels. It begins at the lowest level and gradually rises to the level of intellect and what the intellect understands. God points to this when He says: “Has there not come upon the human being a period of time when he was nothing remembered ... whether grateful or ungrateful?”[^V08-271]

If the soul had no bond with the body other than a passing relation of desire—as a person might love a neighbor without becoming one with them—then an unhealthy bodily temperament or a separation of the body's parts would not cause the soul sensory pain, distinct from intellectual or imagined pain.[^V08-272] The human soul is a sacred being from the spiritual world. It has a unified wholeness, a reflection of divine unity. By its very nature, it is an intellect when it returns to its original home. Yet it also contains animal powers at every level, from imagination down to pain, the lowest animal sense. It also contains plant powers, from nourishment at the lowest level to reproduction at the highest, and a natural power of movement that acts through the body.

As the greatest philosopher, Aristotle, says, the soul has three parts: plant, animal, and rational. He does not mean that it is made up of these powers, since it exists as one simple being. He means that its being is complete and gathers these different forms within itself. Though the soul's powers and actions are many and varied, they all exist in it as one being, in a simple, subtle way suited to the soul's subtle nature. The meanings of bodily kinds also exist in the intellect, joined with it, once they rise from the level of what can be sensed to the level of what can be understood. Just as the intellect contains all natural kinds in a higher way, the soul contains all natural, plant, and animal powers in a higher way than they exist in their own places.

In short, the human soul descends from its highest immaterial state to the level of nature, and then to the levels of sense and what is sensed. In touch, it becomes the very organ that touches; in smell and taste, it becomes the one that smells and tastes. These are the lowest senses. When it rises to the level of imagination, it becomes a formative power. It can then rise beyond these levels to the stations of the sacred intellects and unite with each intellect and what it understands.[^V08-273]

Most later philosophers, such as Avicenna and his followers, denied this because they did not build their account of the soul on a sound understanding of existence—its degrees, its sources, and its ends. They were afraid that, if this were true, the soul would be divided; that the Active Intellect would be split into as many parts as there are knowing souls; and that each of our souls would know what every other soul knows.[^V08-274] We have already given a clear proof that lifts the veil from these doubts, thanks be to God, when discussing the union of the knower and what is known.[^V08-275]

#### Chapter 10 — Some Levels of the Soul Arise Before Others

As we have seen, the soul does not begin with all its abilities fully developed. Some appear before others.

While a child is developing in the womb, the soul is at the level of plant life. This stage comes after the powers found in nonliving matter. At that point, the human embryo already has the life of a plant, but it has animal life only as a possibility, not as an achieved state. In the terms of this account, it does not yet have sensation or self-directed movement. What distinguishes it from plants is precisely its capacity to develop those abilities.

After birth, the child's soul reaches the level of animal life, where it remains until bodily maturity. The person has fully reached the level of animal life, but the soul’s higher human abilities have not yet fully developed.

Next comes the ability to think things through, make judgments, and use reason to guide action. Over time, knowledge, habits, and character become more firmly established. Inner maturity of this kind is often reached around the age of forty.

At this stage, the person has become fully human in the life of the soul, but may still develop in either an angelic or a devilish direction. At the resurrection, the person joins either the angels or the devils and their followers. With God's help, someone who follows the path of truth and God's oneness, develops the intellect through knowledge, and frees it from bodily attachments becomes angelic in actuality, joining the angels who are close to God. Someone who follows error and ignorance instead becomes one of the devils, or is raised among animals and insects.

#### Chapter 11 — Another Account of the Human Powers, Following Those with Insight

One of the mystics, explaining God's words, “None knows the forces of your Lord but He,” said that God has forces in hearts, souls, and other worlds. Only God knows their true nature and number. Here we will describe some of the forces of the heart—that is, the rational soul. This is the part that concerns us. It has two armies: one seen by the eyes, made up of the body's organs and limbs, and one seen only by inner sight, made up of powers and senses. All of them serve the heart, which directs them. They are made to obey it and cannot resist or rebel. When it tells the eye to open, it opens. When it tells the leg to move, it moves. When it decides that the tongue should speak, the tongue speaks. The other organs obey in the same way.

The way the organs and senses serve the heart resembles the way angels serve God. They are made to obey Him. They do not disobey His commands; they do what He tells them to do. The heart needs these forces because it needs a vehicle and provisions for the journey for which it was created: its journey to God, passing through each stage until it meets Him. This is why hearts were made. God says: “I created jinn and human beings only so that they may worship Me.” The body is the heart's vehicle, and knowledge is its provision. The means that lead it to knowledge and help it gather provisions and do good are its forces.

First, the heart must care for the body and protect it from harm. It must bring in what suits the body, such as food, and push away what harms or destroys it. To seek food, it needs two forces: an inner one, the power of desire, and an outer one, the body and its organs, which bring food in. God placed many desires in the heart, all under the power of desire, and created the organs that serve those desires.

To push away what harms and destroys it, the heart also needs two forces. The inner one is anger, which helps it repel dangers and fight enemies. The outer ones are the hands and feet, which act on anger's command, along with things outside the body, such as weapons.

But desire for food and the organs that bring it in are no help if the heart cannot recognize suitable food. So it needs two further forces for knowing: inner powers of sight, hearing, taste, smell, and touch, and the outer organs that serve them—the eyes, ears, nose, and others. Explaining in full why each is needed, and the wisdom behind it, would take many books.

The forces of the heart fall into three groups. The first urges and stirs it to act: desire draws it toward what is useful, and anger drives it away from what is harmful. This urging force is also called the will. The second group moves the organs to carry out these aims. This ability is called power. It is spread through the body, especially in the muscles and tendons.

The third group perceives and directs things, like spies.[^V08-276] These powers are spread through particular organs. Each inner power has an outer one: an organ made to serve it. The power of grasping acts through the fingers, and sight works through the eyes. The same is true of the other powers. We are not discussing these visible forces, the body's organs, because they belong to the outward, physical world. Here we are concerned with the unseen forces that support the heart. This third group—the powers of perception—divides into those placed in the outer parts of the body, the five senses, and those placed inside, in the cavities of the brain. These inner powers are also five. These are the groups of the heart's forces.

The same mystic said that the forces of anger and desire may obey the heart completely and help it along its chosen path and on the journey it is making. Or they may resist it like rebels, overpower and enslave it, and bring about its ruin and cut short the journey that would lead it to lasting happiness. The heart has another force: knowledge, wisdom, and reflection. It should call on this force, which belongs to God's side, to help it against the other two, which belong to the devil's side. If the heart does not call on knowledge for help and lets anger and desire rule it, it will surely perish and suffer a clear loss. This is the state of most people. Their reason has become a servant of their desires, finding clever ways to satisfy them. Their desires should instead serve their reason whenever it needs them. That is what the mystic said. It came, I swear, from deep insight and the source of clear understanding.

He and others like him sometimes call the powers of perception “flying powers” and the powers of movement “traveling powers.” Perception reaches its aim more quickly. Some perceptions arrive at once; some are acts of awareness with no waiting or delay. Powers of movement, by contrast, always need time to pass and events to unfold.

Many great scholars have held that the powers' relation to the soul resembles the angels' relation to God. One example is the author of *The Brethren of Purity*. He tells of a king who asked a wise jinn: “How do the angels obey the Lord of the worlds?” The sage replied, “As the five senses obey the rational soul.” The king asked him to explain. The sage said, “Do you not see, O king, that the five senses perceive what they can sense and report what they perceive to the rational soul without needing a command or warning, or a promise or threat? Whenever the rational soul turns to something it can sense, the sense obeys its intention, perceives the thing, and reports it without delay. The angels obey the Lord of the worlds in the same way. They do not disobey God in what He commands them; they do what they are told, for He is the wisest of judges.”

As long as the soul is attached to the body and has not reached full intellectual development, it can direct only the animal powers we have described, and the powers that branch out from them through the body. But once it is complete in knowledge and action, all the intellectual, spiritual, and sensory beings obey it, just as they obey the King of kings.

The mystic known as the author of *The Meccan Openings* says in Chapter 361, while explaining that the complete human being is God's deputy on earth and was created in the image of the All-Merciful, that a report about the people of Paradise says: an angel comes to them. After asking permission to enter, the angel greets them with peace from God and gives each one a letter from God.[^V08-277] It reads: “From the Living, the Sustainer, to the living, the sustained: I say to a thing, ‘Be,’ and it is; today I have made you able to say to a thing, ‘Be,’ and it is.”[^V08-278]

The Prophet, peace be upon him, said: “No one in Paradise says to a thing, ‘Be,’ without it coming to be.” The mystic brought this example to show the reach of the perfect human being. The word “thing” is among the broadest words of all.[^V08-279] Nature can bring bodies and what they contain into being, but it does not have this reach. The soul can bring particular spirits into being in the natural world, but it does not have universal reach either. Only the perfect human being has it, because that person carries the divine secret.[^V08-280] Everything apart from God belongs to the whole that is the human being.[^V08-281]

If you can understand, consider everything apart from God and what God says about it: “There is nothing that does not praise Him,” and all things bow down before Him.[^V08-282] God has given none of them a command or prohibition, rule over the world, or universal power to bring things into being. He has given this to the perfect human being. So if you want to know that person's perfection, look at their ability to command, forbid, and bring things into being without a tongue, a limb, or any other created being. If their command takes effect, they have clear evidence from their Lord of their perfection, since they witness in themselves the power to command, forbid, and bring things into being without a mediator.

Even if the perfect human being uses a limb as an intermediary, their commands may not always take effect, or may take effect in some cases but not in all. This does not lessen their perfection. The same is true when something does not come about through their command by way of another intermediary. This is how the divine image appears in the world.[^V08-283] God commands His servants through the words of His messengers and in His books. Some obey, and some disobey. When the mediators are removed, only a special kind of obedience remains—one that cannot refuse. The Prophet, peace be upon him, said: “God's hand is with the community, and His power takes effect.” This is why, when a person gathers their own powers into one, their intention can carry out what they will. All those who know God agree that this is something they can directly experience.[^V08-284] God's hand is with the united whole: through it the world appears, and individual beings are nothing but Him.[^V08-285] Consider God's words: “There is no private meeting of three without Him being the fourth among them.”

That ends the mystic's account of the human being's unity and role as God's deputy. Praise be to God, who has shown us through clear proof—lifting every veil and answering every doubt—the way to understand what those who know God have experienced directly. Many of the discussions in this book help establish this subtle teaching and other great divine truths, which most thinkers have failed to grasp. Only a few have brought together the learning of careful thinkers and the insight of those who see inwardly. By God's grace, we have brought together direct insight and experience with inquiry and proof. Now let us return to where we left off.

#### Chapter 12 — Which Organ Forms First?

Doctors and natural philosophers disagree about this. Anatomists say that dissection shows the heart is the first organ to form. Hippocrates says it is the brain. Muhammad ibn Zakariya says it is the liver. The anatomists rely on what they have observed, since questions about particular cases cannot be settled by proof alone. Hippocrates and Muhammad ibn Zakariya do not challenge what dissection shows; they rely instead on weak or faulty comparisons.

Ibn Zakariya argues that the embryo needs the powers of nourishment and growth more than it needs the animal and mental powers.[^V08-286] The liver carries out nourishment and growth, so it must be the first organ to form. Fakhr al-Din al-Razi replies that the organs form before they can nourish themselves. Their formation depends on the animal power and the body's natural heat. Since the heart is the source of these, it should form first.

I say that Ibn Zakariya did not mean that the liver feeds organs that have not formed yet.[^V08-287] He means that the first powers to appear in living matter are plant powers, especially nourishment. After the plant stages have passed, the animal powers appear gradually, and then the powers of the soul. If we follow this sequence, the lump of matter with powers of nourishment and growth should first form into a liver, which is taken to be the source of plant powers. The heart forms after it as the source of animal powers. Then the brain forms, since it is the source of perception, the highest of the first two kinds of power.

This is the order if we look at time and natural development as matter becomes more complete, moving from lower to higher and from less to more noble. But if we look at the order of cause and effect, or at priority in dignity and existence, the brain comes first, as Hippocrates said, then the heart, then the liver, and then the other organs. More precisely, the spirits in the three leading organs come first: the spirit linked to the soul's powers in the brain, then the animal spirit in the heart, then the natural spirit in the liver. After these come the nerves; the veins and arteries; the muscles and tendons; the other simple organs; and finally the organs made up of them.

The powers that serve the soul can appear before the soul that uses them, in time, while the soul comes first in the order of being. The rational soul is first in the order of being, though it appears later in time. This is not an impossible circle: the power that appears earlier and prepares the way for the soul is not numerically the same as the power that later flows from it, though both are the same in kind and in their underlying reality. Once you understand this, you will not be surprised that some organs, such as the liver, form earlier in time than another organ, such as the brain, which is higher in the natural order.

An argument for the heart forming first goes like this. The argument says that semen contains much vital spirit, and that warm vapor gives it its heat and thickness, which is why it is so white. Cold should make it thicken and coagulate; yet when cold acts on it, it loses its whiteness and thickness and becomes runny. If so, the first thing to form must be whatever forms most easily and is needed first. Spirit forms more easily than an organ: it is easier for the airy parts in semen to become spirit than to become an organ. Spirit is also needed sooner, to set the formative power in motion and strengthen it. So spirit must form before an organ.

This spirit needs a place where it can gather and be held. Nature cannot leave it to move and grow at random. So the spirit must form first, gather in one place, and be surrounded by the denser parts of semen. No side has a better claim than another to be the place where the spirit gathers. So it must gather in the middle, with the other parts arranged around it like a sphere. That gathering place cannot be the liver, because the organs need nourishment only after they form, as we have already explained against Ibn Zakariya's view. So it must be the place where the body's balance of qualities has become firm: the heart.

I see a problem with this argument. Even if we accept that the first thing to form from the vaporous fluids is spirit, that spirit is easier to form than organs, and the body needs it sooner, there are different kinds of spirit. One kind is natural, made of fine vaporous parts like those found in plants. Another is animal spirit, which belongs to the animal body's balance of qualities. A third is mental spirit, which is more like the bodies of the heavens.

In animals, the natural spirits form first. The liver is the place that gathers and holds them. The body's nutritive power then draws food parts into them, so they are nourished, grow, and develop. As they grow in their being and size, they need a higher, nobler, and stronger place. So another center forms above the first, on the side with more heat. Its spirit is purer, stronger, warmer, and better able to receive life. This is the heart. The other organs form in turn, some to lead and others to serve, until the body is complete in all its powers and organs. It is like a thriving city: its parts are added one by one. It needs an obeyed ruler, citizens who serve the ruler, and homes, animals, and other things that serve the citizens. The ruler may be the last to appear in the work of building, but first in the plan and purpose.

#### Chapter 13 — When the Rational Soul Joins the Body

As we have said, the rational soul reaches its full power of thought—its ability to understand ideas—in most people around the age of forty. But the human soul becomes distinct from other animal souls earlier, through its readiness for intellectual development. Avicenna says that the woman's seed receives a soul when the man's generative power enters it. The spirit seems to form from the man's seed, and the body from the woman's. Once the seed has a soul, that soul begins to complete the body's organs. At this stage, it is only a nutritive soul, since nourishment is the only work it does.

Once the nutritive power is established, it prepares the way for sensory life. The developing body is then ready to receive a soul with the power of sensation, even though in human beings the sensory and rational souls are one. The sense organs form along with this stage, as do the powers of nourishment and the organs that serve them.[^V08-288] Not every animal organ can sense, but every organ must be nourished. So it is possible that the seed receives a nutritive power from the father, while another nutritive power develops later in the child.[^V08-289] The inherited power may remain until the body's balance changes, then join with the child's own nutritive power. It may be strong enough to guide the body's growth for a time but not for the whole process, so another power is needed. The power received from the father may also change. It is no longer the same kind as the nutritive power in either the father or the child, though it may still do work suited to nourishment.

When the heart and brain have formed in a human being, the rational soul joins them, and sensory life flows from it. The rational soul does not depend on physical matter, but it is not yet exercising reason, as in someone who is drunk or unconscious. It reaches completion only through things that come from outside. This is what Fakhr al-Din al-Razi reports Avicenna as saying. I have not found these exact words in Avicenna's books, though they may appear somewhere. In any case, several parts of this account raise philosophical questions.

The first problem is that it makes no sense for a soul joined to one body to give nutritive power to seed that belongs to another body. This is especially clear because seed can remain after the father and his soul have died.[^V08-290] A soul, or any other power tied to a body, does not carry out natural actions in a body other than its own. Instead, the seed receives successive stages of life from the active source. First comes a mineral-like form that holds its parts together and gives it a balanced mixture. Then come plant life, animal life, and the higher stages. The seed's form of life grows stronger until it rises above matter in its being, then in its ability to understand, govern, act, and have effects.

The second problem is this: if the rational soul were already fully formed when the heart and brain first appeared, it would remain inactive for a long time, unable to carry out its proper work and develop its powers, even though nothing outside it prevented it. Philosophical arguments rule this out. This is not like drunkenness, unconsciousness, or sleep. Those states prevent powers already present in the soul from showing themselves; they are caused by something outside the soul's nature. The soul becomes rational only through a long process of inner change.

The third problem is that this account makes the tool appear before the one who uses it. For example, the nutritive power is one of the rational soul's powers in a human being. If it exists before the rational soul, then the tool exists before its user. On our account, the soul's being grows stronger by degrees. It includes the powers it had at earlier stages, along with new qualities and abilities. These powers arise again as the soul grows stronger and more complete.

One philosopher compares this to the way fire affects coal. One piece becomes warm, another turns red and begins to glow, and a third gives light and burns. At the final stage, it acts like fire and does what the earlier stages did, along with more. As the power grows stronger, it produces more effects. A strong light includes weaker degrees of light, though not as a container holds separate parts or a measured thing contains imagined pieces. In the same way, a stronger form of existence gathers what belongs to weaker forms and does more as it grows stronger. Sometimes it rises from a short-lived stage to one that lasts. It then becomes an intermediary between the Active Intellect and the work of governing bodies. We will explain this at length later.

#### Chapter 14 — Why the Soul's Powers Differ

Some say that each organ's nutritive power differs in kind and nature from the nutritive power in every other organ. Otherwise, their actions would not differ in kind. The same, they say, is true of the powers of growth and reproduction, and of the four powers that serve nourishment. These powers share a broad class, but each is a different kind.[^V08-291]

I say that different kinds of immediate effects do require their source power to differ, either in number or in kind. But effects that come through other powers do not require their source to differ in kind or number. The nearest cause of one heavenly sphere differs in kind from the nearest cause of another, but the one God who governs all the spheres has no equal.

The nutritive power in each organ may act differently and in a different place. But the nutritive power in one person—Zayd, for example—is one in reality. It works on one body, serves one aim, and comes from one agent: the soul.[^V08-292][^V08-293][^V08-294] The same is true of the other powers and causes. Different effects, even effects of different kinds, do not always require different causes. A power may be many in one level of existence and one in a higher level.[^V08-295]

The soul is one, yet contains every kind of perception and movement. When its powers appear in the body, they seem to be different kinds. Within the soul, they are gathered into one. The five senses are gathered in the inner sense that brings them together; all acts of understanding are gathered in the theoretical intellect; plant powers such as drawing in and pushing out are gathered in one plant-life power; and all the moving powers spread through the body are gathered in the animal power that urges movement, and in the human practical intellect.

#### Chapter 15 — Why Human Beings Have an Animal Power

Know that a thing of any kind cannot reach a higher, more complete kind until it has first completed the lower, less complete kind beneath it and gained all its powers and needs. This is a universal rule supported by proof.[^V08-296] The nature that reaches the human level and the rational soul must first complete all the conditions of animal life, in both its basic powers and their branches. So human beings must have an animal power. But this does not mean that a person has one fully formed animal soul and another, separate human soul. As we have explained, the human rational soul is the very same soul as the animal soul, differing in meaning and effect, not in number. The rational power in us is the animal power that has reached the rational level. Here, “animal power” means the power that prepares the organs to receive sensation and movement.[^V08-297]

Avicenna does not discuss this power in his philosophical books, such as *The Healing* and *The Salvation*, but he does mention it in *The Canon*. Most doctors say that it exists, but they treat it as a separate power, already fully present and different from the rational soul.

They argue that a paralyzed organ still has a power of the soul. The body's elements tend to come apart, so some force must hold the organ together. This force cannot be part of the body's balance of qualities, they say, because an effect cannot preserve its own cause. It must be a power of the soul that comes before the organ's balance and keeps it from falling apart.

They say this power must be one of three things: the power of sensation and voluntary movement, a plant-life power such as nourishment, or a different kind of power. The first cannot be right, since a paralyzed organ cannot sense or move at will. The second cannot be right either, they say, for two reasons. First, nourishment may stop while this other power remains. Second, this power prepares the organ to receive sensation and movement if nothing blocks them. But plant life also has a nutritive power. If the two were the same, plants would be ready for sensation and movement, which they are not. So, they conclude, this must be a fourth kind of power, different from both animal and plant powers.

I say that this argument does not prove what it sets out to prove and mixes together different issues. The power in a paralyzed organ could simply be the nutritive power of plant life. They say that nourishment has stopped while this power remains. We do not accept that. If nourishment had truly stopped, the organ would quickly waste away.[^V08-298] At most, its nourishing effect is hidden by some obstacle.

They also say that a plant's nutritive power cannot prepare it for sensation and movement. But the nutritive power in a plant differs in nature from the nutritive power in an animal, just as animal life in other animals differs in nature from human life.[^V08-299] The reason is that a form that already fully realizes one kind of life may leave no room for a higher kind.[^V08-300] When a form is strong and complete, it fills out its matter and closes off its readiness to receive a higher form. Pure fire, earth, water, and air, for example, cannot receive plant or animal life. But when their qualities are mixed and their forms grow weaker, their matter becomes ready to receive a higher form. In the same way, the nutritive power in a fully formed plant is too complete to receive animal powers. The animal's nutritive power is less complete, so it can receive powers of sensation and movement.[^V08-301]

Human beings are weak in animal life, as God says: “The human being was created weak.” Their animal nature is therefore like a seed for another kind of life, one that can last.

The argument is also confused about what “animal power” means. Animal power is the source of sensation and movement. If these powers are absent from a paralyzed organ, how can we say that animal power is present there? The powers that actually remain in such an organ are the nutritive power, among the powers of the soul, and the natural spirit that flows from the liver through the body.[^V08-302][^V08-303]

Someone might instead say that the powers of sensation and voluntary movement are still there, but that paralysis prevents them from acting.[^V08-304] This does not work. These powers depend on the body, and matter is part of their individual existence. If a power exists, its matter must be ready to receive it. How, then, could the same matter both receive the power and prevent it from acting?[^V08-305]

One might answer that the human soul, while joined to the body, is the source of all the rational, animal, plant, and natural powers, but the body's parts differ in how many of these powers they can receive.[^V08-306] The brain and heart receive most of them: powers of perception and voluntary movement, as well as plant powers. Other parts receive only natural powers and a weaker plant-life power, as in the nails, teeth, and bones.

The soul's relation to the body also changes with a person's state. In deep sleep or heavy intoxication, it withdraws most of the powers closest to its own level, as the sun sets and disappears from sight, though light and shadow remain on the earth. If the soul withdrew all its powers from the body during sleep, the body would die at once. If it stayed fully engaged with every power, the body would do in sleep what it does when awake.

From this we know that the soul's presence does not require all its powers to spread through every organ at every moment. A paralyzed organ may receive the natural and plant powers of the soul without receiving its animal powers. So the argument from paralysis does not prove that human beings have a separate animal power. We must follow the account we have established and explained.

### Book Four — The Animal Soul and Its Powers

This book examines the powers found in animal souls. Some give rise to the outer senses; others give rise to inner perception. The inner powers are closer to the unseen realm[^V08-307], while the powers behind voluntary movement rank below the powers of perception. The book has several chapters.

#### Chapter 1 — A general account of these powers and what each does

When God created plants from earthly matter, He gave them a fuller life than the elements, stones, and minerals such as iron and copper. Plants have a power that draws nourishment through their roots. He also gave them tools and powers that serve them. We have already discussed the powers. Their tools are the fine veins you can see in a leaf: they begin as thicker branches, then divide into finer and finer strands that spread through the leaf until they are too small to see. Yet even with this greater life, plants are still incomplete and cannot stand on their own.

If a plant lacks nourishment brought to its root, it dries up and dies. It cannot search for food elsewhere. Searching requires knowing what you seek and moving toward it. The more a being can search, the more it must know; and it can move toward what it knows. A plant cannot do this. If it had the ability to search, that ability would serve no purpose in it. God is too exalted to create a useless power, and proof shows that nothing in the universe is without purpose. Even if a plant had some awareness, it would not reach beyond the limits of its nature.

So God's care extended to beings with fuller life than plants. He gave animals the power to sense and the power to move in search of food. Touch is the first and most basic sense because animals need it most. Nearly every part of an animal's body has it. A land animal is made from elements whose basic qualities oppose one another. These qualities have been brought into balance and made to work together. The animal stays healthy when they remain balanced and becomes ill when they fall out of balance.

Every animal must have touch. Without any sense at all, it would not be an animal. Touch is the simplest sense because it detects what is in direct contact with the body. A sense that detects things at a distance is more complete. Even worms in the soil have touch: if you prick one with a needle, it contracts to escape. A plant does not contract when cut because it does not feel the cut.

But an animal that has only touch, like a worm, has a limited life. It cannot find food at a distance. So it needs a sense that detects distant things. God gave it smell. Smell detects an odor, but not the direction it comes from. The animal must wander around and may or may not find the food. It would still be very limited if it had no other sense. So God gave it sight, which detects distant things and shows where they are, allowing the animal to head straight toward them.

Sight still has limits: it cannot see through walls or other barriers. An animal cannot see food hidden behind a wall, or an enemy concealed from view. The barrier may not clear until the enemy is close, leaving no time to escape. So God gave animals hearing, which lets them detect sounds from beyond walls when something moves.

Human beings also need hearing because sight shows us only what is present. We can learn about what is absent through speech, made of letters and sounds that we hear. This need is especially great for us. God gave us this sense and set us apart from other animals by giving us the ability to understand speech.

None of this would be enough without taste. When food reaches an animal, it must be able to tell whether the food suits it before eating. Otherwise, it may eat something deadly. A tree has no sense of taste. It draws in any liquid poured at its root, and that may cause it to dry up.

Even these senses would not be enough without another power in the front of the brain, called the shared inner sense. The five senses bring their reports to it, and it gathers them together. Without this power, an animal's response would be slow, and it could fall into danger. Some animals, such as moths, lack inner awareness and may keep flying into a fire even after it has hurt them. If they could form and keep an inner image of what they had sensed, they would not return to what had harmed them.[^V08-308]

Still, an animal would be limited if it could avoid only things it had already sensed. Before it has been harmed by something, it does not know to fear it. So God gave more complete animals, such as horses, an inner power that recognizes particular meanings beyond the reach of the senses or imagination. This is the power of judgment. A horse, for example, fears a lion the first time it sees one, without first having been hurt by it. A sheep fears a wolf when it sees one, but does not fear a camel or cow, even though they are larger and more imposing.

Up to this point, human beings share these powers with other animals. The next step is what makes us human: we can understand what may follow from events and grasp things beyond the senses, imagination, and this inner judgment. We do not limit our choices to avoiding immediate harm or seeking immediate benefit. We can avoid future evils and seek lasting goods in the life to come. God set human beings apart from other animals with a higher power that lets us recognize the future benefits and harms of our choices.

Now consider this: if an animal had senses and powers of perception but no natural desire or longing for what suits it, and no will to move toward it, then its sight and other senses would serve no purpose. It must desire what suits it and turn away from what does not. Desire makes it seek what suits it; aversion makes it flee what harms it. This desire and aversion belong to the animal's powers of movement. They are enough for an animal simply as an animal, but they do not make a human being complete, since human beings are the most complete kind of animal.

Desire and anger—whether they concern an individual's need for suitable food or the kind's desire to reproduce—lead only to what helps or harms us in the present. They do not guide us toward what is good or bad in the long term. For this, God gave human beings another motive, beyond desire and anger. It is guided by reason, which, through God's light, shows us what lies ahead and helps us recognize the good of reward and the harm of punishment in the next life. This motive is called the will. Some religious scholars call it the religious motive. God gave it to human beings alone as an honor, just as He set us apart with reason, which recognizes the consequences of our actions. The will follows reason as desire follows the senses. I have summarized the account of one Muslim scholar here because of its clear order and fine expression. Now let us return to the senses and examine them one by one.

#### Chapter 2 — Touch and its features

As you know, an animal that lives on earth has a balanced mixture of qualities. It needs a power that preserves this balance and detects the body around it, such as air or water. That body may be harmful, so the animal can avoid being burned by heat or frozen by cold; or it may suit the animal, so it can seek it out or settle in it. Touch is therefore the earliest sense. Perhaps the need to avoid harm through touch is stronger, while taste is especially important for finding food that keeps an animal alive. But avoiding harm to preserve life comes before gaining benefits that make life better. Food can also be found through the other senses. So touch is the first and oldest sense, and the whole body must have the power of touch.

The proof[^V08-309] is that an animal's body is made from the same basic stuff as the qualities it feels through touch.[^V08-310] The same is true of all its organs. In our view, the one who senses and what is sensed must always be of the same kind. The power of life and perception that reaches through the whole body can therefore only be the power of touch. No other sense can spread through the whole body. The part that carries sight, for example, cannot be all the body's organs, since they are dense and dark, while sight perceives light. Its organ must share the nature of light, as we have explained. The body's organs are not light, either actually or potentially, as a transparent thing is. So sight cannot spread through those organs. The same applies to hearing[^V08-311] and to the inner powers, such as imagination and judgment. The body and its parts are material, not images or meanings. The images and meanings that these inner powers perceive are different forms that resemble them.

From this we can see that imagination, inner judgment, and the other inner powers are not material powers located in an organ such as the brain, as people commonly believe. This follows from the principle that the one who perceives and what is perceived become one in a certain way.[^V08-312]

The other outer senses—taste, hearing, smell, and sight—are bodily powers, but they do not spread through the entire body as touch does. Hearing and sight are especially subtle. Their organs must be a delicate, transparent part, or something like it, suited to what they perceive. Not every organ is delicate enough. Taste and smell are less subtle, but their objects are still fine: vapors or other thin substances, not hard, dense bodies. Not every organ is suited to receive smells or tastes. The inside of the nose receives smells; the tongue receives tastes. Touch is different. Every part of the body—hard or soft, dense or delicate—can receive the power of touch, because touch works through direct contact between surfaces.[^V08-313]

Some people say that touch has four kinds of power: one that distinguishes hot from cold, one that distinguishes wet from dry, one that distinguishes hard from soft, and one that distinguishes smooth from rough. Others add a fifth, which distinguishes heavy from light. We have already explained how to answer this claim.[^V08-314]

Know that every sense organ, even if it is made of the same general kind of thing as what it senses, must be free of the particular sensory qualities it detects. Otherwise it could not be changed by them. Until the organ is changed by a quality, the sense cannot perceive that quality. The change in the organ need not be the very same image that appears to the sense, since the organ's change is physical and the sensed image is mental. For example, the organ that detects heat and cold should not already be hot or cold.

This raises a problem for touch, since the part that feels touch is not free of the qualities it feels. But the rule does not mean that the organ must have no share at all in the general kind of quality it senses. It means that the organ must be free of the particular quality it detects and its opposite.[^V08-315] A balanced, lukewarm state is weak and lies between the extremes. It is almost as if it were neither one nor the other, like water that is neither hot nor cold. It can quickly take on either quality. The closer the organ is to a balance, the more sharply it can sense.[^V08-316]

You might say that we know animals sense things only because they seek what suits them and flee what does not. Even simple animals, such as sponges and shellfish, contract and expand by choice. If we did not see these movements, we would not know that they had touch. But we see similar movements in the elements and plants. Earth moves from high places toward low ones; fire moves from low places toward high ones. When something blocks the fire's upward path, it turns in another direction, sometimes even downward, and rises along an open path. If something blocks a plant's growth, it turns in another direction. It also bends away from shade toward sunlight.

Trees growing beside a river also send their roots toward the water, making it easier to draw it in. These and other movements toward what suits a thing and away from what harms it seem to show that plants and other things can sense what suits them and what does not. You might also argue that an animal's body is made from matter shaped by the basic qualities, and that its health depends on their balance while illness results from their opposites. It must therefore have a power that preserves this balance and senses what suits it or harms it, so that it can seek one and avoid the other. But the same argument applies to plants, lifeless things, and the elements.[^V08-317]

To answer, we must first define perception. Perception is when an image is present to a being that exists in itself. Material things, in which matter and qualities run their course, are not present to themselves in this way. How, then, can they perceive those things?[^V08-318]

The basic elements are constantly changing because they contain opposing qualities. A thing must be free of opposition for life to remain in it. But being a body is not, by itself, a barrier to life. A body can receive a single living form if it is free of opposing forms, as the heavenly spheres do, since they contain no conflict or decay. The elements, by contrast, are pulled apart by opposing qualities and do not keep the same form. Plants are different: the opposition within them is weak because their opposing qualities have been brought into balance. So it is possible that their powers have a faint awareness of what suits them.[^V08-319][^V08-320]

In our view, existence itself is awareness. That is why the wise knowers of God say that all existing things know their Lord and bow before Him, as Scripture teaches. When existence is free of absence and darkness, it brings full perception. Material things are mixed with darkness and hidden by barriers; their existence is sunk in absence and limitation. As these barriers lift and they become connected with the sources of soul and intellect that do not depend on matter, they rise toward life and awareness. Plant souls, though immersed in matter, may be closer to the unseen realm than the forms of elements and minerals, because their opposing qualities are more balanced. That is why plants show some signs of life that these other bodies do not.[^V08-321]

#### Chapter 3 — Taste and its questions

Taste is more widespread among animals than the other senses, except touch, and it is the sense most like touch. It comes second after touch because it detects what suits the body and so helps the animal seek it out. When the need for food grows stronger, taste becomes sharper. Taste also depends on touch, but touching the surface is not enough. The flavored substance must enter the organ of taste—the tongue—either directly or through saliva that comes from the tongue and receives the flavors. The saliva must have no taste of its own if it is to carry the flavors accurately. If it has a taste, as it may in illness, it will not convey them correctly. In truth, the tongue itself must also be tasteless, or nearly so, if it is to take on a flavor and let us sense it.

It is not enough for the saliva to take on a taste or for food to touch it. The soul cannot sense something outside the body. Its natural connection is with the body and its powers, not with things outside its care and control. The soul is joined to its powers and organs in a certain way, so when something is present in them, it is present to the soul. Perception is a thing's being present to the one who perceives it. So the soul senses what changes the body and its powers, not what lies beyond them.

Another question is whether saliva carries the flavored particles into the tongue by mixing with them, so that the sense detects the flavored thing itself; or whether saliva takes on the flavor and conveys it without mixing with those particles. On the first view, saliva serves only as a covering that helps the flavors reach the tongue, and the tongue directly senses the flavored thing. On the second, saliva is what the tongue directly senses. Both accounts are possible. Either way, nothing stands between the sense and what it senses.
