We must also understand that what we truly taste is not the flavor that belongs to the food, nor the flavor that belongs to saliva if saliva takes on a taste, nor the flavor that belongs to the tongue—even though the organ must be changed by a quality, as explained above. What we taste is the particular image of flavor formed in the power of taste. The relation of that power to the image is like the relation of matter to form, or of the intellect's ability to know to its actual knowing. The same is true of the other senses: what we truly perceive is the particular image formed in the soul through the sense, even in sight.

The power of taste is one. Some ask why philosophers say that touch has several powers because it senses several kinds of things, but do not say that taste has several powers because it senses several tastes. One answer may be that a single power can distinguish many tastes because they all stand in one kind of opposition to one another. The qualities sensed by touch do not all oppose one another in the same way: the opposition between hot and cold differs from the opposition between wet and dry.[^V08-322]

#### Chapter 4 — Smell

Smell is subtler than taste, just as taste is subtler than touch. These three are the coarsest and least refined senses. Smell perceives odors when air carrying them reaches the nose. So smell, too, depends on contact. It resembles touch, as if it were a kind of touch. In subtlety, smell stands to taste as taste stands to touch.

One question about smell is whether the air between an odor and the nose takes on the odor's quality and carries it to the nose, or whether it mixes with tiny particles of the scented thing, which then reach the organ of smell and are sensed there. On the second view, it is the particles that have the odor; the air itself does not. People have argued for both accounts.

Those who favor particles argue that, otherwise, heat could not stir up odors through rubbing or vaporizing, and cold could not hide them. We know that vaporizing makes odors stronger.[^V08-323]

They also point to an apple that wilts when it is smelled too often. This seems to show that particles of the scented thing are being used up.[^V08-324]

The other side argues that if odors filled a room only because particles escaped from a scented substance, then that substance should lose weight and shrink. They also point to camphor. If you heat it until all of it has turned to vapor, its smell spreads through a certain area. You can then spread the same smell through many times that area simply by carrying it from place to place, without vaporizing more camphor. The total amount of vapor that would have to escape to fill all those places should be many times greater than the amount produced by the first heating—or at least close to it. The camphor, however, does not lose that much. So a change in the air's quality must also play a part.[^V08-325][^V08-326]

The truth is that either account is possible. Some people have also claimed that smell perceives the scented thing where it is. That is the least convincing view.

Another point is that human beings may be better than other animals at noticing faint, nearby odors, but they are worse at detecting distant ones and at distinguishing one odor from another. Odors do not form as clear and detailed an impression in the human soul as tastes and tactile qualities do. We therefore name odors in only two ways. We call them pleasant or unpleasant, depending on whether they suit us; or we name them by what they belong to, such as a sweet or sour smell, or the smell of a rose or musk.

Some say that human beings perceive odors much as hard-eyed animals see things: their visual impressions are almost like uncertain images in the imagination. Yet many of these animals have a very strong sense of smell and need not even sniff to use it.

Another question about smell concerns a report about Plato, Pythagoras, Hermes, and other ancient thinkers. They said that the heavenly spheres and planets have a sense of smell and contain fragrances far sweeter than musk, ambergris, or flowers. The followers of Aristotle objected that smell requires air and vapor, and that neither is found there. This objection is weak. There is no rational proof that smell always requires air and vapor; that condition applies only to our elemental world.

One distinguished later sage reported that, when he came into contact with that world in sleep or while awake, he smelled fragrances sweeter than musk and ambergris—so much sweeter that our scents cannot be compared with them. Those who study spiritual realities agree that each planet has its own incense, and each spiritual being has a familiar scent that they breathe in and enjoy, along with the aromas of foods prepared for them. They then bestow on those who are ready whatever they are prepared to receive. Later, you will see what supports this claim and others like it.

#### Chapter 5 — Hearing

Hearing is the perception of sound. We have already discussed what sound is in our earlier study of categories, when discussing qualities we can perceive. Here, we should ask how hearing occurs.

The first question is this: some say we hear only when the compressed air between the object that strikes and the object that is struck reaches the hollow of the ear and the nerve spread through it. They call this transmission obvious, and offer several pieces of evidence.[^V08-327][^V08-328]

First, if this hollow is blocked, hearing stops. Second, if a dense body stands between the sound and the listener, hearing becomes difficult or impossible. Third, if you watch someone strike an anvil with a hammer or beat a drum, you hear the sound at the same time as you see the blow when you are close. As you move farther away, you hear it later, with the delay increasing along with the distance.

Fourth, if someone speaks into one end of a tube and the other end is placed at another person's ear, that person hears the speech while the others nearby do not. The air, they say, reaches that person's ear but not the ears of the others. Fifth, when the wind is strong, a nearby person may not hear a sound while someone farther away does. The wind, they say, bends the air carrying the sound away from one listener and toward another.

There is a serious difficulty with this account.[^V08-329] If someone speaks behind a thick wall, the person on the other side should not hear anything, since the wall has no openings. If there are openings, they will be few and narrow, so the sound waves should be distorted and the clear shapes of the letters should be lost.

There is another difficulty.[^V08-330] When a person speaks, the air begins to ripple. If the whole ripple reaches one listener, then no one else should hear the speech, since there is only one ripple. But if each listener receives only part of it, no one should hear the whole speech.

Their answer to the first difficulty is that a barrier with no opening at all completely blocks sound. The fewer openings there are, the weaker the hearing; so with no openings there can be no hearing. Their answer to the second is that letters are formed when air that has been held in a certain way is released. The wave that forms a letter is not a wave in the air as one undivided whole, apart from its parts. Rather, it occurs in each part of the air. Any part that reaches the ear carries enough of the sound for it to be heard. That is their account.

In my view, however, sound is not transmitted in the way they describe. It is extremely unlikely—and indeed not correct—to say that every particle of the air between the listener and the sound takes on the qualities of the letters and words, since air is simple and its parts are alike.[^V08-331] If this were so, hearing would happen again and again every time air reached the ear.

The truth is that the air reaching the organ of hearing alerts the soul to how the sound was produced: by an impact between something striking and something struck, or by something being pulled away.[^V08-332] It is not that one mass of air takes on the sound's quality and carries it all the way to the ear. Nor is it that many rippling masses of air pass the quality from one to another until the air next to the ear takes it on and delivers it to hearing.

This does not conflict with what we said earlier: that the soul perceives only an image formed in the organs through which it naturally acts. There we were discussing what the soul perceives directly. Here, what it perceives directly is the image of the sound formed in the power of hearing. Our present discussion concerns what is perceived indirectly: the sound outside us.

The connection between the listener and that external sound begins with the sound's particular position in relation to the listener, together with the presence of a subtle body between them.[^V08-333] Because of this, the organ, the air connecting it to the sound, and the sound itself act as a single instrument for the soul. The soul perceives what is within that instrument, including the direction from which the sound comes.[^V08-334]

This is why every bodily power needs a particular spatial relation in order to act. The position between the organ that carries the power and what it acts on—or what acts on it—together with the subtle body joining them, makes the whole arrangement seem like one material and one instrument for that power.[^V08-335] The material that carries the power is affected by the power's action, and so too is the connecting body, because it shares that position.

The second question is whether what we hear is only the sound in the air that strikes the ear, or whether we also hear the sound in the air outside the ear.

As we have said, we hold that what is heard is only the sound in the compressed air between what strikes and what is struck.[^V08-336] The reason is that when we hear a sound, we also perceive where it comes from and whether it is near or far. But the direction of the sound does not remain in the ripple once it reaches the ear. If we perceived only the sound that arrives at the ear, we should not be able to tell its direction or whether it began nearby or far away. The sound enters the ear through its motion and is perceived there. The hand, by contrast, senses what touches it only at the point of contact. It does not sense how far away the object was before it touched the hand; it senses only where the object arrived. Since we can distinguish the direction and distance of sounds, we must perceive the sounds outside us where they are.

You might object that we perceive direction because the air carrying the sound comes from that direction, and that we distinguish near from far because a nearby strike makes a stronger impression while a distant one makes a weaker one. But neither answer works.

The first is false because a sound may come from the listener's right and still be heard by the left ear if the right ear is blocked. We still sense that the sound is on the right, even though the ripple can reach the left ear only after bending around from the right. So we do not perceive the direction the sound traveled from; we perceive the direction in which the sound was made.

The second answer is also false. Otherwise, we would not be able to distinguish a strong sound far away from a weak sound nearby. If we heard two sounds at the same distance but with different strengths, we would think one was nearby and the other far away. We would constantly confuse strength with distance, but we do not.

Most people did not accept the view we have explained, so they struggled to account for how we sense the direction of a sound. One account, given by the author of *al-Muʿtabar*, goes like this: hearing begins when rippling air strikes the hollow of the ear. That is why sounds from farther away reach it later. But the direction and distance are not sensed at that first moment. Instead, the mind follows the ripple back along the air through which it came. After hearing the sound at the ear, we trace it to the preceding air, then to the air before that, following the route back toward its source. If some of the ripple remains along the way and we can follow it to where it fades, we then perceive the sound, its source, the source's direction and distance, and whatever strength remains in the waves. A distant sound seems weak because its ripple has weakened. If nothing remains along the path to lead us back to its source, we can judge the distance only from what remains. That is why, on this account, we may fail to distinguish thunder high in the sky from the rumble of a nearby mill. This is the fullest version of their explanation.

Its difficulty is clear. Hearing is not a series of perceptions and inquiries, in which the soul moves from one perception to another and then to another. It is one perception of a thing together with its direction and distance.

Even if we grant that the mind follows the air that reached the ear back along its path, the account still fails. Its supporters say that we first hear a sound without its direction, and only after tracing the waves do we perceive its direction, distance, strength, and weakness. That would make hearing several distinct acts, one after another. But if hearing is first separate from direction, then what it hears is only the sound itself. The direction is not perceived by hearing at all. If hearing does not perceive the direction, it does not perceive the sound as it exists in that direction. It would then hear a sound that would be exactly the same wherever it occurred. That is impossible, because a particular sound must have a particular position and direction.[^V08-337]

A sense cannot perceive a merely general idea, as we have shown. Yet hearing perceives a sound that exists in a particular direction. This is a contradiction.

This contradiction follows only if hearing the sound is separate from hearing its direction, and if direction is discovered later by tracing ripples in the air. It does not follow on the account we have established. Hearing perceives a particular sound in a particular direction and place. A material thing cannot exist apart from its position and direction. Its existence is not one thing and its being in a certain place or direction another. What are called the features that make an individual thing distinct are not truly features added to it; they are features only when considered in relation to that thing's general nature, as we have explained.

So every sense perceives a thing's existence in a particular direction and position. The senses do not first perceive that it exists, then separately perceive its direction, then separately judge whether it is near or far. That is the work of reason. The senses perceive a thing that already has a direction and position.

As we have explained, the existence of a sensed thing and its being in a particular direction and position are one thing in the individual, even though reason can distinguish them in thought. Just as the sensed thing is one, the act of sensing it is one. In that single act, we perceive that the thing exists, that it comes from a certain direction, and that it is in a certain place.

The third question related to hearing is this: air must be present between a listener and a sound for the two to be connected, as long as no dense barrier blocks them. This is not because air carries the sound's quality to the ear.

The ancients thought that hearing sounds from the heavenly spheres required only that the spheres touch one another. They attributed wonderful sounds and melodies to the spheres, sounds that bewilder the intellect and amaze the soul. It is said that Pythagoras rose to the higher world, where, through the purity of his soul and the clarity of his heart, he heard the melodies of the spheres and the sounds of the planets in motion. He then returned to using his bodily powers.

He arranged those sounds into melodies and perfected the art of music.

Know that the heavenly spheres and planets contain tastes, odors, sounds, and all the things perceived by our senses. This is a sound view, supported by direct spiritual experience, rational proofs, and the correspondences among levels of existence.[^V08-338]

Existence, with all its perfections and good things, flows from the highest sources to what proceeds from them, from higher things to lower ones, down to the lowest level of existence. Every perfection found in later effects is present in their nearer sources in a subtler and purer form. Whatever appears in the nearer causes also exists in the highest intellectual causes in a way suited to their realm.

Consider qualities we can sense. In bodily things they are divided, opposed to one another, and subject to change. In the senses and the powers of the soul, they exist in a purer, subtler way because their recipient is more refined; many of the limits that accompany them outside us have fallen away. These qualities also exist in the intellect in a more noble and elevated form.

The heavenly world is more subtle than the world of physical elements, but less subtle than the world of intellect. In its subtlety, it is like the senses. The substance of a sense is subtle enough to receive images of what is sensed. Yet an animal's senses are limited to certain places in its body, where there is a spiritual reality suited to the realm of images, which stands between intellect and body. A sphere, being simple and alike throughout, is like the spirit that fills the place of imagination in us.

There is also no need to wonder that the world of the spheres might contain odors and melodies. Qualities as we perceive them do not exist outside our powers and senses in the same way. As we have often explained, a quality, as something sensed, exists as it is present to the being that senses it. Its existence in the imagination is what makes us know it and experience it. We have also established that imagination, and everything present in it, exists outside this world.

So our powers can behold melodies, sounds, odors, and other things in two ways: sometimes through things outside us that prepare the way for them, and sometimes through inner causes, as in dreams. In sleep we may see astonishing images and hear great sounds, and we have no doubt that these experiences exist. It is therefore no wonder if the world of the spheres contains delightful tastes, pleasant odors, and wonderful melodies. Their causes are not limited to the familiar things we encounter here.

#### Chapter 6 — Sight

Anatomy has established that seven pairs of nerves branch from the brain. The first pair begins deep in the front chambers of the brain, near two small growths shaped like nipples. These are small, hollow nerves. The nerve from the right side crosses toward the left, while the nerve from the left crosses toward the right. They meet in a cross-shaped intersection, then the right-hand nerve enters the right pupil and the left-hand nerve enters the left pupil. The power of sight is carried in the spirit that fills the hollow of these nerves, especially at the point where they meet.

People disagree about how sight occurs. The natural philosophers say that an image of the visible thing is imprinted in part of the eye's crystalline fluid, which is like ice. They compare it to a mirror: when a colored, illuminated thing faces the eye, an image like it appears in the eye, just as a person's image appears in a mirror. They do not mean that a piece of the colored thing breaks off and travels to the eye. The image appears in the viewer's eye through a particular alignment, with transparent air between the eye and the object.

Two objections have been made. First, on this view, what we see would be only an image or likeness of the thing, not the thing itself. Yet we are certain that we see the very colored thing. Second, the image of a thing must be equal to it in size; otherwise it would not be its image or likeness. If so, we could not see anything larger than the crystalline fluid, since it is plainly impossible for something large to be imprinted in something small.

The natural philosophers answer the first objection by saying that when we see something through an image imprinted in the eye, what we see is the thing whose image has appeared, not the image itself. This is like knowing things outside us through our ideas of them.

They answer the second objection by saying that an image need not be the same size as the thing. A small mirror can show a person's whole face. The image need only match the thing in shape and color, not in size. But they cannot explain how a tiny image in the crystalline fluid, carried by the spirit in the nerves to the power of sight, lets us see huge things or perceive the distance between them and the viewer.

The mathematicians say that sight occurs when rays leave the eye in the shape of a cone, with its tip at the eye and its base at the visible object. They disagree about whether this cone is solid or made of lines that meet at the tip and spread apart at the base. Some say the rays do not form a cone, but a level surface: the end near the eye stays fixed while the other end moves across the visible object. Others say that the ray in the eye changes the air to match itself, so that the ray and the air together act as the organ of sight.

The view attributed to the Illuminationists and chosen by the martyred sage Shihab al-Din al-Suhrawardi is that sight involves neither rays nor imprints. Instead, when an illuminated object faces the seeing organ—which has a polished, moist surface—and the other conditions are present with no barrier in the way, the soul has a direct, luminous awareness of the object. It sees it plainly and clearly.

These are the three main views of sight.

In our view, the truth is different from all three.[^V08-339] Sight occurs when, by God's power, an image resembling the visible thing is brought into being in the spiritual world of the soul. It is free of external matter, present to the perceiving soul and held within it as an action is present to the one who acts—not as a form received by something passive. The proof for this comes from what we have shown about the unity of the intellect and what it understands. The same proof applies to all acts of sense and imagination.

We have drawn attention to this point in our discussion of the intellect and what it understands. We said that perception in general does not happen in the familiar way most philosophers describe: the sense strips the very image of the sensed thing from its material object, complete with the surrounding features that belong to it, and imagination strips away even more. Such a transfer cannot happen, since an image embedded in matter cannot move from one place to another.

All perception instead occurs when the Giver brings forth another luminous, perceptual image. Through it, perception and awareness take place. That image itself is the sense in action and the thing sensed in action. The image that exists in matter is neither the sense nor what is sensed. It only prepares the way for the image to flow from the Giver when the necessary conditions are met.[^V08-340]

The great philosopher has stated the view we have chosen in his well-known book, *The Theology of Aristotle*, in a passage we cited there. The other three famous views—the two we have just discussed and the one chosen by the author of *al-Muṭāraḥāt*—each face a number of serious problems.

Many books describe the problems with the view that an image is imprinted in the crystalline fluid and with the view that rays leave the eye. The view chosen by the author of *al-Muṭāraḥāt* also faces several objections.

One is that proof shows that a material body, and the qualities that belong to it, cannot be perceived directly, but only indirectly, as explained earlier.

Another is that a person with crossed eyes sees two images. If what is perceived were the very external thing, then the person would perceive something that does not exist outside. The reply that one image exists outside and the other in the imagination or in an imaginal world has no basis.

Another objection is that, on this view, if many people look at one external image, that image must be present to all their souls. But perception is nothing other than an image's being present to the one who perceives it. For a thing to be present to something, there must be a particular relation between them. A thing is present in itself only to one of its essential causes: its maker, its end, its form, or its matter. If the image were present to the soul, the soul would have to be one of the essential causes of that image. But the soul is neither its matter nor its form, as is plain. Nor can the soul be its maker, because that would mean there are several makers for one individual thing. And it cannot be its end, since one thing cannot have many ends.

There is also no basis for calling the soul's relation, through the body, to an external bodily thing in a particular position an “illuminating relation.” Relations between bodies, or between things connected through bodies, are positional relations: facing, being beside or touching something, being inside or outside it, and so on. All these relations are material and dark. We have established that position prevents perception because it belongs to matter. Perception depends on an image that is free of material position and size.

An illuminating relation properly describes the relation between a thing and the cause of its existence. Existence is manifestation. The maker and the end are the sources of a thing's existence; matter and its subject are the sources of what it can become and of its possibility. They may also be sources of its absence and concealment, because they can receive its opposite. So the relation that rightly deserves to be called illuminating is the relation between the source that brings an image into being and the image itself. On our account, this relation is between the soul and the image that flows from it in sight.

Perhaps the view we have chosen was the teaching of the ancients, and the name was later changed in transmission because the view was difficult and those who passed it on did not fully understand it. Once proof shows that seeing occurs when an image free from external matter is present to the soul, and rules out the theories of rays and imprints in the crystalline fluid or anything like it, our claim is established. We will now examine in detail the problems with the other two famous views.

#### Chapter 7 — Arguments for imprinting, replies to them, and arguments against imprinting

Those who support the imprinting view offer several arguments. Their main one is this: the eye is a smooth, shining body. When a smooth body faces a dense, colored object, the object's likeness is imprinted on it, as happens in a mirror. The general claim is obvious; the claim about the eye is supported by things we observe. In the dark, someone who has just woken may see light when rubbing an eye. A similar flash can appear when a hand is passed over the back of a black cat. When we look toward the end of our nose, we may see a ring of light around it. After waking, we may briefly see objects nearby and then lose sight of them, because the eye is then full of light. If we close one eye, the pupil of the other widens; they say this is because a shining substance fills it. And if bright spirits did not flow from the brain into the eye, they say, the pupils would not be hollow.

Even if this argument is complete, it shows only that an image can be imprinted in the eye. It does not show that sight happens by means of that imprint.[^V08-341]

Their second argument is that the other senses perceive things when an image comes to them, not when something goes out from them to the object. Sight must work the same way. The reply is that this comparison has a point in common, but does not prove that images are imprinted in the eye. In the other senses, neither an image travels to the organ nor does anything travel out from it. Rather, an image suited to the object comes to be present to the soul. So there is a common feature among the senses, but it does not establish the claim they want.

Their third argument is that if someone looks at the sun for a long time and then turns away, its image remains in the eye for a while. The answer is that the image remains in the imagination, not the eye, as we can see when we close our eyes.[^V08-342]

Their fourth argument is that an object looks larger when it is near than when it is far away. They say this is because its image is imprinted along a cone of transparent air, with its point at the pupil and its base at the object. The object spans the base of the cone. A line of the same length subtends a wider angle near the point and a narrower one farther away. So the image at the wider angle is larger than the one at the narrower angle. This explanation works only if sight takes place at the cone's point, as the imprinting view says, and not at its base, as the ray view says. On the ray view, the base does not change in size.

The reply is that this may not be the only reason an object looks larger or smaller. The supporters of rays also offer an explanation. And it is not clear that a wider angle must make the object itself look larger, or a narrower angle make it look smaller.

Their fifth argument is that if sight did not happen by images being imprinted in the crystalline fluid, the eye's layers, fluids, and shapes would serve no purpose.[^V08-343] They say the crystalline fluid is clear so that colors can take effect in it. Its front is slightly flattened: if it were perfectly round, it would meet only a small part of the visible object, while this shape lets it receive more of the object. The iris has a hole in its middle so that it will not block the visible object from reaching the crystalline fluid. The cornea has no hole because it is thin, white, and clear; it therefore lets light and the image carried by the air pass into the eye.[^V08-344]

The answer is that this argument does not prove its conclusion. The layers, shapes, and fluids of the eye may have other purposes besides receiving imprinted images. Their sixth argument is that people with certain illnesses sometimes see distinct images that are not present outside them. Since these images are distinct, they must exist somewhere, either in the eye or in one of its parts. If sight happens by imprinting in these cases, they say, it must happen that way in ordinary sight too, since there is no difference between the two. The reply is that this would show only that sight in this particular condition happens by imprinting. It would not show that we see external things because their images are imprinted in our eyes.[^V08-345]

I say that this is a strong argument against the ray view and against the view that sight directly takes in the external image without rays. But it does not harm our view; it supports it.[^V08-346][^V08-347]

Their seventh argument is that the image of something seen remains in the imagination, so we can picture it whenever we wish. If the power of imagination receives an image of what we imagine, they say, then the power of sight must receive an image in the same way. The answer is that this is only a comparison. We say that images are held in the imagination because the thing pictured is not present outside us; there is no other way to picture it. But when we see something that is present outside us, we do not need to claim that an image is imprinted in the power of sight. Sight can instead be understood as a relation between the visual power and the visible things outside us.

As for the arguments against imprinting, the first is one Galen relied on: a body can receive only a shape as large as itself. If seeing happened by imprinting, we could see no more than a point as large as the eye. Yet we see half the globe of the world.

They raised several objections to Galen's argument. First, a small body can be divided endlessly, just like a large one, so why could it not receive the same shape? Second, suppose the eye receives only an image as large as itself. The soul could compare the image with what it knows and infer the size of the object, just as it does with pictures painted on walls and other surfaces. Third, the image of half the world appears in a mirror. If a huge image can be imprinted on a small mirror, why could it not be imprinted in the eye?

Fourth, they compare this with imagined images. We can imagine a sea of mercury or a mountain of ruby. These images are distinct and affect the soul, so they must exist somewhere, even though they do not exist outside us. The same goes for the huge and frightening images people with certain illnesses see. If such images exist in a bodily part, then a huge thing can be imprinted in a small place, so sight could work the same way. If the soul perceives them, that raises a problem: first, they themselves have argued that the soul cannot perceive particular things. Second, if images of imagined and visible things can be present in a soul that has no size or volume, then it should be even easier for a huge image to be imprinted in a small body. Third, if images can be imprinted in the soul, then imprinting is possible, and the only remaining dispute would be about where the image is imprinted.

The first objection is very weak. A small body and a large one may allow endless division, but that does not make them equal in size or in the size of their parts. The second is answered by the fact that sight happens at once; it does not compare and infer.[^V08-348] The third fails because images are not imprinted in a mirror either. Something else causes us to see what appears there, as will be explained. As for the fourth, we do not think imagined images or the images seen in illness exist in a bodily place or inside the soul. They are free-floating forms that the soul brings forth and sustains as long as they are seen. What the argument proves is only that the soul cannot be the place where particular images are stored, and that the thinking power does not perceive particular things by itself in its immaterial state. It does not prove that every act of perception happens because an image settles inside the perceiver.

There is no proof that every act of perception happens by an image settling in the very being of the person who perceives it. Nor does the comparison help: it is no more reasonable for a huge image to be imprinted in something small than in something without size, especially since we have established that the soul is not the place where imagined images are stored.

It is clear that two measurable things are either equal in size or one is larger than the other. If one is larger, the extra part lies outside the smaller one, so a large measure cannot be imprinted in a smaller measure. But the case of something without measure is different. Many have missed this distinction. That is why the author of *The Discussions* objected to the Shaykh and his followers: since they allow matter, which has no size of its own, to be the subject of all sizes, how can they argue against sight by imprinting on the ground that imagined images are present in the imagination or soul? If an image can be imprinted in the soul, they say, why not in the eye? Imprinting a large image in a small place seems easier than imprinting one in something without size.[^V08-349]

I say there is a difference between something that is definitely without size, such as a point or the soul itself, and a line or surface in some respects; and something that is not yet determined as having size or having none, but can take on either from outside. Matter considered apart from any particular form is like this: it can receive any size, and in itself it is equally open to having or not having size, because it has no fixed nature of its own. This is different from something already defined as having no size, like a point, or a particular kind of size, like a line or a tiny mustard seed. A point cannot receive any size; a line cannot receive the size of a surface or body; a mustard seed cannot receive a huge size. But matter not yet given a particular form can receive any size.

The second argument is that, if sight happened by imprinting, we could not tell near from far. If what we saw were an image imprinted in the eye, that image would be the same whether the object were near or far. Just as we cannot tell by sight alone whether one of two bodies before us is near and the other far, they say, so we should not be able to sense distance. But we do sense it, so sight cannot happen by imprinting. The reply is: why could images of long and short distances not also be imprinted in us? That would allow us to perceive distance.

The third argument is this: if the crystalline fluid is colorless, it should not take on shapes or colors, just as air does not. If it has a color, two impossible things follow. First, its color would mix with the color of the visible object, so we would not sense the object's color. Second, the shape and color imprinted on its surface would not pass through to the meeting point of the optic nerves behind it. A colored body with a shape imprinted on it does not pass that image through to what is behind it.[^V08-350]

The fourth argument is that if visible images were imprinted in the crystalline fluid, we should be able to sense the image in it, just as we can see green light reflected from an object onto a wall. But when we look at the image in another person's crystalline fluid, its position seems to change as we move. If it were imprinted there, its place would be fixed. Since its apparent place changes with the viewer's position, they conclude that the image is not imprinted in the fluid.

I say these two arguments are strong against those who claim that the image of the visible object is imprinted in the crystalline fluid. We do not hold that view. We say the image is present to the soul without being imprinted or lodged in it, as explained earlier.[^V08-351]

Galen's fifth argument is that if an image flowed from the visible object into the crystalline fluid, the object would gradually shrink or disappear. This argument is very weak. Those who support imprinting do not say that anything breaks off from the object and travels to the eye. They say that the crystalline fluid, when facing an object, becomes ready for a matching image to arise in it from its source. That image is what sight perceives.

The sixth argument is that a physical cause cannot act on a distant body without first acting on the nearer body. So if a visible object caused its color and shape to appear in the eye, it should cause them to appear in the air between the object and the eye as well. But our senses show that this does not happen.

The reply is that this would follow only if the nearer body were ready to receive the effect, as the farther one is. The air is not ready to receive an image of the visible object.[^V08-352]

#### Chapter 8 — What the ray theorists say

The science of optics and mirrors is a field of its own, studied carefully by many scholars. They base their account on rays leaving the eye and reaching the visible object, just as rays from the sun, moon, and other shining bodies reach what they face. The rays form a cone, with its point at the light source and its base at the object it faces. This suggests that we see by rays leaving the eye, and that we see whatever the rays reach.

But we have proved that what each sense perceives is a perceptual image separate from physical matter, not an image lodged in a physical body. Even so, for sight to occur, the eye must face what gives rise to an image in the perceiving power. The eye is like a point, and when it faces a body, we can imagine a cone between them.[^V08-353][^V08-354] We do not deny that rays may extend from the eye toward what is seen. We say only that sight also requires the visible object's image to be present to the soul. Nothing the ray theorists say rules this out. Their account may agree with ours in some of its observations; there is no difficulty in two opposing views sharing some results.

For example, they say that a distant object looks smaller than a nearby one because the cone narrows as it extends outward. The angle at the eye becomes narrower, and the circle at the visible object becomes smaller. The farther away the object is, the smaller these become, until it is too far away to see.[^V08-355]

They also say that an object looks larger in water than in air. In air, they say, the rays travel straight. In water, they bend toward the perpendicular and gather together between the water's surface and the object. The object then looks larger because the angle it makes in the crystalline fluid stays wide; the apparent size of the object depends on that angle.[^V08-356] Some rays travel straight, while others bend at the water's surface. If the object is close to the surface, the two paths overlap and we see the object across their combined span. If it is farther away, we see it in two separate places, along the straight and bent paths.[^V08-357]

They also say that when we open our eyes again after closing them, we may briefly see two moons, as if one were separating from the other.[^V08-358] On their account, the rays from the eyes veer away from their usual alignment and cross before reaching the moon. Their ends then fall in two places, so we see two images. They offer the same explanation for people with crossed eyes. They also say that if we place the index and middle fingers against the eye in different positions and look at a lamp, we see two lamps; and that if we look at one of two thin sticks aligned in front of the eye, the other, at a different distance, appears double.[^V08-359] They further say that when the moon rises over water, we see one moon in the water by a ray passing into it, and another in the sky by a ray reflected from the water's surface.

Some scholars report their view this way, but there is a problem with it.[^V08-360] It is better to say[^V08-361] that we see the moon in the sky by a ray reaching it directly, and the image in the water by a ray reflected from the water's surface toward the moon. There is also another effect: in water with a calm, even color, we may see three moons. One is in the sky, seen directly. The other two appear in the water. We see one along a ray reflected from the moon by the water's upper surface; the other is seen along a ray that enters the water, reflects from its curved bottom, and travels back toward the moon.

A similar case occurs when an object appears twice because a mirror is placed between us and it in a certain position: one image is seen directly, and the other along a ray that travels from the eye to the mirror, then reflects toward another object positioned in relation to the mirror as the viewer is.

They also explain why a tree on a riverbank can look upside down in the water. A ray that reaches the water is reflected toward the top of the tree from a point closer to the viewer, and toward the base from a point farther away, until the base of the tree aligns with the base of its reflection. The soul is used to seeing things along straight rays and does not notice the reflection. It therefore takes the reflected ray to have traveled straight through the water. It sees the top as farther away and deeper in the water, and the other parts in order down to the base, so the tree looks inverted. Optics explains these details and similar cases more fully.

#### Chapter 9 — Why people see double, and why the ray and imprint views disagree about its cause

Those who support the imprinting view say that the image of a visible object is first imprinted in the crystalline fluid, but sight does not happen there. They say that, just as an imagined cone extends from the visible object and narrows behind the crystalline layer, the image in the crystalline fluid travels through the spirit flowing in the two hollow optic nerves toward their meeting point. The two cones meet and cross there. Beyond that meeting point, there is no perceiving spirit. So the two images join into one at the point where the spirit carries the visual power. If the two images do not reach the same place, but end at different parts of the visual spirit, a separate image comes from each one through the crystalline fluid, and we see one object as two.[^V08-362][^V08-363]

The supporters of rays say this explanation fails. If we deliberately cross our eyes and look at something, we also see it double, just as a person with crossed eyes does. But crossing our eyes does not undo the way the two optic nerves meet inside the brain. Their meeting is not something that disappears and reappears at will. They also say that if two objects, one near and one far, are aligned in front of us without the near one blocking the far one, we can focus on the nearer object and see it as one, while the farther one appears double. If we focus on the farther object, the reverse happens. If crossed nerves caused double vision, they say, we could not see one object as single and the other as double at the same time.

They explain it this way. A cone of rays extends from each eye. Within each cone, the ray along its axis is strongest; they call this the ray-line. The two ray-lines meet at the object we focus on. When we focus on the nearer object, the two lines meet there. The farther object then lies away from each line, toward the outer edge of both cones. We see the nearer object as one because the two lines meet on it, while the farther one appears twice: the ray from the right eye falls to its left, and the ray from the left eye falls to its right. When we focus on the farther object, the two lines meet there, and they fall on two different places on the nearer object, so the farther one appears single and the nearer one double.

The same applies to someone with crossed eyes. The main rays from the two eyes do not meet at one point; each falls on the nearby side of the cone's base, or they cross in the air before reaching the object. Such people usually see things with the edge of the cone rather than with its central ray. If they can make the two central rays meet on one object, they see it as one, as it is.[^V08-364]

Those who support imprinting have offered other explanations for double vision. One is that the visual spirit moves to the right or left, so the image is formed in one place before the two cones cross, and the object appears twice. This is like seeing one reflection in still water and many reflections in rippling water. Another explanation is that the spirit behind the crossing of the optic nerves moves forward or backward. In that case, it has two opposite movements: one toward the shared sense and the other toward the meeting point of the nerves. The image reaches both before the earlier image has faded, so each place receives another visible image. The difference between the explanations is that the first describes movement from side to side, while the second describes movement forward and back.[^V08-365]

#### Chapter 10 — Why sight needs a transparent medium

Here is the argument. A power that belongs to a body can affect something, or be affected by it, only through a shared position. Action and reaction take place between things linked as cause and effect. This link is direct between a power and the matter, subject, or body it belongs to, since these help make it what it is, set it apart, or complete it. The link is indirect between that power and something else that has a spatial relation to its body.[^V08-366]

For bodies, their position relative to one another matters, much as the relation between cause and effect matters for things that do not depend on matter. Position is a way a body exists and is set apart from other bodies.[^V08-367][^V08-368] If two bodies touch and join at their edges, they can act as if they were one body.

So if something outside one of them affects it, the effect can pass to the other. When fire heats one part of a body, another connected part also warms. When light shines on one surface, it can light a second surface placed in line with the first. I have specified an outside effect because an inward action that does not depend on position does not pass to a neighboring thing.

With this in mind,[^V08-369] we can say that sensing—including sight—is the sense power's being affected by an external physical thing. So there must be a spatial link between the bodily basis of the sense power and the thing it senses. Facing each other is not enough if no physical body lies between them: without some contact or connection in space, there is no natural link. The link must be either a mental relation or a physical connection. So a body must join the sense organ and the object.

If that body were dense, thick, and dark, it could not receive light. How then could it connect the visible object to the eye, or the light source to the thing lit? A link between two things must help their action, not block it. So the body between them must be transparent and must not block light, whether light travels from its source to the lit thing, from the eye to what is seen, or as an image from the object to the eye.[^V08-370]

This shows why the claim that a thinner medium is always better cannot be right. If an empty space lay between the eye and the object, sight would be even stronger, and we might see an ant in the sky. It is not enough to answer that this is impossible, and that making the medium thinner does not always make sight stronger. If the medium needs to be thin so light can pass through, then removing it altogether should make light pass even more easily. The flaw is that thinness does not mean the medium can be absent. If no physical body joined the viewer and the object, there would be no action between them.

You might say that the Shaykh admits this kind of action does not require the cause and the thing affected to touch. If there were empty space between the sense and the object, what would be wrong with an image being imprinted in the sense? The answer is that even without direct touch, there must still be either contact or a physical medium between them. That medium and the thing affected must work together almost as one body: one part is ready to receive the effect, while the other is not. If there were no body between fire and what it heats, nothing would warm. And if there were no body between the sun and the earth, the earth would receive neither light nor heat.

#### Chapter 11 — Why there are only five senses

Most people thought there might be a sixth sense in addition to the five. The philosophers denied it. They argued, as the Shaykh explains in one of his books, that nature does not move from a simpler kind to a more complete kind until it has brought the simpler kind to its full development.[^V08-371] In the same way, nature could not rise above animal life without first completing all the powers of animals. If another sense were possible, animals would have it. Since even the human being, the most complete animal we know, does not have a sixth sense, there is no other sense.[^V08-372]

#### Chapter 12 — What the senses have in common

Some people say that the only things we directly sense are qualities sensed through the body, such as colors, sounds, smells, tastes, and things felt by touch. Everything else is sensed only indirectly. On this view, the senses have no thing they can sense in common.[^V08-373] That is not so.[^V08-374]

By something sensed directly, I mean something that leaves an effect in the sense power. Something sensed indirectly does not do this. It is not truly sensed; it is simply linked to what we do sense. For example, when we see Abu Zayd, the person himself is what we really see, because his image reaches our sight. But we do not see that he is a father. No image of fatherhood enters the sense. The mind works out that relation by comparing him with his child.

Magnitude, number, position, motion and rest, nearness and distance, touching and being apart are different. We may not sense them on their own, but we do sense them together with something else: with color and light in sight, with heat and moisture in touch, and with other qualities through the other senses. A thing does not stop being directly sensed just because we need to sense something else at the same time. What matters is whether the thing itself makes an impression on the sense, not whether another feature helps us notice it.

So anything we call sensed either does or does not leave an effect in the sense. If it does not, it is sensed only indirectly. If it does, then sensing it either depends on sensing something else or does not. If it depends on another sensed thing, it is something sensed through another quality; if it does not, it is sensed on its own. I say this is true when we look at the question broadly. On closer examination, what is directly sensed is the image present to the soul, not the external thing that matches it.

Sight senses size, number, shape, position, motion, and rest through color. Some people say motion and rest are not sensed. They argue that someone sitting on a fast-moving ship does not feel the ship move. They also say rest is an absence, so how could it be sensed?

Understand that motion and rest are sensed when the mind recognizes them with help from the senses. The sense sees a body first near one thing, then farther from it, and continues to register that change. The mind then judges that the body has moved. The idea of something moving from possibility to reality is not itself sensed; it describes a relation. In the same way, the sense sees a body staying in one place, and the mind judges that it is at rest. The sense does not itself perceive that the body has stayed still or failed to move, because one idea is a relation and the other is an absence. Neither is sensed on its own. A person on a ship does not feel it move when the senses register no change in its position compared with something outside it. So motion and rest seem to be judgments of the mind made with help from the senses, as sight is.

Touch also senses all the things listed above through hardness and softness, heat and cold, and other qualities. Taste senses flavor; through flavor it can sense amount by tasting more or less, and number by tasting different flavors. It senses motion and rest only weakly, and then only with help from touch. Smell senses none of these except number, with help from the mind: we know that a smell that has stopped is not the same as one that later returns. Hearing does not sense size, but it helps the mind judge that loud sounds usually come from large bodies. In general, sight senses these shared features more clearly, though the mind helps here too.[^V08-375][^V08-376]

If you want the deeper truth, no act of sensing stands on its own without imagination, and no act of imagination stands on its own without thought. Sense could not exist apart from thought, and it cannot be separated from thought in the way a hammer or chisel can exist without a carpenter. Human hearing and sight cannot be understood apart from the thinking power; in other animals, they cannot be understood apart from the animal soul. The soul cannot exist apart from intellect, and intellect cannot exist apart from God. This has carried us beyond the subject at hand, though we have touched on it before.

If this is how the senses work, then the same is true of the things they sense. Everything sensed is also understood by the intellect, in the basic sense that the intellect ultimately knows it. By convention, however, we call the particular knowledge gained through the senses “sensing,” to distinguish it from the intellect's knowledge of general ideas.

Some people think animals need another sense, beyond the five, to perceive these shared features. That is mistaken. As we have seen, each of the five senses can perceive them. There is no need for a sixth. A sixth sense would add nothing, since the five already do this work, and nature does not create a power that serves no purpose.

### Book Five — The soul’s inner perceptions

#### Chapter 1 — Common sense, or the soul’s tablet

Most people say that common sense is a power located in the front part of the brain.[^V08-377] I hold that it is a power of the soul, prepared to arise in the front of the brain, more precisely in the vital spirit that flows there. It receives the forms carried to it by all the outer senses. The senses are like spies who bring reports from distant regions to the king’s minister.

They offer three arguments for this power. The first is that we judge, for example, that this white thing is sweet, or that this is not that thing. Anyone who judges between two things must have both before them.[^V08-378] The judge cannot be the intellect alone, without the help of a sense. First, we perceive sensible things only through particular organs. Second, animals without reason make such judgments too; without them, they could not stay alive. If smell and shape did not tell an animal what it should seek, or what it should flee, it would neither pursue nor avoid anything. So the things perceived by the outer senses must come together in one particular power of perception. Since none of the outer senses does this, there must be an inner power that perceives particulars. This is what we call common sense.

This argument is not decisive.[^V08-379] In human beings, the intellect gathers and directs all the powers of perception; in animals, estimation does this. Either can judge what the senses perceive. To judge that sugar is white and sweet, for example, it is enough to see its whiteness and taste its sweetness.

There is another case. We first understand the general idea of a human being, then see a particular person and judge, “This person is human.”[^V08-380] The general idea is understood by the intellect; the individual person is perceived by the senses. If a judge must directly perceive both things being compared, then we would need a third power, one that perceives both general ideas and particular things. The intellect perceives general ideas, while the senses perceive particular things. So either this judgment would be impossible, or there would have to be another power that is neither intellect nor sense. Both conclusions are false, so the argument does not hold.

Bahmanyār says that the power that judges that a certain taste belongs to something of a certain color need not itself perceive the sensible forms, just as the power of desire need not perceive the things that stir it. The soul can taste the flavor and see the color, while another power judges that this flavor belongs to something of this color.[^V08-381] I find no fault with this. A critic objects that every judgment requires us to understand both things being judged: how could someone judge that the world is created without first understanding both “world” and “created”? But by “another power,” Bahmanyār means a power that perceives, such as the intellect in a human being[^V08-382] and estimation in other animals. His point is that once taste has made the flavor known and sight has made the color known, the intellect or estimation can judge that this flavor belongs to the thing of that color. So there is no judgment without the two things being understood.[^V08-383]

When philosophers say that the intellect does not perceive particular things, they mean that it does not perceive them on its own, without a perceptual power such as the senses. And when they say estimation does not perceive what the outer senses perceive, they mean it cannot do so on its own, without those senses. In human beings, the intellect is ultimately the power that knows both general ideas and particulars. In other animals, estimation is the power that knows both what they sense and what they infer from it. This objection comes from not following the philosophers’ terms closely enough.

The second argument is that a falling raindrop looks like a straight line, and a spark moving around on a spinning wheel looks like a circle, even though the drop itself is not a line and the spark itself is not a circle. These images must therefore appear in a particular power other than sight. This seems clear whether we think that sight sends out rays toward an object or that the object’s image reaches the eye. In the latter view, the material object must be present for us to see it. But the line and circle we see are not the forms of the object where it is now. So these images must appear in another particular power, not in the eye.

The author of *Eastern Discussions* objects that this argument first assumes that an image is imprinted in the eye, then uses that assumption to prove that an image is imprinted in common sense. Why could the image not remain in the visual spirit for a while? As the raindrop moves from one place to another, its image might be imprinted at each place in turn. Before the first image disappears, a second is made. When the two images are present together, sight perceives a point as a line. Avicenna also accepts that sight perceives motion, and motion could not be perceived unless this happened.

I reply that I have already shown, without relying on this argument, that seeing requires an image of the thing to be present to the soul. But it is not possible for the eye to retain these imprints and perceive a point as a line. Sight is a bodily power; it perceives only things that share a position with it. A thing that has no position in relation to the sense organ cannot affect it or leave an impression on it. The raindrop is in one place at a time. Once it has moved, it is no longer in its earlier place; what is no longer there has no position and cannot affect a bodily power.[^V08-384]

If sight could perceive the image of a point in a position after the point had left that position, it would perceive something absent without a cause. It would also mean that the outer senses could perceive things that are no longer present. But perceiving absent things belongs to the inner senses, since those things are not imprinted in matter. We know by experience that the outer senses do not perceive the past or the future: sight cannot see a color that was present yesterday, and smell cannot detect a scent that will be present tomorrow. The same is true here.[^V08-385]

As for Avicenna’s claim that sight perceives motion, I have already explained that the intellect recognizes motion by comparing what the senses register. Sight does not perceive every part of a movement in a single, instant act. If all the stages of a movement were imprinted at once, as with a falling drop or a moving spark, that would happen in common sense, not in the outer sense of sight.

The third argument, and in my view the strongest,[^V08-386] is that we perceive images that do not exist outside us. People suffering from delirium may see them, as may people in dreams. They see large, distinct figures and hear sounds unlike ordinary ones. Prophets and saints, peace be upon them, may also see beautiful forms and hear sounds that they recognize as different from ordinary sensory experiences, even when their senses are clear. People whose minds are overwhelmed by fear or shock—in war, earthquakes, and other crises—may see images that reflect what they are going through, as their souls turn away from the outer world.

These images must exist in some way, since pure nothingness cannot be distinguished from anything else. Yet they do not exist outside us; if they did, anyone with sound senses would see them. Nor does the intellect perceive them, since it does not grasp bodies, dimensions, and shapes as particulars. The outer senses cannot be their source either: such images usually appear when the senses are quiet or inactive, as in sleep, and some are seen while awake with the eyes closed.

The power that perceives them must therefore be an inner one, distinct from the intellect. It cannot be imagination, since imagination preserves images rather than perceiving them. If it perceived every image it preserved, then everything stored in it would always be present to view, which is not the case. So there must be another power: common sense. It is connected with the front part of the brain, in the first ventricle, and its perceptions appear through the vital spirit there. This spirit is like a mirror. It has no colors of its own, yet it can show the colors of things without having their images imprinted in it. In the same way, because it is clear and balanced, the vital spirit allows unseen images resembling things we sense to appear to the soul.[^V08-387] The soul perceives them through this power, which lets it perceive what the outer senses cannot reach. This is what we set out to show.

Those who deny the existence of this power offer two arguments. First, someone asleep may see a mountain made of ruby or a sea of fire. Such enormous images could not be imprinted in the small mass of the brain; the large cannot be imprinted in the small. Their home must therefore be the soul itself, not another power, so the claim that common sense exists is false. Second, we know by experience that we do not taste or touch with our hands or feet. We know just as certainly that we do not taste or touch with the brain.

I reply that the first objection does not apply to my view. I do not say that perceived images are physically imprinted in a body. They belong to the soul in another way, not by being physically lodged in it. The second objection also misses the point. The brain is not where the images are imprinted. Nor does the soul need the brain in order to perceive images it has received through the senses, or to perceive absent things once it is prepared to do so. The brain is needed as the place where this power can arise and as the bodily basis that prepares the soul to connect with the source from which images take shape. I firmly deny that these enormous images are imprinted in any part of the brain. We have shown that images of imagination do not exist in this physical world, since they do not have positions in space. Still, another power besides the soul itself must be established, because the one that perceives particulars is different from the one that perceives universals. A particular thing and a universal idea are different in kind, and the perceiver must be of the same kind as what it perceives. So the power that perceives particulars cannot be the one that perceives universals.

#### Chapter 2 — Imagination

Imagination, also called the formative power,[^V08-388] is the power that preserves images within us. Three arguments are offered to show that it differs from common sense.

First, common sense can receive images, while imagination preserves them. Receiving and preserving are different powers. Water can take the shape of a vessel without keeping that shape once the vessel is removed. If receiving and preserving were one and the same power, they could not come apart as they do here.[^V08-389] The second difference is that receiving is a capacity to take on an image, while preserving is an established ability to hold it. These are opposite aspects of the same subject.[^V08-390] The objection that this reasoning relies on the rule “one thing cannot produce more than one thing” comes from a lack of care and understanding, as we have already explained.[^V08-391]

The second argument is that common sense judges the things it perceives and assents to them, while imagination does not judge; it only preserves. A single power cannot both judge and refrain from judging. The objection is: why could the same power not judge at one time and preserve at another?[^V08-392]

The third argument is that images of things we sense can be present to us directly, or they can be imagined. Seeing an image and imagining it are different, so the powers that do them must differ. The author of *Eastern Discussions* objects that we may not always be attending to the intelligible images we know. Where are they stored while we are not thinking of them? If the answer is that they come from the source that gives them when the soul turns toward it, and disappear when the soul turns away, then why could imagined images not work the same way? When common sense is ready to bring them to mind, why could they not flow to it from the source of images?[^V08-393]

I answer that a power with an established ability to turn toward, or connect with, a source that gives images can receive them without having to acquire them anew through thought or sensation. This is different from a power without that established ability, which must work to perceive an image—through sensation, as with common sense, or through thought, as with the soul. The soul cannot call up what it has not yet learned whenever it chooses. It needs to search and acquire knowledge, unless it has gained an intellectual habit through repeated acts of perception, thought, and reflection. The strength of that habit is a lasting intellectual light received from its source, which in my view is a being above the soul and below the active intellect.[^V08-394]

The same applies here. At first, perceiving a sensible image requires something that brings it to us, such as a sense. Preserving it requires a further, established ability in the power that perceives it. That ability lets us call back images we have forgotten, even after their causes have disappeared from the senses.[^V08-395][^V08-396]

We have already shown that the power that calls back images is not found in material things. Still, it is not essential to our philosophical account to prove that common sense and imagination are different powers. Even if they were one power with a less developed and a more developed side, nothing important would be lost. What matters is to establish that this is an inner, substantial power, distinct from intellect and the outer senses, with a realm of its own beyond the world of intellect and the world of nature and motion. We have already proved that it is not part of the body or its parts. Through it, the states of the grave, its reward and punishment, the intermediate realm, and the resurrection of bodies become real. Its domain is the whole body, its authority is in the rear of the first brain ventricle, and its instrument is the vital spirit located there.

#### Chapter 3 — Imagination, estimation, and memory

The imaginative power is also called the thinking power when the rational soul uses it to arrange thoughts and their premises. It differs from the other powers of perception because acting and arranging are different from perceiving and judging. We can combine images we have sensed or separate them in a way we have never seen outside us—for example, we can imagine an animal with a human head, a horse’s body, and two wings. None of the other powers does this, so it must belong to a different power.

An objection follows: if this power perceives, then one and the same thing both perceives and rearranges images. If it does not perceive, then how can it rearrange the images and separate or combine them? This would contradict the claim that anyone judging two things must have both present to the mind.

In addition, estimation uses the imaginative power, which would mean that estimation both perceives and rearranges images. Tusi answers the first objection by saying that this power does not perceive; it only has the two things present as it works with them.[^V08-397] Not everything present to a power must be something it perceives. He answers the second objection by saying that one thing can perceive and act in different ways—through itself and through an instrument, or through two different instruments.[^V08-398] The imaginative power is located in the front part of the brain’s middle ventricle. It serves estimation and, through estimation, the intellect.

The estimative power is said to differ from the other powers because we judge what we perceive by meanings that we do not sense directly and that have no physical image. Some meanings are not the kind that can be sensed at all, such as the hostility a sheep perceives in a wolf or the affection a young lamb perceives in its mother. Other meanings can be sensed, but we may judge them without sensing them at that moment. For example, seeing something yellow may lead us to judge that it is honey and sweet, even though we have not tasted it. The power that perceives these meanings is called estimation. It perceives the first kind—the meanings tied to particular things—on its own. It perceives the second kind, images not currently present, by using the formative power. None of the powers we have mentioned can do this, since they perceive only sensible images, whereas estimation perceives meanings. It is therefore another power. The whole brain is involved in its work, but it is especially connected with the middle ventricle.[^V08-399][^V08-400]

In my view, although estimation differs from the powers just mentioned, it is not a separate being from the intellect. It is the intellect turned toward a particular person or thing, concerned with it and directing it. The intellect connected with imagination is what we call estimation. What it perceives are general meanings attached to images of particular things.[^V08-401] Estimation has no existence of its own apart from the intellect, just as a general nature, such as humanity, has no existence apart from actual human beings or the intellect’s understanding of it.

Here is the argument. When estimation perceives the hostility of a particular animal, either it perceives hostility apart from that animal or it perceives hostility only as belonging to that individual. If it is the first, then it has perceived the general idea of hostility, and estimation is simply intellect. If it is the second, then it is clear to the intellect that hostility is not a physical quality residing in that animal. If it were, it would be sensed like the animal’s existence or unity. Perceiving its hostility would then be an act of sense, not estimation.[^V08-402][^V08-403]

In general,[^V08-404] when an intelligible meaning is found in particular things, it is there in one of two ways. It may be a meaning the mind draws from those things, such as cause and effect, before and after, and other relations such as parent and child. Or it may have a sensible form in those things, such as blackness, smell, or taste.

The first kind is perceived by intellect alone when considered apart from what it relates to, and by estimation when it is tied to one or more particular things. The second kind is perceived by one of the senses or by imagination. Hostility is of the first kind. Even when tied to a particular, it remains a general meaning joined to that particular; it does not physically reside in a body. Estimation perceives a general meaning qualified by something particular. As mentioned above, the other class is perceived by intellect or by one of the senses.

The power of memory is said to be a storehouse for estimation, just as imagination is a storehouse for common sense. It holds images of the meanings estimation perceives. It is also called the power of recollection because it can bring those meanings back.

Recollection sometimes moves from an image to its meaning. Estimation, helped by the imaginative power, looks through the images held in imagination until it finds the image that was present when it first perceived a given meaning. Then the meaning stored in memory comes back to mind.

At other times, recollection moves from a meaning to its image. The power looks through the meanings stored in memory until it finds the one that was perceived together with the image being sought. If that does not work—because the image has faded from imagination or some other obstacle gets in the way—we need to sense it again.

The outer sense then supplies the image, imagination stores it, and the meaning held in memory returns.

Some object that preserving meanings differs from perceiving them, and perceiving them differs from searching through them. So recollection must involve separate powers for preserving, perceiving, and searching. That would bring the number of inner powers above the five already mentioned. The answer is that estimation perceives, memory preserves, and the thinking power searches and arranges. These three powers are enough for recollection; no further power is needed. Recollection is one name for their combined work, not a single power in its own right. Remembering likewise combines perception with preservation.

These powers differ in their existence and may develop at different times. Some animals have only outer sensation; others have imagination but no estimation; others lack memory. Yet when fully developed, they return to one underlying self with many ways of acting. I will explain later how the soul itself perceives both particular images and general meanings. I have already offered many hints that can lead a thoughtful reader to this point, but I will make the matter clearer, since it is a profound subject and a precious one.[^V08-405]

Avicenna also says in *The Canon* that there is a philosophical question here: are preservation, recollection, and the recovery of meanings lost from memory one power or two? He adds that a physician does not need to settle it. He does not decide between difference and unity because either way, the answer is not needed for medicine. A physician is not required to know this. For them, the powers of estimation and memory act in one brain region, or in regions so closely joined that an injury to one affects the other.

A theoretical question needs proof, and medical evidence cannot settle it. A full explanation would belong to another field, beyond medicine. Yet in *The Healing*, Avicenna says that the same power is also called remembering: it preserves what it has, recalls it when ready, and brings it back when it has been lost. Estimation then uses its imaginative power to present images one by one until the needed one appears. This suggests that the two are one power. But, as we have seen, remembering involves two acts, while recollection involves three. Their unity is therefore a unity in name and function, as is the unity of the power from which they arise. There is more to say about this, and I will return to it.

Avicenna supports this view in *The Healing*. Near the end of his discussion of the soul’s powers, he says that the estimative power may be the very same power as the thinking, imaginative, and remembering powers, and as the power that judges. It judges by its own nature, and imagines and remembers through its movements and actions. It imagines through what it does with images and meanings, and remembers through the result of that work. Memory is the storehouse in which these are kept.

This does not conflict with what Avicenna says just before this passage; it supports it. He says that the power that combines image with image, image with meaning, or meaning with meaning seems to be the estimative power in its location—not in its role as judge, but in its work of reaching a judgment. He places it in the middle of the brain so that it can reach both storehouses, for images and for meanings. This shows that the source of thinking, remembering, imagining, and preserving is one in its underlying identity, though it branches into different powers according to its changing states and levels.
