There is no real inconsistency in Avicenna’s words, as some have thought. In his view, these powers are distinct but depend on one another, and all are instruments of estimation. Their actions belong to them as a tool’s action belongs to the tool: strictly speaking, the action belongs to the one using the tool, not to the tool itself. Estimation, in turn, is an instrument of intellect in beings that have intellect.

But Avicenna and his followers did not hold that all powers of perception and movement, despite their differences, are one individual reality whose unity gathers all their meanings and identities. Listen closely to what is revealed from the world of mysteries, and guard it from those who are not ready to hear it.

#### Chapter 4 — The soul as the source of all our powers

By this I mean that the one who perceives through all the powers of a human being is the rational soul. It is also what moves the body through the powers found in animals, plants, and nature. This is a profound point, and there are many arguments for it. Some begin from perception, others from movement. I will give three arguments from perception first.

The first begins with what we know. We can compare what we see, feel, imagine, and understand. For example, we can say that something with this color has this taste, or that something with this sound has this smell. To make a judgment between two things, both must be present to the one who judges. To accept or reject a claim, we must first understand both sides. So there must be one power that can perceive all the things the outer senses perceive. Otherwise, we could not judge that this color belongs to this taste, or that this sound belongs to this smell.

The same is true when we imagine a face and then see it with our eyes. We can judge that the imagined face is the face of the person we see, Zayd, for example. One power must perceive both the imagined form and the image we see, so it can judge whether one matches the other. The same happens when we recognize Zayd’s hostility or ‘Amr’s affection: an image we see and a meaning we infer come together in us.

We can also combine or separate imagined forms and particular meanings, bring them together or distinguish them, and judge that one belongs to another—or does not. Any judgment requires both what is being judged and what is said of it to be present. So the power that combines or separates meanings and forms must perceive both.

When we see Zayd or ‘Amr and judge that he is a human being or an animal, and not a stone or a tree, we judge a particular thing by a general idea. When we see a particular horse and judge that it is an animal, not a human being, we judge that this sensible thing is one instance of one general idea, but not another. We must therefore have one power that perceives both general ideas and particular things. This proves that the soul is one power that perceives every kind of thing.

The second argument begins from movement. Human actions are voluntary, so the mover must act by choice. Anyone who acts by choice is aware of the goal of the action, whether the action is guided by reason, by a sense of what is wanted, by anger, or by desire. We move in many ways: sometimes reason guides us, sometimes estimation, sometimes we seek a bodily pleasure, and sometimes we avoid what feels unpleasant. So there must be one thing in us that perceives through every act of perception and moves us in every movement of the soul. This is what we set out to prove.[^V08-406][^V08-407]

If you say that the soul perceives and moves only through its powers, so those powers—not the soul—are the true sources of perception and movement, I reply that the soul is the true perceiver. Its powers are tools. As I have said, an action belongs to a tool only in a loose sense; in the full sense, it belongs to the one who uses it.

If you say that the soul directly perceives sensible things, it must be a sense power; if it directly perceives images, it must be imagination; and if it perceives meanings, it must be estimation. Then one thing would be intellect, estimation, imagination, sense, nature, and a mover; it would be both immaterial and material.

I reply that the soul truly has all these powers and is the source of them. It has levels of existence, from less complete to more complete, and it develops through them. Each time it reaches a more complete level, it takes in more of what came before. The higher kind includes the lower. Just as animal life completes plant life, plant life completes the life of minerals, and minerals complete the life of bodies, human nature completes all the kinds that came before it—animal, plant, and elemental. A complete thing is what it was before, with something more added. So a human being truly contains all these kinds of life, and the human form is the completion of them all.

If you say there is no need to prove that the powers exist, since the soul can do everything, I reply that this is like the question of whether God is the true source of all actions. The philosophers’ account of how existence and action flow in an order does not rule out the conclusion that God is the true source of everything.[^V08-408] In the same way, the soul is one but has many different ways of acting. A wise person must learn these ways and keep their levels distinct. Otherwise, through ignorance, we may assign an action to the wrong source. It would be wrong, for example, to say that the intellect itself senses or desires. Intellect is too noble to be appetite. And it would be a serious mistake to say that the rational power itself removes waste through urination and excretion. It does not move the body directly in this way; it moves it by a rational desire and longing. The same applies to those who blame God for evil and base actions without accounting for the higher and lower powers through which they occur. This is disrespectful, even if they do not realize it.

Knowing what the soul is and how it acts is a way to know God and His actions. If we understand that the soul is the substance that understands, estimates, imagines, senses, moves, smells, tastes, touches, and grows, we can rise to the understanding that nothing truly acts in existence except God.

The second proof begins from the one who knows. You do not doubt that you see things, hear sounds, and understand ideas. You also do not doubt that you are one person. If the one who understands ideas were different from the one who sees and hears, then the self that you truly are would not have perceived both. If it had perceived them both, one and the same self would have done so, which is what I claim. Otherwise, you would be two selves, not one. The same applies to desire and anger: you know that you desire a sexual partner or something else, and that you are angry with an enemy.

You might say that the power of sight in the eye is a tool located in the eye. It sees an object, then passes what it sees to the link between the eye and you, and you become aware of what sight has perceived. I ask: after this information reaches you, do you see the object as the tool did? If you say yes, then your seeing is one thing and the tool’s seeing is another. Even if your seeing depends on what the tool sees, you are a perceiver because perception has reached you, not simply because the tool perceives.

If you say that you do not perceive the object after the information reaches you, then you have not seen, heard, or felt your own pain, pleasure, hunger, or thirst. You only know that the eye or the power of sight, your tool, saw something. But knowing that your eye sees is not the same as seeing. Knowing that the ear hears, the foot walks, or the hand grasps is not itself hearing, walking, or grasping. In the same way, knowing that someone else is hungry, in pain, or pleased is not the same as feeling that hunger, pain, or pleasure. Yet everyone knows directly that we ourselves hear, see, feel pain and pleasure, grasp, and walk. If we could deny this kind of direct knowledge, we could deny everything we sense and experience. So the soul itself is the power by which we hear, see, grasp, and walk.

This proves that the substance that makes you who you are is the one that hears, sees, feels pain and pleasure, understands, thinks, grasps, and walks—even though, while we live in the natural world, each of these actions needs its own bodily tool. There is no disagreement about this. When the soul is no longer joined to the body and exists on its own, it can perform these actions without bodily tools. Those with complete souls have witnessed this, and dreams point to it: in dreams we perform these actions without using the body’s organs.

The third proof begins from our knowledge of ourselves. The soul is a particular, individual thing. It directs and governs the body, as we will see. It cannot govern a universal body; if it did, it would be an intellect wholly separate from the body and would be related to all bodies in the same way. That is not so. The soul governs one particular body. It cannot govern an individual body as an individual without knowing it as that very body. This requires the image of that particular body to be present to the soul. This proves that the soul perceives particular things, as well as general ideas. One individual self has many different levels.[^V08-409]

You might say that your soul governs a universal body, and its activity becomes specific because the body it governs is specific. I reply that this is false. First, everyone knows that they are trying to govern their own body, not a universal body. Second, the activity could become specific because of the body only if that body could receive a kind of governance that other bodies could not. But any body could receive the same kind of governance, so the body’s nature cannot explain why the activity is directed to this body. Third, the soul’s activity makes the body one particular body, since the soul gathers its parts and maintains its balance. If the soul’s activity became specific because of the body’s specific nature, this would lead in a circle. These are the general arguments for saying that the soul is the source of all the powers. They do not deny that the powers exist, as I have already explained.

There are also more specific arguments. The first is that desire and anger do not have the body, or any physical body, as their home. Every body has parts. If desire and aversion belonged to the body, then one part might desire something while another part felt aversion to it. The same person would then desire and reject one thing at the same time.

The second argument is that estimation is not material. Otherwise, affection and hostility would be divided as the body that contains them is divided. They would have measurable parts and could be pointed to: “That quarter of the affection is up there, and this third is down here, in the market or in the house.” But they cannot be divided or located in this way.

The third argument is that imagination and memory are not bodily powers. Strong arguments for this have been given before, including the fact that images seen in dreams or delirium, and forms created by imagination, are real in some way. The body cannot contain them, since the body has a position in space but these images do not. Nor can an enormous image be imprinted in a tiny brain. Such images belong to the soul in another way.[^V08-410][^V08-411]

Here is another argument. If images were physically imprinted in the brain’s vital spirit, as many people claim, then either each image would need its own separate place, or they would all be imprinted in the same place. The first is impossible. One person may remember books, visit many regions and countries, see their wonders, and keep all those images in imagination. The brain’s vital spirit clearly cannot provide enough separate places for all of them. If they all occupied the same place, imagination would be like a writing tablet covered with lines written over one another, so that none could be distinguished. But imagined forms do remain distinct. So they are not imprinted there. Nor can things that share one nature become one being when they come together and still remain distinct. If they became one, none of them could be singled out as the place where a particular image resides.[^V08-412]

Another argument is that the brain’s vital spirit is a body, so it has a size. If we imagine a size, and that size were physically present in the vital spirit, two sizes would occupy the same matter, which is impossible.[^V08-413]

A fourth argument comes from Avicenna’s *Discussions*. It begins with a view that conflicts with many of his other principles: that perceiving something different from oneself requires its image to be imprinted in the perceiver. If the soul perceives images of size, this view would make the soul a place for size. It also says that one substance cannot be both immaterial and material, understanding and sensing.[^V08-414][^V08-415] We take the argument as a proof because it agrees with principles we have established: existence is real in things, not their abstract definitions; existence can become stronger or weaker; a substance can change in its essence; and the knower can become one with what it knows. The soul can become intellect after first becoming sense. Avicenna disagrees with us on many such points, but truth is more worthy of following.

Here is his exact argument from *Discussions*. If the things we perceive or imagine were perceived by a body or something bodily, that body would either be able to break apart and increase as it takes in food, or it would not. The second is false, because our bodies break down and grow as we take in food.[^V08-416]

Someone might say that nature keeps the original parts of the body in place, while new parts are continuously joined to them. These new parts are like reserves, ready to replace the old ones as they break down. The original parts remain, with the new parts adding to the body without changing its essence. I reply that this cannot work. The new part either becomes one with the original part or it does not.[^V08-417] If it becomes one, then either the original and new parts each have their own imagined image, or one image spreads across both. The first would mean that we imagine the same thing twice, once through the original part and once through the part added to it. If one image spreads across both, then when the added part disappears, the remaining part would hold only a partial image. So when the body breaks down, our imagined forms should become incomplete, but they do not. Or, if all parts are subject to the same breakdown and replacement, then the original part is also subject to change, just like the new one.

So the place that holds imagined and remembered things would be a body that breaks apart and grows through food. If so, the same imagined form could not remain. When its bodily subject changes and breaks apart, everything in it must change too.

If the first imagined image disappears, either another similar image comes into being or it does not. It cannot be replaced by another image. A new subject would need to acquire the image through the outer senses, just as the first subject had to. Then nothing would remain in memory. But we know from experience that memories do remain. So memory and recollection are not bodily. They belong to the soul.

The soul gains the ability to call back images that have faded by experiencing them again and again. This prepares it to receive them. Then, whenever it wishes, it can call them back from separate sources.[^V08-418] Remembering and imagining therefore work like understanding general ideas.[^V08-419] Once the soul has gained the ability to connect with the active intellect,[^V08-420] it can call back ideas from it whenever it wishes, even after forgetting them. The same is true of images. The difficulty is to explain how imagined forms are present to the soul.[^V08-421]

At the end of this chapter, Avicenna says that these arguments and others show that even nonrational animal souls are immaterial substances. They are the same enduring selves that perceive and experience things, while the body and its powers are tools that change around them. This completes the evidence for our claim.

#### Chapter 5 — Answering the claim that the soul cannot perceive particulars

There are four general arguments against the idea that the soul perceives particular things. The first is that people know directly that sight happens in the eye and hearing in the ear, and nowhere else. Just as we know that the tongue does not see and the eye does not taste, we know that the tongue tastes and the eye sees. If the soul were the one that perceived everything, these familiar distinctions would be false.

Someone might say that the power of perception is not in these organs but uses them as tools. When the soul turns its attention to the eye, it sees; when it turns to the ear, it hears.[^V08-422] I reply: when the soul turns to the tongue, does the tongue taste? When it turns to the skin, does the skin feel pain when struck? If so, this proves my point. If not, then taste does not belong specially to the tongue; the tongue would be no more than a tool, like the hand.

The answer is that many people feel that general ideas and abstract thoughts come from the heart[^V08-423] or brain. Does this show that general ideas are located in the heart or brain? If not, then the same point applies to the senses. People also know directly that the eye is not the one who sees, the ear is not the one who hears, and the throat is not the one who speaks. It is the person who hears, sees, and speaks. Some people first thought that the whole body was the one with these qualities. But when they saw that no individual part has them all, they became confused.[^V08-424] The more insightful among them recognized the soul. So it is not self-evident that the eye itself sees. What is self-evident is only that the eye has a role in seeing; we do not know at first whether it sees on its own or serves as a tool.

The second argument is that when an organ is damaged, its action stops, weakens, or becomes confused. This is clear for the five outer senses. Medical experience also shows that when the front part of the brain is damaged, imagination is affected; when the middle part is damaged, thinking is affected; and when the back part is damaged, memory is affected. If these powers were not bodily, this would not happen.

The answer is that a power may need an organ in order to act, without needing it in order to exist. People with weak sight may need a crystal lens to see, but that does not mean the lens itself sees. The same applies to the body’s organs.

The third argument is that animals also perceive particular things. If that required a separate rational soul, animals would have one too, which seems unlikely. The reply is: why would that be impossible? In fact, mature animals with memory have souls that are separate from the physical world, though still tied to imagined forms and sizes. They do not rise to the realm of purely intellectual beings. Nor do I claim that particular perception by itself requires a power that does not depend on matter. I claim that our soul perceives particular things in its own right. Since we have also shown that it understands general ideas, and that the power that understands them is immaterial, one power that does not depend on matter in us must perceive both. But we have no such argument for other animals, so their case remains uncertain.

The fourth argument is that when we perceive a sphere, its image must be imprinted in the perceiver. But a sphere cannot be imprinted in something with no position or place, something that cannot be pointed to. I reply[^V08-425] that this objection applies only to those who say perception happens when the image is physically imprinted in the perceiver. I do not hold this view. A form can belong to the perceiver without being physically lodged in it.

Someone might reply that even if the soul does not perceive individual spheres, it still perceives the general idea of a sphere. That idea would have to be imprinted in the soul, so the same objection would arise: how could something with a position be imprinted in something with no position?

This is false. As we have explained in our discussion of mental existence,[^V08-426] a general idea of a sphere is called a sphere in one basic sense, but not in the ordinary sense. When the intellect understands it, it is not a physical sphere and has no size. This differs from a particular imagined sphere, which has a particular size, though it still has no position in the physical world.

This was explained in our discussion of position. The specific arguments say that perception must be bodily. If the soul perceives sensible things, then we should not need those things to be present. The soul is not a body, so it cannot be near or far from bodies. All sensible things, near or far, present or absent, should be perceived in the same way.

Someone might say that the soul perceives sensible things with the help of organs, which can be near or far from them. I reply that if the eye had no power of sight, then whether an object was near the eye would make no difference to the one who sees. It would be like an object being present to Zayd’s eye: that is not enough for ‘Amr to see it.

The answer is that although the soul is the one that perceives sensible things, its perception depends on certain conditions. The organ must be sound, and the sensible object must be present to it in a certain way. Since sight depends on an object’s being present to the organ, perception differs according to presence or absence, distance or nearness. I say that the soul has three levels: intellectual, imaginative, and sensory. It becomes one with intellect, imagination, and sense. When it perceives sensible things, it becomes the senses themselves. Sense is a bodily power affected through a shared position. Two things therefore happen during sensation: the sense organ is affected, and the soul perceives. The need for the object to be present in a certain position belongs to the organ’s physical response, not to the soul’s act of perception.

People also give three arguments for saying that imagination depends on a bodily power. The strongest is this: imagine a square with two equal squares extending from it, one to the right and one to the left. In imagination, the two side squares are clearly distinct. Either they differ in what they are, in something inseparable from what they are, or in an additional feature that need not belong to them. The first two are impossible because the squares are alike in kind. The third option is that some additional feature distinguishes them. This feature must either depend on someone’s choosing to assign it, or it must not.

It cannot depend on someone choosing it. First, we can imagine one square on the right and one on the left without first assigning them some extra feature. If the right-hand square could become the left-hand square simply by our choosing, then this would be possible—but it clearly is not. Second, whoever makes this choice must already distinguish the squares before choosing which one is right and which is left. If the distinction comes from the feature assigned, this leads in a circle.

If their difference does not depend on a choice, it must come either from the original body or from the mind. The first is impossible. We often imagine things that do not exist outside us, and we cannot relate an image to pure nothing. Also, if both squares were held in one place in the mind, neither would have a stronger claim to be the image of one outer square rather than the other. Their distinction must therefore come from the mind. This would mean that one square is held in a different place from the other, which would be possible only if imagination were bodily.

Someone might say that we can understand a general square and connect it with “right” or “left,” and then distinguish the two squares in thought. I reply[^V08-427] that the general square, and the general ideas of right and left, are linked by a choice of the mind. We can change that choice. But imagined squares are not distinguished this way. We cannot simply decide that the particular square on the right is now the particular square on the left. So the two cases differ.

The second argument is that images of the same kind can have different sizes: some imagined squares are smaller, some larger. This difference must come either from what the image was taken from or from the one who receives it. It cannot come from an outer object, since we can imagine things that do not exist outside us. So the difference in size must come from the receiver, which means that the image is placed in a larger or smaller bodily part.

The third argument is that we cannot imagine black and white in one imagined image, but we can imagine them in two separate parts. If those parts did not differ in position, then there would be no difference between what is possible and impossible. So the parts must differ in position.

I answer the first argument in two ways: by showing that it proves too much, and by explaining what happens. First, we can imagine enormous things. If an image equal in size to one part of the thing is imprinted in the vital spirit, then the rest of the thing either is or is not imprinted there. If it is not, then the claim that imagination depends on imprinting fails. If it is, then the part equal in size and the part beyond it are both present in the same place. Yet we can distinguish the one part from the other. So even if both side squares are present in one place, we can still tell them apart.

A person may travel through the world, see many countries, and remember their books and wonders. If each image had to occupy a separate part of the brain, there would not be enough space. But if many images can occupy the same place and still remain distinct, then there is no reason that the two squares must be in different places in order for us to distinguish them.

Here is the explanation. The soul does not merely receive these forms; it makes them.[^V08-428] The two squares are distinct because the soul gives each its own identity. An imagined square does not exist in the same way as an outer square. It is a simple image, not matter that can take on many shapes. The faculty that forms it creates it, rather than physically stamping it in something. Any division or separation of an imagined size into two parts is made by the estimating power. It does not mean that one physical thing has been cut into two. The parts are made afresh by the mind, not taken from the original image.[^V08-429]

Everything imagination or estimation pictures and brings into the mind is created in this way: it comes from the power that makes it, not from a physical object that receives it. This is like the celestial spheres coming from higher sources through thought.

There is a difference: ideas[^V08-430] in these higher sources belong to what they are; they do not come from anything outside them. Here, outer objects help the mind create imagined forms. For example, when we sense a square with two squares attached to it in a certain arrangement, imagination can create a similar form or a different one. The actual square outside us has additional material features, because it exists in matter that can be divided. Imagination can create an image that resembles it or differs from it, all at once and as a single form. That image can have a right side or a left side, or some other arrangement, without the mind itself having a physical direction or position. An image of position does not need to have a position in the physical world.[^V08-431][^V08-432]

Suppose you say that the particular square is not made distinct by someone’s choice. I do not accept this. Its distinctness comes from the mind’s act of imagining and creating it. You say that if so, we should be able to turn the imagined right-hand square into the left-hand one by changing it. I reply that creating a particular image that has no material basis means making it exist as this specific form. It does not mean adding some feature to matter. The imagined square is just the particular square-form created by the soul.

In general, if the soul wants to make this imagined square into a different square, or this imagined size into a different size, it cannot do so. Nor can it split one imagined size into two parts. These forms are not made of a receiving material and a separate image. Each is a form without material. It is identical with the soul’s act of imagining and willing. So when an imagined size is divided, the soul is really creating two new forms from nothing, whether or not the first imagined size is still present. When it disappears, it does not change into something else that could be the matter for the new forms. This would be different from material things in the outer world.[^V08-433][^V08-434]

As for the second argument, the difference in size among imagined forms does not come from the outer object or from a bodily receiver. The mind creates the difference by its own action, rather than receiving it as a quality. As for the third argument, I do not deny that imagined forms differ in size and can be distinguished by the mind. But this does not prove that the soul is material or that it cannot perceive particular forms.

A difference in imagined position does not mean that imagined forms exist in a direction or place in the physical world.[^V08-435] The world of imagination is not this material world, which has place and changes over time. So the soul can imagine forms with size and shape while remaining immaterial. I will explain this further when I discuss the afterlife and the resurrection of bodies.

People also argue that estimation must be bodily. Since imagination is bodily, they say, estimation must also be bodily because it perceives only meanings tied to bodily images. Their reasoning is this: estimation either perceives friendship in general, or the friendship of a particular person. The first is false, since a general idea belongs to intellect, and here we are discussing the perception of particulars. So estimation must perceive the friendship of a particular person. To do so, it must also perceive that person, since we cannot understand a relation between two things without first understanding each one. But the person is a particular image, and they have already said that a power perceiving particular images must be bodily. So estimation must be bodily too.

The answer is that we have already shown that the power perceiving imagined particulars is not bodily. Estimation is even less bodily than imagination. The truth is that estimation and the meanings it perceives do not have an independent existence. Their relation to the things the intellect perceives is like a particular instance of a kind compared with the kind as a whole.[^V08-436] We understand hostility in general with pure intellect. We understand the hostility of a particular person with the intellect connected to imagination. And we understand hostility joined to a particular image with the intellect mixed with imagination.[^V08-437] Pure intellect is free of both the material world and the world of images, in its being and action. Estimation is separate from the material world in its being and has a relation to the world of images. Imagination too is separate from the material world in its being, though it is related to images.

The power that gives an animal the urge to act also relates to bodily desire.[^V08-438] We say that both it and estimation are immaterial powers, though we have not found any philosopher who says that the power that starts animal desire is bodily. The power that carries an action through the nerves and muscles is certainly bodily. It is called ability: a power in the nerves and muscles that moves them by tightening, loosening, pulling, or pushing.[^V08-439] The same is true of natural powers that move simple and complex bodies directly, by natural or forced movement. Forced movement ultimately comes back to natural movement, and movements that come through the soul also need natural powers.[^V08-440]

#### Chapter 6 — Earlier views about the soul, and how Avicenna answers them

I will treat the earlier thinkers’ words as symbols and interpret them as far as I can. Avicenna says that they disagreed because they followed different paths to understanding the soul. Some approached it through movement, some through perception, some used both paths, and others spoke about life without dividing it into parts.[^V08-441]

Those who followed the path of movement thought that movement could come only from something that was itself moving. The first mover, they said, must move by its own nature. Since the soul is the first mover of the body, moving the limbs through muscles and nerves, they concluded that the soul must move by itself. They also believed that anything that moves by itself cannot die.

Avicenna says that, on this view, the heavenly bodies cannot decay because they are always moving. Some thinkers said the soul was not a body, but an immaterial substance that moves by itself.[^V08-442] Others said it was a body and tried to identify which kind. Some said it was made of tiny round particles that could not be divided. They thought these particles kept moving so they could move other things. Animals breathed them in, and the life of the body was sustained as one set of particles left and another entered with each breath. The particles were like dust that is always moving in the air.[^V08-443] Others said these particles were not the soul itself, but that the soul moved them from within and entered the body as they did.

Some said the soul was fire, since fire is always moving. Those who followed the path of perception thought that a thing perceives others because it comes before them and gives rise to them. So they said the soul must be a first source. Some identified it with the kind of thing they thought was the first source: fire, air, earth, or water.[^V08-444] Some favored water because semen is very moist and is the beginning of living things. Others said the soul was a vapor, since they thought vapor was the source of things. All these thinkers said the soul knew everything because it shared the nature of the source from which everything came.

Those who thought number was the source of things said the soul was a number. Others thought a thing perceives only what resembles it, and that the actual knower resembles what it knows. They said the soul was made from the things they considered the basic elements. Empedocles, for example, said that the soul was made from the four elements, Strife, and Love. The soul perceives every thing in itself by perceiving what resembles it there.[^V08-445]

Those who followed both paths said that the soul was a number that moved by itself: a number because it perceives, and self-moving because it is the first mover.

Those who spoke about life without dividing it into perception and movement said that the soul was innate heat, since life depends on heat. Others said it was cold instead. They linked the word for soul to breath, which they thought cooled the body and kept the soul’s substance alive. Others said it was blood because life stops when blood is lost. Others said it was a balance of the body’s elements, since life lasts as long as that balance lasts. Some said it was a harmony among the elements. They thought that a living being needs a certain harmony, and that the soul, being harmony, is drawn to music, pleasant smells, and tastes.[^V08-446]

Some people even thought the soul was God—glory be to Him above what wrongdoers say—or that God exists in each thing in a different way: as nature in one, a soul in another, and intellect in another. Avicenna says that these are the views of the ancient thinkers, and they are all false. He then sets out to refute and discredit them.

I say that rejecting the surface meaning of these views is easy once we have proved that the soul is a substance separate from bodies. Anyone with even a little philosophy knows that the soul is a noble substance, not one of the lowly bodies such as fire, air, water, or earth, and not a relation or a combination of elements. How could earlier thinkers such as Empedocles have missed what even a beginner in the study of the soul can see? It is better to interpret their words than to reject them outright.

Let us begin, with God’s help. Those who said the soul moves because it is the first mover were right. The soul is indeed a kind of movement. This does not mean it is a body. Its movement is a change in its own being, from one level to another. As I have explained, it develops from sensing to understanding. At first it becomes one with sense. Later it reaches imagination. When ideas become present to it, it becomes a separate intellect.

It is absurd to say that the soul of a child or embryo is exactly the same substance as a fully developed intellect among the angels, and that only its relations or outward qualities have changed.[^V08-447] On that view, the souls of simple-minded people and children would be identical in their nature and essence to the souls of prophets, peace be upon them. Any difference between them would come from something outside their humanity: knowledge, power, or other qualities. Then the true greatness would belong to those outside qualities, not to the human being. So when we call a prophet the noblest of human beings, it would mean that something outside his real nature is nobler than what belongs to him and his people. This is wrong.

The soul of a prophet, in its complete being, is nobler than the other human souls. It has more strength, completeness, light, and depth of being.[^V08-448] It is a soul that has reached the highest level of soul and intellect. As the saying goes, “I have a time with God in which no near angel and no sent prophet can be with me.” This does not mean that the Prophet is not human in his ordinary bodily life. The Qur'an says, “I am only a human being like you.”[^V08-449] So the human soul has different inner stages, ways of acting, and levels of development. It is always changing from one state to another. Anyone who looks within can find new states arising in the soul from moment to moment.

Those who said the first mover must move by itself were also right. The first mover means what directly brings about movement. This is either nature or the soul, when a being has a soul. As I explained earlier, the immediate cause of a body’s movement in place, size, quality, or position must be a substance whose existence is changing, even if its basic nature stays the same. By natural movement I also mean movement of the heavenly spheres, which comes from their nature—their soul—and is guided by its perception and desire. The same is true of growth in plants and animals. It is natural in one way and comes from the soul in another.

In general, every movement in the body comes directly from a nature that runs through it. This nature changes in its being, as we have shown. No natural body is without it. In the heavens, animals, and plants, it is inseparable from the soul. The soul is the completion of this nature. This does not mean that nature and soul are two separate substances in one body. That would be impossible. Each natural being has one self and one form. As the source of bodily movement and change, it is nature; as the power that perceives and directs, it is soul.

In the previous chapter, we proved that the soul within us is the source of sensation, movement, imagination, estimation, and thought. So the ancient thinkers were right that the first source of movement is something that moves by itself—not as a body, but in its being and identity. In a living being, the soul is the first source of movement. The movement passes through it to the muscles and limbs because it is one with the nature whose being is always changing. This nature brings about all changes, including forced and accidental movement and voluntary movement. As we have seen, all of these come back to nature and soul.

They were also right to say that whatever moves by itself does not die. They meant that what takes its movement from its own nature, not from something else, does not die. It does not perish; it moves from one world to another. This is not true of bodies simply as bodies, since the source of their movement is outside their material nature.[^V08-450] Anything whose movement comes from itself is drawn toward its own completion.[^V08-451] What is drawn toward completion by its original nature does not die, but moves from one level of existence to a higher one.

Movement that comes from the self is the movement of the soul.[^V08-452] The ordinary movement of simple elements from place to place does not come from their own essence; it depends on their moving out of their natural place. The movements of minerals and plants also need outside conditions, such as heat to cook or ripen, cold to gather, or moisture to help them take shape. The self-movement of nature does come from within, but simple elements do not last as individual things because their opposed qualities destroy them.

The souls of animals and the natures of the heavenly bodies—which are the same as their souls—have life in themselves. They are always drawn toward permanence. Their movement toward completion is part of their own nature. If these souls are intellectual, like the souls of the heavenly bodies and people complete in knowledge, they return to God in the realm of separate intellects. If they are not intellectual, but imaginative or estimative, they still pass into the world of souls, at different levels of honor or lowliness, happiness or suffering. Suffering after death does not prevent a soul from developing in its own way—through cunning, animal desire, or fierce dominance. These may be forms of completion for other kinds of soul, though they are flaws in the human soul.

Those who called the soul a body meant the animal’s imagined, otherworldly form, not the natural body that moves and changes here. They meant a form in the intermediate world, with animal limbs and organs. This living image is not alive because of an added quality. Through it, the rational soul acts on this natural body and perceives particular and sensible things. Those who travel the spiritual path and have direct insight agree on this. It is the form we see in dreams: we see ourselves with limbs and organs, and we see, hear, smell, taste, touch, grasp, and walk. These are spiritual organs, not our natural ones.

Those who said the soul was made from indivisible particles meant imaginal bodies, which have no matter. As we have seen, they cannot be divided even in thought, much less by cutting them in the outer world. When the mind divides an imagined body, it creates two new images; it does not split one material body.

Some said the soul was round because the sphere is the simplest shape, and the heavenly bodies are round.[^V08-453] The soul was thought to be spherical because this is the easiest form to move. Through movement, the soul’s undeveloped abilities become real. We also hold that the soul and body are joined as one. The soul can therefore be described by some bodily features, since it is the source and completion of the body. This does not mean it is matter. A body’s form and material are different ways of looking at it. In the same way, a soul’s form can be described in bodily terms. The heavenly soul’s roundness is one such case.

Those who said that animals draw the soul in through breathing meant that an animal[^V08-454] has a subtle, airy spirit, clear like the heavenly bodies. This spirit serves the soul in the natural body. The soul maintains its balance, and it is nourished by clear air. Since this spirit carries the powers of sensation and movement and is continuously given by the soul to the body, these thinkers called it “the soul.” Avicenna mentions a saying in Persian: “They call the vaporous spirit *jān*, and the rational soul *ravān*.” Those who said that the soul is not this spirit but moves it from within and enters the body with it were right. This agrees with the interpretation I have just given.

Those who said the soul was fire, always in motion, did not mean the basic element fire. They meant another fire: the essential heat that changes and breaks down bodies. This is nature.[^V08-455] Above it is the fire of the commanding soul, made up of desire and anger. This is the fire that “rises over the hearts,” as the Qur'an says. When obedience and discipline calm it, the fire becomes light and the commanding soul becomes peaceful.

Those who followed perception as their path were right to say that a thing perceives another because it comes before it and gives rise to it. What we directly know is an image present to the knower. To perceive something is for it to exist for the knower. This kind of presence depends on an essential relation between them: a relation of cause and effect. But the image is made by the knower; it is not physically imprinted in the knower, as we explained in our discussions of mental existence and sight.

Those who made the soul from the first source—fire, air, earth, or water—may have meant the source that acts directly on the body. Those who called it fire meant what I have already described. Air may stand for desire and love, since the soul itself is a kind of love. Mystics often call the first source love. People differ in what they love.

Those who called it water meant the water of life, which gives life to every living thing. As God says, “We made every living thing from water.” The soul gives life to the body just as intellect gives life to the soul. For this reason, some ancient thinkers called universal intellect the first element and likened it to water. The “first element” may mean existence itself, which flows from God to all things in order.

Those who called the soul earth may have meant that it receives knowledge and images that flow from the heaven of intellect. At the level where the soul is still only potential intellect, it is like the earth of reality. Knowledge descends to it from active intellect as rain revives the earth after it is dead. A saying in the tradition puts it this way: “Hearts live by knowledge, as the earth lives by rain from the sky.”

Those who made number[^V08-456] the source of things, and called the soul a number, meant something like what the Pythagoreans meant. The first source is truly one and gives rise to all things, just as the number one gives rise to other numbers. But the order of first, second, third, and tenth in ordinary numbers is not part of their nature. Anything that is first can become second; anything second can become first. This is not true of God, the first source: His being first belongs to His very nature. The same is true of the first intellect, and of the souls that come after the intellects. Their order in existence cannot change. So the number-like order of intellect and soul is part of their nature. They are numbers in the true sense. Likewise, only God is truly one and first; everything else is made of parts.

Those who said that everything perceives what resembles it were right. As I have repeated in this book, the one who perceives is always of the same kind as what is perceived. Touch perceives what can be touched; taste perceives flavors; sight perceives what can be seen; imagination perceives imagined forms; estimation perceives meanings; and intellect perceives ideas. Each power, by taking on the form of what it perceives, realizes an ability that was previously undeveloped. What is ready to receive a thing is of the same kind as that thing.[^V08-457]

We have also proved that the intellect becomes one with what it understands, and the sense becomes one with what it senses. This union is stronger than mere resemblance. When the soul becomes intellect, it becomes all things. Even now, it becomes one with anything it holds within itself—in other words, with the image of that thing, not the external thing itself.[^V08-458] The soul is not made up of these outside things or their images. The more complete it becomes, the more it can gather things into one, and the simpler it becomes. The truly simple being contains all things, as we have already shown.

Those who combined perception and movement into one account were right if the interpretations I gave for each are right. Those who explained the soul through life alone said it was innate heat, cold, blood, or harmony. Innate heat is a heavenly substance that comes from the soul into the body, and through it the soul carries out natural, plant-like, and animal actions. In my view, this hot substance—the animal spirit—is simple and is not made from the basic elements. As I have already explained, it serves the soul and is joined to it.[^V08-459]

Those who called the soul cold may have meant the body’s condition when it is at rest and safe from harm as long as the soul preserves its balance. Those who called it blood may have meant the carrier of the animal spirit. Blood runs through the body, and through it life reaches the organs. They may have used “soul” as a shared name for blood and spirit, or as a figure of speech. People still use “soul” to mean blood.

Those who called the soul a harmony may have meant that it brings the body’s elements together in a certain balance. Or they may have meant that the soul itself is made of different qualities joined in a noble, spiritual balance. The body’s balance would then be a shadow of the soul’s balance. Everything in the lower, natural world has a likeness in the higher world, but in a nobler form.[^V08-460]

Those who said that the soul was God may have meant the mystics’ idea of losing oneself in God’s unity, not the view of Pharaohs and atheists—God is far above what they say. As for the claim that God exists in each thing differently, as nature in one, soul in another, and intellect in another, I do not endorse it, to avoid disrespect.[^V08-461] I would say instead that the human soul is nature in one sense, sense in another, imagination in another, and intellect in another. It is the substance that understands, imagines, hears, sees, smells, tastes, touches, feeds, grows, and reproduces. Yet it is one simple, undivided substance. God made it a likeness of Himself in being and qualities, and His representative in this world and the next. Knowing the soul is a path to knowing God. This is as far as I can go in interpreting the symbols and bringing out their hidden meaning.

Avicenna also raises several objections to these views. I will address them briefly.

First, he says that those who relied on movement must attribute rest to the soul. If the soul’s movement causes the body to move, then when the soul stops moving, the body should stop too. If the soul remains moving while the body is still, then the movement of the soul cannot be the cause of movement and rest alike.

I reply that the bodily movement caused by the soul is not the voluntary movement from place to place that comes from outside reasons and later events, whether to get something pleasant or to avoid something harmful. I mean the ongoing movement of change in the living body: feeding, growth, ripening, and the other changes that happen every moment. This is the movement of life. Death is the stillness that begins when the soul is no longer joined to the body. The soul causes the movement needed for life by its own nature. It causes voluntary movement only through outside events and causes. The two are related like writing an image of a body’s organs and cooking food in a pot, compared with digesting food in the stomach.

In short, the soul causes movement that does not stop while the body is alive. It does not cause stillness while it remains a soul and the body remains a body.

Avicenna’s second objection is that everything that moves must be moved by something else; nothing moves itself. I agree that every movement needs a source other than the thing that moves. But something that changes in one of the four outward ways—place, amount, quality, or position—needs something to cause that change. Something whose very being changes needs a source that gives it existence.[^V08-462] We have already explained the difference between features of existence and features of definition, and between differences in the outside world and differences created by thought. In substantial movement, movement is part of what a thing is; a being that depends on a cause for its existence depends on a cause that gives it its very being, not on something that adds movement to it. Existence is not an added feature separate from a thing, except in thought.[^V08-463] In either case, everything that moves needs a source other than itself, and all moving things ultimately lead to an unmoving source.

Third, Avicenna says that movement must be movement through place, amount, quality, position, or some other such category. I reply that there is another kind: movement in a thing’s very being. This is substantial movement. Avicenna’s argument against it is weak, as I explained earlier.[^V08-464]

Fourth, he says that if the soul changes in its essence, then it stops being a soul as it changes. Or if it changes in some feature, then that feature cannot be the source of movement.

I reply that here Avicenna mixes up a thing’s existence and its definition.[^V08-465] Substantial movement does not mean that the thing takes on a wholly new definition at every moment. A stronger or weaker state may differ in kind from another stronger or weaker state, while both remain parts of one continuous existence. The thing has one existence through time, and one definition,[^V08-466] though each moment is a stage of its being, moving from possibility toward reality. Animal life can change into another form of animal life; it does not have to become a heavenly body. Anything that can be stronger or weaker can develop in this way.[^V08-467] We say the same about qualities: blackness can become stronger or weaker without ceasing to be blackness. In the same way, the soul’s development does not make it stop being a soul.[^V08-468]

Even if the soul’s being a soul is a relation to the body, it does not follow that any change in the soul would make it stop being a soul.

Avicenna also says that if the soul changes in one of the outward features, then it would not move the body in the same way. I reply that the earlier thinkers only claimed that whatever moves another thing must itself move in some way. They did not say that both movements must be the same kind. In many cases, the movements caused by the soul are different from the soul’s own movement. The movements of the heavenly bodies are movements of position and depend on the soul’s movement of desire and choice, as Avicenna himself admits. So there is no problem in saying that the soul’s changes in its being or desire cause changes in the body, such as digestion or growth.

Fifth, Avicenna says that the claim that a thing perceives what resembles itself is impossible. We know and perceive many things that are not the sources from which they came.

I reply that by what is known, I mean the image present to the one who knows it and preserved by that person. The soul is the highest source of the images present in its own powers. It can take in general ideas when it is still only potential intellect; once joined with the active intellect, it creates and preserves them. In his book *The Origin and Return*, Avicenna says that the potential intellect can strip an image of its material features, then create and receive an intelligible form. It also acts on what is sensed and turns it into something intelligible.[^V08-469]

### Book Six — How the reasoning soul exists without depending on matter, and how it begins to exist

#### Chapter 1 — The reasoning soul is not a body and does not have a physical size

There are several arguments for this. The first begins with our ability to understand general ideas that apply to many different individual things. An idea that applies generally, considered as general, cannot exist inside a body or be imprinted in a bodily part, whether that part is divisible or indivisible.

It cannot exist in an indivisible point. A point cannot contain an intelligible form. Either the point is distinct from the thing whose boundary it marks, or it is not. If it is not distinct, an intelligible form could not be in the point without also being in the body that the point bounds. A point belongs to a magnitude as its boundary. So it could contain only the boundary of something that is already in that magnitude. The form would then be bounded only because the body containing it is bounded. But if the point existed separately from the magnitude, it would itself be divisible—which is impossible, as explained elsewhere.

Someone objects that, even if a point cannot be distinguished from the body it bounds, it does not follow that it can contain only the boundary of something in that body. Colors and light occur on surfaces, not in the depths of a body; the same is true of contact and touching.

The reply is that a surface can be considered in two ways: as the boundary of a body, or as a two-dimensional, extended part of it. Colors and light belong to it as an extended part, not simply as a boundary. For something to receive a quality, it must have a particular readiness or condition that enables it to receive that quality. A boundary, just as a boundary, has no varying power or readiness. So if a point could receive an intelligible form, it would always receive one: nothing in it could change to make it ready at one time and not another. The form would always be present, since the source of its flow gives it continuously.

And if the point were ready by its very nature, every body containing a point would be able to understand. Anyone dying would also remain able to understand, since the point that made this possible would still be there. That conclusion is false, so the claim that a point can receive an intelligible form must also be false.

Nor can an idea understood by the mind be contained in a divisible part of a body. Every magnitude can be divided, and anything present in a divisible thing must itself be divisible. So the intelligible form would have to be divided without end. This is impossible for several reasons.

First, the parts would have to be either alike or different. They cannot be alike. Each part would have to differ from the whole in some way, since a part cannot be equal to the whole in every respect. The difference cannot be in the form’s essential nature or in what necessarily follows from that nature; otherwise, the parts would not be alike. So it would have to come from material features such as size or shape. The form would then be material, which contradicts the claim that it is immaterial.

Second, suppose the form’s being understood depended on its division.[^V08-470] Then the parts would have to differ from the whole, and before the division the form would not be understood, since the required condition was absent. Anything understood in this way would have to be divisible,[^V08-471] though many things we understand are not. If division is not a condition for understanding, then dividing the form would merely cover it with accidental features, such as being gathered or separated. A smaller part of the body would then be enough to contain the form’s nature, because a part of the form[^V08-472] has the same nature as the whole. Its place would be only a part of the body’s place. The entire form could therefore be present in a smaller part of its supposed location. Its presence there would be accidental, not essential. But we are discussing an immaterial form, so this is impossible.

You might object that an idea understood by the mind can be divided into similar parts by adding further universal features. Take the general idea “animal.” It can be divided into the animal nature found in a human being and the animal nature found in a horse. These two animal natures are alike in kind: if we leave out the horse’s neighing and the human being’s rationality, their animal nature is the same. So a universal can apparently be divided into similar parts, even though[^V08-473] those parts have no particular size or shape.

I reply that this is a different kind of division. Adding one universal idea to another is combination, while dividing a whole into parts is separation. For example, adding “rational” to “animal” makes the human kind different from the horse kind. But dividing a whole into parts makes the parts equal to the whole in nature. If the parts of an intelligible form were like its separate instances, then the universal form would have to differ from every one of its parts. Its division would not be a division of magnitude, though we assumed that it was. In short, dividing “animal” into human being, horse, and other kinds divides a universal into its particular forms by adding different limits to it. The proposed division of an intelligible form is a division of a whole into parts. The two are clearly different.[^V08-474]

Nor can the form be divided into parts with different meanings. If it were, those parts would have to be its kinds and distinguishing features. But the parts we can imagine in a body have no limit in number. That would mean that the intelligible form had infinitely many essential parts, which is impossible.

There are further reasons. In any multitude there must be a definite number of units. If the form had infinitely many parts, each part would have to be a simple nature and therefore could not itself be divided into two parts with different natures. Also, if the form were made of infinitely many essential parts, each present in a different bodily part, the body itself would have infinitely many actual parts. That is impossible. Finally, suppose we divide the form so that its distinguishing feature lies on one side and its general kind on the other. If we make a different division, either each side contains half of both the feature and the kind—giving two similar parts, already shown to be impossible—or the kind and feature move into the new parts. Then simply imagining a different division would move the parts of the form from one place to another. Their location would depend on what someone chose to imagine, which is absurd.[^V08-475]

A second argument against dividing an intelligible form begins from the nature of a thing. Every thing has a reality by which it is what it is. That reality must be one, and cannot itself be divided. For something that can be divided must remain through the division. But ten, considered precisely as ten, does not remain when divided: five is not part of ten considered as a kind of number. It is part of the many units that make up ten, not part of ten considered as one reality. When ten is divided into two fives, they are parts of ten as a multitude, not of ten as one. Its multiplicity is measured by what is counted, not by ten itself: ten people are many in relation to one person, but ten is one number. It therefore has one form that cannot be divided.

If knowledge of these immaterial realities were present in a divisible place, then dividing that place would divide the knowledge, and dividing the knowledge would divide what it represents. But what is known is not divided, or is understood precisely as undivided. That is impossible.[^V08-476]

This is how the argument is usually presented in the books of the philosophers. They consider it one of the strongest proofs that the human souls which understand universal meanings are immaterial. But, as I have said, not every human soul has full intellectual immateriality.[^V08-477] In my view, this argument does not apply to every soul. It proves the immateriality of a soul that understands a form as actually intelligible in itself, whether because the form’s nature is immaterial or because the mind has abstracted it from what the senses perceive. Such a soul has moved beyond intellect as a mere capacity and become an intellect in reality, with ideas actually understood. Only some people reach this level.

Most people’s understanding of universal kinds works more like the way natural kinds exist in the world through their particular instances. Consider “animal” as animal. Its nature exists in the world through individual animals. We can consider that nature without limiting it to one place or position, but its shared nature can also be divided into particular instances. In the minds of most people, the nature of an animal exists through an imagined image. The mind can consider this image in different ways. As a particular image in a particular perceptive power, it is an imagined particular. Considered simply as what it is—animal, with no added condition—it is neither imagined, sensed, nor understood, because in that respect its existence is indeterminate, though it exists in reality through the image. Considered as common to many things, it is understood as an immaterial universal.[^V08-478]

So what exists in most people’s minds is not an immaterial form whose own existence is like that of separate intellects. It is something that can be considered in different ways: as particular, as universal, or without either qualification.[^V08-479] The same is true of the form of that nature in the world outside the mind. Nor do I agree that everyone can notice these different ways of considering it, or that everyone can grasp a mental form precisely as universal and shared by many, free of all particular limits.

I also question the second argument’s claim that every thing’s unity is wholly opposed to division, so that its form must be indivisible too. Many things are one while also being divisible, either actually, as a number is, or potentially, as a body or magnitude is. Ten is one number, but its unity is also the many units that compose it. That many[^V08-480] is not the multiplicity opposed to ten. The multiplicity opposed to the unity of ten is a set of tens; the unity of ten is itself the many tenths. Likewise, the unity of five is the many fifths. As we have seen, number has a weak kind of existence because its unity is also a multitude.

The same is true of bodies, magnitudes, and other continuous things. Their unity is their ability to be divided, in thought or in reality, because their unity consists in being continuous and extended. Extension means that they can be divided into parts, either potentially or actually. We therefore cannot find in such things a kind of unity that has no relation to multiplicity at all. Their unity differs from the kind of multiplicity that opposes it, but not from the multiplicity already contained within it. Many scholars, including Avicenna in *The Healing*, have missed this point. To show that unity differs from existence, he says that a multitude is something that exists, but a multitude as multitude is not one. The problem with this argument has already been explained.[^V08-481]

The philosophers also offer another version of this argument. They consider things that cannot be divided by reason, such as God, unity, and the simple realities that make up complex things. If a complex reality exists, it must ultimately contain simple realities, since every multitude includes unity. They then ask: if knowledge of such a simple reality is divided, are its parts each pieces of knowledge or not? If they are not, then knowledge is not the sum of those parts; it is the whole formed when they come together. The question then concerns that whole: if it were divided, would its parts be knowledge? If so, each part must be about something. Is each part about the whole thing known, or about only a part of it? If each part were about the whole, a part of knowledge would equal the whole in every respect, which is impossible. If it were about a part, then the simple reality known would have parts, though we have shown that it has none. They say this is the best form of the argument.

I reply that not every human soul can imagine the true nature of these simple realities. When most people try to understand them, they picture imaginary shapes or forms with size. How could they know the Creator in His essence or simple intellectual realities? The argument is soundest when applied to souls that know God and separate forms as they truly are. Very few souls reach that level. Other souls are free of natural bodies but not of imagined forms.

Some later thinkers object that a point must either exist in a divisible part of a body, in which case an indivisible thing would be in something divisible, or in an indivisible part, which would mean there are indivisible particles. They also object that unity, though far removed from multiplicity, is present in bodies; that fatherhood is present in a father though it cannot be divided; and that estimation is a bodily power even though the hostility it perceives cannot be divided into halves or quarters. The same, they say, is true of friendship and similar meanings.

The first objection is answered by recognizing that a point does not belong to a body considered simply as a divisible thing. It belongs to the body insofar as its extension has an end or is cut off. The body contains a point as its boundary, and a boundary involves an absence: it is where extension runs out. This differs from knowledge, which completes the thing that has it.

As for unity, I have explained that the unity of a body is divisible because it is the body’s continuity and extension. Its existence is also its being a body. It is remarkable that the unity of a body is divided directly when the body itself is divided, not indirectly as other features are divided when their subject is divided. Blackness, for example, is divided only because the body that has it is divided. The unity of a body is its very existence and individual identity.[^V08-482][^V08-483]

As for fatherhood and other relations, I do not agree that every relation is divided along with its subject. Relations that belong to a body just as a body—being alongside or touching another thing, for example—are divided with it. Half a body alongside something is half the relation of the whole body; a quarter of the body is a quarter of that relation. But some relations belong to a body because of something other than its bodily nature. These need not be divided when the body is divided. Zayd’s fatherhood, for example, does not belong to him because of his size or his being a body, but because of another feature, such as a soul’s power to generate. The last objection is answered by what I said earlier: estimation is immaterial, though it remains connected to matter, and the same is true of what it perceives.

The second proof is one Avicenna relied on in his *Discussions*. I believe it is his strongest argument on this subject. His students raised many objections, and he answered them. I found their questions and replies scattered through a collection of letters between him and his students. I will present them here in order, with some additions of my own.

We can understand ourselves. Whoever understands a thing has its reality present to them. So the reality of our own self is present to us. Either we understand ourselves because another image, equal to our self, has appeared in us, or because our self itself is present to us. The first is impossible: it would mean that two identical things came together. So the second must be true. Whatever has itself present to itself exists in itself. The soul, then, is a substance that exists in itself. Every body or bodily thing depends on something else to exist; therefore the soul is not bodily.

The questioner says: I do not accept that being aware of ourselves means that the true reality of our self is present to us. Perhaps some effect of the self appears in us, and we become aware of that effect. It would not be the self itself. In that case, the reality of the self produces an effect in us, and we notice the effect; it is not the self that appears in us twice.

The respondent says: We have already established that awareness means that the reality of a thing is present. If you say that an effect of the self appears in us and we become aware of that effect, either awareness is that same effect, or it is something different that follows from it. If awareness is the effect itself, then saying that we become aware of the effect adds nothing to saying that the effect appears in us. If awareness follows the effect, then either awareness is the presence of the thing’s own reality or the presence of some other reality. If it is some other reality, then awareness would mean that something other than the thing’s reality gives us the thing’s meaning. If it is the thing’s own reality, then the self’s reality would need this effect in order to be present to itself. It would not exist until the effect brought it into being. It would then be created by the effect, not affected by it, which is absurd. Or perhaps you mean that the self’s reality is present to us in another way, by being stripped of some features or newly formed. Then what we understand is that changed or stripped-down form. But our question concerns the enduring substance of the self, which is understood as the same in both cases.[^V08-484][^V08-485][^V08-486]

The questioner says: Let us agree that we understand ourselves. Why say that whoever understands a thing has the reality of that thing present? If that were so, when we understood God and the active intellects, their true realities would have to be present in us.

The respondent says: What we receive from the active intellect, if we can understand it, is its kind and nature, not its individual self. The two are not the same in every respect. But what we understand of your reality does not differ from your reality in kind, in nature, or in any added feature; nor does it differ from you as an individual. It is you yourself in kind and as an individual. The active intellect and what we understand of it are the same in meaning, but not the same individual.

I reply that the specific nature of the active intellect cannot have multiple individuals, either in the world or in the mind. Proof shows that each separate intellect is the only individual of its kind. If one nature had multiple individuals, each would have to be distinguished by some feature that was not essential to the kind. But an accidental feature would require an external cause and a subject capable of change. The intellect would then not be separate from matter, which is impossible. The better answer is that what appears in our minds of these simple realities is not their innermost nature. We know only general ideas such as “separate substance,” “intellect,” and the like.[^V08-487][^V08-488]

The questioner says: If an image equal to God’s nature appears in our intellect, then God’s nature must apply to many things in the world.

The respondent says: Proof shows only that God’s nature does not apply to many things in the world; it does not show that it cannot apply to many things in the mind.

I reply: God’s reality cannot be a nature that could be shared by many, either in the world or in the mind. He is pure, self-subsisting existence, complete beyond anything else. Anything with a general nature has an identity beyond that nature. Existence itself is individual identity. So anything that has a nature or essence is only possibly existent. The right answer is that we do not know the Creator’s essence itself. We know Him through features that point to Him, or by saying what He is not.

The questioner says: Let us agree that when we understand something, its nature is present to us. Why can’t the self be present in our power of estimation, which then becomes aware of it—just as the intellect is aware of estimation? The intellect would then not be present to itself, only to estimation, just as you say that estimation is not present to itself but to the intellect.

The respondent says: Your awareness of your own identity is not the work of one of your powers. Otherwise, the one aware of your self would not be the self you are aware of. Yet when you are aware of yourself, you know that you yourself are both the one who is aware and the one you are aware of. If another power were aware of your self, either that power would exist in you, or it would not. If it existed in you, then your enduring self would be present to that power, and so would be present to itself—which is what we wanted to show. If the power were not in you but in a body, then either your self would exist in that body or it would not. If it did not, there would be no awareness of your self at all, only a body sensing something other than itself, as your hand or foot senses something. If your self did exist in that body, then both the self and the power aware of it would exist in something else. The self could not be aware of itself through that power, since both the self and the power would belong to that body.[^V08-489]

The questioner asks: Could I be aware of myself because an image of me appears in something whose relation to me is like a mirror’s relation to sight?[^V08-490]

The respondent says: Even if we agree that your image appears in the mirror, it must then appear a second time in the eye. In the same way, the image of the self would have to appear in the self again.

The questioner asks: Could awareness of myself come from another image appearing in me? When I understand Zayd’s soul, either I am not aware of myself at that time—which is impossible, since anyone who understands something is also potentially aware of themselves—or I am aware of myself too. In that case, either two images are present, one of me and one of Zayd, or there is only one. If there is one image, then I would be Zayd and Zayd would be me: the same image would have my features in one respect and Zayd’s in another. If there are two images, that proves my point.

The respondent says: When you understand a soul, you understand one part of your own reality. When you understand that Zayd is human, you add something else to your self and join it to what you already know. The idea “humanity” does not appear twice in you; it is one idea considered in two ways. Keep in mind that there is a difference between humanity considered in itself and humanity considered as universal and shared by many. The first is an essential part; the second is humanity with the added condition of being universal.[^V08-491][^V08-492]

The questioner says: The distinction you have made is false. If we say that a thing exists in itself, this can mean three things. First, it does not depend on anything else in order to exist. Second, it is not present in something else, as whiteness is present in a body. Both meanings are negative, while awareness is something positive: it means that the known form is present to the knower. Third, it could mean that the thing is related to itself. But that is impossible, since a relation requires two things and unity rules this out. You cannot reply that the one who relates and the thing related may be either the same or different. That is a verbal trick. “A cause requires an effect” can also mean that the cause and effect are either the same or different, but that does not prove that a thing can cause itself.
