The same applies here.[^V08-493][^V08-494]

The respondent says: A thing’s true reality is one thing; its individual identity is another. The underlying whole is one, while its individual identity is something else. This is true whether that identity necessarily belongs to its nature, as with God and the active intellects, or whether it does not, as with kinds that have many individual members. That degree of difference is enough for a relation to be valid. It is therefore right to say “my self” and “your self.”

I reply that this answer is weak. In a truly simple being, whose identity is its very own, there are not two distinct aspects, a reality and an identity. I have already answered this problem about a simple being’s knowledge of itself, in the discussion of relations and of knowledge.

The questioner objects that animals perceive themselves, even though their souls are not immaterial. We should not accept the claim that they are unaware of themselves. They seek what suits them and flee what harms them. Their goal cannot be simply whatever suits them in general; otherwise, they would seek what suits other animals as eagerly as what suits themselves. Nor can they seek what suits them specifically because it is suitable in general: that would require them to understand a universal, which animals do not. So an animal seeks what suits itself in particular, and to do so it must perceive itself. Knowing that one thing is related to another requires knowing both things.

The respondent says: The human soul is aware of itself directly, but other animal souls are aware of themselves through their power of estimation, using the organ of estimation, just as they perceive other things through the senses and their organs. In animals, estimation perceives particular meanings that cannot be sensed but are linked to what is sensed. That is how animals perceive their own selves. They do not perceive themselves through their essence or through the heart, which is the organ of their essence, but through estimation and its organ. So an animal’s self is present once in the organ of its own life—the heart—and again in the organ of estimation, where it is perceived as present.[^V08-495][^V08-496]

The questioner asks: What proves that our awareness of ourselves differs from an animal’s?

The respondent says: The power that understands universal ideas can understand its own nature stripped of all accidental features. When we are aware of our particular self, mixed with other things, we are aware of a whole made of parts that we can recognize separately: our own true self and the things mixed with it. The self can be represented to us even when everything else is absent. An animal’s awareness of itself is not like this. That shows the difference.

I reply that this is really a retreat from the second proof to the first. The truth is that animals are aware of themselves through their own awareness, by an imagined rather than intellectual awareness. This does not depend on an organ of imagination. It shows that they are separate from natural bodies, though not from imagined forms. I have already proved that the imaginative souls of this world are immaterial. The difference between them and the highest human souls is that the latter can perceive themselves free of all distances, images, shapes, and the like.[^V08-497][^V08-498]

The questioner says: Even if we can distinguish ourselves in the mind from what is mixed with us, it does not follow that this distinction exists outside the mind. We make this distinction by thought, while reality may be different. This applies whether we perceive ourselves as universal or as particular and mixed with other things. In truth, anything that perceives something has that thing present to it, whether universal or particular. When a donkey perceives its own mixed-up self, its self is present to it. So in every case, the donkey’s self exists for it. That can only be through one power, which means the self is immaterial.[^V08-499]

The argument that a power other than the donkey’s self perceives it also fails. If the perceiver is not the self but another power, that power is either in the donkey or elsewhere. If it is in the donkey, then the donkey’s self is present in the donkey. If it is elsewhere, then the perceiver is not what it perceives, and the donkey does not perceive itself—something already disproved. Even if we agree that the donkey perceives itself through one of its parts, that part must contain an image of the donkey’s self, and so that part too would be immaterial. Also, if the donkey’s self were present in the organ of its power of estimation, though mixed with other things, that organ should be alive through the donkey’s self just as the organ of the self is alive through it. Some reply that the image in estimation is like the green color that appears by reflection, while its presence in its own organ is like the original green color. This is all that has been said on the question; the fuller answer is the one I have already indicated.[^V08-500][^V08-501]

The third argument is closely related to the first.[^V08-502] Our souls can understand the universal idea “human being,” which is shared by all human individuals. The idea must be free of any one position or shape, or it could not be shared by people with different positions and shapes. This universal form clearly exists. Since universals do not exist outside the mind, it must exist in the mind. Its home must either be a body or something nonbodily. It cannot be a body, since it would then have a particular size and position through its bodily subject, and would no longer be universal. So the place where this form exists is not a body. It is a being whose existence does not depend on matter.

Someone may ask: Does the universal intelligible form of a human being exist? If it does not, how can we say that it must have a certain kind of home? If it does, then surely it is a particular image in the particular human soul. Universals cannot exist as such in the outside world. As a particular image in one person’s soul, it is not shared by many people. First, what is particular cannot be shared.[^V08-503] Second, the image is an accident in the soul, while individual human beings are substances that exist in themselves. How can the reality of a substance be an accident that exists in something else?

They may answer that a form is universal because whichever human being first acts on the soul, the same kind of effect would appear in it. If a horse had been the first thing to act on the soul, a different effect would have appeared.

But then why could such an image not exist in a bodily place? Suppose seeing one particular human being creates an image in the brain that would be the same no matter which human being was first seen. They might reply that, if the image existed in the body, it would have a particular size and shape because of the body, which would prevent it from being universal.

But the image in an individual soul is also particular and an accident in a particular subject. That too should prevent it from being universal. If the size and shape an image gains by existing in a body prevent it from being universal, then the individual unity and status as an accident that it gains by existing in a particular soul should also prevent it. If, however, the image in the soul can be considered in another way and so count as universal, then an image in the brain or another bodily matter could also be considered in another way and count as universal, even though it is particular in size, shape, and position. In short, whether a form exists in the soul or in the body, it is not shared in every respect, because its individual unity prevents that. Yet in another respect it can still be shared.

I reply that this is a difficult objection, raised in discussions of knowledge and universals. We have answered it by saying that what makes a form universal and shareable is its intellectual mode of existence. A form can be one particular thing with its own identity and still, through its intellectual existence, apply equally to many. An intellectual identity does not prevent universality. What prevents sharing is material existence: a bodily individual has a specific position, place, and size, so its relation to other bodily individuals differs.[^V08-504] We do not need to posit one way of considering the form as universal and another way of considering it as an individual thing. Its intellectual existence is itself a different, fuller kind of existence, not confined to one particular instance. Natures and meanings can be applied to many things,[^V08-505] and this remains true when they exist intellectually without any particular position or size.

You already know from our account of intellect and intelligible forms that understanding is not a matter of an intelligible image settling into the intellect as an accident in a substance. The intellectual forms of substances are themselves substances, existing in themselves and through their source. In being and in their way of existing as substances, they are stronger than the material forms they represent, which depend on matter that keeps changing and being changed. But does this argument truly prove that souls are immaterial?

I say that the argument is decisive, but it proves that some human souls do not depend on matter, not that this is true of ordinary people in general. It applies to a soul that understands things without relying on bodily images and apprehends them in the way intellect knows them, a mode shared by many individual instances. This is possible only after one meaning has been stripped of its limits, additions, and particular features. In short, it applies to anyone who can consider, for example, the form of an animal in a way that gives it no particular size, place, position, or time.

The same is true of understanding the purely intellectual form of a human being.[^V08-506] We must think of a human being with all features that are not humanity itself removed, such as a particular place, size, and position, while keeping all the parts and powers that belong to a human being. We must understand it as having a head, face, eyes, hands, feet, and belly,[^V08-507] all in a way that is intellectual and shared by many individuals. This is what it means to understand a universal nature. As the First Teacher says in the *Theology of Aristotle*, the intellectual human being is spiritual, and all its parts are spiritual. The eye’s place is not separate from the hand’s place, and the bodily parts do not occupy different locations. In the same way, we can understand intellectual earth, water, or heaven, and hold in mind the form of any nature in its intellectual mode, as explained above. This is what it means to understand universals. Few people can understand in this way. Most people imagine, for example, a human image in their imagination. When they sense another individual,[^V08-508] they notice that this one is like the first and imagine the common feature between them. This is an imaginative understanding,[^V08-509] like sensing that all the different portions of water are water, or that separate drops are all water.

The fourth argument is that the intellect can perform an unlimited number of acts, while no bodily power can do so. Therefore, no intellectual power is bodily. The first claim is clear: we can understand numbers, and their sequence has no end. This is one kind of thing the intellect understands. The proof of the second claim was given earlier. The author of the *Discussions* says that to prove bodily powers are limited in their activity, we must show that they are divided along with their bodily subject. That brings back the objections to the first argument: the point, unity, relations, and the particular meanings perceived by estimation.

I have already answered these objections, praise be to God. The writer of *The Discussions* then raises further questions about this argument. First, we do not accept that the rational power can understand infinitely many things all at once. It can keep understanding more, without reaching a final limit, but that does not mean it can perform infinitely many acts. A body too can be divided again and again without reaching a final limit, but it cannot actually undergo infinitely many divisions. The same may be true of the intellect: it may be capable of an unending sequence of acts without being capable of infinitely many acts at once.

Second, even if the intellect can deal with infinitely many things, why call this an unlimited number of acts? Understanding is a kind of being affected, not an act. You allow that bodily things can be affected an unlimited number of times. Your own theory says that prime matter has received an unlimited number of forms over time.

Third, this argument seems to fail for the souls of the heavenly spheres. You say these souls depend on bodies, yet their act—circular motion—continues without end.

The reply to the first question is that the rational power plainly does not reach a final limit beyond which it could no longer understand more. We have also proved that bodily powers cannot be like this. That is enough for the argument.[^V08-510]

Someone may object: A bodily power might continue to exist forever. Either it must reach a time when it can no longer exist and becomes impossible, or it never reaches such a point. The first option is false, since what is possible in itself cannot become impossible in itself. So perhaps a bodily power can always continue to exist and act. If so, it might have unlimited effects, which would undermine the argument.

I reply that possibility and impossibility in themselves are features of a nature considered in relation to existence. Powers, however, are existing, individual things, and their particular existence includes a particular time. A certain kind of existence may be necessary, while another kind is impossible, without changing the possibility of the shared nature considered in itself. The proof concerns one individual bodily thing performing unlimited acts, not a kind of thing that has many individuals. Numerical unity is not the same as unity of kind.

As for the second question, even if understanding itself is being affected, the soul acts when it arranges and analyzes its premises. That is enough for our argument.

I reply that a power’s ability to be affected cannot be separated from its ability to act, even though these are different aspects. A thing must exist before it can be affected by something else. A bodily power cannot perform unlimited acts, and so it cannot undergo unlimited effects either. The objection from prime matter fails because prime matter does not remain as one enduring individual. Its unity is weak and indeterminate; as it takes on different forms, it changes and is renewed, as explained earlier.[^V08-511][^V08-512]

You might ask: Haven’t you distinguished the common sense, which perceives, from the imaginative power, which rearranges what it perceives? A single power cannot both perceive and rearrange. How, then, can you say that the soul acts by arranging and joining premises and is affected by receiving images?

I reply that the soul has two powers, a practical one and a knowing one. It acts through one and receives through the other.

As for the third objection, some say the souls of the heavenly spheres depend on bodies but do not act independently. Their power is limited in themselves but becomes unlimited through the light of intellect that reaches them. An objection follows: if the intellect gives the sphere a moving power, then that power is bodily, and we have a bodily source of unlimited action. If the intellect itself produces the movement, then the bodily power is not its source, and the movement would not truly be the sphere’s own. This also seems to make a body receptive to the separate intellect’s influence without any bodily power, which is impossible.

I reply that this objection would hold only if the intellect’s influence passed through one numerically identical power or one fixed addition to the sphere. But the separate intellect may continuously give the sphere new powers, or new qualities that keep changing. Neither problem would then follow. We have already discussed something similar.

Still, if this is possible for the bodily powers of the spheres, why could the rational power not also be bodily, yet capable of unlimited acts through the light it receives from the active intellect? The imaginative power seems to support this concern: although it is not intellect, it can form an unlimited number of images because it draws from the intellectual world.

The fifth argument is this. If the intellect were imprinted in a body, such as the heart or brain, it would either always understand that body or never understand it. The conclusion is false, so the intellect is not imprinted in a body. The reasoning is that it understands the body either because the body’s own outward form is present to the intellect, or because another image of that bodily organ appears in the intellect. If the first, the intellect would always understand that organ. If the second, two matching images would have to exist in the same place: the image of the organ that understands and the image of the organ being understood. Then understanding would be impossible. Also, there is no reason why one of the two images should be the perceiver and the other the perceived.[^V08-513][^V08-514]

Someone might say that the second image exists in the first image, not in its bodily matter. That would make the first image the perceiver and the second the thing perceived. I reply that we would again have to explain why one image is the home of the other and not the other way around. So if understanding the body requires another image of it, the intellect cannot understand that body.

The conclusion that the intellect either always or never understands the body is also false, since sometimes we understand a bodily organ and sometimes we do not. The argument is a conditional proof: if the intellect were in a body, then it would either always understand that body or never understand it; but in fact it sometimes understands it and sometimes does not. So it is not in a body. To establish this, we need four points. First, to understand a thing, its form must be present to whoever understands it. Second, if something perceives through itself, the form must be present in it. If it perceives through an organ, the form must be present in that organ. Third, bodily things act through the bodies in which they exist. Those bodies serve as their tools. Fourth, things of the same kind differ only through features of their matter.[^V08-515]

I reply that we do not accept the second point. You know that sight perceives visible things without their forms being imprinted in the eye, and imagination perceives bodily images without their being imprinted in the brain. This argument therefore fails because one of its premises is unsound.[^V08-516][^V08-517]

The author of the *Discussions* challenges the first point. He has often said that knowing something does not require the thing’s form to be present to the knower. Knowledge is a relation that may occur with or without the form being present. In every case, he says, knowledge is this particular relation. Why could the rational soul not exist in a body, with knowledge arising whenever this relation exists between the soul and the body?

I have already shown that knowledge cannot be a bare relation. A relation can exist only when something exists that the relation connects. We have explained how a relation depends on its terms and is not an independent thing.[^V08-518][^V08-519]

The author also replies that the thing we understand as “heaven” is not equal in nature to the actual heaven. If it were, then black would be the same in nature as white. Black and white have more in common—both are qualities in a body we can sense—than the intelligible form of heaven has with the actual heaven, which is a substance we can sense and that surrounds the earth.

The reply is that what we understand by “heaven” is the nature that makes heaven what it is, present in an immaterial mode of existence, without bodily position or direction. If the claim is that the intelligible heaven differs from the perceptible heaven because one is understood and the other sensed, that is true. If the claim is that what we understand is not the nature of heaven, it is false. And if the claim is that one is a substance and the other an accident, that too is false, as explained above.

Al-Tusi, in his commentary on *Pointers*, accepts that the intelligible heaven is an accident in an immaterial subject. He distinguishes its relation to the perceptible heaven from the relation between blackness and whiteness. The first difference is between one nature understood with its features and the same nature considered alongside their opposites. The difference between black and white, by contrast, is between two species formed by opposite distinguishing features.

I reply that stripping away accompanying features is not required in order to understand a nature. The intellect can understand a human being with all their features—size, quality, place, position, clothing, shape, and body parts—but in a universal, intellectual way. This is established in the discussion of God’s knowledge of particular things. To understand something intellectually, we must strip away its bodily mode of existence and consider it in an intellectual mode. The difference between the understood heaven and the sensed heaven lies only in their modes of existence. Whatever exists in the one true, luminous mode, which cannot be hidden, multiplied, or divided, is intelligible in itself. Whatever exists in this bodily, material mode is hidden even from itself, let alone from anything else. It knows itself only through a faint light it receives from the higher existence.[^V08-520][^V08-521][^V08-522]

Another objection is that if the intellect contains an image equal to the body it understands, there is no problem of two matching forms in one place: one form exists in the intellect, while the other exists in the intellect’s bodily subject.

The reply,[^V08-523] as given in the commentary on *Pointers*, is that if the intellectual power could contain a form without that form being present in its bodily subject, then a bodily power would act without the body’s participation. But every bodily agent acts with the body’s participation.[^V08-524] Anything that acts without such participation is not bodily. The intellect would then be nonbodily, which is the very point at issue. If the form were present in the subject as well, the original problem of two matching forms would return.[^V08-525] Someone may say that the difference is clear: one form exists in the intellect and its subject, while the other exists only in the subject. I reply that this kind of “presence” is just a pairing of two things. It makes no sense to pair a form with one of two closely connected things and not with the other. In any case, the problem of two forms of the same kind in one place remains.

The same argument would also seem to show that the soul either always understands its own qualities and features or never understands any of them.

It would also seem to show that it always knows all its changing features, so long as they are present. Otherwise, it would need another image of each feature in order to know it, and two matching forms would be present in one place.

Al-Tusi replies that the soul’s features fall into two groups. Some belong to it by its very nature, such as being aware of itself. Others belong to it when compared with things different from itself, such as not depending on matter and not belonging to another thing as one of its properties. The soul is always aware of the first group,[^V08-526] just as it is always aware of itself. It is aware of the second only when the conditions are present.

I reply that this does not answer the second version of the objection. We can answer it by saying that the soul’s features are its knowledge and acts of awareness, together with what follows from them. Whenever these kinds of knowledge are present, the soul is aware of them through the very forms that are present. When the soul is unaware of them, they are no longer present. The same applies to feelings such as anger, desire, embarrassment, and fear, which involve the body.

The sixth argument is that if the intellect were bodily, it would always weaken in old age. But it does not always weaken then. So the intellect is not bodily. We can express this as a proof: the power by which an older person understands is an intellect, and not every power by which an older person understands grows weak with age. Therefore, not every intellect grows weak with age. It is enough for this argument if even one person’s intellect does not weaken; we need not claim that no older person ever loses intellectual power.

The argument does not say that an older person’s understanding is never affected when the body is ill or weak. That would merely show that bodily powers can interfere with understanding. A bodily power[^V08-527] may be needed at first to prepare something for the intellect to understand. Or the soul’s attention to the body may distract it from other acts. This is like a traveler with a poor horse: having to look after the horse may keep the traveler from other tasks, though the tasks themselves are not performed by the horse. Likewise, an older person may turn from intellectual activity to caring for the body, or a bodily organ may be impaired and needed at the beginning of intellectual activity because the soul is still weak and its understanding not yet established.

If someone says that an older person can keep thinking clearly because the intellect uses a bodily organ that decays later than the others, the reply is that the outer organs weaken because the inner organs have already begun to fail. If those inner organs remained healthy, the outer ones would keep their power. But an older person’s liver, brain, and heart are not in their healthy state, or anything close to it. We can see this from changes in the pulse, urine, and brain activity. And the intellect does not need the hands, feet, or skin in order to complete its work.

I reply that the changes that come with old age also follow changes in the soul. As you know, the soul is what first shapes the body and its balance. The body and its powers grow weak because the soul turns, by its nature, from caring for this life toward caring for another. This is the wisdom behind natural death, not the cause given by doctors and others.

If you say that perhaps an older person’s bodily balance is better suited to intellectual activity and this is why their intellect is stronger, I reply: no. An older person’s body is either cold and dry or weak and frail. Both conditions occur in people who are not old, and they do not give them greater intellectual ability. Weakness of the body may well suit what does not depend on the body. This is a divinely inspired insight.

Know that this argument does not prove that every person has a separate intellectual substance. It shows that the intellect is not bodily. The same reasoning applies to imagination and estimation: some older or sick people continue to imagine and perceive particular meanings clearly. If imagination were a natural power that existed in this body, then every bodily illness would disrupt it. But that does not always happen. One counterexample is enough to disprove the claim that it always does. Since the philosophers did not recognize that imagination is immaterial, they went to great lengths to explain how it could survive old age and bodily decline.

The seventh argument is similar to the previous one. Sustained thinking and deep reflection on things understood by the mind dry the brain and weaken the body by stirring up heat. At the same time, they complete the soul by developing its ability to understand into actual understanding. One thing cannot cause both the completion and the weakening of another in the same respect and at the same time. If the body’s death also caused the soul’s death, then thought—while weakening or killing the body—would also weaken or kill the soul, even though it completes the soul. So we know that the soul does not depend on the body.

Someone may object that the same thing cannot cause both completion and weakening in the same respect and at the same time, but it could do so at different times or in different respects.

The eighth argument is that the soul acts without depending on the body, and whatever acts without depending on a subject also exists without depending on it. So the soul exists without depending on a bodily subject. There are three reasons to say that it acts without a bodily instrument. First, it knows itself, and it cannot use an instrument to know itself. Second, it knows that it knows itself, again without an instrument. Third, it knows its instruments without using yet another instrument to do so. So the soul acts without a bodily tool or subject.

Anything that acts without depending on a subject also exists without depending on it, because acting depends on existing. If existence depended on a subject, then action would depend on it too: bringing something about requires that the agent already exist, and the action follows from the agent. The dependence of the agent and its existence on a subject would therefore entail dependence of its action on that subject, though the reverse does not follow. This is why bodily powers act only with the body’s participation. The senses and other bodily powers cannot know themselves, know their awareness of themselves, or know their bodily tools. If the soul were bodily, it too would be unable to do these things. But it can. So it is not bodily.[^V08-528][^V08-529][^V08-530]

I reply that this proof shows the soul to be separate from the body whether it is an intellectual or an imaginative power. Imagination too can picture itself and its tools, and act without the body’s participation. That is why it can picture things outside the natural world, with no spatial relation of nearness or distance to it. Its acts do not depend on bodily tools, even if it needs the body at first to prepare it and give it a particular readiness, just as the intellect does.

The author of the *Discussions* objects to one premise in this argument in two ways. First, images and qualities depend on their subjects, but they depend on them simply by their nature. We do not infer from their ability to exist in that way that they can exist without their subjects.

Second, all effects produced by bodies come from powers and qualities in those bodies. The bodies that contain those powers do not contribute to the effects. A body as body cannot produce a particular effect, such as heat, cold, shape, or movement, since otherwise all bodies would produce the same effect. So the powers and qualities themselves are what produce those effects. Yet we do not infer from their being the direct source of an effect that they can exist without their subjects.

I reply to the first objection that dependence and possibility are ideas the mind considers when it looks at natures in themselves. We are discussing acts and effects in the world. For an image or quality to exist in a subject is not something added to its existence; its very existence depends on the subject. Its existence is present in the subject. How could it be independent of the very thing on which it depends in order to exist?[^V08-531][^V08-532]

As for the second objection, I do not agree that the subject of a power or quality plays no part in its effect. An effect requires a particular arrangement and relation between the agent and what it acts on. Such an arrangement belongs only to a body. Natural philosophy says that a body as body cannot cause a particular effect, such as heat, cold, shape, or movement, because all bodies would share that cause. This does not rule out the body’s contributing to the effect. It only denies that the body is its independent cause. If heat existed apart from bodies, it could still cause warmth; if blackness existed apart from bodies, it could still dazzle the eye. The point that a bodily power acts with the participation of its bodily subject is certain and admits no serious doubt.[^V08-533]

The ninth argument is that bodily powers tire through repeated activity and cannot perform a strong action after a weak one. This happens because material powers exist in matter that can change, decay, and weaken. They change as their matter changes. They tire and weaken as their matter is worn away. They also cannot perform a weak action after a strong one because powers are suited to particular acts. Since such powers can grow stronger or weaker and change, they come into being according to varying degrees of readiness. When a bodily tool is used for a strong perception or act, a strong power must flow into it from its source.[^V08-534]

Once a strong act has occurred, it may be difficult for that same power to perform a weak act. It is not suited to it; a weaker power would be. Powers arise gradually, as we have seen. That is why a strong power may not be able to perform a weak act, just as the power moving a heavy stone may not be able to make it fall slowly, or intense heat may not be able to warm something only slightly. Nonbodily powers, by contrast, relate equally to all things they can affect and have no limit to their strength. They can perform strong and weak acts one after another, depending on the conditions that call for each. Repeated action does not weaken them. They can perform a strong act after a weak one and a weak act after a strong one.[^V08-535]

Once this point is established, we can say that the intellect too is not bodily. It can understand ideas of different levels, one after another, without tiring. The same argument shows that imagination is not bodily. It can picture things both great and small: the outermost sphere and a mustard seed; sunlight and the heat of a fire, as well as a spark and the mild warmth of air; the roar of thunder and a whisper. Those who did not realize that imagination is free of changing matter struggled to answer this problem. They said that the power of perception is different from what it perceives: the sun, moon, and sky are powerful objects, but our imagining them need not be powerful, and so it does not prevent us from imagining weak things. If we become absorbed in imagining them, however, they say we cannot then imagine something small.

I reply that perception is either the form received from a thing or a relation between the perceiver and what is perceived, as this writer believes. On the first view, “strong” and “weak” perception can mean only that the perceived form is strong or weak, since they are one and the same. In truth, what is perceived is the form in the mind, not the outer thing, which may not even exist when the form is present. The imagined form of the sky is clearly stronger than the imagined form of a mustard seed. On the second view, a relation cannot be strong or weak, large or small, except by reference to the things it relates. The claim that becoming absorbed in imagining something strong makes it impossible to imagine something small also contradicts the earlier claim, since it calls the imagined thing small, not the act of imagination.[^V08-536][^V08-537][^V08-538]

Further, being absorbed in perceiving something really means that the perceiver’s awareness is weak in comparison with the existence of the thing perceived. The perceived thing overwhelms the perceiver and prevents it from grasping the thing fully. Being absorbed does not mean that perception has gained control over what it perceives; it is the perceived thing that has taken control of perception. The same is true of intellectual and imaginative understanding. When we are absorbed in understanding something with a strong and elevated existence, we can no longer understand other things. Someone absorbed in God’s majesty cannot turn their attention to themselves, let alone to other intelligible things.

The tenth argument starts from the claim already established that the same thing in a human being perceives through all kinds of perception and perceives all kinds of things. This perceiver must be one of three things: a body simply as a body; a power or quality that exists in a body, as a form or accident; or something separate from the body and not present in it. The first option is clearly false. A body, simply as a body, cannot perceive anything. As we have explained many times, a body has no single, unified presence in which something can be present to it or it can be present to something else. Also, if bodies perceived simply by being bodies, every body would perceive.

The second option is also impossible. The power would either exist in every part of the body or only in some parts. If in every part, then every part of the body would see, hear, imagine, think, and understand. But that is obviously false: sight does not hear, the hand does not imagine, and the foot does not think. Nor can we say that the one power that performs all these acts exists in just one organ. That would mean the body has one organ that hears, sees, imagines, thinks, and understands, but we do not know where it is. This also shows why it is wrong to say that one power which performs all these acts exists in a subtle body enclosed within some organ.

If that were true, we should be able to find somewhere in our bodies a body that hears, sees, thinks, and understands. You might say that not knowing where it is does not prove it is not there. But we know ourselves as the ones who hear, see, imagine, and understand. If some body, whether a part of our body or something enclosed within it, possessed the power to perform all these kinds of perception, then that body would be our true identity. If we do not know that body, then we do not know who we are.

You already know that the perceiver belongs to the same kind as what it perceives. It is therefore impossible that one and the same bodily instrument could perform hearing, sight, taste, imagination, and understanding. You might say this objection applies to our own view, since we say that one immaterial power can hear, see, touch, imagine, and understand. I reply that these powers are branches and effects of the intellect. The intellect completes and produces them. What is complete, and what produces another thing, can do everything the incomplete thing can do, but not the other way around. An effect of an effect is also an effect of the first cause; but an effect of a cause is not thereby the cause’s effect, or the cause would be an effect of itself. So the one thing that perceives all things through all kinds of perception is one nonbodily power, as we wanted to show.[^V08-539][^V08-540]

The eleventh argument is this. When a form or quality comes to exist in a body for some reason, and then leaves it, the body needs a new cause in order to receive that form or quality again. For example, water becomes hot because of fire. Once the heat has gone, it cannot return without a new cause. The soul is different. We often gain an idea through thought or through a teacher, then lose it when our attention wanders. Yet the soul can bring the idea back by itself, without repeating the original cause. In short, the soul can regain its own perfections by its own power, while no body can do so. The soul is therefore beyond bodily form.[^V08-541][^V08-542]

One might object that a bodily quality can return without a new cause. Water may lose its natural coldness when heated, then regain that coldness once the heat is gone. I reply that we are speaking of a quality brought about by a cause outside the thing itself. The coldness of water belongs to its nature, provided nothing blocks it. When the coldness returns, it returns with its cause: the nature of water and the absence of anything that prevents it. These ten proofs[^V08-543] give certainty that the human soul is immaterial, whether or not it has reached the level of intellect in reality. The soul that imagines in reality is also free, in its being and action, from the material world. But intellect is independent of both the physical world and the world of images; imagination is independent only of the physical world.

#### Chapter 2 — Scriptural evidence on this subject

Most people gain more from reports and scripture than from philosophical arguments. They believe[^V08-544] something only when their senses seem to confirm it, and they do not accept abstract reasoning unless it can be traced back to something they can sense. So let us give scriptural evidence for this teaching and show that revelation and reason agree here, as they do in other philosophical matters. The true divine law cannot conflict with necessary, certain knowledge. A philosophy whose principles fail to agree with the Qur'an and the Prophet’s teaching is no sound philosophy.

Among the Qur'anic verses that point to the soul’s immateriality are God’s words about Adam[^V08-545] and his descendants: “I breathed into him of My spirit,” and His words about Jesus, the Word cast to Mary, “and a spirit from Him.” This way of speaking[^V08-546] about the spirit shows its high rank and its freedom from the world of bodies. God also says of Abraham,[^V08-547] the father of the prophets, “We showed Abraham the kingdom of the heavens and the earth, so that he would be among those with certainty,” and tells us that Abraham said, “I have turned my face toward the One who created the heavens and the earth, turning away from all else.” A body and its powers[^V08-548] cannot have these noble qualities: seeing the kingdom of the unseen, having certainty, turning one’s whole self toward the Creator of the heavens, and being pure.

Other verses include: “Then We brought him forth as another creation[^V08-549]—so blessed is God, the best of creators”; “Glory be to the One who created all pairs[^V08-550]: those the earth grows, human beings themselves, and things they do not know”; “To Him the good word rises”; “We created the human being in the finest form”; and “O soul at peace, return to your Lord, content and pleasing to Him.” In truth, all verses that speak of resurrection and the conditions of human beings in the next life point to the soul’s immateriality. The dead[^V08-551] cannot simply be brought back as the same things after ceasing to exist, and an accident or a bodily power imprinted in matter cannot move from one place to another.

Among the Prophet’s sayings are: “Whoever knows[^V08-552] himself knows his Lord”; “The one among you who knows himself best knows his Lord best”; “Whoever has seen me has seen the Real”; “I am the bare warner”; “I have a time with God[^V08-553] in which neither a near angel nor a sent prophet can share”; and “I spend the night with my Lord; He gives me food and drink.” These and similar reports point to the soul’s high rank and its nearness to God when it reaches completion.

The Prophet also prayed, “My Lord, show me things as they are.” His prayer was answered. But we can know things with causes as they truly are only by knowing their causes and the One who brings them about, as has been proved elsewhere. “Seeing” here means direct, witnessing knowledge. Abraham prayed in the same way when he said, as God tells us, “My Lord, show me how You give life to the dead.” Seeing an act[^V08-554] cannot be separated from seeing the one who acts. A body and its senses cannot see the Lord of lords, the Cause of causes.

The Prophet also said: “The heart of the believer is God’s throne,” and “The believer’s heart lies between two fingers of the Merciful.” The heart here cannot mean the pine-shaped piece of flesh in the chest.

Nor are God’s “fingers” bodily limbs. The true heart is the human being’s reasoning self. The two fingers are the universal intellect and soul, or the intellectual and spiritual powers.[^V08-555] The Prophet also said, “The believer is greater in worth before God than the Throne.” This greatness is not bodily, nor does it belong to a power confined to one of the body’s organs. He also said, “God created the spirits two thousand years before the bodies.”[^V08-556]

When a Jewish scholar asked the Commander of the Faithful, the Imam of those who affirm God’s oneness, “Did you see your Lord when you worshipped Him?” he replied, “Woe to you! I would not worship a lord I had not seen.” The scholar asked how he saw Him. He replied, “Eyes do not see Him as they see visible things, but hearts see Him through the truth of faith.” Intellectual vision cannot come from a bodily power.

When asked how he had pulled down the gate at Khaybar,[^V08-557] the Imam replied, “I did it with a divine power, not a bodily power.” He also said, “The spirit is one of God’s angels, with seventy thousand faces.”[^V08-558] It is said that Jesus, the Spirit of God, is the light that shines from the pavilion of the unseen. “No one enters the kingdom of heaven unless they are born twice”; and “Only one who came down from heaven can rise to heaven.”

As for the ancient philosophers, Aristotle, the teacher of ancient wisdom, says in the book known among the Greeks as the *Theology of Aristotle*—a work on the knowledge of divine realities and separate intellects:

> At times I withdrew into myself, laid my body aside, and became as though I were a being free of a body.[^V08-559] I entered into myself and left all other things behind. Within myself I saw such beauty and splendor that I remained amazed and struck silent. I knew that I was a living and active part of the noble, divine world. When I became certain of this, I raised my mind from that world to the divine Cause. I became as though I were within it, joined to it, above the whole intellectual world.

Aristotle says much more. He also says that whoever strives for that world and rises toward it will receive the finest reward there. No one should give up seeking, striving, and working to rise to it, even if the effort is tiring, because a rest lies ahead that will never end. His work known as *The Apple* also supports this teaching. It contains words he spoke near the end of his life, explaining the worth of philosophy and how the philosopher is rewarded after the soul leaves the body. He also says that the soul is not in the body; rather, the body is in the soul, because the soul is greater than it.

Empedocles says that the soul once dwelt in a high and noble place. When it made a mistake,[^V08-560] it fell into this world to escape God’s anger. Once it descended, it became a support for souls whose intellects had become confused. He called out to people and urged them to leave this world and return to their first, noble home. The philosopher Agathodaemon urged people to turn away from this world too, but spoke in parables and symbols.

Pythagoras, the philosopher of number, states the same view in his famous *Golden Verses*, which we also have. At the end of his advice to Diogenes, he says: “When you leave this body, you will be free in the high air. You will wander there, never to return to human life or face death again.”

Plato, the noble, divine philosopher, is praised in the *Theology* for describing the soul in so many ways that it is as if we could see it with our own eyes. But his descriptions differ. At times he does not rely on the senses in speaking about the soul; at other times he does not fully reject them. He criticizes and looks down on the soul’s bond with the body. He says that the soul is imprisoned and silenced in the body, which is like a cave. Empedocles agreed, calling the body an echo; by this he meant the world.

I say that this agrees with the divine saying: “God has sealed their hearts,” and “No, their hearts have been stained by what they have done.” Plato also says that freeing the soul from its bonds means leaving the cave of this world and rising to its intellectual home. His different descriptions of the soul reflect what I explained earlier: souls differ in how bodily or spiritual they are, and one soul can be both in different periods or, at the same time, in different respects.[^V08-561]

In a book called *Phaedo*, Plato says that the soul descended into this world because it lost its wings; when its wings grow back, it rises to its first home.[^V08-562] Elsewhere, he gives several reasons for its descent, including a fault it committed and other causes. In the book called *Timaeus*, he speaks against the soul’s descent and its dwelling in these bodies. Yet he also praises this world, calling it a noble and blessed substance. The soul came here through the action of the good Creator, so that this world would be alive and intelligent. If this world is so carefully made, it would be wrong for it to have no intellect. But the world cannot have an intellect without a soul. So God sent the soul into this world and settled it here. He then sent our souls to dwell in our bodies, so that this world could be complete and not fall short of the intellectual world. The world we perceive through the senses should contain the kinds of living beings that exist in the world understood by intellect.[^V08-563][^V08-564]

Muslim thinkers have also discussed what the spirit is. Some[^V08-565] used argument and reflection; others spoke from direct experience, without relying on reasoning. The Sufi masters spoke about it too, though they followed the Prophet’s example and revealed the spirit’s secret only through hints and symbols. I will speak only about some of the spirit’s levels, powers, and stations in the soul and intellect, not its deepest essence, which we cannot know.

Junayd said: “The spirit is something God has kept for Himself to know. We can say no more of it than that it exists.” Perhaps he meant that it is pure existence, like other simple intellectual beings, which are not made up of a general kind and a feature separating them from others, but differ in how strong or weak their existence is.

Abu Yazid al-Bistami said: “I searched for myself in both worlds and did not find it.” He meant that the self is beyond both the natural world and the world of images, and belongs among the separate intellects. He also said: “I slipped out of my skin and saw who I am.” He called the body a skin, making clear that the human being’s identity is something other than the body. He also said: “If the Throne and everything it contains fell into one corner of Abu Yazid’s heart, he would not even feel it.” This vastness cannot belong to his body or to any other body.[^V08-566]

Ibn ‘Ata said: “God created the spirits before the bodies,” citing God’s words, “We created you”—meaning the spirits—“then shaped you.” Another thinker said: “The spirit is a subtle thing that stands in what is dense.” This needs explanation, but it can be understood correctly.

Some say that “spirit” is only a name, and that the living source present in things is life itself. This also needs explanation,[^V08-567] but it can be understood to mean that life comes from the spirit. In any case, the saying suggests that the spirit is alive in itself, not because the body gives it life. Some commentators, explaining God’s words “The spirit is from the command of my Lord,” say that the command is God’s word. The living being becomes alive through His word, “Be alive.” On this view, the spirit is not in the body.[^V08-568]

Know[^V08-569] that scholars of religion have differed about the spirit the Prophet was asked about. Some reports suggest that the spirit is eternal; others say it came to be. Some say the spirit is Gabriel. This resembles the view that the soul becomes one with the active intellect when it reaches completion. It is also close to the report already quoted from the Commander of the Faithful, that the spirit is an angel of God with seventy thousand faces.

Ibn ‘Abbas is reported to have said that the spirit is one of God’s creations, made in the form[^V08-570] of human beings, and that no angel comes down from heaven without a spirit beside it. Abu Salih said that the spirit is shaped like a human being, though it is not human. Mujahid said that spirits resemble human beings, with hands, feet, and heads; they eat, though they are not angels.

I say that when these thinkers speak of the spirit as having limbs, they do not mean bodily organs. They mean spiritual parts and powers, suited to the spirit’s subtle nature. The First Teacher, Aristotle, says something similar in the *Theology*: the human being in the sensible world is an image of the intellectual human being. The intellectual human being is spiritual, and all its parts are spiritual. The place of the hand is not separate from that of the foot; all the parts are in one place.

Sa‘id ibn Jubayr[^V08-571] said: “God created nothing greater than the spirit except the Throne. If He wished, the spirit could swallow the seven heavens and the earth in one mouthful. God shaped its body like the angels and its face like human beings. On the Day of Resurrection it will stand on the right of the Throne, with the angels in a single row. It will intercede for those who affirm God’s oneness. If a screen of light did not stand between it and the heavens, the people of heaven would be burned by its light.” These statements must have reached these thinkers through reports from the Prophet.

Some say the spirit did not come from the word “Be,” because if it had, it would have been humiliated. They were asked what it came from. They replied: “From between His beauty and majesty. He honored it with His presence and greeted it with His word, so it was spared the humiliation of ‘Be.’”

I say that they meant the spirit is not one of the natural things that come to be and pass away. We have proved that intellectual spirits belong to the divine realm and are expressions of the self-sufficient One. The possibilities attributed to them exist only in the mind, not in reality; they arise from considering them in different ways. Another thinker said: “The spirit is a subtle presence that comes from God and reaches known places. We can say no more of it than that it exists because of another.”[^V08-572]

I have already explained this to mean that the spirit is pure existence, unknown to us, brought into being directly rather than made out of something else such as a subject or matter.

Abu Sa‘id al-Kharraz was asked whether the spirit is created. He replied: “Yes. If it were not, it could not have affirmed the Lordship of God when it said, ‘Yes.’ The spirit is what the body stands through and what gives it the name ‘living.’ Through the spirit, intellect is established and proof becomes binding. Without the spirit, intellect would be idle, with no proof for it or against it.”

Some say that the spirit is a created substance, the subtlest of created things, the purest and brightest of substances. Through it, people see what is hidden; through it, people of direct knowledge receive insight. When the spirit is prevented from attending to the innermost secret, the bodily limbs lose their proper discipline.[^V08-573][^V08-574]

Another thinker said: “This world and the next are the same to the spirit.” Some say that spirits wander in the intermediate world and see the affairs of this world, while angels in heaven speak about human beings. Some spirits are beneath the Throne; others fly to the gardens of paradise or wherever their efforts to draw near to God have taken them in life.

Sa‘id ibn al-Musayyib reports from Salman that the spirits of believers go to an intermediate place on earth, wherever they wish between earth and heaven, until they return to their bodies.[^V08-575] It is also said that when a dead person’s spirit arrives, the spirits of the living dead meet and talk with it, asking about one another. God appoints angels to show them the deeds of the living. If they see that a person is being punished in this world for a sin, that gives the dead a clear excuse; no one is more willing to accept an excuse than God.[^V08-576]

Something similar appears in a report by Abu Ja‘far Muhammad ibn al-Hasan al-Tusi. Abu Basir asked Ja‘far al-Sadiq about the spirits of believers. He replied: “They are in paradise in forms like their bodies. If you saw one, you would say, ‘That is so-and-so.’” Muhammad ibn Ya‘qub al-Kulayni also reports in the final section of *The Book of Funerals* in *al-Kafi* that Ja‘far al-Sadiq said: “The spirits are in forms like their bodies, in trees in paradise. They recognize and question one another. When a new spirit arrives, they say, ‘Leave it alone; it has just come from a terrible ordeal.’ Then they ask what happened to this person and that person. If the spirit says it left them alive, they hope to see them again. If it says they have died, they say, ‘They have fallen to their fate.’”[^V08-577]

In *al-Kafi*, the Imam is also reported to have said that the spirits of believers live in chambers in paradise, where they eat and drink and pray: “Our Lord, bring the Hour upon us, fulfill what You promised us, and join those who come later to those who came before.” Reports describe the spirits of unbelievers as being in the opposite state.

Al-Tusi also relates in *al-Tahdhib* that Ja‘far al-Sadiq said to Yunus ibn Zabyan: “Yunus, what do people say about the spirits of believers?” Yunus replied, “They say the spirits are in the crops of green birds, in lamps beneath the Throne.” The Imam said: “Glory be to God! A believer is too honored by God for Him to put the spirit in a bird’s crop. When God takes a believer, He places the spirit in a form like the person’s form in this world. They eat and drink, and when someone newly arrived joins them, they recognize that person in the form they had in the world.”

There are more reports from the Imams of the Prophet’s family than can be counted. Do not think that descriptions of spirits as having shape, form, speech, eating, or drinking contradict their freedom from this natural world. They confirm it. The spirit can take shape and form and become embodied in a world other than this one. God has many worlds, each subtler than the last. All are separate from this world and from the matter and potential that belong to it. In the sections on resurrection, you will learn how spirits can take on forms like bodies while remaining immaterial.[^V08-578][^V08-579]

In their very existence, spirits do not depend on bodily size or dimensions, though they can act and take on forms. Al-Wasiti was asked why the Prophet was the wisest of all creation. He replied: “Because his spirit was created first, and so it was the first to share in firm grounding and stability. Do you not hear him say, ‘I was a prophet while Adam was still between water and clay’?” Another version says, “between spirit and body,” meaning that the Prophet was neither spirit nor body. That is the end of his words.

Some say that the spirit was created from the light of divine might, while Satan was created from its fire. That is why he said, “You created me from fire,” not realizing that light is better than fire. Some also say that God joined knowledge to the spirit: the spirit grows through knowledge, just as the body grows through food.

Most theologians hold that humanity and animal life are qualities created in a person, and death removes them. They say the spirit is life itself: the body is alive while that life is in it, and will live again when life is returned to it on the Day of Resurrection.

I say this view can be sound and well understood if it comes from someone with insight. The same is true of the view held by some theologians that the spirit is a subtle body joined to dense bodies as water joins green wood. This was the view of Abu al-Ma‘ali al-Juwayni, the teacher of Abu Hamid al-Ghazali. The imaginal body is one of the appearances of the spirit. Its presence in this body is not literally a matter of two bodies entering and interlocking, though it resembles that.[^V08-580][^V08-581]

Some later theologians insisted that the spirit is an accident, despite the reports that describe it as an independent substance—rising, descending, and moving through the intermediate worlds.

Ibn ‘Abbas was asked where spirits go when they leave their bodies. He replied: “Where does the light of a lamp go when its oil runs out?” He meant that the spirit returns to the place from which it came. Anyone who understands how a spirit comes from the intellectual realm without moving or becoming embodied can understand how it returns there without gaining another location or becoming divided. He was also asked where bodies go when they decay. He replied: “Where does their flesh go when they become sick?”[^V08-582]

In *The Nourishment of Hearts*, Abu Talib al-Makki seems to say that spirits and souls are distinct presences in the body. He says that when the spirit moves, a light appears in the heart that an angel sees, and the angel then inspires good. When the soul moves, darkness appears in the heart, which Satan sees and uses as an opening for temptation.

I reply that these movements are movements of thought. Some are sound arguments that cause a bright intellectual form to flow into the soul. Others are false or misleading judgments, which make the soul take on an ugly, dark form and lead to a false conclusion. This is the work of Satan. Such movements do not contradict the soul’s immateriality.[^V08-583]

Al-Ghazali says that when the spirit leaves the body, it carries the power of imagination with it through the rational soul. It then perceives sensible meanings, since the state of the body cannot simply disappear. At death the spirit knows that it is dying. After death it imagines itself buried, recalls what it believed during life, and senses reward or punishment in the grave. This is a sound and well-founded account. It supports our view that the imaginative power and what accompanies it are immaterial. More on this will follow.[^V08-584]

The author of *The Gifts of the Knowers* and *The Way of Knowledge* says, in a passage also found in *The Book of the Ta-Sin* and *The Book of Ya-Sin*, that the heavenly spirit is from the world of command,[^V08-585] while the human animal spirit is from the world of creation. The animal spirit is a subtle bodily presence that carries the powers of sense and movement. All animals have it. It gives rise to the senses, is strengthened by food, and is studied by physicians, who use it to explain the balance of bodily fluids. When the human spirit enters it,[^V08-586] it becomes distinct from animal spirits and gains a new quality: it becomes a soul able to speak and receive inspiration. God says, “By the soul and the One who shaped it, then inspired it with its wickedness and its piety.” It is shaped when the human spirit enters it and separates it from the kind of animal spirits. God forms the soul from the heavenly spirit in the world of command, just as Eve was formed from Adam in the world of creation. The two are joined by affection, as Adam and Eve were, and each tastes death when separated from the other. God says, “He created from it its mate, so that it might find rest in her.” Adam found rest in Eve; the heavenly spirit finds rest in the animal spirit and makes it a soul.

When the spirit rests in the soul, the heart comes to be.[^V08-587] I mean the subtle heart whose place is the piece of flesh in the chest. That piece of flesh belongs to the world of creation, but the subtle heart belongs to the world of command. The heart comes from the spirit and soul in the world of command, just as descendants come from Adam and Eve in the world of creation. Without the joining of the two—spirit and soul—the heart would not come to be. The heart looks toward its father, the heavenly spirit, and longs for it. This is the “heart made firm” of which the Prophet spoke in a report from Hudhayfa: “There are four kinds of hearts.[^V08-588] One is pure, with a shining lamp within it: that is the believer’s heart. One is black and turned upside down: that is the unbeliever’s heart.”

The report continues: “One heart is tied to a cord: that is the hypocrite’s heart. One is mixed, with faith and hypocrisy together. Faith in it is like a plant watered by clean water; hypocrisy is like a sore fed by pus and blood. Whichever one grows stronger determines the heart’s state.” The inverted heart leans toward the mother, the soul that urges evil. Some hearts waver between the two. The heart’s dominant leaning determines whether it is blessed or wretched.

The intellect is the substance of the heavenly spirit and the sign by which it is known. Its care for the firm heart and the pure, peaceful soul is like a father’s care for a good child and a faithful wife. Its care for the inverted heart and the soul that urges evil is like a father’s care for a disobedient child and a bad wife. The spirit resists them in one respect but is drawn into caring for them in another, since it must be connected with both. The heavenly spirit longs to rise toward its Lord and leave the world of creation behind. Yet the heart and soul also belong to that world.

When the spirit rises, the heart longs for it as a good child longs for a father; the soul longs for the heart as a tender mother longs for her child. When the soul longs in this way, it rises from the earth, draws in the roots it has sent into the lower world, burns away its desires, grows still, turns away from worldly life and the home of illusion, and returns to the lasting home. But the soul, the mother, may remain earthbound because of its natural state. It comes from the animal spirit and takes its shape from the elements of the lower world. God says: “Had We wished, We could have raised him up, but he clung to the earth and followed his desires.” When the soul remains earthbound, the inverted heart is drawn to it, as a child with a bent nature is drawn to a flawed mother rather than a sound and complete father. The spirit is in turn drawn to the heart, its child, as a father is naturally drawn to his child. Then the spirit falls short of its duty toward its Lord. The pull of these two relations is where happiness and misery appear. This is the decree of the Almighty, the All-Knowing.[^V08-589]

Abu Sa‘id al-Qurashi said that there are two spirits, the spirit of life and the spirit of death. When they are together, the body understands. The spirit of death is what leaves the body and makes a living being dead. The spirit of life is what enables breathing, eating, drinking, and similar acts.

Some say that the soul is a subtle presence placed in the body, and that blameworthy habits and qualities come from it. The spirit is another subtle presence in the body, and praiseworthy habits and qualities come from it. Just as the eye is the place of sight, the ear of hearing, the nose of smell, and the mouth of taste, the soul is the place of blameworthy qualities and the spirit the place of praiseworthy ones. The soul’s qualities come from two sources: impulsiveness and greed. Its impulsiveness comes from ignorance, its greed from craving. In its impulsiveness the soul is like a ball rolling on smooth ground, always moved by its nature. In its greed it is like a moth that sees a lamp, is not satisfied with its small light, and rushes into the flame that will destroy it.

These are some of the views of religious scholars and hints from the Sufi masters. They show that a human being has a spiritual substance. Everything we have quoted has a sound meaning, even if some statements seem open to objection at first. Those who understand the secrets of divine law can give them a proper interpretation. These scholars followed the sayings of the Prophet and the Imams. Much of what they say can be found in reports attributed to those who “do not speak from desire; it is only revelation revealed to them, taught by one mighty in power.” If I did not fear making this section too long, I would explain their words and aims in a way that agrees with decisive proofs and sound principles, without doubt or fault. God is the source of mercy and generosity.[^V08-590]

### Book Seven — Aspects of the rational soul in its relation to the natural world

#### Chapter 1 — How the soul is joined to the body

Know that things differ in how strongly they depend on other things. The strongest kind of dependence is dependence in meaning and reality, both in the mind and outside it, as a thing’s nature depends on its existence.[^V08-591] The second kind is dependence in being and identity, when one thing’s very self depends on another, as a possible thing depends on the Necessary Being.[^V08-592] The third kind is dependence both in being and in kind, when a thing depends on what it exists in, as a quality such as blackness depends on its subject, the body.[^V08-593]

The fourth kind is dependence in existence and individual identity, both when something comes to be and as it continues to exist, on the kind of thing it depends on. An image depends on matter in this way. To be a particular image, it needs some matter or other, but not any one specific piece of matter. It is like a roof held up by pillars: it needs one of them or another, but not any particular pillar. A natural body likewise needs some place or other to exist in. That is why it can move from one place to another so easily.[^V08-594]

The fifth kind is dependence in existence and individual identity when something comes to be, but not as it continues to exist. In our view, this is how the soul depends on the body. At the beginning of its existence, the soul is like material nature: it needs matter whose identity is not yet fixed. The soul too depends on an indeterminate bodily matter, whose identity changes as the body undergoes transformations and changes in size. The human being is one individual in the sense of its soul, but not one individual in the bodily sense of matter or subject. It was explained earlier that the subject of change in size is one in kind as a body, but particular as a natural thing or soul.[^V08-595]

The sixth kind is dependence on something for completion and growth in excellence, not for existence itself. Most philosophers say the soul depends on the body in this way throughout its existence. In my view, it depends on the body in this way after it has acquired its bodily form and become a soul with the power to think and act, but before its theoretical intellect has moved from capacity to reality. This is the weakest kind of dependence. It resembles a craft worker’s dependence on a tool, though the soul’s dependence on bodily tools is natural and essential, while a carpenter’s dependence on tools is external and incidental.

At the beginning of their existence, all souls lack the perfections and qualities that give them fuller existence, whether animal qualities in general or human qualities in particular. They can acquire them only by using tools. These tools must differ from one another. Some are for movement; others are for perception. Among the tools for movement, some serve animal desire and others serve anger.[^V08-596]

Among the tools for perception, some serve touch, others smell, taste, sight, or hearing.If the soul did not have different tools, the different actions and perceptions would crowd together in it and become confused. It would not perform any one of them fully. The forms of things first appear in the senses, then in imagination, and then in the theoretical intellect. That is why it is said: “Whoever loses a sense loses a kind of knowledge.” No sensible thing brings together in one existence all the different qualities we can sense. What is seen is not heard; a smell is not a taste. So the sense powers that perceive these different qualities must also differ.

Intellect is different. In one intellectual existence, one thing may be sight, taste, smell, sound, color, heat, cold, and much else, but in a higher and nobler way, as the great philosopher explains in his book. So the soul must use different tools to sense different qualities, lest they become confused and its perceptions be muddled. Because the tools differ, when the soul wants to see, it turns to the eye and can see fully; when it wants to hear, it turns to the ear and can hear fully. The same applies to all the other acts and powers. Through repeated action with these tools, the soul gains a habit and an ability to acquire things that could not first become present to it without the tools. Later, it can bring them to mind and work with them as it wishes, by its own power and in its own world.[^V08-597][^V08-598]

We can see from this that, at first, the soul is like matter before it receives a particular form: it has not yet acquired any complete form, whether sensed, imagined, or understood. Later it becomes able to produce forms free of matter, whether particular or universal. These forms are much nobler than forms that come to be and pass away. What a shallow view, then, to say that the soul is one unchanging thing from the first moment it is joined to the body until it ceases to exist! At the beginning, the soul is nothing at all, as the divine scripture says: “Has there not been a time when the human being was not even mentioned?” When it reaches completion, it becomes an active intellect.[^V08-599][^V08-600]

You might say: We have established that the soul is the first perfection of a natural body. Perfection and form are one in reality, though we use the words in different ways. How, then, can we say that the soul begins without any form?

I reply that there are two kinds of form. The first is a material form. It exists as something extended, located, and divisible. Its unity is inseparable from its ability to be divided; its permanence is inseparable from change and passing away; and its action is inseparable from the potential of the things it acts on. It is a form only while also being matter.

The second kind is a form not mixed with matter. It may or may not depend on matter in a particular relation to the tool through which it appears. Both kinds deserve the name “form” more than the first, because the first has only weak unity and weak existence. It is like a mirage in a barren place: the thirsty person thinks it is water, but when they reach it, it is nothing. The first kind has no perceptive existence. Its existence in the world does not by itself make it sensed, imagined, or understood. The second kind has an intellectual or perceptive existence without matter. If it needs a spatial relation to a bodily instrument or to something that appears before it, it is sensed. Otherwise, it is imagined or understood.[^V08-601]

Given what we have explained, it is clear that at the beginning the soul is not any of the forms in the second sense. Nor has it yet received any sensible, imagined, or intellectual form. For something to be present to another in a particular way, it must exist in that way. To be present as an outward thing, it must exist outwardly. To be present to the senses, it must exist as a sensation. To be present in imagination, it must exist as an imagined form. And to be present to the intellect, it must exist as something understood.

At its coming to be, the soul is the final stage of material forms and the first stage of perceptive forms. Its existence is the last of the bodily shells and the first of the spiritual cores.

#### Chapter 2 — How human souls come to be

The previous discussion is enough to show that human souls come to be when bodies do. We have seen that the soul changes, moving from the lowest substantial states to the highest. If it were eternal by its own nature, it would already be complete from the start, with no lack or weakness. If it had no lack in its existence, it would not need organs or powers, some plant-like and some animal.[^V08-602][^V08-603] Also, if it were eternal, the kind would be confined to a single individual. In the creative realm, it could not divide and multiply. Several individuals of one kind arise only among bodies and material things, whose existence involves potentiality, motion, matter, and change. A kind whose existence involved none of these would exist as one individual. But human souls exist in many individuals and still belong to one kind. So these individual souls cannot have existed before their bodies, much less have been eternal.[^V08-604][^V08-605]

Some of the ancient philosophers, including Plato, are said to have taught that human souls are eternal. Two well-known reports seem to support this view: “I was a prophet while Adam was still between water and clay,” and “Spirits are like gathered troops: those who recognized one another draw together, while those who did not recognize one another stay apart.” But perhaps this does not mean that individual human souls existed before bodies with the same individual identities they have now. That would lead to the impossible results already mentioned and would leave their powers idle. A soul, simply as a soul, is a form that exists to govern a body. It has powers and means of perception, some animal and some plant-like. Instead, the claim may be that souls have another mode of being: their beginnings exist in God’s knowledge as separate intellectual forms, the divine patterns affirmed by Plato and those before him.

Perfected human souls have several modes of existence: some with nature, some before nature, and some beyond nature. This is what those firmly grounded in higher wisdom have come to understand. Their view rests on the fact that existence can be stronger or weaker even within a substance, that material substances undergo a movement toward greater strength, and that beginnings and ends are connected: the end of a thing is also its beginning.[^V08-606][^V08-607] Aristotle and later thinkers in his school, by contrast, agreed that these souls come to be. This is one of the famous disagreements between the two great philosophers. As I have said, we can understand their views of the soul’s eternity and coming to be in a way that brings their deeper meanings together.

Those devoted to Plato’s view offered three arguments for the soul’s eternity. First, everything that comes to be must have some matter that can receive it. The matter’s readiness makes the new thing more fit to exist than not to exist. So if souls came to be, they would have to be material. But that is false, so the claim that they come to be must also be false.[^V08-608]

Second, if souls came to be with bodies, then an infinite number of bodies existed in the past, and an infinite number of souls came to be with them. Since everyone agrees that souls survive the bodies they leave, the number of souls now in existence would be infinite. But that is impossible: anything that can increase or decrease in number must be limited. So the souls that exist now must be limited in number. Their coming to be therefore cannot depend on the coming to be of bodies or on matter’s readiness. They must be eternal.

Third, if souls came to be, they would also pass away, since everything that comes to be can perish. But the soul is everlasting, as will be shown later. So it must also be eternal.[^V08-609]

The answer to the first argument follows from our view that the soul comes to be through the body but survives as a spirit. On the other view, the soul comes to be together with matter, but not inside it.
