As for the second argument, the souls that have left bodies may be infinite in number, but they are not all arranged together in space or in a natural order. The proof that an infinite number is impossible applies only when all the things are ordered together in this way. No proof has been given that an unlimited number is impossible in every sense.[^V08-610]

As for the third argument, the human soul, considered in its immaterial nature, neither comes to be nor perishes. But in the way it enters the world of coming-to-be, it can perish just as it can come to be.

Those who say that souls come to be offer their own arguments. One is that, if souls existed before bodies, they would either be one or many. If there were one soul, it would either divide when it became connected with bodies or remain one. If it remained one, the same soul would belong to every body. Then everything one person knew would be known by everyone, and everything one person did not know would be unknown to everyone. That is impossible. But if one soul divided into many, something immaterial would have to be divisible. If many souls existed before bodies, each would need to be distinguished from the others by its nature, by something that necessarily follows from its nature, or by an accidental feature. The first two are impossible because all human souls belong to one kind and share the same essential features. Accidental differences, however, arise from matter; the body is, in one sense, the soul’s matter. Before the body, then, there could be no different accidental features. So souls could not have existed before bodies, whether as one or as many. The view that they are eternal is therefore false.

The author of *The Summary* objects to this argument in several ways. First, why could souls not have been one before bodies and then become many? It is not enough to reply that anything which is one and can be divided must be a body with a continuous kind of unity. We cannot accept that everything whose unity is continuous can be divided, or that everything divisible must have continuous unity. A universal statement does not always hold when its terms are reversed.[^V08-611]

Second, suppose that souls were many before bodies. Why must each have a distinguishing feature? If each is distinct because it has some special feature, that feature too must differ from the others. It must either differ by the same feature, which leads in a circle, or by a third feature, which leads to an endless chain. And a thing can have a distinguishing feature only after it is already distinguished; so the explanation circles back on itself.[^V08-612]

Third, even if many things must have distinguishing features, why could these not be essential features? The difference between souls in kind has already been established.

Fourth, suppose that none of the usual categories can distinguish the souls. Why could accidental features not do so? You say that accidents depend on matter, and the matter here is the body. Before the body, there is no body. But why could a soul connected with one body not have been connected with an earlier body, and the soul’s connection with each body before that with another, without end? The demand to explain their differences can be stopped only by disproving the movement of souls from one body to another. Yet philosophers who disprove that movement rely on souls coming to be with bodies. They say that when a body is ready, a new soul must come from its source to govern it. If a soul that had already lived in another body then entered it as well, one body would have two souls, which is impossible. Their proof depends on souls coming to be; but their account of souls coming to be depends on disproving the movement from one body to another. This seems to be a circle in their argument. Abu al-Barakat al-Baghdadi raised this objection in *The Considered View* and wondered how earlier thinkers had missed such an important problem.

Fifth, even if souls do not pass from one body to another, why could they not have had earlier accidental features that distinguished them, each caused by an earlier feature, without a beginning?

Sixth, there is a counterargument. After souls leave their bodies, they are not distinguished by their nature or by what necessarily follows from it, but by accidental features. Yet souls that never gained any further qualities while in their bodies would have no accidents after death, only their own individual nature, which had already belonged to them while they governed different bodies. If that is enough[^V08-613] to distinguish them after death, then it should also be enough for them to be distinct before they entered those bodies. Nor can anyone answer, as Avicenna does, that each soul has an awareness of its own identity that other souls do not have. We say that a thing’s awareness of itself is the thing itself, as has been established in the discussion of knowledge. If[^V08-614] two souls differed in their awareness of themselves, they would differ in their very nature, and that would undo the original argument. And if this kind of awareness is enough to distinguish souls, why could they not be distinguished by it before they entered bodies?

In answer to the first objection, whenever one individual thing is divided into many after being one, its parts must differ from the whole. A part is not the same thing as the whole without that part. If the difference is in their nature or in what necessarily follows from it, then the parts must always have been distinct, not become distinct only at a particular time. What we assumed was one thing would already have been many. But if the difference is not in nature or in what follows from it, then the parts differ only in size. A part and a whole can differ in size only if the whole is extended and has magnitude. Otherwise, there would be no reason to call one the whole and the other a part.

Also,[^V08-615] if the parts of a thing belong to its nature, then the thing must be extended or have magnitude. The soul would then have size, which is impossible. Even if we accepted that an immaterial being could divide into parts, whether alike or different in nature, each part would become a distinct soul only after its connection with a body. Each soul, considered as an individual, would therefore come to be at that point. That is exactly what we are trying to show.

I should add one question. These souls become distinct through the change in their very being. Once their powers of thought have fully developed, each has an intellectual existence unlike that of other souls that began as the same kind. Do they also have a distinct intellectual existence before the body? Or do they have no existence before the body at all? This question calls for a new inquiry of its own. Not everyone is able to understand this view; many people turn away from it as someone with a blocked nose recoils from the smell of a rose.

As for the second objection, it is a difficulty about how things are distinguished by accidental features. The same person raised it elsewhere, where he could not solve it, and then tried to dismiss it here. He says: even if things are as you claim, we know by common sense that every kind of soul includes many individuals. We know that one person does not have to differ in nature from everyone else. Once we find two souls of one kind, the argument is finished.

I reply: perhaps this is only an impression formed by the imagination, after you have proved that individuals cannot be distinguished by accidents rather than by essential features. And there is more than one problem with the reply.[^V08-616] First, the person claims that we know by common sense that there are many individuals of each kind, then explains this by saying that one human being need not differ in nature from every other human being. That does not establish that every kind of soul has many individuals. It would be clearer to speak of the many forms within each kind.

The sound answer is that what distinguishes one kind from another can be essential to one thing and accidental to the shared kind. The rational feature, for example, distinguishes a human being from a horse, even though it is not part of the shared animal nature. This use of “accidental” does not refer to a separate feature added after something exists. It refers to a distinction made when we analyze its nature in thought. If the rational feature came to be only after the animal nature already existed, then the animal nature would have to be distinguished and completed by yet another feature. That would lead to an endless chain or a circle. Instead, the distinction exists in the mind’s analysis, while animal and rational exist together in the living thing. The same is true of the different kinds within one broader kind. So a kind does not first need to be individually distinguished before its distinguishing feature can belong to it; the feature makes the kind definite in the first place.[^V08-617]

This is also true of individuals within one kind: what makes an individual distinct is its own existence. Existence comes before nature in the real world, even though we can think about nature first. As for the third objection, we will show that human souls belong to one kind while they exist in their natural, body-related state, before their potential intellects become actual. There is also a proof from their purpose: kinds spread among many individuals because material things cannot last forever as individual beings. Their kind survives through one generation after another. Separate intellects and heavenly beings do not have this limitation.[^V08-618]

As for the fourth objection, we will show that the movement of souls from one body to another is impossible for reasons that do not depend on souls coming to be. The fifth objection fails because one accidental feature cannot follow another in an endless sequence unless the thing receiving them is changing and becoming ready for them. Such changes can take place only in bodily matter. Before bodies, souls would have no bodily readiness to receive one feature after another.

As for the sixth objection, souls that begin as potential are distinguished from one another by features that come from matter. Since they come to be through bodies, their state is like that of material forms and natural powers, which are made distinct by bodily differences. Each soul then becomes a particular individual through its own existence, which is also its awareness of itself. This identity continues through a further course of substantial change. So the souls remain distinct even as each one changes in its existence from its beginning to its highest completion.[^V08-619] The basic point is that individuals of one kind must first be distinguished by something outside their nature and its necessary features. Only material features can do this. The souls that survive bodies carry with them the distinction they gained while embodied, because some trace of it remains. Souls before bodies could not be distinguished in this way, either directly or by an inherited trace.[^V08-620]

It has also been established[^V08-621] that before the world of movement and events, there can be no separate feature that makes one soul distinct from another. There can be no distinction by accidents after agreement in nature. This is the most that can be said for this argument.

Abu al-Barakat gives another argument for souls coming to be. If a soul existed before its body, it must either have been connected to another body or have been without a body. The first would mean that it had moved from body to body. He rejects this on the basis of an argument made by religious scholars: if our souls had been in other bodies, we would remember some of the conditions we experienced then. Since we do not remember any, we know that we were not in other bodies. The second option would leave the souls inactive, though nothing in nature is inactive.

This argument has several weaknesses. First, the proof against moving from one body to another is weak. Earlier memories might disappear when the powers and senses that held them are lost or changed. Only lasting tendencies might remain. This could explain why people differ in their readiness to learn, their intelligence, their dullness, and their ability to form habits. The note below explains this point further.[^V08-622] Second, souls would be inactive before birth only if they existed then as souls but were not connected with bodies. If they had another mode of existence—as intellects, or as something below soul, like natural forms—then they would not be inactive.

Third, souls may have existed before these bodies in another world, connected to other bodies that are not natural bodies made of the elements or the heavenly spheres. The impossibility of a soul moving from one body to another has been proved only when it means moving between material bodies in this world, as matter becomes ready to receive the soul. As will be shown later, the proof against that kind of movement does not rule out every other mode of existence.[^V08-623][^V08-624]

#### Chapter 3 — A fuller account of the question: examining earlier claims and answering their arguments

This question is hard to follow and goes very deep. That is why the philosophers of earlier times disagreed about it. The reason is that the human soul has no single, fixed place in its existence, as other natural things, souls, and intellects do. Each of those has a known place and level. The human soul, by contrast, passes through many different levels. It has earlier and later modes of existence, and takes a different form in each world and at each level. As the saying goes: “My heart can take on every form: it is a pasture for gazelles and a monastery for monks.”[^V08-625] This makes the soul hard to understand.

What people have understood about the soul is mainly what comes from its connection to the body and the powers of perception and movement it exercises through it. All animals share perception and movement. The further fact that the soul is immaterial and survives when it stops governing this body has been inferred from the fact that it receives knowledge: knowledge cannot be divided, so its receiver cannot be divided either. From this, they conclude that the soul is simple in its nature. Whatever is simple in this way cannot perish, or it would have to be made up of the power to exist and the power not to exist, and of actuality and potentiality. That is impossible. This is the most they know of the soul, or something close to it. Anyone who thinks this is enough to know the soul’s true nature is like someone mistaking a swelling for real substance. Many problems remain that this account cannot solve.

For example, the soul’s simplicity seems to conflict with its coming to be. Its spiritual and intellectual nature seems to conflict with its connection to the body and its bodily changes, such as health, illness, and bodily pleasure and pain. Its simplicity and freedom from matter also seem to conflict with the fact that many souls exist, one for each body.

Another problem follows for those who deny that the soul develops through different stages and changes through its sensory, imaginal, and intellectual states. They must say that each soul is one and the same thing from the moment it first joins a body until it reaches its highest level of immateriality and intellect. It would remain one substance under one human kind, just as a human being belongs to the wider kind of animal.

Here is another difficulty for them. They say that the soul is the feature that makes a human being what it is, just as the ability to sense makes an animal what it is and the ability to reason makes a human being what it is. They also say that a general kind and the feature that distinguishes it from other kinds correspond to matter and form in natural things. The distinguishing feature that completes a kind also completes the existence of the broader kind. And when they call the broader kind an “accidental” feature of the distinguishing one, they do not mean it is a quality that can exist separately from it. They mean only that we can separate the two in thought, even though they cannot be separated in reality.[^V08-626][^V08-627][^V08-628]

If all this is granted, then without a substantial change in the human soul, it would have to remain joined in existence to the growing, sensing body.[^V08-629] The soul is the source of the feature that makes the human kind what it is—reasoning—and of the feature that makes the animal kind what it is—sensing. These features are also the natural forms of their bodies. In the theory just described, a feature that distinguishes a kind cannot exist separately from the kind whose existence it completes. So the soul would have to be a body in one of the senses already described, even though these philosophers say that reason is immaterial from the beginning and remains so. This is another proof that a substance can grow stronger, just as qualities and quantities can. It resolves many problems about how the soul can come to be and survive beyond nature.

Most people failed to see this principle.[^V08-630] They were confused about the soul’s coming to be, its survival, its immateriality, and its connection to the body. Some denied that it is immaterial; some denied that it survives the body; others accepted the movement of souls from one body to another. Those firmly grounded in knowledge, who bring reasoning and proof together with direct insight and experience, understand that the soul has many states and stages.[^V08-631] Though one in itself, it has several modes of existence: before nature, with nature, and beyond nature. They saw that human souls exist before bodies through the completeness of their cause.[^V08-632] A complete cause exists along with its effect, because it fully gives rise to that effect. If the cause is complete and fully productive, its effect cannot be separate from it. But the soul’s ability to act through a body depends on a particular readiness and certain conditions. It is clear that the soul comes to be when the body is fully ready for it, and survives the body once it has reached completion. This is because its cause lasts forever.

If you know with certainty that the soul’s cause exists before the body,[^V08-633] and understand what it means for a cause and its effect to belong together, you will know that the soul exists before the body in the fullness of its being. The soul’s dependence on the body concerns only one of its modes of existence: its lower, natural mode. This is the side of the soul marked by need, possibility, and lack—not the side marked by completeness and freedom from need. If the body were needed for the soul’s full identity and complete existence, as it is for other animals and plants[^V08-634], then when the body vanished, the soul would vanish too. The craftsman’s work also depends on tools, but the craftsman does not perish when a tool breaks. The soul has a power of intellect that works with intellectual realities by itself, without using a bodily tool. This is its own completion and the source of its freedom from the body and other physical things. Through the completeness of its cause, the soul is beyond the world of things that are always changing.

The truth is that the human soul comes to be through the body and acts in the body, but survives and understands as a spirit. Its work on bodies is bodily; its understanding of itself and of its Maker is spiritual. Separate intellects are spiritual in both what they are and what they do. Natural powers are bodily in both what they are and what they do. Each of those kinds of substance has one fixed place, but the human soul does not. That is why it develops through different stages. Its action in the body is not like the action of a separate intellect on a body: the soul directly moves and senses particular things, and is changed and completed through this. It does not simply give existence to things from above.

Let us return to the remaining arguments about whether the human soul comes to be or is eternal, and examine them.

Among the remaining arguments for the soul’s coming to be is one[^V08-635] from the author of the book called *The Wisdom of Illumination*. He says that the human soul does not exist before the body, because each human being has a self and private inner states that others cannot know. The souls that govern human beings cannot all be one;[^V08-636] if they were, what one person knew would be known by everyone. If these souls existed before bodies, they could not have been one, since they do not divide later: they have no size, and are not bodies.[^V08-637] Nor could they have been many, because before bodies they had no greater or lesser strength,[^V08-638] no accidental features, and no differences caused by movement. So they could exist neither as one nor as many before bodies.

I see several problems with this. First, to reject the claim that souls were one before bodies, the argument assumes that they are individual instances of one kind. I do not accept that. They may instead be parts of one reality, with a unity closer to intellectual unity than to the unity of many instances of one kind. Some philosophers say that intellectual substances are pure acts of existence without a nature of their own. Their existence can be stronger or weaker. A stronger existence contains, as it were, what is found in a weaker one and brings about more than it can. On this view, before entering bodies the souls are distinguished by intellectual aspects that belong to their existence itself, and that come before their natural existence. They are not distinguished by accidental features that depend on a material receiver.[^V08-639][^V08-640] The reports “We are the first and the last” and “I was a prophet while Adam was still between water and clay” point to this.

Second, the argument says that if the souls were one, what one person knew would be known by everyone. That follows only if we mean kinds of understanding that depend on bodily tools. If we mean understanding that does not depend on tools, then all souls may share it. They share awareness of themselves and awareness of many first truths that need no bodily tools.[^V08-641][^V08-642]

You might say: if souls were one, then one being would have every bodily tool, and would perceive through all of them. Each of us would know what every other person knows.[^V08-643]

I reply: we are discussing souls before they are joined to bodies, not their state once they have become embodied. Their unity in one mode of existence is not the same as their unity in another. No one doubts that the soul’s bodily state differs from its state without a body. In its bodily state, each soul becomes joined to one body and, together with it, forms a natural living kind.

In its intellectual state, once complete, the soul is joined to a separate intellect, as the greatest of the early philosophers taught. How can we treat the soul’s unity in the world of intellect and gathering as if it were the same as its unity in the bodily world of separation? If souls could be one in the bodily world, bodies could also be one, since the body is an individual body only through its soul.

Perception through an instrument depends on that instrument. Since souls perceive through tools, each perceives through the tools it has. This does not mean that whatever one soul perceives through its tools is also perceived by the soul itself through some other tools, or by another soul through a different set of tools.

Third, the argument says souls cannot have been one before bodies because they could not later divide. But unity can mean many things: intellectual unity, unity of kind, unity of number, or unity of size. Each kind of unity has a different opposite. Not every kind of multiplicity is the opposite of every kind of unity. The subject of intellectual unity may be the same as the subject of numerical multiplicity; likewise, something can be one by nature while made up of many parts. As I explained earlier, simple intellect contains every intelligible thing. So in this case, descending from the intellectual state to the bodily state can make the one many, while rising from this state to the intellectual one can make the many one. This does not have to mean that souls become extended bodies, as the argument assumes.

Another argument from *The Wisdom of Illumination* says that if souls existed before bodies, nothing would have kept them from the realm of pure light. Nothing there distracts them or changes their state. They would therefore have been complete already. Their later work in bodies would be pointless. There would also be no reason for one soul to be joined to one particular body rather than another, since chance belongs to bodies, not to the world of pure light. The claim that souls experience a state that makes them fall from their earlier rank is also false, since there is no change outside the world of movement.

In my notes on *The Wisdom of Illumination*, I replied that souls have one mode of existence in the intellectual world and another in the natural, sensory world. In the intellectual world they are pure and unobstructed, but they still lack many good things that can be gained only by descending into bodies and using bodily tools through time and through different kinds of readiness. Their work in individual bodies is not pointless. It serves a great purpose known only to God and those firmly grounded in knowledge.[^V08-644][^V08-645]

It is striking that this same philosopher, and many who follow him, accept that some souls rise in this life to the world of pure light and intellect. They do not say that this requires change in that world or that something new must happen there. The same is true of the soul’s return as of its beginning: both are hard to understand. If someone accepts that souls connected with bodies can join a world of intellect free of change, then they should also be able to accept that souls can leave that world and join bodies without change there. Whatever makes the return possible also makes the beginning possible. For this reason, scripture often uses the first creation as proof of the return: “Just as We began the first creation, We will bring it back.”[^V08-646]

The story of the human soul descending from the holy world of its father into the bodily world of nature, its cradle, and the animal soul, its mother, appears often in revealed books, and in the symbols of prophets, saints, and great thinkers. The Qur'an speaks of the soul’s descent and ascent in many verses: “We created the human being in the best form, then brought him down to the lowest of the low, except those who believe and do good works”; “We said, ‘Go down from it, all of you. When guidance comes to you from Me, whoever follows My guidance will have no fear, nor will they grieve’”; “Go down, some of you enemies to others. On earth you will have a home and provision for a time”; “Rivalry for more distracts you until you visit the graves,” followed by “Then, on that day, you will surely be asked about your blessings”; “There is not one of you who will not come to it; this is a decree your Lord must carry out. Then We will save those who were mindful of God and leave the wrongdoers kneeling there”; and “Just as He began you, so you will return: some He has guided, while others have deserved to go astray.”

The Prophet says, “People are like mines of gold and silver.” This points to the soul’s prior existence in its own source: the separate intellects, which are storehouses of God’s knowledge. The way the soul exists before the body has been explained: the soul has an intellectual, immaterial mode of existence as well as a body-related one. In the same way, each particular thing exists in God’s prior decree, where it is protected from change and erasure, and also exists here in the world of change, coming to be and passing away. God says, “God erases what He wills and confirms what He wills. With Him is the Mother of the Book,” the Preserved Tablet, which does not change.[^V08-647]

The Commander of the Faithful, Imam ‘Ali, says: “May God have mercy on one who knows where they came from, where they are, and where they are going.” The first refers to the soul’s state before this world, the second to its state in this world, and the third to what comes after. He also says: “Let a person be ready in mind and be among the people of the next world, for from there they came and to it they will return.”

A report from Imam ‘Ali about the soul’s nature, beginning, and return says: “Know that the human form is God’s greatest[^V08-648] proof against His creation. It is a book He has written with His own hand, a summary of both worlds, a shortened form of the Preserved Tablet, a witness to all that is hidden, and a straight road to every good—a path stretched between Heaven and Hell.” Aristotle, the teacher of the philosophers, says in *Theology* that the soul gains from descending into this world. Its descent does not harm it. It learns[^V08-649] about the nature of things by pouring its powers into them. Its noble works and actions,[^V08-650] which were hidden while it was in the intellectual world, become visible. If it did not reveal these powers and make them visible, they would remain unused and it would forget its virtues and excellent abilities. We know the soul’s power and nobility through what it does. An ability that never appears in action would be as if it had never existed.

Early philosophers left subtle hints and noble symbols about the soul’s descent and return, along with stories that point the way. These include the story of Salaman and Absal,[^V08-651] the ringdove in *Kalila and Dimna*, and the story of Hayy ibn Yaqzan. Avicenna wrote a poem asking why the soul descended, beginning: “From the highest place came a dove...”[^V08-652] All of these point to the soul’s existence before the body in the high divine world, and its return to the place from which it descended.

The saying, “Those who act in bodies experience a state that makes them fall from their rank,” can be understood this way. The soul’s “fall” means that it comes from its original cause, its holy intellectual father. What brings about this fall is the source’s own creative aspects and powers.[^V08-653] Effects descend from their causes through the causes’ own features and limitations. The lower effects come from the possible and limited sides of their causes; their higher effects come from their fuller sides.[^V08-654] Some of these limitations are called the “sin” of our father Adam. The soul’s coming from its source is called its “flight from God’s anger.” This is simply how existence is ordered: a weaker light cannot remain where a stronger light shines. If you try to solve a very difficult question in divine matters before your mind is ready, you may become mentally exhausted and turn to something less demanding before you have learned how to return to the question. The brightness of intellectual reality can overwhelm the mind, just as sunlight overwhelms a bat’s eyes.

A prophetic report says that God has seventy veils of light; if He removed them, the splendor of His face would burn everything reached by His sight.[^V08-655][^V08-656] Earlier philosophers used many symbolic explanations for the soul’s descent. Empedocles says the soul once lived in a high and noble place, but fell after making a mistake. It came here to escape God’s anger, and after descending it became a support for souls whose intellects had become mixed and confused. Plato says that the soul descends when its wings fall away; once it grows them back, it rises to its first world.[^V08-657]

Plato also says in *Timaeus* that souls descend for different reasons. Some descend because of a mistake and are punished for it here. Others descend for another reason, which he does not explain; he simply criticizes the soul’s descent and its life in these bodies. In another passage of *Timaeus*, he says the soul is a noble and happy substance. The good Creator sent it into this world when He made the world, so that the world would have intellect. A world made with such skill and care should not be without intellect. But it could not have intellect without a soul. So God sent a soul into this world and placed it there. He then sent our souls and placed them in our bodies, so that this world might be complete and not fall short of the world of intellect. The kinds of animals in the sensory world should therefore match those in the intellectual world.

Aristotle says in many places in *Theology* that nature has two forms, intellectual and sensory. The soul is better and nobler in the intellectual world. In the sensory world it is lower because of the body it enters. Still, even though the soul is intellectual and comes from the higher world, it must receive something from the sensory world and enter it, because its nature connects the two worlds. We should not blame it for leaving the intellectual world and living here. It lies between the two worlds. It is the last of the noble, divine substances and the first of the natural, sensory substances. Since it borders the sensory world, its powers flow into that world and adorn it. The soul may also take on something of the world’s lower nature, unless it guards itself.

In another passage, Aristotle says that the noble soul leaves its higher world and descends by its own ability and strength, so it can shape and govern what lies below it. If it then leaves this world after having shaped and governed it, and quickly returns to its own world, the descent does it no harm. It benefits from the descent by learning about the nature of things. In another passage, he says that when the soul refuses to unite with the intellect and insists on being separate from it, it longs to be alone and to make itself and the intellect two. It looks toward something in this world, away from intellect, and begins to remember. If it remembers the things above, it does not descend here. If it remembers the lower world, it comes down from its noble world.[^V08-658][^V08-659][^V08-660]

Aristotle asks: if the soul can imagine this world before coming here, must it not remember it after it leaves and returns to the higher world? If so, that would conflict with the claim that the soul remembers nothing from this world in the higher world.

We reply: before it comes here, the soul imagines this world through intellectual understanding, not through ordinary mental images.[^V08-661][^V08-662]

Aristotle’s words also show that the soul exists in the intellectual world and in the natural world, though these are different modes of existence. He says in his second treatise that we should ask whether the soul can be divided, and if so, whether this happens in its nature or only by accident. The soul, he says, is divided only by accident when it is in the body and divided along with the body. One part of the soul thinks, another is animal-like; one part desires, another becomes angry. The soul is not divided in its own nature. It exists in every part of the body so that the body can be alive through it. This shows that in the intellectual world the soul exists without division, either by nature or by accident. In its natural existence, by contrast, it can be divided by accident. These two modes of existence cannot be the same.[^V08-663]

Aristotle also says that when intellect is in its own world, it looks only at itself and not at anything below it. When it is outside that world—in the sensory world—it sometimes looks at things and sometimes at itself. The body it enters brings about this change through the soul. If the soul is strongly affected by the body, it looks outward at things. If it becomes less affected by the body, it looks only at itself. Intellect itself does not change from one state to another, except in the way just described. The soul, however, does change when it seeks knowledge of things. These passages, and others like them, show that the soul exists in the world of intellect before this life and returns there after it. We can see this by reading and thinking carefully about Aristotle’s words.[^V08-664][^V08-665]

The early philosophers, like the prophets, often spoke through symbols and figurative language. They did so to make their meaning easier for people with limited understanding and to protect it from misunderstanding. When they say that the soul sinned, fell, or fled from God’s anger, they knew that no sin or act of disobedience could arise in the holy world, where no new effects of movement can take place. By the soul’s “sin,” they meant its possible, limited nature and its coming from its source. This limitation makes it depend on the body, or means that it is only potentially an intellect. It cannot complete its intellectual activity until later, after its practical powers have acted in animal and human life. The soul turns away from its holy source because it is bound to the body, a relation that is one of its modes of existence. Its flight from God’s anger is its natural desire to govern the body and reach its own completion, overcoming its substantial lack through a more complete, immaterial existence.[^V08-666]

A story told by some Persian philosophers of the East says that darkness surrounded and imprisoned light for a time. The angels strengthened the light, which then overcame Ahriman, the darkness, though it allowed darkness to remain until a set time. Darkness came from the light through a flawed thought. This story, too, refers to the soul. The soul is a luminous substance because it can become intellect. Darkness means the animal and natural powers that confine it and draw it toward the lower world. The angels’ help is the aid of divine purpose, which guides the soul toward intellectual light and actuality. Darkness remaining until a set time means that these powers remain until death or until the soul’s relation with the body ends. The flawed thought means the soul’s attention to material things.

Another argument against the souls’ existence before their bodies comes from the author of *The Wisdom of Illumination* and *The Book of Flashes*. He says: if the souls that govern bodies existed before them, then either some of them did not govern bodies at all, or every one of them did. If any did not govern a body, they would be idle. If none were idle, there must have been a time when all souls were already governing bodies and no governing soul remained.

I reply that we can choose the first option. A soul’s existence as a separate being differs from its existence as something that governs a body. The philosophers who say the soul exists in the intellectual world before the body do not mean that it exists there as a soul. They mean it has another mode of existence. So, if it does not govern a body in the intellectual world, it is not idle. It is only idle if it exists there specifically as a soul but does not govern a body. A soul as intellect has no work with bodies; a soul as soul must always govern and act in a body.

We can also choose the second option.[^V08-667] It does not follow that there must be a time when all souls are governing bodies. If “a time” means one particular, limited time, and “all” means every soul, that conclusion does not follow. If it means every time, or some broader range of times, there is no problem. Time has no end. Even if there were infinitely many souls and each existed at some time, this would mean only that all of them exist across an endless range of times. It would not mean they all exist at one time. So the required conclusion—that all souls must be governing bodies at once—does not follow.

The intellectual source from which souls come into this world has unlimited powers and ways of existing. Whenever souls proceed from it, its unlimited power remains as it was; it is not used up, because it comes from the Source of all things. The souls are not present in the intellectual world as an unlimited number of separate beings arranged in a natural or spatial order. So the question of whether each soul is acting or idle does not lead to the result that there must have been a time when no soul remained.

Do not think that souls exist in their intellectual source only as possibilities, like the unlimited number of forms waiting in prime matter. A thing exists differently in a source that acts and a matter that receives it. Existence in an active source is fuller and more complete than the thing’s own separate existence. Existence in a receiver may be weaker than the thing’s own existence. In a receptive material source, something exists as a possibility and may either come to be or not. In itself it is simply able to exist or not. In its active source, it exists necessarily. The souls exist in their intellectual source and holy origin in a full, noble, undivided way. To grasp this, the heart must rise from knowing something by proof to seeing it directly.[^V08-668][^V08-669]

You might say: what you have described means that one reality changes into another, which is impossible.

I reply: that is not what we mean by one reality turning into another. That would mean that the nature of one thing becomes the nature of another in meaning, or that the existence of one nature becomes the existence of another without shared matter changing through a series of forms, or that one simple reality becomes a different simple reality. All of these are impossible. But existence can grow stronger and a thing can develop in its very being until qualities that belong to what it is differ from those it had before. This is not impossible, because existence comes before nature, and nature follows existence.[^V08-670][^V08-671] The natural forms develop and grow stronger[^V08-672] until they leave matter and become intellectual forms in the higher world. In the same way, human souls move in the opposite direction: in the intellectual world they are one simple intellectual reality, but when they descend into this world, they become many. Their existence weakens, and they become attached to natural bodies in lower places.

There is no impossible change of reality in either case. Things of one kind, such as human beings, the heavens, the earth, or water, can have different modes and stages of existence: natural, psychic, intellectual, and divine. If you understand or imagine the earth or sky, then there is an intellectual sky in your intellect and an imagined sky in your imagination. Each is truly a sky, not merely a figure of speech, just as the sky outside you is a sky. The first two forms are even more deserving of the name,[^V08-673] because the outward sky is mixed with extra features, darkness, absence, and constant change.

This is true of every natural kind. In God’s prior decree, all things have a pure intellectual existence. So what is wrong with the soul, which is the form of a natural kind, having another mode of existence in the intellectual world?

Anyone who thinks that one meaning cannot exist through different modes without changing its nature[^V08-674] must also deny that we can know anything real. To know something absent is to have a matching form of it in the mind.[^V08-675] That form may be intellectual, imagined, or sensed, depending on the knower’s power. A simple intellect can know things through one simple intellectual form that matches many different individuals and kinds in the world. Those individuals differ in material features and their natural identities, but they share one kind when we consider what they are without adding a condition of unity or number.

Likewise, when the knower is an imaginal power, its mental form matches a sensible image or a form found in matter, though its mode of existence is different. The same applies to other kinds of perception. One reality can have different modes of existence, some more immaterial and less divided than others, and less caught up in movement and bodily matter.

If one intellectual form, wholly free of matter, can match many bodily forms and be one with them, then one intellectual form—the Holy Spirit—can also match many human souls and complete their existence. A report attributed to Pythagoras supports this idea. A spiritual being once revealed knowledge, and when asked who it was, said: “I am your complete nature.” My dear friend, if you could rise through the levels of your own existence, you would see many different modes of being. Each would be the completion of your whole identity, and you would point to each one and say, “I.”[^V08-676] The familiar saying puts it this way: “You are I; then who am I?”

Another argument from *The Wisdom of Illumination* says that events in time have no beginning, and that souls cannot move into human bodies from other kinds of beings. So if souls were not created, there would have to be infinitely many of them. That would require infinitely many actual aspects in the separate intellects, which is impossible.

I reply that the souls’ existence in the world understood by the mind is not like their existence in the world of the senses. There they are not many individuals arranged in time, space, or any other order. If infinitely many souls in this world have one intellectual form, then that form has an unlimited power to act in the sense of strength. We have already distinguished this from having an unlimited number or duration. An unlimited strength is not impossible. What is impossible is an unlimited number of separate, actual aspects of possibility and deficiency in the intellects. The aspects of good and necessity can be unlimited in strength; the aspects of lack and possibility have a limit in strength and number, except potentially across different times and cycles, as the philosophers explain.[^V08-677]

It is strange that this philosopher says every bodily kind has a guiding light in the separate world, and that human souls have their own guiding intellectual light. He also says that souls are weak lights compared with the separate light, like rays compared with the sun, and that all light belongs to one simple kind, differing only in strength and weakness. If souls stand in this relation to their intellectual source, then the source’s existence must be the full existence of all souls, and its light the completion of their light.[^V08-678][^V08-679]

In short, when souls exist apart from bodies in the world of separate intellects, they are one with their active intellectual source. In the world of bodies, by contrast, they exist as many individuals and parts. The part of the soul connected with the heart differs from the part connected with the brain and other organs. The thinking part differs from the desiring part, and the desiring part from the angry part. But this division is different from bodily division. The soul can be divided in other ways known to people who have reached completion. Its existence in the intermediate world between intellect and sense is divided in yet another way: there it is an imaginal, perceiving substance, free of physical bodies but not of imagined forms. That mode of existence is also life and perception.

As I have explained, imagination is a being that does not depend on the brain or on other natural bodies. It is a complete, individual living being that moves through the world of life and the gardens of paradise.

A note of clarification: the commentator of *The Wisdom of Illumination*, the scholar of Shiraz, criticizes these arguments against the soul’s existence before the body. He calls them persuasive but not conclusive, because they all depend[^V08-680] on rejecting the movement of souls from one body to another. He claims that the movement of souls is a strong and even correct view, held by great ancient philosophers such as Plato. Though we have seen that this view is false and have been given a clear proof that it is impossible, we interpret Plato and the early philosophers differently from the way others understand them, as will be shown later. I have explained the weaknesses in the commentator’s arguments in my notes; repeating them here would take too long. The commentator then says that Plato taught that souls are eternal, and that this must be true because of the report “Spirits are like gathered troops…” and the report “God created spirits two thousand years before bodies.” The number “two thousand” was used simply to help ordinary people understand;[^V08-681] the soul’s existence before the body has no measurable length of time. It is eternal, while the body comes to be.

I reply: if Plato meant that individual souls existed forever as souls, as the commentator assumes, impossible results would follow. They would have been inactive for an endless time, since their nature as souls is defined by their relation to bodies. This is not like the accidental relation of a parent to a child, a sailor to a ship, or a homeowner to a house. Those relations can end and begin while the person remains the same. The soul’s relation to a body is like the relation of matter and form: it is part of what the soul is. As long as it is a soul, it exists in relation to a body. Once it becomes a separate intellect, its mode of existence changes, and it returns to its own world. If it had existed forever as a soul, it would have been inactive forever, which is impossible.

The view that separate souls existed forever would also imply many individuals of one kind with no matter to receive change and no accidental features to distinguish them. It would also place infinitely many actual aspects in the intellectual source, damaging its unity. If only a limited number of souls had existed forever, the souls would have had to move from one body to another, with many other problems already discussed.[^V08-682]

In short, it is false to attribute to Plato or other great ancient philosophers the claim that individual souls, as souls, have always existed. They taught that the world comes to be, and that nature itself changes and passes away. All bodies flow and vanish, as I have explained elsewhere.

But if the claim is only that souls have an intellectual mode of existence before their body-related mode of existence, then it does not mean that they are eternal as souls, nor that they move from one body to another. Those views are both false. The reports and verses that say souls existed before bodies must be understood in the sense I have explained.[^V08-683]

The commentator then reports an argument attributed to Plato. If the complete cause of the soul existed before the body ready to govern it, then the soul must also have existed before that body, since an effect cannot be delayed once its complete cause exists. But if the soul’s complete cause did not exist without the body, then the body must be part of the cause or a condition for the soul’s existence.[^V08-684] Yet the soul does not depend on the body, since it would not perish when the body perishes. The philosophers have proved that the soul remains through the source that gives it existence. The body is therefore not needed for the soul’s existence, only for its work in the body. The body is like a wick prepared to catch fire from a great flame: the soul is drawn to it by its nature. Or the body draws the soul as a magnet draws iron. A magnet and iron need not begin to exist at the same time.

I reply: I will explain how the soul comes to be and becomes connected with the body; then the answer to this difficulty will be clear. The argument is sound for a reality that is simple and complete in every way, and it also applies to the separate intellects and to the soul in its simple intellectual mode of existence, which is one form of what exists in God’s knowledge and one of the divine aspects. But the soul’s existence and its being a soul are one and the same. In this mode of existence, the soul is already a form related to a body and governs it. The relation and its action are not features that arise only after the soul exists, as the relation between a magnet and iron does. What makes the soul need a body is its body-related mode of existence: its being a soul, its activity in the body, and its completion through the body. This mode of existence comes to be when the body comes to be, and passes away when the body perishes. That means the soul ceases to exist as a soul with a bodily nature; its substance changes into another mode of existence as it reaches its goals. A thing’s passing into its own goal and source is a higher form of completion.[^V08-685]

The argument is right to say that if the soul depended on the body, it would perish when the body perished. But the claim that it does not perish because it survives through a source that gives it existence proves only that what is simple and complete in its existence cannot perish. No proof known to us shows that the soul, simply as a soul, remains forever. Nor is the soul the only thing with a bodily form and material nature that completes a body. Someone may object: does this mean that every soul that has not reached the level of simple intellect perishes with the body, so only a rare few souls survive beyond it?

I answer that after this natural mode of existence, souls have two further modes. One is an animal mode, between intellect and nature, for those who are in between or incomplete. The other is an intellectual mode, for those who are complete and close to God.

#### A clarification

The claim that the body is needed only for the soul’s action, not for its existence, confuses two kinds of action: an action that belongs to a thing by its nature, and an action that is external and incidental. It treats the soul as if it already had a complete existence in itself, and only afterward began to act on a body—to govern, move, nourish, and complete it—like a builder or gardener who improves a building or a tree by adding soil or water. That work may complete the thing, and it may also develop the builder or gardener, but it is external to both.

That is not the soul’s relation to the body. As long as the soul is a soul, its existence itself involves its relation to a body. In this basic mode of existence, the soul depends on the body through some of its sensory and natural powers. In short, the soul’s action in the body belongs to its nature; it is one way the soul exists. A form’s completion of matter is likewise part of how it exists, and a quality such as whiteness exists by being in a body. This does not mean the soul, form, or quality belongs to the category of relations.[^V08-686] When the soul stops acting in the body, it also stops existing in that mode, unless it changes into a higher mode in which its existence is its own or that of a separate intellect. At that point it is no longer a soul, but something higher. Before a natural form reaches the level of a soul—the first completion of a natural body—it is something lower than a soul.[^V08-687]

Some scholars argue that a soul cannot exist before a body because one thing cannot at once be separate from matter and mixed with it. This is not quite right. Some say that the soul is never mixed with matter; this avoids the problem for the soul but still leaves it for forms and qualities. Others say that “mixed with matter” simply means having a relation to a body. Then the argument proves only that one thing cannot be both related and unrelated to matter at the same time. That would be impossible only if both states happened at once. There is no problem if the soul is separate at one time and related to a body at another, as before and after death.

The first objection fails because the soul begins as a form connected with matter and later comes to exist without depending on matter. One and the same thing is therefore both mixed with matter and separate from it. The second objection fails because the soul’s relation to the body is part of its nature and mode of existence. The soul is one of the kinds of substance, alongside intellect; it is not a complete, separate being that later gains a new relation to a body, like a shopkeeper acquiring a shop. The true answer is that separation from matter and connection to it can belong to one thing at different stages only if its modes of existence change. The soul exists in one way before its bodily connection, in another way while connected, and in another way after the connection ends. These modes are linked as cause and effect; they are not entirely separate from one another.[^V08-688]

In short, human souls have modes of existence that come before and after one another. Earlier modes include animal and plant life, the life of simple matter, and the forms of the elements. Later modes include the potential intellect, then the actual intellect, then the active intellect, and what lies beyond it.

An argument to support the position now under discussion has been raised by a later scholar. If the soul comes to be, it must depend on a cause that makes its existence necessary. That cause must either exist before the soul or not. If it exists before the soul, then the soul must already exist, because a complete cause cannot be separated from its effect. This is impossible. If the cause does not exist before the soul, then it must be simple or made of parts. It cannot be simple, since, as a new thing, it would need another cause that also came to be, and that cause would be simple too. This would lead to an endless chain.

Nor can the cause be made of parts. If each part could cause the soul by itself, then the other parts would not be needed. If one part caused one part of the soul and another caused the rest, then the soul would be made of parts, which it cannot be. If none of the parts had any effect on its own, then something new would have to arise when they came together. If this new thing were nothing, it could not cause the soul to exist. If it were something, then either it too would be made of parts or the original parts would need another cause to bring it about. Either way, the argument ends in an endless chain. The philosopher of Shiraz objects that the argument depends on the impossible idea that a simple thing cannot come from a complex cause.

I reply that I have already answered these objections when discussing the rule that a simple thing can come from a complex cause. See that discussion if you want the full argument. And none of this proves that the soul, simply as a soul, existed eternally. The soul is not a completely simple reality; neither is anything that exists in time and movement, such as natural forms. God is the source of generosity.

#### Chapter 4 — The soul does not perish when the body perishes

The philosophers’ familiar proof that the soul does not perish with the body goes as follows. The soul must come to be when the body comes to be, so the two either exist at the same time or one comes before the other. If they exist at the same time, either they are joined in nature or only in existence. They cannot be joined in nature, since that would mean that the soul and body were two relative things rather than two substances. If they are joined only in existence and neither depends on the other, then when either one ends, the fact that they exist together ends. But that would not cause the other to end. So suppose one depends on the other in order to exist.

The soul cannot come before the body in time, since we have shown that it does not exist before the body. Suppose instead that the soul comes before the body by nature. If the body depended on the soul, then the body could not cease to exist unless the soul also ceased to exist. A cause must be necessary for its effect, and if the cause remained while the effect perished, it would not be enough to cause it. But a body can perish for reasons such as a bad balance of its qualities, poor structure, or its parts coming apart. So the soul cannot be the cause of the body.

Nor can the body be the cause of the soul. It cannot be the soul’s producing cause, simply by being a body, or by some further feature. If being a body were enough, then every body would cause a soul. If some further material feature were needed, it could affect only things in a certain bodily position; what can act only through a bodily relation cannot cause something that exists without one.[^V08-689][^V08-690] Also, a material form is weaker than an immaterial being and cannot cause something stronger than itself. The body cannot be the soul’s receiving cause either, since the soul is immaterial and does not depend on matter. Nor can the body be the soul’s form or purpose. It is more likely that the reverse is true. Thus, the soul and body cannot depend on one another in a way that requires them to exist together.

Someone might ask: did you not say that the body is a cause of the soul’s coming to be? Coming to be means existence after nonexistence. If the body is a condition for the soul’s existence, then its absence should be a cause of the soul’s nonexistence.

The philosophers reply that a source free of change produces an effect at a particular time only when a condition for its coming to be is present. That condition is needed for the soul to begin, but the soul can continue to exist without it. So the absence of that condition does not cause the soul to perish. Once the body is ready to serve as a tool for the soul’s completion, the soul naturally longs to govern it. The lack of this condition cannot cause the soul to cease to exist.[^V08-691][^V08-692]

This is the argument as later philosophers, including Avicenna, have presented it. It contains several loose steps. First, the idea that the soul and body are joined only by chance, without an essential relation, is false. These philosophers themselves say that the soul is the body’s completing form and define the soul as the first completion of a natural body equipped with organs and life. They also say the soul and body form one natural kind, like a human being or a heavenly body. Such a whole cannot be made of two things with no relation of cause and effect between them.

The truth is that the soul and body are necessarily linked, but not as two relative things, nor as two effects of the same cause with no bond between them. They are linked like matter and form. Each needs the other in its own way, without an impossible circular dependence. The body needs the soul—not one particular soul, but a soul in general—in order to be a living body. The soul needs the body only for its particular, individual existence and for its coming to be as a soul.[^V08-693]

With this in mind, we can say that the soul comes before the body by nature.

Someone may say: if so, the body cannot end unless the soul ends too.

I reply: that is right for the body considered as a body. A body cannot exist as a living body while its soul exists in it, or continue as that same living body once the soul is gone. What remains after the soul departs is no longer that body, but matter or a different kind of body. The living body as such depends on its soul. The soul and body are causes of one another in different ways.

Nor can someone object that, if the soul causes the body, it must depend on the body in order to act. Matter does not help the source that gives it its own form to act on it. If the soul could act without any dependence on matter, then it would no longer be a soul but an intellect.[^V08-694]

We reply that every form, whether it exists in matter or acts through matter, affects the matter and its conditions together with a separate source. It does not act independently or in one particular form, but together with the source beyond it, according to its nature in general. The soul, as a general kind of soul, remains one and acts as a cause that sustains the body. But each particular soul depends on the body for the features that make it an individual, just as a form depends on matter for its particular states.

As for the objection that the body cannot be the soul’s cause because the soul is immaterial, we reply that an immaterial intellect is something that acts without any relation to bodies. The soul is not like that. The argument works if it is applied to an intellect that exists intellectually in itself and has no relation to bodies at all. Such an intellect does not perish when a body perishes. But intellect as a separate being is not the same as a soul that governs a particular body and is changed and completed through it.

The body is not simply a tool that the soul picks up and puts down, like a carpenter using a chisel or a saw. Nor is the soul in the body like a captain in a ship or a homeowner in a house: the ship and house remain what they are whether the captain or owner is there or not. These arguments do not prove that the soul, while it exists as a soul, continues after the body. They prove only that a separate intellect does not perish with the body. The remaining question is whether some or all souls change into a being that no longer depends on a body or uses it for completion. We will soon see how this change happens.

As for the philosophers’ claim that a cause untouched by change can act only when a condition appears, and that the condition for something’s beginning is not needed for its continued existence, this is mistaken. Coming to be is not a feature added to a thing’s existence; it is simply that existence after nonexistence. The condition for its coming to be is therefore also a condition for its existence. When the condition is gone, so is the thing that depends on it.[^V08-695][^V08-696]

Nor does it make sense to say that matter can prepare or provide a condition for the existence of a substance that is separate from matter and needs no matter or material conditions. In general, matter can prepare only for what can become one of its states. There is no sense in saying that something can be prepared for a being whose nature is entirely different from its own. Keep this question in mind, for it will come up again.[^V08-697]

#### Chapter 5 — The soul cannot perish

Earlier, we argued that the soul cannot be destroyed when the body is destroyed. Here, we make a stronger claim: the soul cannot perish at all.

Two arguments support this.[^V08-698] First, the soul is something that can exist, and everything that can exist has a cause. As long as that cause remains, together with all the things that make it a cause, its effect cannot cease. We have explained this in the discussion of cause and effect. If the soul could cease to exist, it would have to be because its cause—or one part of its complete cause—had ceased.

There are four kinds of cause: what brings a thing about, what it is made from, the form that makes it what it is, and its end or purpose. The soul cannot perish because its maker disappears. As we will show, its maker is an intellect entirely separate from matter, and the same argument applies to that intellect: it cannot perish. Nor can the soul perish because its material cause disappears, since the soul is not material but immaterial. Nor can it perish because its form disappears, since the soul's form is its very nature. If that form could disappear, we would face the same question about the soul itself. If a second form were needed to explain why the first disappeared, that second form would need another form, and so on without end. The soul cannot perish because its end or purpose disappears either. So the soul cannot perish at all.

By contrast, forms and qualities that can disappear do so because one of their causes, even the cause that brings them about, can disappear.[^V08-699] Their immediate causes are temporary: they arise from different mixtures in the body, and those mixtures create different conditions for them. We have already shown that the soul does not arise in this way.

The second argument begins with the claim that anything new must first be able to exist.[^V08-700] If it could not, then it would be impossible, and what is impossible cannot exist. If something became new without first being able to become new, it would not really be new at all, which is a contradiction. By “able to exist,” we mean that it is fully ready to exist. Such readiness must belong to something that can receive it: the thing that is close to coming into being must have a basis that can support it.

If the soul could perish, there would have to be something that could hold the possibility of its perishing. That cannot be the soul itself, because the soul would not remain as it perished. Yet whatever holds this possibility must remain while the soul perishes. So the soul would have to have matter. We would then ask the same question about that matter. If it could perish, it too would need matter, and this would continue without end. If the chain ends, something must remain that cannot perish. That would be part of the soul. It could not have a position or occupy space, since then the soul would also have a position and occupy space, and could not receive intellectual forms. But what we have shown must remain is without position or place and can receive intellectual forms. That is exactly what we mean by the soul: an immaterial being able to understand. So the soul cannot perish.

Someone might ask: does the soul not have matter that holds the possibility of its coming into being? Why could that matter not also hold the possibility of the soul's perishing? The difference is this: the body can remain as the soul comes into being. But if the body held the possibility of the soul's perishing, it would have to remain after the soul perished. Everyone agrees that the body does not remain when the soul is gone.

Still, these arguments prove only that a simple being whose existence is wholly separate from matter cannot be destroyed. They do not prove that an existence tied to the body can survive the body's destruction. If the soul exists only through its connection to the body, that connected existence would not survive the body's end. But it could not have begun when the body began either, nor could it have been renewed as the body arose. In that case, it would never truly be new. The same individual existence cannot both begin as part of matter and be separate from matter, unless it changes deeply in its very being and becomes separate after first being tied to matter.

The remaining question is how the soul's separate existence can begin if a separate being has no tie to matter and no material readiness to exist. We will answer this later. For now, we can say this: the soul does not become separate by gaining a new, immaterial existence. Rather, its existence through its connection to the body comes to an end, and it returns to its original source. In truth, something becomes separate when a new relation to it begins. This is like the soul's memory faculty coming into being as a storehouse for what it understands.[^V08-701][^V08-702]

The argument that the body holds the possibility of the soul's coming into being, but not the possibility of its perishing, also trades on two meanings of “receiver.” Sometimes this means something ready to receive a form; sometimes it means something that is acted on or completed by a form. The body that holds the possibility of the soul's coming into being is not the living body, but something earlier, such as the seed from which the body's parts develop. That earlier thing does not remain when the soul-form comes into being. The body that remains with the soul receives it in another sense: the soul completes the body, as the form completes the material part of a living thing. The body may also be what receives the possibility of the soul's perishing; but the thing that can receive that perishing is something else, something that remains when the soul is gone. For both coming into being and perishing, we must distinguish what a thing comes from and what it exists in. If we ignore the difference, we confuse the two cases.[^V08-703][^V08-704]

#### Chapter 6 — An Eastern account of the soul's return

The distinguished scholar Nasir al-Din Muhammad al-Tusi once wrote to one of his learned contemporaries, Shams al-Din al-Khusrawshahi, and asked him to explain several difficult questions. His correspondent did not answer. One of the unanswered questions concerned whether the soul survives after the body dies. Al-Tusi put it this way:

“Why do those who say that a thing cannot come into being or perish unless there is something that can hold the possibility of its coming or going say that the human soul comes into being but cannot perish? If the body holds the possibility of the soul's coming into being, why does it not also hold the possibility of its perishing? If the soul has no basis for the possibility of perishing because it is separate from matter, why does that same reason not mean it has no basis for the possibility of coming into being? Then it could never begin. How can a material body hold the possibility of a being free of matter, so different from itself?[^V08-705] If the body holds this possibility because the soul is the body's particular form, why does it not also hold the possibility of the soul's nonexistence for that very reason? Why are the two cases treated differently, when their relation to the body seems the same?”

This clear statement shows that al-Tusi was not satisfied with the answers he found in books, even in his own commentary on Ibn Sina's *Remarks and Admonitions*.

We answered this question earlier. The human body, through its special readiness, calls on the Giver of Forms for a form that will govern it and act within it, preserving both the individual and the human kind. But a form that can guide human life and sustain the body's balanced condition must have a spiritual power: it must perceive, reason, think, and distinguish. So the generous source gives the soul and its true nature.[^V08-706][^V08-707]

The body's readiness asks only for a form that is present with it and acts in it as its form. But the generosity of the source gives a form that also has a separate nature, or a separate source. One thing can be a substance in one respect and a quality in another. For example, a form understood by the mind is a substance by its nature, but as it exists in the mind it depends on the mind and is a mental quality. A thing can also be caused in one respect but not in another: its existence is caused, while its nature is not. In the same way, the human soul can be immaterial in its nature, as an intellect, and material in how it acts by governing the body. Insofar as it acts on the body, its coming into being depends on the body's readiness, and it ceases when the body does. But in its original nature, or in the source of its nature, it does not depend on the body's readiness except indirectly, and it does not perish when the body perishes. The limits of material things touch it only indirectly.

That was our earlier answer, reached through rational argument. Here is the answer I now offer: the human soul has several levels of being. Some belong to the world of divine command and direction—“Say: The spirit is from the command of my Lord.” Others belong to the world of creation and shaping—“From it We created you, to it We return you, and from it We bring you out.” Coming into being and change apply only to some of the soul's levels.[^V08-708][^V08-709][^V08-710]

The soul develops and passes from one level of existence to another. The Qur'an points to this when it says, “We created you, then shaped you, then said to the angels, ‘Bow before Adam.’” When the soul rises from the world of creation to the world of command, its existence becomes an intellect separate from matter. It no longer needs the body, its conditions, or its readiness. The body's loss of readiness can affect the soul's connection to the body and its activity through it, but not the soul's own nature or continued existence.

The soul's existence when it first comes into being is not the same as its existence when it continues. The first depends on matter; the second is separate from it. The soul is therefore bodily in how it begins and spiritual in how it continues. A child needs the womb at first but not later. A captured animal needs the net to be caught, but not to remain alive after it has been caught. The body's decay, like the womb's or net's, does not prevent the child or captured animal from continuing to exist.

Also, a thing's need for something does not mean that everything belonging to it by its nature needs that same thing. A caused existence needs a cause, but a thing's nature need not. The existence of a triangle is caused, but its having three angles is not.

A preparatory cause is a cause only indirectly. Its role is not like that of a necessary cause, whose loss would bring about the effect's loss. The rule that something without a basis for the possibility of existence or nonexistence cannot begin after it was absent or end after it existed[^V08-711] does not mean that something without a basis for possible existence cannot exist again after it has already existed. Its earlier existence may be enough to support its later existence[^V08-712], if the later existence is a new and fuller stage of the earlier one.[^V08-713]

In a gradual change, such as a color growing deeper or a coal becoming hotter, the readiness for each weaker stage disappears, but the thing itself remains and grows stronger. What vanishes is its weakness and lack. So in a movement toward completion, the loss of a thing's readiness does not always end its existence. It may change into a stronger and more complete existence. In the same way, when the body loses its readiness for the soul, the soul's bodily mode ends, but that does not mean the soul becomes nothing.

If someone asks how the soul's separate existence begins when everything new must have matter and what is separate from matter has none, the answer is that the new thing is not the separate intellect itself. What begins is the soul's connection with that intellect and its transformation into it. That connection is preceded by readiness. While the soul is tied to the body, it holds the possibility of this connection. Once it unites with the intellect, it holds the actual connection. As we have said, what holds the possibility of something need not be what holds that thing's completed existence, though the two cannot be wholly separate.[^V08-714]

The philosophers who study divine things say that every natural kind has a built-in movement toward its own end, and that every incomplete thing has an inborn desire for completion. When a thing reaches its completion, it unites with it and becomes something new. The human being's natural movement toward holiness is clear to anyone who can see it. When the soul reaches the level of intellect, it becomes pure intellect and joins the Active Intellect. It then becomes the Active Intellect. What had been a soul capable of understanding, but not yet fully understanding, now leaves matter behind. Its unrealized capacities fall away, and it remains through God.[^V08-715]

In short, we can fully understand this only if we understand how the soul joins the Active Intellect and how all things return to the Highest Source. The stages of being follow one another and differ in strength, yet remain connected. The end of one level is the beginning of the next. The last step of existence tied to the body is the first step of existence separate from matter. The world that is wholly separate from matter contains no coming into being, change, or new states.
