The soul's entry into that world does not change it, just as the soul's coming from it did not change it. The soul enters that world in the same way it came from it, without any change taking place in that world. Think carefully about this and the meaning will become clear.

#### Chapter 7 — The rational soul comes from an intellect separate from matter

We have already shown that the cause of souls cannot be a body simply because it is a body. If it could, every body would be a cause of souls. Nor can the cause be a power that depends on a body. Such a power either needs the body in order to exist, or it exists without depending on the body but needs the body only to act. Both possibilities are impossible. So such a power cannot cause the soul to exist.

The first possibility is impossible for several reasons. First, a bodily form can act only in cooperation with what receives its action, and that receiver is the body. Then the body would be part of the cause that produces the soul, which is impossible, as we have already explained. Second, a bodily form acts through its position: position is part of the way a body exists. But a body cannot have a position in relation to something that has no position.[^V08-716][^V08-717]

Third, a cause must be fuller and stronger than its effect. A body depends on matter for its existence, while an immaterial being does not. So the cause of the soul cannot depend on a body for its own existence.

The second possibility is also impossible. If a power needs a body only in order to act, the body must serve as a tool between that power and what it affects. A body can serve as a tool only through its position and size. Its nearness to different things would vary, and so would its effects. To affect something, it would have to be near it to some degree. If nearness made no difference—if the power worked on nearby and distant things in exactly the same way—then the body would play no part in the action. A body's own existence and identity depend on its particular position. And if the action on a nearby thing happened before the action on a distant thing, then the action could be near or far. It would not be the action of an immaterial spirit. So anything that acts through a body has a position; and anything without a position cannot act through a body. Since the soul has no position, it cannot be caused by a body or through one. Its cause must be free of matter both in its own existence and in its action. It must be a sacred, separate being, whether a form or another soul. Philosophers call it the Active Intellect; ancient Persian sages called it rūwān-bakhsh, “giver of the spirit.”

It is called an intellect because it is a form separate from matter and understands itself. Every being separate from matter understands itself, and its knowledge of itself is its very existence. It is called “active” for three reasons. First, it gives existence to our souls and brings them from the ability to understand in principle to actual understanding. Second, it is fully active, without anything still only possible in it. It contains all intelligible things—or indeed all things—in intellectual form. Here, “active” also expresses the fullness of its activity.[^V08-718] Third,[^V08-719] it gives existence to this world and to the forms that enter matter. But it is not God, the Necessary Being, because it is the last of the separate intellects and contains some multiplicity. God is perfectly one; souls are many.

Someone may ask: why can one soul not cause another, as a parent's soul might cause a child's? We answer that a soul acts through its relation to a body. It cannot act on something that has no position in relation to it.

This is better than the argument Ibn Sina gives in *The Discussions*: souls belong to the same kind. If one soul caused another, either it would be one particular soul or several souls. If it were one particular soul, there would be no reason to choose it as the cause rather than the other soul. If it were an unspecified soul, it could not cause a particular soul, since a particular effect needs a particular cause. If several souls were the cause, there would be no reason to choose one number of souls rather than another. Then all the souls existing in the world would have to cause the new soul. But the smaller number of souls that existed earlier would already have been enough to cause it. If just one or a few of them were enough, the whole group would not be needed. Two separate causes that are each enough to produce the same effect cannot both be its cause. So the soul cannot be caused by all earlier souls together or by some of them rather than others.

This argument assumes that all souls remain the same kind. In our view, souls can become different kinds as their habits and character grow firm—angelic, demonic, animal-like, or predatory. They may be one kind at the beginning, before their habits and knowledge have developed. If a soul whose character has become fully actual causes another soul, Ibn Sina's argument does not apply.[^V08-720]

This completes the first part of the journey of the soul. The printed edition calls it the Fourth Journey. The handwritten copy used here, traced through other copies to the author's copy dated 1210 AH, calls it the Third Journey. That may fit better with the book's arrangement around the stages of the mystic's journey toward God. The editors worked hard to prepare and correct the text and to present it in a clear order. We thank God for enabling this work. We ask Him to help us publish the remaining volumes, and we thank the scholars who helped us print and correct this book. May God reward them. God is the giver of success and kindness. (Publisher)

## Notes

[^V08-1]: By “source” here, the author means the receptive source: the beginning of formation in the world of becoming, which comes with substantial motion and the natural renewal of things. In the case of the four elements, this means their ongoing change and their relation to pure receptivity—the first matter, which is pure potential and is drawn toward forms. The elements and natural forms move by their very nature. Since first matter is pure potential, it cannot by itself actively cause the soul or anything else to exist; if it could, it would no longer be pure potential. Nor can the elements, as bodies, be the active cause that brings something from nonexistence into existence. A giver must possess what it gives in a higher way than the recipient does. The cause must therefore be higher in existence than its effect. The elements are lower in existence than the soul, so they cannot be its active cause. The active cause of the soul and of all the perfections that come to first matter and its earliest forms is God’s pure providence, flowing down through every level.

[^V08-2]: The soul’s unity, which is identical with its existence, is a distinctive kind of unity: a comprehensive unity that gathers many levels of being. In one respect it is many within one; in another, one within many. In its own nature, the soul has a single, unified existence, including the forms that appear through it. Its branches and dependent powers are spread through it, as it were, by a process of descent. Taken in its full sense, the soul includes both this unified existence and its many branches. Its origin is one of its levels: prior to it in time, but later than it in essence. The levels that come earlier in time are one with those that come later in essence, though they differ in the direction of their development. So the soul is low in its height and high in its lowliness; it is pure even while embodied, and embodied even while immaterial. It is present in its branches without being inside them as one thing is inside another, and beyond them without being outside them as one thing is outside another. “Whoever knows himself knows his Lord.” Guard this insight from those who deny the mysteries of divine unity. The Imams have described it as a middle way between compulsion and complete freedom, between likeness and denial of likeness, and between negating God and comparing Him to created things.

[^V08-3]: “Possible” here means what can be brought into being by the Holy Emanation. Through the Most Holy Emanation, things are first measured out and given their timeless ordering. God’s providence is His knowledge of the best order before creation; that knowledge is active and brings what is known into existence. After His self-disclosure to Himself, God discloses Himself through His names and attributes and what follows from them, then through the individual things themselves. The first is His disclosure through the names and attributes, called the Most Holy Emanation; the second is His disclosure through the things that exist, called the Holy Emanation. The first is the mercy of His attributes, encompassing the intelligible meanings of things; the second is His active mercy, encompassing the existence of possible things.

[^V08-4]: If things were not brought into existence in this order, they would either all issue at once from the simple First Principle, which would mean that many things came from a source that is one in every respect, or they would issue in order with the least complete things first. The first option would make the First Principle composite; the second would make the lower things exist before the higher. Both are impossible. A cause must be most closely matched to its direct effect. If it were equally matched to several effects, there would be no reason for one rather than another to issue from it. The more complete thing is more closely matched to the cause than the less complete one. Also, the complete cause must contain in itself the full basis for its direct effect. If a lower effect came first and then caused the higher one, the First Cause would not fully account for the higher effect, and the cause would be less complete than its effect.

[^V08-5]: The reason is the one already given: if God’s providence stopped at a limit, His own being would be limited too. Far exalted is He above that.

[^V08-6]: “The first thing it received” follows from the earlier point that the outpouring cannot stop, or else the received thing would remain unchanged. The “rays” are an analogy for the giving of the generous First Principle through its intermediaries. The analogy is about how the effect spreads; the rays themselves are preparatory conditions, not active causes.

[^V08-7]: This gives an example of a sensible effect that even someone who relies only on the senses will recognize.

[^V08-8]: Matter is prepared for its kinds of perfection by its specific forms. Its specific perfections arise from those forms; its incidental readiness is an effect of its essential readiness. Matter is prepared by the forms it receives, not by itself, since as pure potential it is equally open to all perfections.

[^V08-9]: Strictly speaking, plants should not be called “living” in the sense of perceiving and acting: perception begins at least with touch, and action at least with movement by choice, neither of which plants have. It is true that existence itself is life flowing through all things, as the author insists elsewhere, though he criticizes Ibn Arabi for applying this idea to bodies in his discussion of the afterlife. But that broader sense does not fit this passage, where plants are distinguished from minerals and animals by the kind of life they have. Nor does it fit the phrase “the first thing matter receives,” which suggests that what came before plants lacked life in that sense.

[^V08-10]: A simple reality, such as necessary existence or existence in its absolute sense, has no source from which one could define it or prove it. One may still prove it through its effects or through the way it is affected, as one proves a reality from what it does. The same applies, even more clearly, to souls and forms. A form is another name for what makes a thing actual. A simple reality has no material or formal parts from which to define it or demonstrate it.

[^V08-11]: This follows the usual account. In the author’s own view, sensible things are perceived through the soul’s activity, and intelligible things through union with them and with the Active Intellect, as explained later.

[^V08-12]: One could say that the soul, as soul, is material and exists in the body, as the author himself holds. His point is that calling it a “form” does not show that it has another level of existence, while “perfection” does not imply that it must inhere in something. A king completes a city, for example, without being a form impressed in it. The author is also reporting the philosophers’ preferred wording.

[^V08-13]: The soul’s perfection was just compared to a kind, and a kind is closer to a general category because it can be predicated of its instances. Form, by contrast, is compared with matter, which is farther from a general category: matter is considered on its own as something distinct, while a general category is predicated of things. The point is that something whose comparison is closer to the general category is a better candidate for the broader term in a definition. One could turn this argument around: form may be closer to meaning, or even be the same as the general category, while kind and matter differ only in how they are considered. These are arguments about what seems most fitting, not decisive proofs.

[^V08-14]: Matter is included in the idea of form because form points to the matter it informs. The idea of perfection, by contrast, points to the kind it completes, and a kind may be bodily or separate from matter. Thus defining the soul through perfection can include material souls such as plant souls and souls that are separate in their own nature.

[^V08-15]: This objection applies only if the definition is meant for animal soul alone and “power” means the power of movement. It does not apply if the definition is for soul in general, whether plant or animal, and “power” is understood broadly.

[^V08-16]: The issue is that a cause may be favored over another without any reason. One might reply that “power” means actuality in a broad sense and applies to all souls. The author’s point, however, is that definitions are about realities, not just words, and the soul in general has no existence apart from its particular instances.

[^V08-17]: “Perfection” can be used with the same meaning for both powers: it can mean the completion provided by the power of movement or the completion provided by both powers, as in the animal soul. “Power,” by contrast, is used in two different senses. If both are used in a definition, the word is equivocal; if one is chosen, there is no reason to prefer one over the other, and the definition leaves out part of the soul. It also fails to describe the soul as a whole, since the animal soul includes both powers. The point is that one and the same word, “perfection,” can apply to both without changing its meaning.

[^V08-18]: This objection has force. “Perfection” means that the soul completes a kind, as explained earlier. Although in reality the soul can also be called a perfection in another sense—because its two powers are one in being—this does not follow from the word itself.

[^V08-19]: “Limited” here may mean limited by one of the soul’s levels and by the body, or limited by the two together, or limited by the relation described in the definition.

[^V08-20]: The definition points to the soul and is measured by it; it does not mean that existence itself has definitions, as if definitions belonged to existence rather than to what a thing is.

[^V08-21]: The active cause here is the First Cause, God, and the active intellects. Things that depend on causes can be known only through their causes.

[^V08-22]: The question is what the soul is. People have held many views. Some, following Galen and his school, say that the soul is the body’s temperament or mixture. As long as the body keeps the temperament suitable to its kind, it is protected from corruption; once it loses that balance, the body dies. Others, including the theologian Imām al-Ḥaramayn, say that the soul is a fine, luminous body flowing through the body as water flows through a flower or fire through a coal. They say that this subtle body is addressed, rewarded, and punished, and that the visible body is kept together by it; when it leaves, the visible body quickly falls apart. Most Muʿtazilites and some Ashʿarites say that the soul is the visible body itself. Naẓẓām says it is an indivisible part located in the heart. In short, theologians have held nearly forty views, none supported by decisive proof. The most familiar is that the soul consists of original bodily parts that remain from the beginning to the end of life. It is astonishing that anyone who claims to follow reason would accept such accounts. The truth is what careful philosophers and mystics who have direct insight have taught: the soul is an immaterial substance that governs and preserves the body. Proofs will follow. A person can also test this by reflecting on the elements—earth, water, fire, and air—from which every bodily being is formed. Imagine the human being returned to those elements and set beside the elements outside the body. Could reason attribute the knowledge, wisdom, and power that make a fully developed human being God’s representative to earth, water, fire, air, or their mixture? No. Search within the earth and you will find a precious substance: a divine command and a sacred mystery. The author will later report the ancient philosophers’ views from Avicenna’s *The Healing* and interpret them.

[^V08-23]: The point is that it is false to say the soul’s true nature cannot be known from this definition. From the beginning, the soul is this relation to the body, and this relation is one level of its own being. The claim that it is a separate substance in its essence but related to the body only in its activity is wrong from the outset. If it existed before the body, that would be false; if it began to exist later, it would have no subject in which to begin, as will be explained.

[^V08-24]: A definition “by name alone” does not include a real nature. For example, the descriptions “black” and similar derived words do not correspond to one further thing in the world. “Black” refers to a body, which is a substance, and blackness, which is a quality; there is no third entity corresponding to the word. A definition of this kind does not include a distinct nature.

[^V08-25]: The exception is not quite of the same kind. The soul’s movement from bodily nature toward the Active Intellect is a strengthening of existence, one level after another. The soul that reaches the level of intellect preserves its earlier level as soul.

[^V08-26]: Most philosophers hold that the soul’s connection with the body follows from its coming into existence, rather than belonging to its nature. They also do not think it impossible for an immaterial and a material thing to form one kind. So neither premise in the two arguments is accepted by them. Both premises must be established for the arguments to work.

[^V08-27]: One might object that the proof applies only if the soul’s nature necessarily requires a body, while the soul might be eternal or might have begun before the body. The author replies that the point is the conflict between saying the soul is a separate intellectual substance and saying it is connected to matter. There is also a problem if the soul is new: what subject could have received it? The argument does not depend on the word “then” meaning a later moment in time. In any case, the soul’s eternity has already been disproved. If someone says that a soul that comes into being must also perish, we answer that the soul’s bodily coming into being does not prevent its continued existence as a spirit.

[^V08-28]: There are two kinds of relation. In one, the relation is part of what a thing is, or its nature is nothing but that relation; this is a category of relation. In the other, a thing’s nature belongs to another category, but its existence involves a relation. Matter, for example, is a substance by nature, though its existence involves a relation; blackness is a quality by nature, though its existence is entirely dependent on a subject. God has no created nature or category, yet descriptions such as creator and powerful apply to Him. The same distinction appears in things that flow or change. Motion and time are flowing by their very nature, while qualities, quantities, place, position, and natural forms may be stable in what they are but flowing in their existence. In one way or another, the physical world, with all its substances and accidents, is changing and passing away.

[^V08-29]: The author uses this as a proof that existence is fundamental. If existence were only an idea drawn from things, blackness would be nothing but its particular nature. But blackness exists only as the existence of a subject; it is therefore a relation in its existence, even if it is a quality by nature.

[^V08-30]: That is, separate from the body which the soul governs and completes.

[^V08-31]: Earlier statements by the author point to this: the soul’s relation to the body follows from its relation of governing and acting on bodies; he also says that the soul is the body’s completion.

[^V08-32]: A kind counts as an actual kind when it has the active source of these actions; it does not have to be carrying them all out. Having the power and source of the actions is enough.

[^V08-33]: At first this may seem to conflict with the author’s claim elsewhere that we use a description such as “knower” only when the thing actually has knowledge. One way to resolve the issue is to say that an “actual geometer” is someone who actually has the capacity to measure, and an “actual doctor” someone who actually has the capacity to treat. We do not call someone “treating” while they only have the capacity to treat. There is a difference between being actually an animal or a geometer, and actually sensing, acting, or measuring.

[^V08-34]: The soul is the first perfection of a body taken with an additional condition—that it be a plant, animal, or human being. But for body considered simply as body, without that condition, the soul is a later perfection, perhaps even a third one after elemental and mineral forms. If body is taken as something separate from all these forms, the soul is not its perfection at all.

[^V08-35]: The soul is not the first perfection of an animal considered without qualification, and may not be any perfection of it if the animal is taken as a complete kind already constituted. The soul is a first perfection of an animal only when it is considered with the condition that it can receive the soul. The same applies to the soul in relation to body generally. The author later says that the soul is the first perfection of body considered without qualification; he means body without a restriction that would exclude its becoming a plant, animal, or human being. The soul is not a perfection of body taken as something complete and separate from all its forms. The author’s wording refers to both points.

[^V08-36]: “Without any condition” means body as a general kind, not body considered as something already complete on its own.

[^V08-37]: Material body means body taken as something complete and separate from whatever is joined to it. What is joined to this kind of body cannot be its first perfection. Generic body, by contrast, is taken without excluding what is joined to it; it can be united with that further feature and made complete by it. That is how the soul is a first perfection of body as a general kind.

[^V08-38]: “Natural body” can mean a body that is not a mathematical abstraction, a body that is not an imaginal or intermediate-world body, or a body that is not made by craft. The last meaning is intended here.

[^V08-39]: The phrase “natural body” excludes forms such as the forms of a bed or other made objects. The author also wants to exclude the way a pilot’s soul completes a ship or a king’s soul completes a city. Such a soul is not the soul of the ship or city, because a thing’s first perfection—its true form and distinguishing feature—must be one with it. Even if one grants that a pilot’s soul completes a ship or a king’s soul completes a city, it is a perfection of an artificial body, not a natural one. If the entire ship or city together with the pilot or king were treated as one thing, that whole would not exist as one thing in the world. Existence and unity go together.

[^V08-40]: “Tools” may be read as an adjective describing the natural body, or as a description of the soul, which acts through tools. Either reading is possible; the first is preferable. The phrase “not every natural body” qualifies “natural body,” not “perfection.”

[^V08-41]: “Immaterial” is not being used here in its strict technical sense of “not existing in matter,” which would exclude plant and animal souls. It means that the soul is higher in its own level of existence than matter: its existence is not identical with the matter it informs.

[^V08-42]: The intellect and soul, simply as intellect or soul, do not directly move the heavens. Yet the motion is still governed by the soul, because nature is subject to it. Otherwise, all motion would be called natural, since nature is the immediate agent in every case. Aristotle therefore calls the motions of the heavenly bodies natural.

[^V08-43]: The heavenly powers are distinct from the four elements, which are subject to coming-to-be and passing away.

[^V08-44]: The theoretical and practical intellects in their potential or material stage.

[^V08-45]: There is no potential intellect in the heavens, since all the perfections of a heavenly body are already actual except for its positions.

[^V08-46]: The intellect that forms part of the definition of “rational”—as in “a rational being is a soul with intellect”—does not mean a fully actual intellect. It means the shared capacity that includes both intellect in potential and intellect in act. Otherwise, a human being would not be rational while the intellect remained only potential.

[^V08-47]: These are images received from things that already have images. They affect an organ and the vital spirit, as when the two eyes or the vital spirit are affected at the meeting point of vision. This kind of reception does not occur in the heavens. The images there are not received from human beings, horses, cattle, and other things; they are the source and foundation of such images. They are like causal forms that precede what they cause, as God’s knowledge precedes what is known. There is no affected organ there, since the heavens have no eyes, ears, or anything like them. The forms flow to the heavenly spheres from within.

[^V08-48]: This responds to Avicenna’s claim that this kind of perception does not occur in the heavens. Intellectual perception—the highest kind of knowledge through mental forms—is enough to call the heavens living, even if they do not sense or do not have forms impressed in them. If Avicenna means that sensation cannot occur there because no organ passively receives an image, the reply is the next argument. The author does not explicitly answer Avicenna’s claim that the heavenly intellect is actual while an animal’s is only potential. A better answer is that “living” means a being that can perceive and act; it does not require perception to be fully actual or passively received. One might ask why “power” can have two meanings here, when the soul’s definition was not allowed to use a word with two unrelated meanings. The reason is that “power” was equivocal between two categories: acting and being acted upon. These categories are different kinds of possible things, with no shared essence. Perception, by contrast, is grounded in existence. Knowledge, life, will, and power follow existence and are identical with it in their reality, each according to its own level. Perception is therefore one shared idea, even though one kind of perception is active and another receptive.

[^V08-49]: The author’s point is that the concept of a body’s being able to act does not rule out its being preceded by another state. It differs from “perfection” in the definition above, which cannot be preceded by another perfection. These concepts have opposite meanings, so the aspects they express cannot be identical in the soul.

[^V08-50]: “A ship’s being useful” means its being in a condition where it can perform its function. The concept does not exclude the possibility that an earlier condition was needed; it does not mean that such a condition must have been needed.

[^V08-51]: At first, the sentence might seem to mean that, just as a body’s being able to burn and its having heat are one thing, a body’s being able to carry out the actions of life and its soul are also one thing, though the soul came first. That is not the point. The author means that the soul and the body’s ability to perform the actions of life are not one thing. As the ship’s usefulness may be preceded in time, the body’s ability to perform the actions of life is preceded by the soul. The body’s ability to burn and the heat in it, by contrast, are the same thing: its ability to burn is simply its having heat.

[^V08-52]: Avicenna says in *The Healing* that a body’s being able to perform an action or have a quality can happen in two ways. In the first, some other thing in the body produces that action or quality. For example, the ship’s being useful depends on the pilot. One must prove that the pilot and the ship’s usefulness are distinct. In the second, nothing other than that state exists in the body: for someone who identifies heat with the body’s being able to burn, the heat in the body is exactly that capacity. At first glance, the soul might seem to be like heat in this second sense, but that cannot be right. The soul is not the same as a body’s being able to produce the acts of life.

[^V08-53]: “Rather” marks a stronger claim: we establish that life must be preceded by another condition, not merely that it might be.

[^V08-54]: The author is not repeating his earlier point that life itself is the soul, where life was defined as a body’s ability to carry out the actions of life. The life that makes the body complete and actual, and that can be identical with the soul, is a later state in the body’s development. It follows the bare state of being a body.

[^V08-55]: “Second” means second in the order of the discussion, not second in rank.

[^V08-56]: This is the second kind of case: something that is not a body may have an existence that is the same as its being alive. This applies to most nonbodily beings, including the First Being, the intellects, and souls.

[^V08-57]: The issue is not whether this part exists in something that is not a subject. Simply existing in something other than a subject does not make a thing a substance; blackness, for instance, exists in a body, and the body itself does not exist in a subject. The clue is the author’s reply that something may be a part of a whole.

[^V08-58]: For example, blackness exists in a compound whose nature depends on that part. The unstated premise is that anything inhering in a subject depends on that subject.

[^V08-59]: “Essential” and “accidental” are relative terms. Animal is essential to human being but accidental to “rational,” which is a distinguishing feature of animal. In the same way, the general kind is a general feature in relation to the distinguishing feature, while the distinguishing feature is a specific feature in relation to the general kind.

[^V08-60]: Not every substantial feature is a substance—color, for example, is substantial to blackness—and not every accidental feature is an accident in the technical sense. “Human” is accidental to “rational,” but is neither identical with it nor one of its parts.

[^V08-61]: The answer follows from the earlier point that a part can exist within a whole without being in a subject, as blackness exists in the whole made up of blackness and its subject. If something is not an accident in relation to a particular thing, it need not be a substance in relation to it; it may be neither. Zayd, for example, is neither one nor many in the sky, though he is one in himself and in the place he occupies.

[^V08-62]: “Accident” in the classification of kinds—such as general or specific accident, essential or nonessential feature—means a feature that can be predicated of something, such as “walking” or “laughing.” “Accident” in the ten categories means something that exists in a subject that does not depend on it, such as walking or laughter. The soul might exist in a subject and so be an accident in this technical sense, while also being a part of a compound, as already explained. Or the souls in question may be different in nature; perhaps the reference is to plant souls and animal souls that are impressed in matter.

[^V08-63]: The “feature” here is like what logicians call a describing predicate. It may be identical with its subject, be part of it, or be something outside it. It does not mean an accidental quality that exists in something else. Life is essential to body because both exist through one and the same existence; being alive does not require adding another feature to an already complete body, as a white body requires adding whiteness. Here “body” means body considered together with life—in other words, animal. “Completes” means that it makes the thing actual. The proof is this: we observe life’s effects in animals. They cannot come from bodyhood shared by all bodies, or every body would produce them. They cannot come from first matter, which is purely receptive and cannot act. Life must therefore be essential to the body in the sense of body that includes life. Its source is a form that completes the matter from which the general kind is made. A form that completes matter is a substance, so the soul is a substance. The argument depends on life being essential to the animal body; if life were accidental, its source would also be an accident, as blackness is the source of being black. Some people thought life was accidental because they saw that bodies decay, and failed to distinguish body considered as complete and separate from further forms from body considered as open to them. The latter is alive in itself. An animal is a kind of body, and “rational” is true of some bodies as well; predication means that the subject and predicate are one in existence. The view that every body is lifeless also conflicts with the principle that a simple thing contains the perfections of what lies beneath it. The author’s account is consistent with substantial motion: all the stages from beginning to end are one continuous existence. This may also help answer those who accept only the spiritual afterlife, provided they still affirm a body—though the author considers that view incomplete. The body required for the afterlife is a formed body, and the afterlife is a realm of living beings.

[^V08-64]: A universal considered with “universality” added to it is only an idea; it does not exist in the world. “A hidden individual” here does not mean that a universal may exist as a universal. It means that the term “universal” can be applied to something that is not itself qualified by universality, or to an individual that falls under it.

[^V08-65]: The proof establishes that the soul is a substance in the sense that it completes a substantial kind—just as first matter, bodily form, and specific forms are called substances in that sense. It does not show that the soul itself belongs to the category of substance as a fully defined kind. A substantial form and the distinguishing features drawn from it are not themselves kinds within the category of substance; they are called substances because they complete a substantial kind.

[^V08-66]: The apparent circle is resolved by saying that the soul’s coming into being depends on temperament, while the temperament’s continued existence depends on the soul.

[^V08-67]: The author’s considered view is that the elements do not retain their pure forms. In truth, they do not remain pure, though they remain in an intermediate way: broken-down earth is still earth, and a broken-down quality remains that quality in a changed form. The earth in a human body is one stage of earth, though not pure earth; if it were pure, its qualities would also be pure. Different effects follow from different sources. This is clear if substantial forms can grow stronger or weaker and change while preserving something of their original nature.

[^V08-68]: The first possibility—matter made of several elements—refers to the view that elemental forms remain. The second—simple matter—refers to the view that they do not.

[^V08-69]: The question is whether the celestial sphere could force the elements to stay together. In that case, their mixture would result from a period of interaction under force. The point is that elements trapped beneath the earth are held by the narrowness of a passage, as in an earthquake; that is not what happens in semen. Nor are the fiery and airy particles merely so scarce that they take a long time to separate. Oil stirred into water is another case: while the particles move apart, they remain mixed for a time. The source’s argument interweaves these examples rather than presenting them in sequence.

[^V08-70]: Small size cannot explain why the elements stay mixed when the amount of fluid around them is small. It could matter if fire and air were submerged in a large volume of fluid. But semen is not like that. If tiny particles were enough to keep it mixed, it should remain thick when the womb does not draw it in. Since it thins, the large volume of surrounding fluid must be what is missing.

[^V08-71]: The Arabic word here means “thickness,” the opposite of thinness.

[^V08-72]: The objection grants that the fluid is not large in absolute terms, but says that it is large relative to the small amount of fire and air, and so could still hold them in place. The reply is that, if this were enough, the mixture should stay together after leaving the womb, when that relative difference remains.

[^V08-73]: This note explains the claim that the fiery and airy parts should remain after semen leaves the womb.

[^V08-74]: The elements are arranged in space according to their natural affinities. Air leaves the gaps in the earth because they do not suit it; earth then dries the water that suits it, since the water must leave to make room. The author has discussed a similar example earlier.

[^V08-75]: The objection treats fire’s remaining attached to wood as an example of how fire might stay with the other elements and form a mixture. But the fire in wood does not persist as one thing. It is continuously renewed, as one can see in a flame. It therefore cannot remain as a single fire long enough to create a stable temperament. Mixture requires both a change in quality and a movement in place among the parts.

[^V08-76]: The answer uses the dual form because it joins the second objection to the first. The parents may gather the original material, but the child’s soul—or its earliest level, natural power—joins the material added later and preserves the union. These actions and all that follows belong to the child’s own soul, not to something external. Incomplete and complete compounds, plants, and animals alike act through powers and natures within them. Their principles are even more complete and capable. Yet all this remains under God’s power: the separate principles are levels of His power, and their activity is His activity.

[^V08-77]: The soul’s relation to the body is not entirely cut off as long as an effect of the body remains. This may explain the practice of visiting graves and fits the author’s view. In life, the body’s natural power is a level of the soul, a ray or sign of it, like warmth and redness arising in coal from a great fire. In death, it is like heat remaining in walls after sunset. In the analogy, take the fire and sun to include their light and warmth as manifestations of them, not as things separate from them.

[^V08-78]: Some manuscripts read “and” here, which is better, though “or” can be read as denying that both elements separate at once.

[^V08-79]: This objection does not fit the view it targets. The problem was raised against those who say the soul comes into being after the body is prepared. They adopt that view because of a report that spirits were created two thousand years before bodies. They took “year” to mean measurable time, though it refers to a divine year in the higher realm. The same problem applies to other scriptural reports about the covenant. There are further difficulties: this view would make God’s creative activity stop, and it would make the soul begin at a particular time without a cause that explains why then. These problems do not arise if the soul comes into being with the body.

[^V08-80]: This reply would mean that a natural agent hands over its work of governing the body to another. Such a transfer is possible for a craftsperson, but not for a natural agent, as will be explained.

[^V08-81]: This reply is also weak. That soul has no natural power over things unrelated to it, just as Zayd’s soul cannot use the powers present in ʿAmr’s body.

[^V08-82]: The qualification is needed because transferring governance would also be unintelligible even for an agent whose will is fixed and eternal.

[^V08-83]: The comparison with fire suits the author’s account of the soul as having levels, beginning with natural power and ending, as God wills, at the highest level. But it does not fit the earlier claims that the matter becomes ready to receive a more complete soul, or that substantial motion is denied. I say that the commentator’s phrase “one unfolding process” points to substantial motion: the earlier and later stages are not separate things, one merely deficient and the other complete, but are like dim and bright light. The author’s denial of substantial motion should be understood as affirming that the divine aspect within every thing is stable. His use of “substance” for a thing’s inner reality is not unusual. Since his writings on Avicenna’s *Pointers and Reminders* and elsewhere clearly affirm the primacy of existence, it would also have been apt to speak of this process as existence itself developing, both in the soul and in the greater world.

[^V08-84]: A careful reader will see that the passage quoted from Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Tusi plainly affirms that substance grows stronger. It cannot be read in any of the three ways the author lists.

[^V08-85]: The second option means that the earlier forms remain but hand over the work of governing to a later form, then become inactive. The third means that the earlier form is destroyed and another form comes into being to act for itself. There is also a fourth possibility: the earlier form remains when the later one arrives, does not hand over its actions, and each effect comes from the source suited to it. Until this option too is ruled out, the conclusion does not follow that all these effects come from one source with different levels of strength. The commentator leaves it aside because it is clearly false. If forms develop by degrees from complete to more complete, each later form must do what the earlier one could do, and more. This also follows from the principles that one matter must have one form, one kind one distinguishing feature, and one effect one cause.

[^V08-86]: In the first option, the power is said to destroy its matter; in the second, it is said to make the matter ready for something that will destroy it. “Or” here introduces a stronger correction: the power prepares its own matter to receive what destroys the power itself. One might object that the questioner did not say what destroys the earlier form; perhaps outside events do so, as one material form replaces another. The answer is that we are discussing natural development and completion, not destruction by outside or inside causes. A form in matter exists to constitute and complete that matter, and in a compound to preserve its mixture and structure. It cannot exist to destroy the matter or prepare it to receive what will destroy the form. Avicenna distinguishes two kinds of change. In one, the earlier thing naturally moves toward a more complete state: a child becoming an adult does not lose a substantial part of its nature, but completes it. In the other, the earlier thing’s nature does not tend toward the later one, as when water becomes air. In that case, the first nature does not remain in the second; it is destroyed. Here, the stages of one soul are like the first kind, not the second. If each stage were created and destroyed in an instant, there would be infinitely many separate acts of coming-to-be and passing away between fixed endpoints, and instants would follow one another without any duration. The same issue arises whenever one soul seems to change into another, as from plant to animal, or between different levels within plant or animal life. Nature does not leap over stages. Nor is this a case of forced change: when a plant is cut or an animal burned or drowned, the nearby matter is destroyed. In natural development, the same matter remains. If all upward development were forced, it would have to be forced either always or in most cases, which is impossible. So the mover that restores the body to health is not its temperament. It is like the natural power that restores an ordinary state after an unnatural, forced one has been removed; it is neither of the two states themselves.

[^V08-87]: The alternatives exclude one another: if the power acts through the shared bodily nature, it also acts through the body’s position and contact.

[^V08-88]: This reading also shows why the second alternative is false. It cannot mean that one animal, separate from its body, moves another by means of bodyhood shared by all bodies. It must mean that the animal’s own nature acts through its body. Consider the difference.

[^V08-89]: The text probably means “the mover,” not “the thing moved,” in both places. The body is what is moved.

[^V08-90]: The reference to imagination shows that the animal soul is immaterial through its imaginative power, not necessarily through touch alone. Some animals have touch but lack this kind of immateriality. Earlier, the author rejected an argument that infers substantiality from immateriality; there he meant intellectual immateriality. Here he argues from imaginal, or intermediate, immateriality. This proof still does not cover every soul, such as a plant soul.

[^V08-91]: The comparison assumes that the whole and each part have the same shape. “Impossible” is a predicate with an implied subject.

[^V08-92]: The sphere’s nature is one and has no perception or choice. If it changes the shape, each part should be spherical too, which is not the case.

[^V08-93]: Strictly speaking, there are no separate parts here: the sphere is one continuous whole, and continuity goes with its being one individual. Once the whole matter has one shape, a part cannot have another. One could make the same argument for temperament: once the whole is shaped by a temperament, the same temperament cannot shape its part differently. But elemental forms remain in generated bodies, so their parts are not truly continuous; the elements’ particles are small and touch one another. The argument therefore concerns the nearby matter, such as nerve or bone, which is one whole. If it concerned more distant matter, different parts of the matter could have different shapes.

[^V08-94]: Most premises in these proofs are taken, for the sake of argument, from natural science or medicine. Their proper explanation belongs to those fields.

[^V08-95]: The point is that the soul is not temperament. “Power” is used broadly enough to include active accidents such as heat, which begins a change. The expression “temperamental power” means a general power considered through its particular forms, like calling a form a “substantial form” or matter a “material form.” The comparison with mineral forms is a comparison with an opposite case, which supports the point.

[^V08-96]: This is the principle Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Tusi states in *The Abstract*: “What does not change is not what changes.” Al-Qūshjī objects that this would prove the animal soul immaterial too, so the argument would prove too much. But the conclusion is meant to be general. The bodily resurrection depends on the animal soul’s being immaterial through its imaginative power; without that, the resurrection could not be proved.

[^V08-97]: “Exists” here means exists in full actuality. Prime matter is not in full actuality; it is only the actuality of being potential.

[^V08-98]: One might say that the argument proves only that self-awareness differs from awareness of the organs. The reply is that knowledge and what is known are identical in their reality, as has already been established.

[^V08-99]: One might think the argument says: if self-awareness were acquired through sense, then it would be acquired through sense—which is just the denial of the claim. But the statement that an animal’s awareness of itself is constant means that it is not acquired through sense or any other means, since what is acquired is not constant. “Not acquired through sense” names one specific case after the general claim.

[^V08-100]: As in the thought experiment of the animal suspended in open air, described below.

[^V08-101]: One might object that this argument concerns the human soul, not the animal soul. The reply is that if human beings know themselves in this way, animals do so all the more. Most people are not much different from animals in this respect.

[^V08-102]: It is not far-fetched that the imagined condition could occur, especially for animals other than human beings.

[^V08-103]: The claim is that these powers are substances only, with no trace of being accidents.

[^V08-104]: If “separate” means wholly free from matter, the claim is correct, but the animal soul is not separate to that degree. If it means separate in some respect, the claim is false.

[^V08-105]: One might ask whether a separate substance can perceive its own particular identity. It can, through direct presence. Universality and particularity are features of representational knowledge; the impossibility concerns perceiving a changing particular through changing bodily tools, not perceiving a separate particular.

[^V08-106]: If the claim is that every separate being—even one separate only in imagination—cannot perceive particular things or act on them, that is false. If the claim concerns an intellect wholly separate from matter, it may be right. But that would show only that the animal soul is not wholly separate in the intellectual sense, not that it is not separate in any sense. The argument requires the latter distinction.

[^V08-107]: The soul would be an incomplete cause of the whole. As part of the whole, it would be a substance; because the body exists without it in the general sense of body, it would be an accident in relation to that subject.

[^V08-108]: Their reason is the circularity they will now describe: an accident depends on its subject, so if the subject also depended on the accident, each would depend on the other. They therefore say that a subject is independent of what exists in it. This is why they call specific forms accidents. But they do not accept prime matter, so this rule would not make bodily form an accident in the same way.

[^V08-109]: The second view is false for the reasons already given about substances and accidents. A state depends on its subject to be an individual, but the subject need not depend on that state in the same way. The subject may depend on it for its existence as a body, or for its specific kind; neither relation makes the dependence circular. An accident, by contrast, depends on a subject that is independent of it in both existence and kind. The first view is also false: if the compound forms a genuine unity, one existence cannot be both a substance and an accident. If it does not form a genuine unity, then the soul is not truly a part of it, as a stone placed beside a person is not part of that person.

[^V08-110]: A feature belongs essentially to a thing only when the thing is understood in its full nature, not when it is merely described by something true of it in a general way. A definition, for example, gives the full nature, while an incomplete description gives only part of it.

[^V08-111]: More precisely, one could say, “I find only an existence that perceives itself,” since the exception in the sentence is open-ended. One may also read it as “an existence that perceives itself.” The point is that the soul, like its Creator, has a simple level and a unified level. At the simple level, it is considered just as itself, without considering all the qualities that belong to it. At the unified level, it is considered together with its names and qualities: life, thought, imagination, perception, power, and immateriality.

[^V08-112]: This is not the preparatory temperament that existed before the soul and made the matter ready to receive it. It is a temperament that proceeds from the soul as a shadow from its source, and that the soul brings about. The soul is prior to this temperament in its own right, as a higher level is prior to a lower level with which it is joined in one existence.

[^V08-113]: “Subject” here means anything in which another thing exists, not only a subject that is already independent of what exists in it. The point is that plant and animal souls constitute their immediate subjects. Whatever constitutes a subject in this way is a substance.

[^V08-114]: This is the right account. The soul’s relation to the body is not completely cut off; it remains as long as an effect of the body remains. This explains the point of visiting graves. The body’s natural power during life is a level of the soul, a ray or early sign of it, like heat and redness arising in coal from a great fire. At death, something like the fire’s heat remains in a wall after sunset. The bones that are most thoroughly reduced to their remains may have the strongest capacity for renewed life, contrary to what ordinary people and those who ask how God can revive decayed bones suppose.

[^V08-115]: This objection works only if the temperament associated with the plant soul is the very same one associated with the animal soul. If they differ, each soul constitutes its own matter. The author’s earlier statement that each of these souls has matter with its own particular temperament and structure points to this.

[^V08-116]: The particular temperament that the animal’s matter requires is preserved by the animal soul, as already explained. How, then, could the soul arrive only after the matter had already been fully constituted by another substantial form, as though it were an accident added to a complete subject? Think this through.

[^V08-117]: A general kind taken without further qualification produces an effect only when joined to a distinguishing feature. Philosophers sometimes attribute a general effect to a general cause, but without such a feature the general kind has no independent existence and cannot produce an effect. There is no separate instance of “animal” apart from its kinds. The universal is present only through the particular kinds in which it is realized. This is why the source of the effect is the distinguishing feature. One may object that we can imagine “animal” without adding a distinguishing feature. The reply is that this imagined “animal” is a kind of mental matter, not the general kind as it exists in a real thing. What is vague in existence is not the same as what is vague in thought.

[^V08-118]: The point is that if the plant power were not a service of the animal power, it would have to operate through an artificial arrangement of parts, which is false. If it were a service of the animal power, it would be used before the power that uses it existed. According to later philosophers, the animal soul exists first and uses the plant power. In the author’s account of substantial motion, the power of nourishment, growth, and reproduction is an early level or sign of the animal or human soul, the enduring source from which the powers arise. At the beginning, its only actions are nourishment and related functions.

[^V08-119]: A simple reality contains all the perfections of the things below it in a higher, more unified way. The powers are the soul itself as its aspects, expressions, and places where its light appears: many powers within one soul, and one soul present in many powers. The soul sees the detailed powers within its unity, and the powers reveal the soul’s unity within their detail. The cause contains a complete account of its effect; the effect contains only an incomplete account of its cause. So the soul is its powers, and its powers are the soul.

[^V08-120]: “Perceptive actions” are clear. “Animal actions” are those of desire and anger. “Plant actions” include digestion, nourishment, and growth. “Natural actions” include preserving the temperament, shaping the matter, and joining its parts.

[^V08-121]: The soul’s different kinds make things different in their essential nature. One might object that each individual person—Zayd, ʿAmr, Khālid, and the rest—would then be a separate kind, though this is usually said only of intellects. That is indeed true at the inner level, as will be explained later: through their acquired habits, individual people become distinct kinds.

[^V08-122]: The word “powers” is often used for actual powers and active sources, as when Aristotle calls God the “divine power.” It may also be used because, relative to the earthly soul, the soul is a general kind with two kinds beneath it: heavenly and earthly. The earthly soul is itself a general kind, with human, animal, and plant kinds beneath it. Each of these can be divided further: animal souls include the souls of horses, donkeys, and other animals, and plant souls are divided in the same way. Inwardly, the human soul too is a general kind with distinct kinds beneath it; outwardly, it is the final, indivisible species, because its definition is one. Avicenna may be using “kinds” in its technical sense, or simply to mean classes in the ordinary sense.

[^V08-123]: That is, the body grows from food. This matches the usual definition, which says “insofar as it is nourished.” It also makes the definition complete by pointing to the three powers of nourishment, growth, and reproduction. It need not mention the formative power, since in the deepest account all well-ordered actions come from the wise Maker, exalted is He.

[^V08-124]: The powers branching from the soul, and the organs assigned to them, are described here as earlier philosophers described them. This differs from what anatomists and biologists say today. The author’s account classifies and counts the powers by the kinds of activity attributed to the soul, which fits the psychological discussion that follows.

[^V08-125]: Read “then” rather than “and,” and read the verb in the passive. The shaping and actual likeness occur in the womb. The reproductive power prepares each part of the material to receive its own particular form, as explained earlier.

[^V08-126]: The motive power is what sets desire for an end in motion. There are several ways to understand Avicenna’s claim that it is the final cause. First, in reality it may be identical with the final cause: the end is what desire seeks, and the seeker and what is sought belong to one kind. Light seeks light, fire seeks fire, and good things seek what is good for them. This is why one says that love begins in the substantial likeness between lover and beloved. In truth, seeker and sought are one; lover and beloved come from one source, as in the saying: “I am the one I love, and the one I love is me.” Second, the motive power may be identical with the final cause in thought. The end comes first in thought but last in the action itself. Voluntary actions are preceded by imagining and judging the end. The imagined end is the object perceived, and the perceiving power is also the power that moves, since all the powers are aspects of one soul. Though Avicenna did not affirm the union of knower and known except in *The Origin and Return*, he allows a meaningful connection between the powers and between the powers and what they perceive. Third, the claim may mean that the motive power’s own fulfillment, as it achieves what it desires, is the true final cause of the soul’s actions—not merely the apparent ends of particular crafts, such as a king sitting on a throne as the carpenter’s goal, or Zayd taking shelter in a house as the builder’s goal.

[^V08-127]: This count treats touch as four senses.

[^V08-128]: This assumes that touch is a general kind containing four powers and is one only in a qualified sense. Avicenna’s correct view is that the five senses are counted according to the more familiar view that touch is one power in itself.

[^V08-129]: Touch perceives more strongly in the parts close to the skin and less strongly near the bone. That is why the author says in *The Origin and Return* that it is found chiefly in the flesh. He specifies opposition between the perceiver and what is perceived because, when they have the same quality, there is no effect unless the quality in what is perceived is stronger. “Or changing its structure” refers to a disruption in the arrangement of parts. The wording “some people seem to think” is grammatically a qualified denial: the power is not another distinct kind like sight, but a general kind. The author examines this view later in this journey.

[^V08-130]: The power is called “imaginative” because it is the one that combines and works with images. Avicenna has just said that it both perceives and works with them, though attributing perception to it is unusual and does not seem right. By the philosophers’ own rules, what we perceive is either a meaning or an image; a meaning may be particular or universal. There are therefore three perceiving powers, because a relation and its object must match in number: common sense perceives images, and imagination stores them; estimation perceives particular meanings, and memory stores them; intellect perceives universal meanings, and the Active Intellect is their storehouse. The power that works with them all is the one that combines and separates.

[^V08-131]: Avicenna does not give imagination a separate location, since one can infer that its storehouse is located where the power that owns it is located.

[^V08-132]: “Power” here includes both an active power and a receptive one, as explained earlier in the discussion of potentiality and actuality. “Source” also includes both an active cause and a receptive cause. The first example—the compound of matter and form—belongs especially to this second kind. It also covers the body’s matter and bodily form, which differ in meaning as potential and actual. The source can even include existence and nonexistence, since philosophers commonly treat nonexistence as a kind of source. So the second example, which distinguishes actions by their presence or absence, also makes sense. The author lists possible reasons for different sources and powers in general, then asks which ones account specifically for the different powers of the soul. In discussions of the soul, “power” means an active principle of change.

[^V08-133]: Since knowledge and perception are identical with what is known and perceived, perceiving black and perceiving white differ in the same way that the objects differ. Their close general kind is likewise the same. The same applies to the examples involving different general kinds.

[^V08-134]: Distinct meanings call for distinct powers only when the meanings are opposed, as with cause and effect, mover and moved, or agent and recipient in a changing process.

[^V08-135]: That is, the power is not wholly absent, even if it is absent at one stage of its substantial development. Consider this carefully.

[^V08-136]: For example, nature makes a body rest when it is in its natural place, and move when it is outside that place.

[^V08-137]: This qualifies the earlier statement that the third kind of difference does not require distinct powers.

[^V08-138]: Differences in speed and slowness in movement are like differences in strength and weakness among qualities.

[^V08-139]: This restates the fifth kind of difference, then distinguishes the author’s account with “once you understand…” The present version chooses a more specific case and says that effects differing in kind call for different powers, while effects within one kind do not.

[^V08-140]: This does not merely narrow the claim that differences of kind require different powers. It takes a case that seems more likely to require distinct powers and less likely to be false. Once we see that difference of kind does not require a plurality of powers, it is even clearer that differences within one kind do not.

[^V08-141]: The common sense is one power that independently perceives what all the external senses perceive, even though their objects differ in kind. This is a counterexample to the claim.

[^V08-142]: That is, four or five, depending on whether touch is divided into its parts.

[^V08-143]: The author does not mean that sight perceives “color in general” as though that existed on its own. He means that if color existed outside the mind without its particular shades, it would be perceived in the same way that people say the essential features of a thing would belong to it even if it existed without actual existence. The premise is obvious and agreed on; the disagreement is whether such an object exists. The theologians say that everything, even the Necessary Being, has several aspects. Nothing is one in every respect, they say, since then it could produce only one thing.

[^V08-144]: There are several problems with this argument. First, the author himself applies the principle to nature, following the philosophers: one nature in one recipient produces one effect, such as a sphere. Second, in the discussion of divine matters he expressly states the principle: what is purely one, and anything considered simply as one, produces only one effect through that aspect. Third, although something that is not purely one may have several aspects, not every aspect suits every effect. One nature might produce a polygon because its general aspect gives the surface and its distinctive aspect gives the lines. Otherwise, why could fire not produce both light and darkness, or nature both motion and rest, with no condition such as being in or out of the natural place?

[^V08-145]: The philosophers did reply that color itself is what sight perceives directly, while the particular shade is perceived incidentally and as a secondary object of attention. The author does not consider this a meaningful answer, because abstract color has no existence without particular shades.

[^V08-146]: In fact, both proofs apply, and there is also a third kind mentioned earlier: concepts may differ because they oppose one another, as two actions can contradict each other.

[^V08-147]: This is not the earlier, rejected argument from an action’s being present or absent. That argument concerned the actions themselves; this one concerns the powers that produce them. This kind of evidence is what careful investigators rely on for all powers. In natural powers, the examples are as already given. In inner perception, imagination may be impaired while memory and rational thought remain sound. For example, a patient with a disturbance of imagination may think that people are in his house playing music without pause and ask his family to remove them, yet still recognize friends and enemies because his memory is sound. A different patient may have a disorder of thought while imagination and memory remain intact: he locks himself in a room, opens a window, and asks passers-by whether they would like him to throw something to them. When they name an object, he throws it to them. Unlike the first patient, he does not imagine anything; his memory tells him what he is throwing and what it is for, but his impaired reasoning does not let him see that his action is mistaken. A disturbance in the rear of the brain may impair memory while leaving the other powers intact. At the beginning of an illness, the defect can be isolated in one power. As it worsens, it may spread to neighboring powers because their locations are close, and then the powers can no longer be distinguished in this way.

[^V08-148]: Do not think that growth continues in hair and nails, and that this rescues the claim that the power of growth never fails. Growth means a proportional increase in the three dimensions of the body’s original parts. Hair and the like are surplus material driven out by smoky vapour. There is another reason why an essential feature may cease to appear.

[^V08-149]: We can say that one power is absent while another remains only by knowing that the effect of one has ceased while the effect of the other continues. If different effects always required different powers, the author’s first argument would succeed. Keep this point in mind.

[^V08-150]: One might object that this reasoning also undermines the author’s view. If one cause can have several aspects and produce many effects, why could one power not produce an effect when a condition is present and cease when it is absent? Similarly, opposite effects might follow from two opposing conditions, as heat softens wax while the nature of salt makes it hard. One might also cite matter and form: the author says they are united, yet they can be separated.

    We answer that if the conditions are merely explanatory or qualifying aspects in thought, they cannot account for genuinely opposite effects, since they do not make the subject itself multiple. If they are real, joined aspects, then this is precisely what we mean by several powers. One could say that the soul grows when considered under its growth aspect and nourishes when considered under its nutritive aspect. If you agree that there are distinct powers, there is no dispute about what to call them: call them powers, conditions, or real aspects. This differs from the merely conceptual aspects assigned to the First Intellect, which do not make its sources of action multiple. Its effects do not come from one cause in the same way: some issue from it directly, others incidentally. As for matter and form, the real separation is between an imaginal form and matter; they were never united in the first place.

[^V08-151]: In response to the objection that several powers are required, one could instead ask why the objects perceived are not one and the same, while different powers and instruments give them different appearances. Some later thinkers say that universality and particularity are two ways of perceiving, not differences in the thing perceived. After all, all things have one source and one underlying reality. This proposal mirrors the earlier claim that one soul has several powers; it is no more decisive than that claim.

[^V08-152]: Some copies read “not needed.” Both readings make sense: reproduction is needed for preserving the species, but not for preserving the individual.

[^V08-153]: The criticism is that this view ends up abolishing powers and natural causes altogether, as the author’s Ashʿarite opponents do.

[^V08-154]: The commentator is saying that the claim that the soul knows particulars through instruments is also held by the philosophers. On their account, the soul perceives universals in itself, while particulars are perceived by its instruments; the instruments perceive them, and the soul has no direct awareness of them. The commentator thinks the author’s words show that he mistakenly attributes this view to earlier philosophers, and that he himself held it for much of his life before changing his mind. But “perceiving particulars through instruments” does not mean that the soul is unaware of them. On the earlier philosophers’ account, particular images are impressed in the instruments, and the soul is directly aware of the instruments and their contents. It does not receive an image reflected from the instrument, nor is the image of a particular impressed in an immaterial power. On the author’s account, the images subsist in the powers that receive them, but that existence is an expression of the soul’s own existence, since those powers are its essential aspects.

[^V08-155]: That is, the perceiver is present to itself. Perception is presence, which is why the author can say that the soul perceives through a given thing. Affirming something requires first conceiving both sides of the claim.

[^V08-156]: That is, it is aware of itself while perceiving something else.

[^V08-157]: Examples include bone, nails, horns, and hooves, which carry out plant-like actions without sensation; or the hands and feet, if sensation is limited to the senses other than touch; or a paralysed hand, which continues to be nourished, since otherwise it would quickly decay. The organ may be called “healthy” in a relative sense. Another possibility is that it cannot be affected by the power—that is, by the directly perceived forms. Since these powers are bodily, their instruments must be affected by their action, at least as a preparation for perception, as the author holds.

[^V08-158]: “Receptive” may mean receptive alongside the absence of the quality. In that case, “heat” and the other terms mean qualities that can be perceived directly, not merely indirectly, and these bodies can receive them insofar as they can be permeated by the vital spirit. Or “receptive” may mean receptive while the quality is actually present. Then heat and the other terms refer to the qualities in these bodies and instruments, which always have some such qualities. The point is not that matter is itself perceived—matter is not present to a perceiving power. Rather, when the sense power is present and a similar quality reaches it, perception follows, as it does in other comparable cases. These qualities prepare the organ for perception. This is the more likely reading.

[^V08-159]: The organs can receive a power that exists in them once they are fully prepared for it. They can therefore receive what that power perceives, since a recipient must be suited to what it receives, just as the perceiver must be suited to what it perceives.

[^V08-160]: “Conditions” and “impediments” are used in their ordinary sense of defining features, as when we say that a human being, on the condition of being able to speak, has this nature, or that a donkey, on the condition of braying, has that nature.

[^V08-161]: From the perspective of divine metaphysics, all possible existence—the realm of action—is a shadow of the divine realm of unity. That realm in turn follows from the divine attributes, gathered in the simple unity of the divine essence. If the complete order of possible existence, in its downward or upward levels and in its length and breadth, were deficient, existence itself would be deficient. Consider this carefully.

[^V08-162]: In their terminology, the instruments of perception include the nerves and vital spirit, especially in the language of natural philosophers. That is likely what the author means by “powers in the sensory instruments.” Sometimes “instrument” also means a power considered as an instrument of the soul, as in the definition of the soul as the first perfection of an instrumental body. If “instrument” means a bodily organ, the author’s point is clear. If it means a power, he is denying that the image inheres in the power as a quality, as is commonly supposed. When he says that perception is not the organ’s reaction to an external thing, he is also denying that perception is an effect of the material object itself. The organ’s reaction, together with its position relative to matter bearing a quality, prepares the soul to produce within its own world an image that resembles that quality. On the author’s view, sensation is therefore an active process. The claim he rejects—that perception consists in an image being present in an instrument—is not the same as his claim that the image exists in the perceiver itself. If the instrument means a power, what he rejects is the image’s inherence in that power; what he affirms is that it exists through the power as an expression of the soul.

[^V08-163]: This supports the earlier point that the recipient must be considered together with the quality actually present in it. The argument here sums up what was just explained.

[^V08-164]: One might object that this concedes the point: different defining features produce different effects, and sharing a general kind does not help. The author’s point is that the animal’s nutritive power shares something real with the plant’s nutritive power, and also includes sensation and voluntary movement. If the shared “nutritive power” were only a word used in two unrelated ways, or a concept common to two beings that differ completely in their real natures, then the powers of nourishment and sensation would be one and the same. Nourishment would come from the sensitive power, and sensation and movement from the nutritive power, because the general nature would itself differ rather than being specified by distinct features. That is like color, whose general existence is one with the distinctive features that separate its kinds.

    But if the shared kind is a real general nature specified in different ways—as with compound bodies, which can be considered as substance, as having dimensions, as body, as growing body, or as sensing body—then the question remains whether the general nature and its distinguishing feature are one existence or several. If sensation and voluntary movement came from the shared, fully formed general nature, plants would also sense and move by choice. Otherwise, the existence of distinct powers is established. This is the philosophers’ view; the author’s own account will be given later, when he defines nourishment and growth and treats powers as simple. The philosophers also hold that effects differing in kind come from different sources. Since the actions of nutritive powers share a kind, they come from a nutritive power of that kind; sensation and voluntary movement differ in kind from nourishment and therefore come from another source. In that sense, their use of “nutritive power” for distinct beings is only verbal: there is no common meaning between powers whose whole natures differ.

[^V08-165]: The critic assumes that the soul’s existence is confined to the immaterial realm and separate from the lower material world. He finds it unlikely that one thing could be both immaterial and material. He has not understood the soul’s comprehensive unity: through one unified existence, the soul has both an immaterial level and a material level.

[^V08-166]: The Illuminationists say that God acts through His own self-disclosure: His knowledge of things is identical with their existence. Here, knowledge is not active in the sense of causing an effect; it is identical with the effect itself.
