[^V08-167]: “Form” here means what makes something actual. It may also mean the shared feature of the different meanings of form, or any actual meaning that can be understood. In *The Healing*, Avicenna uses “form” in several ways: for any intelligible actuality, including separate intellects; for any state or actuality present in a single recipient or in a compound, such as motions and accidents; for what makes matter actual; for what completes matter even without constituting it, as health does; for shapes made in matter by craft; for a thing’s species, broader kind, or distinguishing feature; and for the whole definition, whose general nature may be said to be present in its parts. “Knowledge” here includes both direct awareness and knowledge through a mental representation. In the latter, the image exists in the knower, not in matter. The two kinds of image differ in eight ways, discussed earlier in the section on intellect and intelligible things.

[^V08-168]: This refers to the other phrase in the passage: the second part of the contrast that answers the first part of the earlier contrast—“what is known is not knowledge.”

[^V08-169]: The image comes from matter, but has actual existence and perfection; the thing represented is pure potentiality and deficiency. Because the thing is so weak, the image cannot resemble it in the same way an image of a fully actual thing does. In this case, knowledge is stronger than what is known. In the earlier case, what is known is stronger than another knower’s knowledge of it, although it is knowledge of itself.

[^V08-170]: The knowledge we have of it is still one aspect of it. Consider this carefully.

[^V08-171]: In truth, the soul, insofar as it produces natural actions, is not aware of itself or of those actions. If knowing the cause entails knowing its effect, then not knowing the cause entails not knowing its effect as well. The soul, in this respect, is nature, since nature is one of its lower levels. Nature is plainly not aware of itself or of its effects.

[^V08-172]: The author has granted that knowledge of the cause exists, but that knowledge of the effect does not. More precisely, knowledge of the cause does not exist at the soul’s low level in this instance. The soul acts naturally in producing its bodily effects, just as natural powers do. Yet in relation to those powers, which are its own aspects, the soul acts through self-disclosure; its awareness of them is direct and unified. Its knowledge of the powers as they exist at their own levels is like God’s complete, unified knowledge of things through their own existence. The powers here are like the things there: they are relations whose existence depends entirely on the knower.

[^V08-173]: The First Intellect does not exist at the same level as the lower things of which it is said to be ignorant. If those things’ existence is their knowledge, then the First Intellect’s lack of their existence is its ignorance of them. Yet the First Intellect’s existence is nobler than theirs. Consider this carefully.

[^V08-174]: This “ignorance” is the absence, in the First Intellect, of a lower level interwoven with nonexistence. The First Intellect contains that level in a higher way, but its own intellectual level is precisely the absence of the lower level. That absence is ignorance; this ignorance is the source of all beings and is nobler than knowledge of those lower things. The philosopher’s words may also mean that the First Intellect is unaware of everything below God because it is absorbed in witnessing divine beauty and majesty. There is a report that God has creatures who do not know that He created Adam or Iblīs. A reading closer to the view held by Aristotle’s followers is this: natural things, considered as material, do not exist as knowledge for the intellect, because they exist in matter, remain hidden by spatial distance and temporal extension, and are not present as unified forms. Knowledge depends on formal existence and presence. They are known through images that exist in the intellect, but in a way that expresses the intellect rather than inhering in it. They may also be said to be known directly in another sense: knowledge is a form present to the knower, and this is true of the thing represented as well as of the image; the image is a tool for attending to the thing, not the thing attended to in itself.

[^V08-175]: That is, inward insight: the vision of an intellect illuminated by inner awareness, gained through spiritual discipline and exercises that keep the soul turned towards the source of knowledge and its light.

[^V08-176]: This does not apply to attraction. The body does not seek it by nature; it is harmful to the patient, and the body resists it.

[^V08-177]: Recall what we said near the beginning of the First Journey about definitions and proofs. The author’s definition of nourishment—and likewise his definition of growth—is a complete demonstration: it gives the full proof. If he had said only “the nutritive power replaces what is lost,” that would be a definition that serves as the starting point of a proof. If he had said only “it changes food so that it resembles the substance being nourished,” that would give the result of the proof.

[^V08-178]: Maybūdī says that the power should be called “the growth-giving power,” but the philosophers used a name that parallels “nutritive power.” I say that this wording hints at what the author has established: the body is a level of the soul. They describe the power through a bodily feature to point to a kind of unity between body and soul. We can infer the same unity from their calling power “a quality of the soul,” even though it is a quality that exists in the force spread through the muscles. Unless that force were a level of the soul, it would not have a quality of the soul. Hold to this point.

[^V08-179]: That is, after the age when growth stops, from then until the end of life. The author mentions this case because it makes the difference clearer. Fatness before that age is not growth either; nor is thinness during that age the same as decline.

[^V08-180]: This refers to the kind of cause called *aṭrūqiyā*.

[^V08-181]: In reality, there are two powers here. The wording suggests this by introducing them with “as for,” even though the author has not yet stated that two powers preserve the species. The reproductive power clearly does so. The formative power’s role is less clear, since the body needs the forms of bones, veins, nerves, and flesh, and its individual matter does not need these forms in the same way it needs reproductive moisture to preserve the individual. The answer is that “forms” here means the shapes and patterns that distinguish individuals as members of their kinds. Without the formative power’s work, the kinds would not remain distinct. The test for a cause is whether its absence would entail the absence of its effect. Without reproduction, the species would not continue; without formation and its patterns, the species would not continue as distinct kinds. This does not mean that the individual would immediately cease to exist, since the nutritive power replaces what the body loses. But saying that this individual would no longer remain an individual of its kind amounts to saying that the kind itself would not remain.

[^V08-182]: Unlike yellow bile and black bile, phlegm has no single organ of its own. It is spread through the other organs because of their moisture and helps keep them from drying out. When the body lacks food, phlegm may be transformed into blood—unless the point is that some of it flows to the brain, and likewise the lungs, to nourish them.

[^V08-183]: One might say that all this proves only that the fluids are distinguished, not that the nutritive power does the distinguishing; perhaps a separate distinguishing power does that. We reply that attraction already accounts for the distinction: the spleen attracts black bile and the gall bladder attracts yellow bile, for example. A separate power would do no further work and would be an unnecessary, idle addition. Attraction is needed in every organ, while a distinct sorting power is not. Also, the fluids are not entirely separated: a little yellow bile remains in blood to thin it and help it pass through narrow vessels, and a little black bile remains to give it a consistent texture.

[^V08-184]: This qualification excludes some forms of jaundice caused by a blockage in the bile duct.

[^V08-185]: The point is that “end” can mean either the point a movement reaches or the purpose for which it is undertaken. Since these are different, they require different powers, just as an animal has an active power and a power of desire. The active power always has an endpoint, even in a pointless or random action: like nature, it brings movement to a particular limit. It carries out its assigned task, but it is not what supplies the purpose. For example, a stone thrown at Zayd’s head reaches and wounds him; the hand’s active power strikes a hated person. But the purpose—relieving the thrower’s anger or making Zayd submit—is the work of desire. These are two different ends and require two sources. The same applies to the plant powers: digestion moves food to the point where its blood form ends, while nourishment acts for the sake of the organ’s form.

[^V08-186]: Pure matter has no feature that would make it ready for one form rather than another. If the first matter of wheat and barley were exactly the same, there would be no reason for one to become wheat and the other barley. Their elements are the same, but each stage is prepared by the form that came before it: an earlier wheat form prepares matter for the next wheat form, and an earlier barley form prepares it for the next barley form. The same question can be asked about those earlier forms, and so on. The philosophers allow—and indeed require—this succession, so that the flow of existence does not stop. The same applies to a sequence of forms in one matter, as in semen: each new stage, such as a clot or a small lump of flesh, changes what the matter is ready to receive.

[^V08-187]: The objector would not accept that the inference from “matter alone is not enough” is valid, because he recognizes no agent other than God and considers the matter’s reactions sufficient for the outpouring. The commentator therefore adds the principle that every reaction and readiness in matter depends on an agent’s action.

[^V08-188]: One argument is specific to the stomach and intestines; the other applies to all the organs.

[^V08-189]: The difference is that one power may be weak where another is strong, and vice versa. It is not that one of the powers is completely absent.

[^V08-190]: The food drawn from outside into the stomach is not what nourishes the stomach. The blood drawn from the liver through vessels in the stomach wall nourishes it; the text itself points to this when it mentions what the stomach draws from the liver. The three actions mentioned next concern nourishment of the whole body; the later three concern blood drawn from the liver to nourish the stomach itself. Some copies read “the powers that retain in the substance of the stomach,” which is the better reading.

[^V08-191]: The commentator means that some writers loosely call this a doubling. More accurately, there are four powers that produce food and four preparatory powers that resemble them.

[^V08-192]: The four powers that produce nourishment are clear. The four preparatory powers that help digestion and expulsion throughout the body may be understood as follows. Digestion strengthens the qualities of fluids in the stomach. The spleen releases some black bile at the entrance to the stomach, which irritates and contracts it and thereby arouses hunger. The gall bladder releases some yellow bile into the intestine; its irritating quality prompts the bowel to expel its contents. Expulsion also occurs in the testes.

[^V08-193]: Some copies say “not resembling it.” This may refer to the condition of people with leprosy or vitiligo. Consider the wording carefully.

[^V08-194]: One might object that plants feed on a simple body, namely water. The answer is that water does not become food for a plant until it mixes with earth.

[^V08-195]: This is like air around an overturned bowl placed over a fire: when its form is disrupted, the air is prepared to receive the form of water from the giver of forms. In the same way, suitable moist bodies next to the living body prepare its matter to receive the actual form of nourishment from the soul, continuously and through renewed instances. The same idea applies to the body’s light or color: it comes from the soul’s emanations and levels. What the soul preserves is not simply the same material body that remains after death, but a different matter and temperament; the soul is not the immediate cause of the color and shape left behind.

    The nature that brings fatigue is also not the same as the nature that serves the soul. The bodily matter, nature, and powers are levels of the soul when considered without restriction, and are linked to it spiritually. Something even more surprising has been said in divine metaphysics: the air that carries speech, or the tablet on which a speaker writes, is not a separate object in the ordinary sense. Considered as the medium of the speaker’s act, the speech exists through the speaker.

    The soul is a complete expression of divine unity and bears all the names of God. It has natural qualities as well as the qualities of spiritual beings, potentially or in actuality. So it contains natural powers and bodily aspects in its own right. Just as a higher thing contains the perfections of what is lower in a higher way, the lower effects that flow from it do not make the higher thing lose its level; they are its appearance and expression at lower levels. Some souls possess knowledge and perfection from within themselves, without needing the preparation of hearing or sight. Others acquire them after preparation, but even then the perfection comes from within the soul, not from foreign material things.

    The same applies to nourishment. In the senses and intellect, the soul is completed by forms that are present to it directly, not by external things known only indirectly. Its bodily nourishment at the level of nature and body likewise comes from the soul. So the “food from the world of the soul” is not food that is merely potential, even if only nearly ready to nourish: it is food in actuality. And actual food here means its form, not its matter. The author speaks as though food were the matter because, in his view, matter and form are united and matter is absorbed into form. “The world of the soul” means its lower, formal level. This should not seem too strange: the original forms of the organs come from the soul, since the formative power is a lower level of the soul; the replacement of what those organs lose comes from the soul as well. The soul brings together the worlds of meaning and form. It is like clear air touched by a cold wind: clouds appear without vapour rising from below, yet the air does not leave its own higher state.

    Forgive this long explanation. This is a difficult point and a common source of doubt and missteps.

[^V08-196]: At every moment, the body that is nourished is made up of different parts from the body at the previous moment. The whole would cease to exist whenever any part disappeared. That is not so if the stages of its form appear through one enduring, individual soul, which remains the source of all the stages. This applies the principle already mentioned: every natural or psychic substance is one thing in actuality. Keep this point in mind.

[^V08-197]: The perceiver must be one with what it perceives; otherwise it would not be completed by it or move from potentiality to actuality. This is the major premise of an unstated argument: the sense is completed by what it senses, and whatever is completed by something is identical with it. Therefore the sense is identical with what it senses.

[^V08-198]: Just as the intellect has four stages, from intellect in potentiality to intellect in actuality, digestion also has four stages, from food in potentiality to food in actuality. The note may instead compare the four degrees of abstraction in knowledge: perception by the senses abstracts from matter while requiring the presence of matter; imagination does not require matter, though its images retain shape, size, and similar features; estimation is more abstract, though its objects remain tied to measurable things; and intellect is fully abstract and free of matter. The four stages of cleansing and digesting food follow the same pattern. The author’s phrase “understanding” in place of “knowledge” is not a serious problem, since all forms are present to the intellect, as he has explained and will explain again. The separate discussion of digestion stages in this chapter may not fully match the chapter heading, but that is a minor point.

[^V08-199]: An abscess is a large, hot swelling; a boil is a broader term and can include cold swellings.

[^V08-200]: This wording suggests that the end of this digestion comes when food reaches the stomach, where another digestion also takes place. The usual view is that there is only one digestion in the stomach. One could say that the first digestion is completed there, though it begins in the mouth: “completed” would mean perfected or brought to its fullest stage. The four stages are the four main visible transformations in a fully developed animal. In fact, food changes at every moment from the time it is chewed until it becomes part of an organ, so its transformations cannot be limited to four.

[^V08-201]: The word “of” may explain which powers these are, or where they come from: they may be the governing nature itself, in its branches and stages, or powers that arise from nature.

[^V08-202]: The account is not quite right. The mesenteric vessels carry the finer part of food, after it has been fully digested in the stomach, toward the vessels leading to the liver. It seems the phrase about reaching the liver originally followed the words “to another level, the liver,” and was moved here by a copyist. The author would not have mistaken the role of the mesentery.

[^V08-203]: This is the third place where the two powers are defined. The first definition comes from Avicenna; the second appears incidentally at the end of the division of powers; this third one is the main definition here. The phrase “in their true sense” signals that the earlier definition was only nominal, since it came before the existence of these powers was established. As Avicenna says in *The Healing*, definitions given before a thing's existence is proved are nominal definitions; once it is proved, the same definitions become real ones.

[^V08-204]: Nourishment occurs directly, while growth occurs through nourishment. Since the nutritive power acts through three operations, some say it is only a conceptual unity: the power that gathers food, joins it to the body, and changes it into the body's substance. The fuller account is that it is one power with different aspects and stages, just as the chief of all the powers—the soul's commanding light—is one, and the other powers are its modes and expressions. As the Persian saying puts it, “In the house, all is the householder.”

[^V08-205]: The phrase “natural ends” excludes ends chosen by will, since a change in chosen ends does not by itself mean a change in the one who chooses.

[^V08-206]: Another proof, besides the difference in ends, is that the actions themselves differ and the two powers can operate independently. The nutritive power brings about a qualitative change, using digestion and then adhesion; it also produces the body's substantial forms. The growth power brings about a weak, gradual change in quantity.

[^V08-207]: Fire changes air. An opponent could therefore say that the nutritive power is air, since both it and air “nourish”: air is said to nourish fire by changing into it. But air destroys fire by changing it into itself; otherwise the fire would rise to the sphere of ether or become earth.

[^V08-208]: The point is that bodily powers, insofar as they are bodily, cannot act without limit. But insofar as they are connected to intelligible and immaterial things, they may do so: through that connection they serve as powers and instruments of those things. Still, since they are also connected to matter and body, the continued flow of intelligible forms into them depends on the recipient's continuing to exist. A recipient made of parts with opposing natures, each tending to separate from the others, cannot last forever. Otherwise those parts would be prevented from acting according to their own natures and forced to do the opposite, which is impossible according to the philosophers.

[^V08-209]: Since the appeal to the body being made up of basic elements is the same as the second argument, the author says he will examine that argument.

[^V08-210]: The discussion concerns the loss of the moisture supplied by food; what weakens the nutritive power is the loss of the body's innate moisture, which sustains its natural heat. There is no circle. The innate moisture declines through the weakening of the cause that sustains it. A thing disappears or weakens when the cause that keeps it present or strong is absent; it may also weaken because of some other cause, such as increased heat. Two things that depend on one another—especially when one exists in the other—may decline together, each in a different causal respect. For example, when matter loses its form, that form's individual existence ends; when the form disappears, the matter also ceases to exist. Here, the form preserves the body's material organization. As the form weakens, the material becomes less stable; that, in turn, weakens the form's activity further, though in a new degree. The process continues until the body breaks down.

[^V08-211]: The objection is that a longer period of nourishment does not help, since a natural power acts steadily and accidental causes do not simply join it. The answer is that the forces consuming moisture grow stronger because of poor care of food, air, and other necessary conditions. Even a careful, wise, medically trained person, who watches over their health and does everything they can, cannot know all the events decreed for them or ward off every stroke of chance. One may object that poor care also occurs in youth, and that nourishment repairs the damage. The reply is that such chance events weaken not only the body's mixture and the matter that receives it, but also the power and agent. The nutritive power cannot make good all the damage; it too keeps weakening. It is like someone pitching a tent in a storm that blows from every direction. Each time the wind twists the tent and threatens to bring it down, the person repairs it, raising the poles and tightening the pegs. But as the wind shakes the tent's supports, it also weakens the person doing the repairs, until the person is exhausted and the tent collapses.

[^V08-212]: Since things are naturally inclined to seek their own good and perfection, nothing acts by its nature in a way that leads to its own destruction. The natural heat's gradual drying of moisture must therefore be a form of completion. Once it has reached completion, it no longer needs that connection, so its relation to the moisture ends. Understand this if you are able, and do not be heedless.

[^V08-213]: The author is considering the issue from the standpoint of divine causes; this note addresses natural causes, though it too is sound. As for the claim that semen's temperament is finer and stronger, the key is to distinguish mature, firmly established moisture from a finer temperament. Moisture, understood as a substance that binds the original organs together—especially the heart—is not the same as temperament, which is an attribute. Even if this moisture comes from semen, the argument concerns the moisture in the organs, one of the four secondary humours described by physicians. It is clearly original and innate, unlike later or foreign moisture. To deny this is unreasonable. The objection that a more balanced temperament should at once give rise to the soul's powers is also answered by the fact that the temperament of a young adult is more balanced than that of an older person, even though the latter has fuller mental powers, especially inward speech and thought. These powers do not flow into semen at the outset: it does not yet contain the heart, brain, and liver, where the three vital spirits reside. Rather, the powers and perfections flow through those spirits. Yet without semen and the original, governing organs, no such perfections would arise at all.

[^V08-214]: This objection is extremely weak. Seminal moisture—the secondary moisture that binds the organs—does not disappear until death, even in a body that has become thin and fragile. Could one say that naturally moist skin, even after being soaked in water, contains no moisture of its own?

[^V08-215]: Who restricted it to those particular vessels? The issue is whether original moisture is stronger and firmer than moisture acquired later, not whether one temperament is stronger than another. What does this objection have to do with that?

[^V08-216]: This is a subtle allusion. The main point is that mixing foreign moisture with moisture from food weakens its quality and makes it less like the body's own moisture. It is called a subtle allusion because the third argument says that even the best chyle cannot replace what the original organs lose: its quality is too different, not merely because it has mixed with foreign moisture.

[^V08-217]: The author does not mention the “originated” being, though he is setting out to list all the rational possibilities. Here, “originated” has a broad sense: a generated thing is preceded by both matter and time, while an originated thing is not; it is the opposite of this combined condition. “Complete” and “incomplete” are not used in their usual sense of having, or lacking, a further state to reach. The author himself says that some incomplete beings are self-sufficient souls, not things made to become matter for something else. Nor are the souls of the heavenly spheres merely supposed to last forever; they are held to do so in fact. Here “complete” means a being that is not made to serve as matter or a means for another being; “incomplete” means one that does serve this role. Elsewhere, the author says that every being is either complete or incomplete, and that complete beings may be above perfection or simply perfect, while incomplete beings may or may not be self-sufficient.

[^V08-218]: One might ask why a body's complete cause could not preserve its life through an endless succession of motions, just as successive motions preserve it for a time during its life. This proof and the following point about complete beings appear to form one argument: it proceeds from substantial motion to the claim that each level of a moving being has an end. Plants, animals, and human beings are levels along this motion, and the moving being must pass from one level to the next; natural death is that transition. The proof is sound, though the claim that a plant's end is to feed an animal, and an animal's end is to feed a human, needs qualification. It concerns a plant on the way to animal life, serving as its matter, and an animal on the way to human life—not a fully formed plant such as an almond tree or a complete animal such as a horse or cow. It would be strange to call a tree's becoming food for an animal its natural death, rather than an accidental death, and the same is true of animals. Keep this distinction in mind.

[^V08-219]: The purpose belongs naturally to the rational soul, though not to the estimative faculty.

[^V08-220]: This points back to the earlier argument that composite bodies cannot be sustained indefinitely, even if the nutritive power continually replaced what was lost. If the soul were always turned toward this world and used its powers, it would keep providing the replacement. But by nature it is always turning toward its goal and independence from the body. Likewise, if bodily powers could act without limit, the rational soul would set them aside once it reached its goal. Since the soul naturally turns toward God and is little concerned with the body, the body breaks down.

[^V08-221]: The soul's manifestation of dominion is a perfection in its existence, even if it is a moral failing. Whether through mercy or power, the soul no longer needs the body's natural functions.

[^V08-222]: That is, human souls bearing the traits of non-speaking animals do not have a spiritual fire; they are consumed by the fire of nature.

[^V08-223]: The person meant may be al-Ghazali; the disagreement may be only about words. By “angels” the philosophers may mean those who govern events, not the highest ranks of angels who stand before God and are too exalted to manage bodily matter. This applies if “powers” means causes that are separate from bodies or joined to them, rather than beings tied to time and matter. Those temporal beings work within matter and are the places where its changes take place.

[^V08-224]: That is, the Muʿtazilites say one action produces another. The truth is that everything happens through God's power; nothing else acts independently. But the Muʿtazilites say that an effect does not need God in order to continue existing. If God were absent, they claim, it would make no difference to the thing's own being. In their view, God makes things independent in themselves and in their actions. Philosophers instead treat other causes as means through which God's generosity flows, not as independent creators. The Ashʿarites reject these means and deny all cause and effect between created things.

[^V08-225]: For us, there is no difficulty in explaining how an unaware formative power can produce well-ordered results. Order alone does not prove awareness; we see ordered actions in nature. We have also shown that powers have direct awareness of themselves and their actions.

[^V08-226]: This does not mean that semen is already an animal; it is matter that still needs a form. The problem is that the argument says it contains some part from every organ, which would make it a complete body with organs. The reference to the “problem returning” points to the claim that the animal should be a sphere. If the parts of semen differ, that difference leads to an impossible circle or endless chain. Once that difference is rejected, the original problem returns. There is no contradiction between the two objections: the first assumes the parts are alike, while the second follows from the claim that they differ.

[^V08-227]: At first this may look like the familiar problem of which came first, the egg or the chicken. One might also ask why the cause must differ; why could the matter's readiness not be enough? Philosophers often explain differences between individuals by different matter, and the differences in matter by earlier forms, in a sequence that continues over time. But here the organs differ by kind. Different readiness in matter cannot explain differences between kinds; the active cause, or its different aspects, must also differ. The causes must exist together, so the chain would be simultaneous, not a sequence through time.

[^V08-228]: Since the question concerns the cause of the difference itself, the endless chain would have to exist all at once. It would not be a series of causes following one another over time.

[^V08-229]: The circle and endless chain are impossible here because the things that depend on one another are active causes arranged in order, not passive materials that receive forms.

[^V08-230]: Since animal organs differ by kind, differences in the matter that receives them are not enough to explain them. The active cause or its aspects must differ too. This resembles the view of some Persian philosophers that distinct kinds in this lower world are shadows of distinct higher beings, and the view of other philosophers that different aspects of the Active Intellect give rise to different kinds.

[^V08-231]: The pleasure meant is bodily pleasure. The commentary explains it by saying that semen contains many spirits and much heat, which stimulate and rub against the place from which it is released. Once it leaves, the body feels relief and ease, and the friction and pain end.

[^V08-232]: The words “those parts” are missing from some copies. The editor considers the shorter reading, without them, more likely to be correct.

[^V08-233]: A child may resemble a parent in the roots of the nails or hair, even if no part of the nail itself is carried in semen. An illness at a nail root may affect the nail, and heat at a hair root may darken the hair. Resemblance is direct at the roots and indirect in the nails or hair themselves.

[^V08-234]: The objection is that bodily arrangement as a whole may resemble between parent and child even when particular parts do not. For example, a parent's nose may be hooked and the child's flat, though both noses are placed in relation to the mouth in a similar way. If semen comes from the whole body, the general arrangement may resemble even when a particular feature does not; some obstacle may prevent that particular resemblance.

[^V08-235]: This can be denied, as the author has said earlier. The argument may mean only that semen would contain different organs, as a dead embryo does, not that it is already a living animal.

[^V08-236]: This can also be denied. Perhaps the woman's emission helps the man's semen form the child but does not itself produce another child. This also answers the next argument.

[^V08-237]: The commentator suggests that a child's sex depends on which semen is stronger: the man's or the woman's. If the man's semen overpowers the woman's and changes it to its own temperament, the child's body takes on male traits.

[^V08-238]: The example is the mule. Some copies say “not of its kind,” meaning not of the same species.

[^V08-239]: This means the author's proof goes further than Aristotle's position. Matter, in every case, is indeterminate and only potentially what it may become; it is not yet a complete, actual thing.

[^V08-240]: Semen is not fully formed even as a distinct kind of matter, much less as a complete body with nerves, ligaments, bones, and other parts. This is related to the verse: “The human being was created weak.” The argument assumes that semen's different parts would themselves produce the organs, without needing a formative or reproductive power. If the argument only means that different parts of matter help the powers form organs, this objection does not apply.

[^V08-241]: Being closer to readiness for a particular organ does not mean that semen already differs in kind. A thing can be closer to becoming something and still not be that thing. Unless every way it could fail to become that thing is closed off, it is not yet actual.

[^V08-242]: The commentator replies that even if al-Razi accepts that food only prepares matter to receive forms from within, this still supports the objection. Different organs are fed by different powers, and their food may therefore differ after the fourth digestion. Can the soul create distinct forms of sensation directly, without prior sensory material to prepare it?

[^V08-243]: The waste need only resemble what it replaces; it need not be identical or equally strong. The bran of flour resembles the grain, and the sediment of a liquid resembles the liquid closely enough.

[^V08-244]: The question about preserving the order of the parts is valid if the claim is that they keep their positions after being separated from the organs. But if the claim is only that the parts differ in kind, then separating them and bringing them together does not make their differences disappear.

[^V08-245]: The source of weakness need not be only psychic; natural causes also have an effect, and this cannot be doubted. The claim that a flowing fluid cannot contain differences in arrangement is also challenged in another copy: mixtures such as milk and vinegar contain different powers. This is a separate objection to the author's point.

[^V08-246]: Because the organs are made from the material of semen, the author gives this brief reminder of the main argument for why bodily matter cannot explain their different forms. He has already set out the core of it.

[^V08-247]: One might ask why the Throne has four supports, as the reports say. First, Azazil acts only in this world, unlike the four angels, whose actions appear in the inner worlds and extend toward the divine presence. Second, evil exists only as a consequence, not as something created for its own sake. The partial appearances of these angels sometimes seen by prophets and saints may be stronger and clearer than their universal source. In light, intellect, and mercy, the source is stronger than its appearance; in darkness, ignorance, and anger, the appearance can seem stronger than its source.

[^V08-248]: The cited philosopher's point is that the formative power is one level of the soul.

[^V08-249]: The soul has a unified whole. Its “organs” are the body's subtle spirits and parts; its “qualities” are luminous features that exist within the soul's unity. Its powers are aspects of that unity, like shadows of those qualities.

[^V08-250]: Just as the things matter may receive differ in actuality, its potential readiness for them also differs. One cannot say that the remote readiness is actual while the different readinesses are only potential; the discussion applies to all degrees of readiness.

[^V08-251]: What makes matter ready is its temperament, unless one follows the view, discussed in the First Journey, that “readiness” and temperament name the same quality.

[^V08-252]: A single, unified being can contain the good and perfection found in many separate beings. The complete human being is one, yet contains something like an angel, a heavenly body, an animal, a plant, and even the powers of lifeless matter. This is “many in one.” Do not confuse it with “one in many,” which describes the simple divine reality as the source of all things. These are two different levels. Mystics call them seeing the detailed in the summary, and the summary in the detailed. The author says elsewhere that few have reached this insight; he names Aristotle as one who did.

[^V08-253]: The wording is not exact. If “organs” means individual vessels and nerves, they spread according to the parts that receive them, not the other way around. Also, most powers of perception are located in the brain; only touch spreads through the body. If “organs” means powers, that is not the usual meaning here. The author also speaks loosely of the spirit spreading from the heart through veins and arteries: the sensory and moving powers use the brain's spirit in the nerves, while the heart's spirit is called animal and the liver's natural. The philosophers describe three kinds of spirit.

[^V08-254]: The previous paragraph describes the soul as one containing many powers. This paragraph describes many powers gathered in one soul. The soul has all these powers without becoming many beings.

[^V08-255]: One might object that in thought we perceive mental images of colors and sounds, not actual sight and hearing. The reply is that “sight” here means the presence of light and color to the soul with the same vividness as when the eye sees them. One may call this imagination instead; the disagreement is about the name.

[^V08-256]: If the shapes of the organs depend on the powers' structures, the human powers lead to a circle in the explanation. The power of sight is ready only after the eye and the crossing of the optic nerves have formed, yet those forms are said to depend on the power of sight.

[^V08-257]: This point applies not only to the human and animal powers but to every power that receives something. Readiness to receive something near always depends on a form already present.

[^V08-258]: The point is that forms and powers have stages. Each earlier form helps prepare the matter for the one that follows, and that later form is the shadow of a higher power.

[^V08-259]: The heart is an example of the chief organ, not the only one. It is like the trunk of a tree, with the other organs as its branches.

[^V08-260]: A better example may be the way a simple intellect contains, in a unified way, the many intellects that flow from it.

[^V08-261]: The union meant here is that all things are contained in the simple reality in a higher, unified way.

[^V08-262]: These are the higher patterns or sources of kinds. They are complete beings with no further stage to reach, so they do not move from their station. Because they encompass lower levels, the particular forms below them descend and take shape. In this sense, the universal patterns themselves do not descend; their weaker forms do.

[^V08-263]: The partial form is the same reality in a weaker degree, just as the universal reality is the same in a stronger degree.

[^V08-264]: “Every soul” is meant generally, as in the verse “There is no creature that moves on the earth or bird that flies with its wings” (Qur'an 6:38).

[^V08-265]: Just as Israfil and his partial forms breathe spirit into matter, they also clothe matter in forms. For example, matter loses the form of chyle through a partial form of Azrael and receives the form of chyme through a partial form of Israfil.

[^V08-266]: Some powers are immaterial in themselves; others are bodily only through their effects. The latter are not fully free of matter. If Azrael is counted among the four great angels, he should also be named here with his partial forms; the author appears to include him in the phrase “the same applies to all powers.”

[^V08-267]: Natural philosophers place the animal soul in the brain because it branches into powers of perception and movement, both of which are there. The human soul itself has no bodily location; the author says only that its rule is in the brain. Another animal power governs the heart and arteries through the spirit within them, as explained later.

[^V08-268]: The pronoun refers to all the powers, not just to the human power. It could refer to practical intellect if “human power” is meant here as the practical intellect, which serves the theoretical one.

[^V08-269]: If seeing all things in the soul means that many things exist in it in a unified way, they exist there without parts. If it means that many separate powers are one in the soul, they are one because being is primary: what makes them different is the same existence that they share. Each is also a mirror of the simple self, which contains all things without becoming many. As the Persian verse says: “When we sent Adam out, we laid our beauty across the plain.”

[^V08-270]: The soul is a substance because the perceiver must exist in itself; an accident exists only in its subject, and so cannot perceive in its own right. The soul is sacred and free of matter because, if it existed only in matter, it would not perceive. The same reasoning shows that it cannot decay.

[^V08-271]: In the upward journey, the human being once stood at the level of prime matter: it was not yet a particular thing in actuality, though it held the potential for later forms. The verse describes a stage the human being passed through. The commentator also suggests a stage before creation, in God's knowledge, where the human being did not exist on its own; only God and His knowledge existed. The fixed realities of things have no independent existence there, and even less so at the beginning of their descent into the world.

[^V08-272]: The author builds this argument on the real bond between soul and body. A bond of being brings some kind of unity: if one exists for the other, it is a level of the other's existence. If the body is an accident, like a color, it exists in its subject. If body and soul are both substances, as matter and form are, they are higher and lower levels of one being. The stronger being contains the weaker, but does not erase the forms below it. This is an ordered relation, not two complete beings standing side by side.

[^V08-273]: The commentator offers a further explanation of the union between knower and known. Things in the external world and things in the mind are two forms of one reality. For something to exist in sight does not mean the eye is one thing and the visible object another, joined like a container and its contents. It means that the thing exists in a way that can be seen. In the same way, something existing in the mind is a way the soul exists; it is not a separate thing placed inside it. This is not just a comparison: both are forms of existence. If matter and form are one being, the union of the knower and known becomes clear. Alexander of Aphrodisias is said to have explained it in this way.

[^V08-274]: If each soul joined the whole Active Intellect, each would know what the others know. If each joined only a part, the Active Intellect would be divided. The answer is that the intellect exists in itself and also in each soul; the soul joins the way it exists in the soul, not the way it exists in itself.

[^V08-275]: The objections to the union of knower and known were answered in the First Journey. The author's own proof for this point is not complete. A better basis is the real bond between a power and what it can do. If an intelligible form has weaker existence than the knower, it is one of the knower's states, like an accident in its subject. If it has stronger existence, the knower is one of its levels, like an effect depends on its cause. This is especially clear of second matter in relation to its form.

[^V08-276]: Some copies read “the power that recognizes” instead of “the power that perceives and directs.”

[^V08-277]: Either the object of “says” is understood from the context, or the verb means simply “speaks.” The author quotes this report in the opening of the book's section on theology, but leaves out the words from “he says” to “when he enters.”

[^V08-278]: This follows an Arabic letter-writing form: “From Zayd ibn ʿAmr to Khalid ibn Bakr.” Here, “the Living” lives through the life of the First, and “the Sustainer” sustains through His sustaining power. Neither is an independent being beside Him; there is no partner in God's rule.

[^V08-279]: The mystic stresses the word “thing,” one of the broadest terms. The point is not that “thing” is simply the most indefinite word, as the grammarian al-Azhari says. Rather, being a thing goes with existing, so “thing” applies broadly to whatever exists.

[^V08-280]: In short, neither the natural human being nor the soul has unlimited power. Their work is limited to bodies and individual spirits. Only the human being at the intellectual level has universal reach.

[^V08-281]: “Part of the whole” means that every being is contained within the broad reality of the human being. The simple reality, because it is one, gathers all good and all the levels of existence. The author compares this with the teaching that the purified Imams contain all the souls, spirits, and bodies of the world. Other writers say that all creatures reflect human traits: animals reflect desire, wild beasts anger, ants greed, and other creatures resentment, pride, or vanity. Avicenna says in one of his prefaces, “Praise be to God, who created the human being and created all other worlds from what overflowed from him.” He means that the human being contains something like the heavens, angels, animals, plants, and other things. If you look at the meaning and not just the outward form, you see no difference in God's creation. Your hunger, thirst, greed, desire, joy, and sorrow are like what exists in animals and other beings. The true general ideas and beliefs within you also exist in others like you. The author adds that all things have their source, higher form, and fullest state in the human being, which is why a person may rise above the angels or fall below devils and animals.

[^V08-282]: The word means “described.” The author links “there is nothing that does not praise Him” with the statement that all beings bow down to God. Both describe the obedience and submission of all things.

[^V08-283]: The Persian line means, “This first movement was sought from the Eternal Beauty.” The author links this to the report that God created Adam in His image: the human being reflects both God's commands that bring things into being and His commands that guide conduct.

[^V08-284]: This explains why all those who know God agree on the point.

[^V08-285]: The author gives several possible meanings. The world may come into being through the unity that reflects God's simple reality, through the totality of God's beautiful names, or through the whole of created beings. On the last reading, “Him” in “the individual realities are nothing but Him” refers to that whole. On the first two readings, it refers to the totality of God's names and their created reflections. In each case, the point is that God is present with the whole and that, without Him, it could bring nothing about. The verse says He is “the fourth” of three, not the third, because God is not one limited being set beside the others. He is not a third item in a set of three. The verse points to God's presence within multiplicity and, by extension, to unity within multiplicity. Mulla Sadra cites the first idea here, though his main discussion concerns unity within multiplicity.

[^V08-286]: Here “animal power” means the term used by natural philosophers and doctors for the power that expands and contracts the heart and arteries to refresh the body. The author discusses it again two chapters later.

[^V08-287]: The argument relies on the principle that lower levels can give way to higher ones: plant powers come before animal powers, and animal powers before powers of the soul. If we look at how matter becomes ready over time, the liver comes first; if we look at priority in being and nobility, the brain comes first. The heart's priority is relative to the other organs. The author brings these views together by distinguishing the different ways in which each organ can be said to come first.

[^V08-288]: The words up to “it is not unlikely” explain why Avicenna says the body is prepared for sensory life. “The organs of the animal are completed with it” means that the sense organs form with the sensory soul, just as plant organs form with the plant soul, whose first power is nourishment. At first, the phrase about animal organs may seem unrelated and even wrong, since every animal organ needs nourishment or it would quickly decay—even nails and hair, which cannot feel touch. The three bodily spirits—natural, animal, and soul-related—are also well known in medical writing. The phrase is meant to stress that the animal's organs form with the sensory soul, while nourishment reaches more organs than sensation does. Avicenna often uses the name of a power's action for the power itself: “nourishment” for the nutritive power, “perception” for the perceiving power, and “movement” for the power that moves.

[^V08-289]: The author means there are three nutritive powers at issue: the father's own power, a power received from the father, and one that later develops in the child. He does not say it comes from the mother, even though that might seem more likely, because the account says the spirit comes from the father's seed.

[^V08-290]: An effect needs its cause to keep existing, not only to begin. If a force pushes a stone upward and then stops, but the stone keeps moving upward, we know that the force prepared the motion and another cause now carries it on. Here, the father's soul cannot simply prepare the inherited nutritive power and then leave it to continue by itself. Nor can the power come directly from the Active Source, since the argument concerns the power inherited from the father, not the child's own nutritive power.

[^V08-291]: This is not true of growth or the four powers that serve nourishment, except in number or in degree as they act on different organs. Growth is simply stretching, whether it happens in a nerve, vein, artery, or other organ. The same is true of drawing in, holding, and expelling. Digestion may differ because it produces different stages of processed food. But the difference does apply to nourishment and reproduction, since each changes the body's material in a different way.

[^V08-292]: One might ask whether the point the author accepts—that different effects require their sources to differ in number or kind—is enough for his opponent. The author's aim is to deny that the powers are wholly separate. They can differ in the bodily level while existing together in a simpler, more unified way in the soul.

[^V08-293]: An object's nature does not change depending on what it is compared with. If these powers truly differed in kind, their natures could not become one in the soul. The author's account of difference below and unity above makes sense in terms of existence, not nature: material kinds are distinct, while their existence is gathered into one at the higher level. The wording in the main text is not fully clear.

[^V08-294]: The causes in the bodily world also have a kind of unity. The powers are ways of existing, and their common features and differences belong to the same reality. They act in one body, come from one soul, and serve one aim.

[^V08-295]: “Rather” corrects the earlier claim: many effects do not always mean many causes. There may be many causes at a lower level and one cause at a higher level.

[^V08-296]: Mulla Sadra also uses this rule in *The Origin and the Return* to argue against a second world existing beside this one. The proof relies on the possibility of the lower level in an ascending series, corresponding to the possibility of the higher in a descending series. It also rules out a jump in the soul's inner development, just as there can be no jump in its change of place.

[^V08-297]: “Animal power” has more than one meaning. It can refer to the powers of sensation and movement found in animals, or to the power that prepares the organs to receive those abilities. The second meaning is intended here and is common among doctors. They divide the chief organs into the liver, heart, and brain, and the body's spirits into three kinds: natural spirit, which begins in the liver and flows through the veins; animal spirit, which begins in the heart and flows through the arteries; and soul-related spirit, which begins in the brain and flows through the nerves. They also distinguish plant, animal, and rational powers. In their account, animal power manages the vital spirit, expands and contracts the heart and arteries to draw in air and remove smoky vapor, and supports fear, anger, grief, and joy. By circulating vital spirit through the organs, it keeps them alive and prepares them to receive sensation and movement.

[^V08-298]: What about atrophy, a disease caused by lack of nourishment?

[^V08-299]: This conflicts with what the author said earlier. There he argued that if powers of sensation and movement were the same as plant powers, plants would sense and move at will. Here, he answers the claim that the powers differ by saying they share a broader class.

[^V08-300]: This explains how a broader class can also be a distinct kind. A fully grown plant, such as a tree, has settled into its kind and cannot keep developing toward animal life. But a seed or embryo is still developing: its plant life is not yet complete and remains open to higher stages. Its power to grow is therefore a broader class of power, still in the process of becoming a particular kind.

[^V08-301]: The ability to receive something is not enough by itself; it also needs a power that makes it ready to receive. Animal power is what allows nutritive power to receive the powers of sensation and movement. In the same way, mixed elements need a balanced temperament before they can receive a new form.

[^V08-302]: The author's statement is very surprising. Animal power prepares the organ to receive sensation and movement; it does not produce them. Their active source is the power associated with the brain. The author himself says this earlier, and the medical tradition agrees.

[^V08-303]: The doctors' point is that the absence of sensation and movement does not prove that the animal power is gone. They say it still maintains the vital spirit in the paralyzed organ, expands and contracts its arteries, and prepares it to sense and move if paralysis were not in the way.

[^V08-304]: The wording is faulty. The text should delete either “from” or “from it.”

[^V08-305]: The author has already considered this objection. The matter itself may not be the obstacle; something in it may block the power, as happens in forced movement. The matter may be ready for a body's natural movement while another factor prevents that movement. One might ask whether the matter is truly ready if it cannot receive the movement's effect. In the author's account, the effect is a weaker form of the power, so matter that receives the power is also ready for its effects. This point does not settle the present case, since it would still allow the power in a paralyzed organ to be one of sensation and movement.

[^V08-306]: The Arabic sentence begins with “if” but does not complete the conditional. The wording appears to be incomplete.

[^V08-307]: Here “the unseen realm” includes the higher realm of pure intellect. Otherwise, these inner powers belong to the unseen realm; they are not simply closer to it. They belong to its lower level, not its highest level.

[^V08-308]: The example actually shows that animals need imagination and memory, though the author means to show why they need the shared inner sense. He may be relying on the close link among these powers. More precisely, imagining is becoming aware of what is stored in the imagination. The shared inner sense can perceive both what comes from outside and what arises within, while imagination keeps that inner image.

[^V08-309]: This proof is stated loosely. The author has said elsewhere that perception is not a power spread through matter or attached to a material thing. The outer senses and the places linked to inner perception are signs of perception, not the true perceivers. So this passage resembles a proof, but it does not establish the claim on its own. The next chapter supports this point.

[^V08-310]: “The matter of the qualities felt through touch” means the basic elements that bear them. It may also mean the active and passive qualities. Saying that the body is “of the same kind” means that it shares their general nature. The organ must still be free of the particular quality it senses, as the author explains next. Although the body has a balanced quality, a middle state between two extremes is almost as if it had neither.

[^V08-311]: One might say that sight senses light, so its organ can be like light. The meeting point of the optic nerves is even called the meeting point of two lights. But how could the organ of hearing be like what it hears? How could it be made of sounds, as the organ of sight is said to be light? The answer is that the hearing organ is like air, which carries sound. The body's matter and its form join as one, and the hearing organ shares the air's clear, open quality. The text's references to something “like transparent material” concern hearing; “or something similar” refers to sight.

[^V08-312]: This argument seems to apply to the outer senses too. Elsewhere, the author says that the images we perceive—even through touch—are not the same as the physical images that are present outside us. Perhaps “material” here means that a physical organ must be present. These sensory images are less separate from matter than images held in imagination. Some call their realm Hurqalya, a Persian term for the imaginal world or its heavens.

[^V08-313]: “All bodies” means all the body's parts. Touch can be present in all of them, and it can sense all kinds of elemental bodies, not only vapor or soft material.

[^V08-314]: The author is repeating a view attributed to Avicenna: some people may not treat touch as one final kind of sense. I do not recall this argument earlier in the book. The author may have discussed it in *The Origin and the Return* and says he will return to it later.

[^V08-315]: At first, the rule seems to contradict itself: if the subtle spirit that carries a sense is of the same kind as what it senses, how can it be free of that quality? There is no contradiction. The organ and the sense power must be free of the particular quality, while still sharing its general nature. Their relation is like that between matter and form: matter is free of the form it receives, yet is suited to receive it. The organ of sight must be free of the images it sees, yet share their nature. The one who senses and what is sensed can then come together in perception.

[^V08-316]: The problem only shows that touch senses a difference in degree. It cannot detect heat if the organ has heat at exactly the same level. The organ's heat must differ: if it is weaker, the difference feels cold; if it is stronger, the sense detects the added heat. Experience confirms this, so there is no contradiction.

[^V08-317]: “Also” continues the earlier point: “All this...” The idea is that the balance of qualities in an animal's body needs a power to preserve it. The same idea can be applied to plants and other things, or restated as the need for something to preserve each body's form. But this argument is weak: a body's own form can preserve its arrangement and balance for some time. Animals need touch because their bodies are more delicate, their balance is finer, and they are more exposed to harm.

[^V08-318]: The sentence leaves out “them”: the question is how material things can perceive the forms within them, since those forms are not present to the things themselves. The point concerns how what is perceived is present to the perceiver, not how the perceiver is present to what is perceived.

[^V08-319]: The author's wording is loose in several ways. These points should be read alongside the earlier discussions of what knows and what is known, and of material and immaterial things.

[^V08-320]: The sentence means that the elements are not aware because they are divided by opposing qualities and are constantly changing. Opposition keeps one quality from being fully present; constant change also prevents lasting awareness. An animal's body changes too, but its soul is connected to a higher, more unified realm. The author also argues earlier that, if plants had touch, they would need powers of desire and movement and would count as animals.

[^V08-321]: The main text is discussing awareness through touch. Its argument may suggest that plants and lifeless things have a simple kind of awareness, but that is different from the sense of touch at issue here.

[^V08-322]: Mulla Sadra gives a fuller answer in *The Origin and the Return*. One might object that the senses detect opposing qualities, such as heat and cold, not “opposition” itself, which is an idea grasped by reason. Why, then, should the number of touch powers depend on kinds of opposition? Also, sight perceives opposites such as black and white through one power. Why could touch not do the same?

    One weak answer is that the basic qualities felt by touch—heat, cold, wetness, and dryness—differ more sharply than colors, smells, and tastes, so touch needs separate powers. The better answer is this: an animal's body has a balanced mixture of these basic qualities. When one of them is opposed by what the body touches, the animal is affected. The balanced state is cold compared with pure heat and hot compared with pure cold; it is wet compared with pure dryness and dry compared with pure wetness. So the power of touch can be counted as four powers, one for each way the body can be affected by an opposing quality. It is not necessary to count separate powers for every related difference, such as rough and smooth or hard and soft. Those are effects of the basic qualities, and counting them separately would lead to far more than four. The point is that touch responds to what opposes the body's balanced state, not to qualities that match it.

[^V08-323]: The objection does not settle the question. Why could the odor not be stirred up by the change caused by rubbing and vaporizing?

[^V08-324]: This, too, is not conclusive. The apple might wilt because the surrounding air repeatedly strikes it, just as air around an object can dry it out.

[^V08-325]: That is, heat the camphor until it becomes vapor and its whole substance disappears. The word “until” here means that the heating brings about this result.

[^V08-326]: “It” means the scented substance. First, vaporize the camphor; then spread the same amount of it through many different places by carrying it there, without further heating. The first process uses up more substance but produces a weaker smell; the second uses almost no additional substance yet produces a stronger smell. Here, “vapor” means the odor, and “vaporizing” means giving the air a quality.

[^V08-327]: What is carried onward is the air's quality, not the air itself. Striking or pulling something away makes the air ripple. The air that moves then draws the air beside it into the same movement. These ripples and changing qualities pass from one stretch of air to the next until they reach air that no longer follows them.

[^V08-328]: The argument is circular: it says that transmission causes hearing because sound travels, but sound's travel is what it needs to prove. A circular argument gives us only probability, not the certainty this question requires. As Fakhr al-Din al-Razi notes in discussing how rippling air produces sound, strong intuition may sometimes make it feel certain.

[^V08-329]: Today, natural philosophers and mathematicians have made deep studies of hearing, sight, and the other outer senses, supported by extensive experiments. Their valuable findings can help resolve some of the problems raised here and should be consulted in a fuller examination of these questions.

[^V08-330]: The person raising this objection should first ask how air ripples and takes on a shape. He seems not to have understood it, perhaps imagining that air takes the shape of letters as writing takes shape on a page. That is why he raised this objection. The rippling is more like the waves made when a stone is dropped into water; the changing quality is more like water taking on different colors in succession. For example, when someone says the Arabic word *ḍaraba* (“he struck”), the air ripples outward in circles, like water around a stone. As the speaker says the first sound, the air around them takes on that sound's quality, as water might turn yellow; with the next sound it takes on another quality, as if turning green; and with the last, another, as if turning red. These changing qualities spread around the speaker. The whole ripple and its quality cannot reach one person but only part of it reach another, since a quality cannot be divided in this way.

[^V08-331]: The very feature that seems to cause the problem also resolves it. Air takes on the letters in succession. Sound and speech do not persist unchanged. If the air held each letter as writing holds a shape on a page, the likeness of all its parts would indeed be a problem. But sound is not fixed either as it begins or as it continues. Each part of the similar air takes on each letter in turn. In this account, hearing does not repeat. If the air around you is warmed by your breath, then quickly cooled and returned to its usual state, does everyone nearby feel the heat over and over? No: the warmth has already vanished by the time it reaches touch. The same goes for the cold and the return to normal temperature. Apply this to the successive sounds ḍ, r, and b in *ḍarb*. One might object that, since there is no indivisible instant, the air's first quality lasts for some time, so each new arrival of air should make hearing repeat. But air is continuous: one part blocks another from arriving all at once, just as hearing cannot be divided into separate acts. Or, if we allow the point for the sake of argument, each quality in one stretch of air may prepare the neighboring stretch to take on the next quality. The preparation comes and goes as the new quality appears.

[^V08-332]: No one holds that the same parcel of air travels all the way from the striker to the struck object and then to the ear; this is only a far-fetched possibility. The air released by the strike at one end of a long distance cannot cut through all the air between there and the ear. The note may instead point to what we said: one stretch of air prepares the next to take on the quality, so that the quality reaches the ear without the original air itself traveling there.

    The view chosen by the author is strong and avoids the difficulties about hearing direction, nearness, and distance. It also accounts for cases that challenge the other account: people hear outside sounds with their heads under water, and can hear a stick striking the side of a water-filled basin. Ringing in the head comes from movement of inner vapors and air. We have already seen that the evidence from sound traveling in air does not prove that rippling causes sound or that transmission causes hearing. This view could also make sense of reports that Pythagoras and others heard sounds from the heavenly spheres. Still, the third and fifth pieces of evidence given earlier seem to require transmission through air. Perhaps there is another hidden condition, or some obstacle such as air that is too thin or too dense.

[^V08-333]: The position need not involve contact in the same way for every sense. Powers differ in subtlety and in how far they rise above matter and mixture with it. The spatial conditions they require differ accordingly. This answers the objection that touch and taste involve contact even though what they perceive is not material: what they perceive must touch the sense organ.

[^V08-334]: This is only an example. On this view, the subtle body between the sound and the organ might be water, or it might be a heavenly sphere.

[^V08-335]: The argument uses one word, “power,” in both of its meanings at once. This is generally disallowed by writers on logic and language. Here the two meanings are the capacity to be affected and the ability to act.

[^V08-336]: An editor objects that this account is made less likely by the fact that we sometimes hear sounds incorrectly; there is no sense in saying that the external sound itself is mistaken. The sounds we hear are also approximate rather than exact.

[^V08-337]: The objection also applies to touch. As explained earlier, touch does not perceive the direction from which something came, yet what it senses is not universal. A sound, too, has particular features besides its direction. The reply is that, while touch may not perceive the direction from which a touched thing came, it does perceive the direction of the body part that carries the sense of touch. So its object is not universal.

    If hearing first perceives a nearby sound and only later, by tracing the waves, a distant one, it would perceive the same sound as both near and far. If it does not perceive the sound at the ear and has not yet traced it back to its distance, then it first hears a sound without any place or direction and so hears a universal sound. That is impossible. It would also fail to perceive the other details that make the sound particular. If it cannot perceive where the air carrying the sound is, then it cannot perceive the direction of the sound's source either.

[^V08-338]: Even if the premises of this argument were proved with certainty, it would show only that a higher level of existence contains what is found in a lower one—for example, intellect and the imaginal realm contain what is found in the bodily realm. It would not prove that one body contains every perfection found in another body, even if one body is subtler and more refined than the other. In any case, the argument offered here is not a proof.

[^V08-339]: I say: the view he chose about sight is a powerful one, with sound insight, and well suited to teaching and learning. Yet what the Shaykh of Illumination, al-Suhrawardi, says is also lofty and precious. It points to the soul's highest states. He explicitly says that souls are pure lights without a fixed essence, because they never stop rising beyond each level they reach. Their existence has no boundary at which it comes to rest.

    An essence is what can be bounded by a definition that includes what belongs to it and excludes what does not. It expresses a limit in existence, if existence can be limited. The author's path concerns the perceiver's union with what is directly perceived; the Shaykh's path concerns its union with what is perceived indirectly. The latter is included in the former. Union with what is directly perceived entails union with what is indirectly perceived, since things enter the mind as they are. Their essence is the same in both realms, while existence is both what distinguishes them and what they share. A single reality is multiplied only when something else, of whatever kind, intervenes between its instances.

    Illuminationist knowledge can also be stronger or weaker. The soul's knowledge of itself, its powers, and its body is all direct and present knowledge, since all of them are present to it as a thing is present to itself. This means simply that a thing is never separate from itself, though this presence can vary in strength. Even when the soul forgets its Lord and is made to forget itself, its knowledge of itself remains present knowledge. When it comes to know itself and thereby knows its Lord, that too is present knowledge. But what a difference there is between those two states!

    How could the soul fail to know things through an illuminating relation, when pure existence is the basis of the perceiver's own existence? Every being has what it is in, why it is, what it is, and whether it is. In the divine section of *The Four Journeys*, while explaining the saying “The perfection of knowing Him is to affirm Him,” the author says that anyone who understands the meaning of Necessary Existence—as the fullest and most complete existence, on which all possible beings with their incomplete natures depend—also understands that there must be a Necessary Existent. If there were not, nothing in the world would exist. But that conclusion is false, so its premise must be false as well. The truth of existence is known fully through direct, witnessing knowledge, because, as we established earlier, the image of a thing in knowledge must itself be the reality of the thing known.

    The author appears to limit direct, present knowledge to two cases, but that is only when the question is viewed broadly. The objections against the Shaykh can be answered.

    The first objection is answered by saying that what is material in relation to the first principles is not material in relation to those principles. The author himself cites the learned Mirdamad to this effect in the discussion of forms in the First Journey. The same can be said of the soul, considered from its hidden and more inward level.

    The second objection is answered by the Shaykh's suggestion that one of the images exists in the imaginal world. That is a reasonable view, but the author gives no reason for dismissing it.

    The third objection is answered by noting that the author's claim—that perception is only an image of a thing being present to the perceiver—requires a particular relation between them. This is contradicted by our knowledge of ourselves, where everyone agrees that knower and known are one. It is also contradicted by cases in which the author, Porphyry, and others say that the knower becomes one with what it knows. The soul has another kind of unity as well.

    The fourth objection is answered by what we have said about the name “illuminating relation.”

    I have discussed this at length even though it goes beyond the scope of these notes, because the author rejects this view in many of his works, including *The Four Journeys*, *The Throne of Wisdom*, and his notes on *The Wisdom of Illumination*. I have found the view true, and so I have not concealed it. I have lifted some of its veils and brought out a few of its secrets, on the condition that you keep them from those who should not hear them. God is witness between you and me.

[^V08-340]: The principles proved in the higher study of the intellect and what it understands show that the thing perceived is an image free of matter, and that it is perceived by the immaterial soul. But this does not remove the need for natural inquiry into the connection between the bodily power rooted in the body and external matter. Experience shows that perception is connected to the body and to external material things. So the questions of how an image is imprinted or how rays leave the eye still need to be studied, even after we establish that the image perceived is immaterial.

[^V08-341]: As explained earlier, saying that sight and the other senses produce a knowing image in the soul does not remove the need to study how material sensation occurs—that is, how a bodily power interacts with external matter. So the author's argument against the theories of imprinting and rays, based only on the claim that perception belongs to the soul, does not refute either theory.

[^V08-342]: As the scholar al-Qushji says, the image is in the shared inner sense. There is a difference between actually seeing something and imagining it, and there is no doubt that the state described here is one of seeing.

[^V08-343]: A Persian verse arranges the parts of the eye in this order: the hard outer coat, the choroid, the retina, the vitreous fluid, the crystalline fluid, the arachnoid layer, the albuminous fluid, the iris, the cornea, and the joining layer. The traditional anatomy described here divides the eye into seven coats and three fluids. The hard coat begins at the end of the tough membrane around the hollow optic nerve. Anatomists say there are two membranes inside the skull around the brain, one thick and one thin, as well as an outer membrane covering the skull.

    The choroid is described as woven from the ends of the thin membrane and from veins and arteries. It is compared to the placenta because of its many vessels, or because it forms a net around the eye's contents. The retina is said to begin at the ends of the hollow optic nerve. It surrounds the vitreous and crystalline fluids and extends to the arachnoid layer, like a net holding a catch. Others say its name comes from the many vessels that pass through it from the thin membrane.

    The vitreous fluid is described as clear, thick, and slightly reddish, like melted glass. It surrounds the back half of the crystalline fluid and feeds it. Since the crystalline fluid is very white, clear, and bright, the account says it cannot be made directly from blood; the vitreous fluid serves as an intermediary.
