It is clear because it feeds what is clear, reddish because it comes from blood, and thick so that it does not leak away. It lies behind the crystalline fluid because its supply comes from the brain through the retina, and so it must connect to it and lie closer to its source of nourishment.

    The crystalline fluid, also called the icy fluid, is described as clear and firm like ice. It is the eye's most important part, where sight occurs; the other parts serve and protect it. It is placed in the center because of its importance and is compared to a mirror in which images form. The arachnoid layer is said to be extremely thin, like a spider's web, and to cover the front of the crystalline fluid. It begins at the retina and separates the crystalline fluid from the albuminous fluid, which is excess nourishment. Constant contact with this excess would be harmful, so the layer is thin enough not to block light or images from reaching the crystalline fluid.

    The albuminous fluid is described as resembling egg white in color, clarity, and thickness. It lies in front of the crystalline fluid to soften strong light before it reaches it, keep it moist so air does not dry it out, and protect it from the roughness of the iris. The iris is described as a thick layer, hard on the outside and soft and sponge-like on the inside. Its central opening faces the crystalline fluid and lets light through, like the hole left when a grape is pulled from its stem. The iris also holds the albuminous fluid in place and keeps debris from reaching the pupil. Aristotle is said to have considered the eye's natural color black, because black gathers and strengthens sight and moderates light. Galen is said to have favored blue, arguing that black compresses the visual spirit too much while blue, with its whiteness, spreads it out and strengthens sight. The note adds that Aristotle supposedly had dark eyes and Galen very blue ones.

    The cornea is described as a hard, clear layer like white horn thinned by scraping. It begins at the hard outer coat and protects the layers and fluids beneath it. It is said to have four layers, like horn, so that if one is damaged, the others still protect the eye. It is clear so that light can pass through, like the glass of a lantern that protects the flame without blocking its light. The joining layer is described as a firm, clear, cartilage-like covering, connected to muscles and containing soft white tissue that lubricates the eye and eyelid and keeps them from drying out as they move. Hippocrates is said to trace it to the outer membrane over the skull, while Rufus traces it to the inner tough membrane. Unlike the other layers, it joins around the cornea rather than covering it; the note says that this is why it is called the joining layer. The account says these details are stated in medical works.

[^V08-344]: The front of the crystalline fluid is slightly flattened. At the back, it extends lengthwise so that it can fit into the hollow optic nerve.

[^V08-345]: It is better to say that these images are present in the shared inner sense. That sense receives images both from the outer senses and from inner imagination, as happens in sleep and similar states. Usually, the outer senses occupy it and prevent images from within from appearing. This obstacle is removed in sleep, when the outer senses weaken, or when they are deliberately weakened. The intellect's use of imagination for matters that are not sensed is another obstacle; illness can remove it. In some conditions affecting the brain, the first obstacle also falls away, either because the bodily vital spirit is too weak or scant to spread outward or because inner images become unusually strong. Once both obstacles are gone, the shared sense sees the images imagination creates, and imagination then retains them. The inner powers are like mirrors facing one another; the shared sense is like a two-sided mirror, facing outward and inward. The note adds that the answer in the main text may also mean that proof of how sight works in one case does not by itself establish how it works in every case.

[^V08-346]: The argument is not strong against this view. Someone who says that the second image seen by a person with crossed eyes, or an image in a mirror, exists in the imaginal world can also say that the images seen by people with certain illnesses exist there and are present to them.

[^V08-347]: On this account, visible objects that have images in matter are present directly to the soul with all their features: shape, position, direction, distance, and so on. The images seen by people with certain illnesses are in their own souls and arise from them, so they are present to them even more clearly. The first claim is hard for many people to accept, but the second should be clearer.

[^V08-348]: The claim that sight happens at once, without comparison or inference, is disputed. Careful experiments and investigation point the other way.

[^V08-349]: The author of *The Discussions* objects because the distinction between something with no size and something that can take on any size has been overlooked.

[^V08-350]: One can object that this argument is disproved by clear water, crystal, and similar things: they have no color, yet images appear in them. The reply is that, although the crystalline fluid has no color, it has a substance or thickness like water, unlike air. Anything with substance and a distinct form can receive an image. This means that the author's praise of the next two arguments as strong applies only to the last one.

[^V08-351]: The first argument assumes, without proof, that whatever color reaches the eye remains pure. The second says that what someone sees in another person's crystalline fluid is not the visual image by which sight itself occurs. This has been established, so both arguments apply only against those who say that an image is imprinted in the crystalline fluid.

[^V08-352]: First, it is not established that a physical body can act on a distant body only after acting on every nearer one. The sun warms and lights things here, but does not do the same to things nearer to itself.

[^V08-353]: In the author's view, all these factors still play a part by preparing the conditions for sight.

[^V08-354]: This is a fair statement. But the author should allow the same point to those who support imprinting, since their claim does not conflict with the soul being the one that perceives.

[^V08-355]: One might object that the angles and the circle become smaller, but the visible object does not shrink. Why not let the base of the cone cover only part of the object, so we see that part and then mistake the whole for something small? The reply is that on the ray view, the base of the cone is where sight occurs. At a distance it works like a small mirror: the whole object appears in it, but small. On the imprint view, the object's image is imprinted in a small area of the crystalline fluid, forming an imagined cone whose point is at the center of the fluid and whose base is the object. As the object moves farther away, the angle narrows and the part of the fluid receiving its image becomes smaller, so the image is smaller too. On the ray view, a distant object also looks less clear because the ray weakens as it travels farther from its source.

[^V08-356]: In air, the visible object does not stay large because the cone narrows as the object gets farther away. In water, the rays gather together, so the image looks like it is in a larger mirror and the object appears larger.

[^V08-357]: One might object that rays and water are both simple, with similar parts, so all the rays should either travel straight or bend in the same way. The reply is that the ray along the axis is stronger. Differences in strength could explain why some rays travel straight and others bend.

[^V08-358]: The point is that after shutting and reopening the eyes, at first we may see the moon—or any other object—as two, as if one image were pulling away from the other. The main text gives only a brief account.

[^V08-359]: The fingers are not placed on the eyes in different positions; they are placed on one eye in a way that changes its position. The finger presses the eyelid inward and pushes the eyeball outward, changing its usual arrangement.

[^V08-360]: The wording is sound; it simply leaves out “in the sky” in the first part. In the second, the scholars say that we see a moon in the sky, not in the water. Their view is that rays reflect from the mirror toward the visible object, forming an angle there. When the viewer faces a polished surface, the ray from their sight reflects from it toward the viewer, who sees themself rather than an image or likeness. Because they do not notice the reflection, they think they see themself directly.

[^V08-361]: So the source reads.

[^V08-362]: Many scholars give this explanation. But if it were right, we should hear one sound as two, since we have two ear canals, unless something brings the sound together as one.

[^V08-363]: The ray theorists' point would work if it were established that a person with one crossed eye does not see one object as two. The note's author says he has not verified this.

[^V08-364]: This is a good explanation of how sight might work, but it is not the only possible one. The same arrangement could be explained by rays entering the eye from the visible object rather than leaving the eye and traveling toward it.

[^V08-365]: These two explanations are weak. First, if we deliberately cross our eyes, or look at two aligned sticks at different distances and focus on one of them, the visual spirit would have to be both moving and still at the same time, or moving in two opposite states. Second, the explanations could lead us to see more than two images, as when a lamp is reflected many times in rippling water.

[^V08-366]: Here, “indirectly related” means related through something else, in a loose sense. Strictly speaking, an indirect relation is only a figure of speech and cannot prove the point. The argument also falls short because it assumes that a physical effect must take place outside the body that causes it. That is not established; forms can act on the matter and bodies that receive them. Nor has it shown that the affected body must be separate from the cause. The claim that two connected bodies act like one is an analogy, not a proof. This question should be settled by observation, not by reasoning alone.

[^V08-367]: The size that allows a natural body to be divided, whether by actual cuts or in thought, is an essential feature of that body, not a feature that exists separately from it. The arrangement of a body's parts, set by how they relate to one another, is part of how the body exists and what makes it the body it is. “Position” can mean a simple relation to something else, such as being beside or facing it. It can also mean the arrangement of a body's parts in relation to one another and to what surrounds it, as when a person stands upright. If the parts keep the same arrangement but their relation to the surroundings changes, the first kind of position changes but the second does not. The author means this second kind of position: the body's own arrangement, which is part of its existence. Even if he also means an outside relation, such as nearness, the reasoning can still be followed: making an effect depends on existence, and a body's existence depends on an arrangement that connects it with what surrounds it, so its effect also requires a suitable outside relation, such as nearness.

[^V08-368]: “Position is a way the body exists” means that position is one part of what determines the body's identity. Some philosophers also say that visible, measurable shape is a stage in the existence of a natural body. The idea becomes clearer on that view.

[^V08-369]: One might object that if being very close makes contact, sight should occur even more easily when the object is extremely close. Why then must a transparent medium lie between them? The reply is that each kind of effect requires a certain arrangement: the object must not be too close or too far, and the connection must run through a transparent medium.

[^V08-370]: The three possibilities match the three views of sight. The first is the ray view, in which light travels from its source to what it lights. The second is the view that rays leave the eye and reach the object. The third is the imprint view, in which an image travels from the object to the eye.

[^V08-371]: The argument is well formed, but one of its starting points is only assumed: that human beings are the most complete animals among all living things. Earlier philosophers based their account of creation on the natural sciences and astronomy of their time. They believed that all living animals lived on the earth. Of the kinds they had examined and tested, they regarded humans, with reason and thought as well as animal senses, as the most complete. On that basis, they concluded that humans are the most complete animals known to us. If we accept modern scientific ideas that life might exist elsewhere, it is hard to prove that no other animal could be more complete than humans. We would need a separate argument to show that no such animal could exist.

[^V08-372]: On the common view, the argument is incomplete because something may be possible in itself without being able to occur in practice. It works if “possible” here means possible in the natural world. It also works on the view that everything possible in itself exists in the world of intellect, where it needs no material preparation; intellect can then bring about what lies below it.

[^V08-373]: On the view being discussed, these might instead be called shared things sensed indirectly, since the five senses are said to sense only qualities. Size, position, and similar features would then not be directly sensed. The author replies that these features themselves affect the sense and are present to it. We sense them in their own right, not merely because they accompany another sensible quality. They are not indirect just because another feature helps us recognize them.

[^V08-374]: The source passage is not fully clear. The note's likely meaning is that each sense has its own proper object, while shared features—such as distance or shape—can be perceived through more than one sense and with help from thought.

[^V08-375]: Direction is a shared feature that all the senses can perceive. Even taste can tell whether a flavor comes from outside the mouth or from the throat. It is harder to say that senses other than sight and touch perceive size directly.

[^V08-376]: This refers to where something is. Taste senses qualities such as flavor strongly, but senses where something is only weakly.

[^V08-377]: This is why some philosophers found bodily resurrection difficult and turned to the idea of transmigration. In the author’s view, the front part of the brain is where common sense appears, not where it resides. Calling it a power of the soul means that it belongs to the soul, not the body. Saying that it is prepared to arise in the brain means that the soul’s power is disposed to act there, not that the power itself is physically located there. The soul is likewise prepared to be connected with the body, but is not physically situated in it.

[^V08-378]: An objection: the one who judges must have both things being compared, and the relation between them, present to the mind. Since that relation is a meaning perceived by estimation, this argument does not prove common sense. The reply is that relations among images are themselves image-like, just as relations among meanings are conceptual. The sky’s being above the earth is a relation of position; the soul’s being above its powers is a conceptual relation. Common sense perceives the first, while estimation perceives the second when it concerns a particular thing.

[^V08-379]: I say that the argument is strong. Matter and form, though both kinds of substance, are not a body by themselves. Their combination becomes one, forming a new kind of thing: a body. In the same way, the whiteness we see and the sweetness we taste do not by themselves ground the judgment that “this sugar is sweet.” Nor is their real combination enough. Combining things is the work of a power that acts, not one that perceives—just as wood can join one thing to another without being aware that it has done so. The judgment itself is the perception of the real combination. Judgment is a kind of knowledge: perceiving that a relation does or does not hold. This particular judgment, the perception of how these qualities come together, belongs to common sense, not to intellect at its higher level, since intellect does not perceive or judge particulars there. Even if intellect does perceive particulars, it does so at its lower level, the level of the senses. The power that judges particulars at that level is common sense. If intellect perceived particular sensible things at its higher level, there would be no need for the senses.

[^V08-380]: I say this is not a real combination or identity, but a unity of another kind. It does not require a separate power. For example, Zayd is a limited, material instance of human nature, while the universal human nature is immaterial. How can one simply be the other, as if they were both particulars or both universals? Their relation is more like that of a trace to its reality. A material thing and an immaterial thing do not form one natural kind, so there is no need for a separate power that unites them. The spiritual heart is enough. In mystical terminology, the heart is the substance that perceives both general ideas and particular things.

[^V08-381]: The point is that intellect can judge between particulars even though it does not perceive them directly. But the same objection raised against the author applies: if the judge does not perceive particular images, how can it judge? If it perceives them through bodily powers and organs, but is unaware of what those organs perceive, how can it judge then? And if their images are reflected onto the intellect itself, how can they be imprinted on something immaterial? If that is possible, why would the senses be needed?

[^V08-382]: Bahmanyār’s “another power” means another power in relation to taste and sight, not a power separate from the soul. It would be clearer to say “a different power.”

[^V08-383]: Even if it does not follow that the two things being judged must be perceived without any intermediary, a particular judgment cannot take place without common sense. I think Bahmanyār means common sense by “another power,” and by denying perception he means denying direct perception.

[^V08-384]: The moving drop occupies one position after another; the point seen as a line is spread across those positions.

[^V08-385]: There is no difference between the past or future being separated from the present by centuries or by a few seconds. If sight could perceive these absent things, it could perceive them all, since they are equally absent.

[^V08-386]: There is an easier argument than the one given by the author: if common sense did not exist, imagination would not be possible. Imagination’s work is to preserve the forms of the five senses. Common sense perceives them in imagination, while imagination preserves them. Each power has its own role, just as each natural power has a distinct role. For example, common sense perceives the image of a speaking or nonspeaking animal; imagination preserves the image and the perception of it.

[^V08-387]: The phrase is vocalized with two ḍamma vowels.

[^V08-388]: The name is shared with the formative power found in plants. One might say that “formative” should describe common sense, since common sense perceives and imagination forms. But common sense is like a mirror with two faces, one turned outward and one inward. Imagination forms the inward face.

[^V08-389]: The point is to prove in general that the powers differ: if one were identical with the other, no case could arise in which one worked without the other. The example of water is not the point of the argument. So the objection that it is only an example does not apply. This proof establishes difference because one power can work without the other; the next two arguments establish it because the powers do opposite things. These are the grounds for distinguishing powers.

[^V08-390]: The Imam objects that imagination also receives an image before preserving it. The answer is that receiving an image in imagination means forming it there.

[^V08-391]: This objection concerns the main claim, not the argument for it. The proof rests on the fact that opposing functions cannot belong to the same power in the same way.

[^V08-392]: I say this objection can be answered: something can be preserved without being judged or accepted. The active intellect, for example, can preserve false ideas without accepting them as true. This is like the distinction between receiving and preserving. Why does the objection work in the first case but not the second?

[^V08-393]: I think this argument is weak. If imagining means preserving an image, then it repeats the first argument. If it means arranging or perceiving images, it proves the imaginative or estimative power, not the power of imagination that preserves them. A better proof is recollection: we may sense an image and later remember that it is the same image we sensed before. To judge that they are the same, the image must have been preserved in a stable place—namely, imagination.

[^V08-394]: The source meant here may be the imaginal world, the soul impressed on the celestial sphere, or the imaginal forms and the Trumpet mentioned in scripture. It does not mean God or the active intellect, since immaterial beings are not places where particular imagined forms are stored.

[^V08-395]: That is, the actual intellect.

[^V08-396]: The established habit is an effect of imagination. If “the source that gives images” means this habit, there is no disagreement. If it means an external source, that is not right, since our own experience tells us the habit is within us. Immediate perception as a present act differs from the stable ability to recall something. The first belongs to common sense; the second to imagination.

[^V08-397]: I have already said that the imaginative power’s work is to act, as when it joins or arranges things without perceiving them. So its work requires the things to be present, but not perceived. Tusi’s reply in effect denies that this work falls under the rule that a judge must have both things before it. This is an act of arranging, not a judgment that a relation holds.

[^V08-398]: The objector calls using a power an act of that power. If a power acts through an instrument, then the instrument itself must be used by another instrument, and so on without end. Yet no intermediate power is required if the instrument is a sense faculty. It is unlikely that “instrument” here means the vital spirit or bodily organs. The correct reply is that perception and action are one here: estimation’s acting on images is itself how it perceives them. The same applies at each higher level in relation to the lower. The soul uses and perceives its powers, and its perception of them is the same as using them, since it knows them directly. Just as God’s knowledge is His power, the soul’s knowledge of its powers is their use.

[^V08-399]: The yellow thing may not really be honey. Estimation makes the mistaken judgment that it is honey. When the thing really is honey, the correct judgment belongs to common sense.

[^V08-400]: Estimation is the ruler of all the animal powers, and the imaginative power, which is its instrument, works in both the front and rear parts of the brain.

[^V08-401]: If this argument worked, it would show that estimation is intellect restricted to particular things. But it does not. Affection, one of the things estimation perceives, is not merely an abstract relation with no individual instances, nor is it a general kind that has only one instance. There are many particular affections: a person’s love for a child, an animal’s love for its young, or a donkey’s attachment to something. One person may love a child, a spouse, and many other things; a thousand distinct affections may arise in a single day. These inclinations, longings, and affections are particular meanings that exist in the soul: they were not there, then came to be, and later pass away.

    A particular meaning of this kind must be perceived in relation to what it is about. The outer senses, common sense, and imagination cannot perceive it, since they perceive and preserve images, while affection and hostility are meanings. Intellect cannot perceive them either, since it perceives universals, while these are particular experiences. Their particularity is not merely a matter of being related to something. So there must be a power besides sense, imagination, and intellect. This is what philosophers mean by estimation. Intellect sees no difference in kind between whiteness and affection as general kinds that can have many instances. The difference is that whiteness has particular visible instances, while affection has particular inwardly experienced instances. The same goes for hostility, dislike, and similar feelings.

    The author’s reasoning depends on two claims. First, affection and hostility have no true particular instances of their own; they are only general relations attached to particular images. This is not so. In his discussion of will and aversion in *Theology*, he himself says that will and aversion are psychic qualities, like other mental qualities, and are known inwardly, like pleasure and pain. Their particular instances are easy to recognize. He adds that perceiving particular instances of each is linked to perceiving particular instances of other things.

    Second, the argument assumes that animals without reason perceive particular meanings that are only relations by having their imagination connect with the governing forms of their kinds. Since they have no intellect, their estimation would then be intellect restricted to particulars. If this idea were meant to deny that evil has a real existence—some philosophers say evil is only incidental in the divine order—it should be limited to mistaken estimation, such as wrongly judging that a yellow object is honey or being afraid of a dead person. It should not be extended to a trained dog’s estimation, a parent’s love, trust, or fear of a dangerous enemy.

    If the reason for not treating these meanings as a separate realm is that they are not distinct from the images imagination perceives, that would be an arbitrary rule. One could just as well say they belong to a lower spiritual realm, since they are particular meanings in souls connected to spiritual and physical forms, or to a higher realm because of their nobility. I suspect the author was driven by his deep commitment to unity. Since inward meanings do not have the many visible forms, positions, places, and directions found in physical images, it was easier to treat them as one. Yet their subjects and times still differ. Strikingly, the author says this in several places, including *The Keys to the Unseen*.

[^V08-402]: There are obvious problems here. The intellect’s recognition of a meaning does not require another power to perceive its opposite. Sight and touch often make mistakes, and the intellect judges that what they perceive is false. Nor do we accept that hostility must be sensed if it is present in an individual. We do not sense bodyhood, unity, or existence with any of the five senses; the senses perceive only a body’s qualities. The author’s proof may be weak, but there is another way to question whether estimation is a separate power. Sensible things are perceived by common sense after they have first been present to the outer senses, such as sight, hearing, and touch. The soul knows these things directly through a kind of union with the bodily powers. In the same way, our ordinary, representational knowledge of inner meanings such as affection and hostility may be preceded by the soul’s direct awareness of them. Why could common sense not perceive these inner qualities, just as it perceives external qualities? The fact that they are called meanings, in contrast to images, because qualities such as heat and blackness can belong to things outside the soul and body, does not prove that they require another independent power. Consider this carefully. If common sense is enough to perceive meanings, then imagination may also be enough to preserve them, and there may be no need for a separate memory power.

[^V08-403]: Affection, hostility, and similar states belong to the soul, not the body. The soul itself is a particular meaning, which estimation perceives in a general way; this will be discussed when the author treats the soul’s immateriality.

[^V08-404]: After saying that estimation perceives general meanings attached to images, the author clarifies that he means meanings the mind draws from particulars, such as causality, not qualities such as blackness. For the sake of consistency, however, he should have said that the second kind is perceived either by intellect alone or by one of the senses.

[^V08-405]: There is room to object to this. Our experience suggests otherwise: it is hard to imagine voluntary actions in animals without a judgment, and judgment requires estimation or intellect.

[^V08-406]: “The soul is all the powers” does not mean that the soul is merely their sum, since a sum has no separate existence or unity. It does not mean that two things become one, or that the soul leaves one level to take on another. Rather, the soul is the source that remains present at every level: the spirit behind spirits and the form behind forms. It does not leave its higher level when it takes on the features of a lower one, nor does it leave its lower level behind when it develops into a more complete one. This happens through the soul’s own development and change in its being. It has many powers in one, and one soul through many powers. Its existence is one, though the ideas we form from its different levels are not all the same.

[^V08-407]: This is also an argument from what we know. What we know is something we want or value, and the one who judges must have both sides before them. But if the one who knows what is desirable were not also the one moved to want it, knowledge would not give rise to desire, and desire would not give rise to knowledge. That is not so. So the one who knows and the one who desires are one.

[^V08-408]: The ideas we draw from the soul’s powers are not all the same. Each action has its own name and meaning.

[^V08-409]: One might say that this third argument adds nothing, since it is obvious that the soul perceives sensible things. I reply that the argument is not about ordinary, image-based perception. Many people think that this perception belongs to the body’s organs, not the soul. But direct awareness of the body and its powers does belong to the soul. It must happen without an instrument; otherwise, another instrument would be needed to perceive the first one, and the process would never end. So one soul, in different states, perceives both particular things and general ideas.

[^V08-410]: This can be challenged. The argument assumes, without proof, that the body cannot be a place for desire or anger. Another factor might prevent a desire or anger from arising in a particular part of the body.

[^V08-411]: The argument shows that desire and anger are immaterial powers, but not by itself that they are the soul. They could belong to another immaterial power. I say the conclusion does follow: a material power would not belong to the soul, but an immaterial power does, since the soul has material, imaginal, and intellectual levels.

[^V08-412]: This objection is fair. The claim assumes that the brain’s vital spirit has no more capacity to hold images than any other body, but there is no proof of that.

[^V08-413]: If the imagined size is an immaterial, imaginal form, and imagination is one with what it imagines, then this already proves that imagination is immaterial at the imaginal level.

[^V08-414]: If Avicenna allowed that the soul creates an image rather than receiving an imprint, this conclusion would not follow. The soul is also immaterial at the imaginal level.

[^V08-415]: This is based on Avicenna’s rejection of change in a thing’s essence and of the soul’s becoming one with intellect after first becoming one with sense. He does not reject the claim that existence is real in the world or that it can become stronger and weaker.

[^V08-416]: This applies especially to the vital spirit. If a body or bodily thing could perceive, it would be the most likely place for perception, since the powers are said to exist in it as qualities or forms. The vital spirit is finer than the rest of the body, breaks down more quickly, and needs more replacement. That is why doctors try to strengthen and restore it through food and medicine.

[^V08-417]: There is another possibility: one image could be formed in the original part only. The author does not mention it because a new part would have to take the place of one that broke down, and the replacement should have the same form as what it replaces.

[^V08-418]: “Separate sources” here means the soul that is spread through the heavens. Separate intellects cannot contain particular images. Since the author means the soul in the heavens, he raises the question only about the soul.

[^V08-419]: The text may originally have read: “Just as when the soul gains the ability to connect with intellect, it can recover images from the active intellect after they fade, so it can do the same here.” The copies I have seen read as printed.

[^V08-420]: A copy made in 1211 AH reads: “When the soul gains the ability to connect with intellect, and the images fade, it can recover them from the active intellect.”

[^V08-421]: There is no problem on the right account. At the imaginal level, the soul is immaterial in a way that allows it to take on imaginal forms. The difficulty is how these images are made present to the soul, not how they are physically lodged in it.

[^V08-422]: The words “although it is” should be removed from the sentence.

[^V08-423]: People may think general ideas come from the heart or brain because they meet them clothed in words and sensible images. Words are sounds made by the lungs and mouth; images, even of touchable things, appear in the head. This can make it seem as if general ideas are located in the heart or brain. But general ideas are immaterial. When a person understands one, the immaterial self takes in all its outward and inward instances. No pine-shaped heart, sticky brain, or bodily spirit could do this.

[^V08-424]: The individual parts of the body do not truly hear, see, or speak as a person does. “The whole” here means the physical body, which many people think is the human being. Jami’s *Golden Chain* jokes about this common view: it defines a human as an upright animal with broad nails, bare skin, and no hair. Anyone who looks like that is then assumed to be human.

[^V08-425]: Even someone who believes that images are physically imprinted can answer this objection. Position and place may belong to the image’s existence without belonging to its definition. The fire imagined in the mind does not burn, and imagining a straight line does not make the mind straight.

[^V08-426]: The author’s wording is surprising. Mental existence includes both imagined forms and intellectual ideas; both are called what they are in a basic sense, not in the ordinary sense. A mental sphere is not an ordinary material sphere. It is an imaginal or intellectual form.

[^V08-427]: The stronger reply is like the earlier one about a general sphere. A general square, and the general ideas of right and left, are not right or left in the ordinary sense. If the right and left are particular, the mind is not simply choosing their position; a choice that conflicts with reality would be false. The author does not use the distinction between these two ways of saying “square,” though it appears often in his books, and the people making the argument have missed it.

[^V08-428]: This refers to “not made of matter.”

[^V08-429]: The forms made by the soul are the source from which the heavenly spheres come.

[^V08-430]: Calling the knowledge of higher, immaterial sources “conceptions” is not exact. Their knowledge is direct; it is not made up of ideas and judgments in the ordinary sense.

[^V08-431]: There is no matter here to receive a new form, as wax receives the shape of a square. Giving an image a specific identity is not a second act added to its creation. The defining feature follows from the image itself.

[^V08-432]: This is like the forms of the afterlife, which do not have the place and position found in this world.

[^V08-433]: Changes in the next world do not happen because matter takes on one form after another. There is no matter there. Changes such as new skins or new kinds of pleasure come from one creation followed by another. The details will be discussed in the sections on the afterlife.

[^V08-434]: When an imagined form disappears, it does not turn into something else that can serve as its matter. It simply ceases to be. This is different from material things, which change into something else.

[^V08-435]: The distinction between images in the world of imagination does not mean they have positions in the physical world. This note corrects the wording of the sentence it follows.

[^V08-436]: The relation is part of what makes a particular instance what it is; it is not an added thing alongside it. If the relation were an extra object, the difference between the general kind and the particular instance would not be only a matter of thought. This also challenges the argument that estimation perceives a compound of meaning and image. If you ask how estimation can link a meaning to an image without knowing the image, the answer is like saying that Zayd is a human being: it is not a real joining of two separate things. The image is perceived by imagination; the particular meaning is perceived by intellect linked to imagination, or by estimation in the usual account. The relation makes it particular.

[^V08-437]: Hostility joined to a particular image is not one single thing. It is perceived by intellect working with imagination, not by intellect connected to imagination alone. This also challenges the argument just given.

[^V08-438]: The power of resolve is the firm wish of practical reason, such as deciding to visit someone who is ill. Animal desire is the firm wish of the bodily motive power, such as visiting someone for personal gain. In a human being, theoretical and practical reason play roles like perception and movement in an animal. The higher source of an inclination is called will or love; the animal source is called appetite. Religious language also distinguishes them. Mystics sometimes use “love” of God and the soul, though prophets usually teach with simpler, proper terms.

    The author identifies will with the power that wills, as he identifies knowledge with the one who knows. Both estimation and imagination are immaterial, but estimation is at a higher level. The note that no one calls the power that urges action material is meant as a small exception to praise, a familiar figure of speech.

[^V08-439]: The philosophers’ classification of ability as a mental quality supports the author’s account that the soul is one through all its levels, reaching into the body.

[^V08-440]: The working power spreads through the body much like nature does. It acts through the muscles and limbs, while nature acts through simple and complex bodies.

[^V08-441]: These thinkers may have spoken about life without separating it into perception and movement. Yet they did, in effect, understand animal life through both. Those who followed the path of life also included perception and movement in their account, but did not treat them as separate paths.

[^V08-442]: This can be read as “a substance moved by itself,” or as meaning that the soul is a self-moving substance. In the author’s interpretation, this means the changing nature that is one level of the soul.

[^V08-443]: The copies I have seen read this way, though I cannot check Avicenna’s text now. “And some said” would be a clearer transition: those who did not avoid calling the soul a body divided into several groups, including those who said it was made of indivisible particles.

[^V08-444]: Here “source” means the source of the world, though some thinkers made it a source only through the first source, God. Thales of Miletus is said to have called water the first element, containing the forms of everything that exists or could exist. From it, earth formed by thickening and freezing, while fire and air formed by thinning. The sky, some said, came from smoke rising from fire. A story attributed to the Torah says that God created a substance, looked upon it, and its parts melted into water. Vapor rose from the water and formed the heavens; foam appeared on its surface and formed the earth and mountains. Similar stories appear in reports from the Imams. Other thinkers said that earth came first, or that air did, becoming fire when thin and earth when dense. Some named fire or vapor as the first source. Anaxagoras spoke of a mixture. Democritus proposed small, hard particles in an endless empty space. Dualists said light and darkness were the world’s two roots. Some Iranian thinkers proposed five eternal things: God, soul, matter, time, and empty space. The Pythagoreans said numbers came from unity and were the source of things.

    These views are false if taken literally, though many can be understood in a better way. Their argument can be set out like this: the soul perceives other things; anything that perceives other things is a source; every source is fire, air, earth, or water; so the soul is one of these. The same pattern lies behind the other views.

[^V08-445]: These thinkers counted the four elements as the roots of the world’s coming to be and continuing to exist. The world arises from them, while its survival depends on drawing what is useful and driving away what is harmful, through what they called Love and Strife.

[^V08-446]: These thinkers treated life as their route to the soul, and explained it through what gives life, supports it, prepares for it, or follows from it.

[^V08-447]: For these thinkers, the soul changes only in its qualities: it gains more knowledge, power, and similar features, while its underlying nature remains fixed. The author’s view is different. Through substantial movement, the soul itself changes in its being, while one underlying thread remains from its beginning to its end. This follows from his view that existence is what is real, and that what makes a thing distinct from others is the same as what makes it continuous through change. So the soul’s level as a potential intellect and its level as an actual intellect belong to one changing soul.

    The difference between a Prophet and the rest of the community is therefore a difference in the soul’s being, not merely in outside qualities. If such a difference exists within one soul, the difference between two souls may be greater still—like the difference between spirit and body, or between the spirit that gives itself up in the light of the One and the body it leaves behind.

[^V08-448]: In this account, every earlier form becomes like the material for the final, most complete form. The final form gathers and completes what came before. At the inner level, individuals of the human kind may therefore differ as kinds: some are intellects only in possibility, others are intellects in reality. The rising intellects relate to the final, perfected intellect as earlier forms relate to the final form. In one sense, they are that intellect, and it is them. The author points to sayings about the Prophet and the Imams: “You are remembered among those who remember; your names among the names; your selves among the selves; your spirits among the spirits; your bodies among the bodies.” He also cites the saying that a believer is a mirror for another believer.

[^V08-449]: The author has briefly pointed to the Prophet’s spiritual reality as the highest completion, for whose sake the rest was created. In this spiritual aspect, no angel or prophet equals him, since every soul and intellect comes from that source. He then turns to the Prophet’s ordinary human life, where likeness and comparison apply. A true knower must hold together both levels: the image and the meaning, the human form and its spiritual reality. The Qur'an says, “If We had made him an angel, We would have made him a man,” to avoid both exaggerating his status and denying the high rank of holy souls.

    What is said about the Prophets and Imams—their purity and their likeness to God—is true of their spiritual aspect. What is said about their human limits is also true of their bodily aspect. They eat, drink, sleep, and receive knowledge from Gabriel, because all their gifts come from God. Yet the body’s physical nature is overwhelmed by their spiritual nature. This is why it is said that the Prophet cast no shadow, saw behind him, and that the body of a believer does not decay.

[^V08-450]: Even a heavenly body, considered only as a body, can perish. The source of its motion is its soul or nature, which is outside the body when “body” means matter considered on its own. The soul and nature are not outside the body when body is considered together with its form.

[^V08-451]: The soul’s original nature must lead it toward its goal. Otherwise, this natural drive would be pointless. The goal of living souls is something lasting: God and the immaterial intellects, or the world of imagined and estimative forms. These remain through God’s lasting existence.

[^V08-452]: Movement can be divided into six kinds. Three begin from outside the moving thing: movement through place may begin because something is uncomfortable; the changes in mixed bodies depend on outside heat, cold, or moisture; and the movement of simple elements depends on the conditions around them. The changing nature within a body also moves by itself, but the conflicting elements destroy one another. The other three kinds are the movements of souls. They come from within and do not conflict with one another, so they continue.

[^V08-453]: The sphere stands for the simple shape of imagined bodies. Its roundness is also explained by the way soul and body are joined as form and matter. Here “body” means a heavenly body, not an imaginal body.

[^V08-454]: This seems to conflict with the earlier claim that the particles breathed in are imaginal forms, since breathing brings in the bodily vital spirit. The reply is that the vital spirit carries the powers and displays the imaginal forms. Without it, the soul’s image would have no bodily appearance. So refreshing the vital spirit also refreshes the forms it carries.

    A clearer reading is that the earlier thinkers meant imaginal bodies by “indivisible spheres,” while by what is breathed in they meant the vital spirit. They may instead have followed the theory of small, hard particles.

[^V08-455]: This explains the “fire with three branches”: a body has length, width, and depth. The author also links the body’s attachment to the natural world with the fire of punishment in the next life.

[^V08-456]: There is another way to interpret the number sequence. The first source is one and has no separate definition beyond its unity. From it comes the first intellect, which has two aspects: existence and essence. From that comes the soul, which has a third relation, its direction toward and care for matter. From the soul comes nature, which adds a fourth relation: being present in and acting directly on matter. This completes the active sources.

[^V08-457]: Potential intellect is like the matter of ideas, and sense is like the matter of sensible forms. Each is suited to receive its own kind, just as silver is suited to take the shape of what is made from it.

[^V08-458]: The soul does not become one with an object merely because the object is seen or understood indirectly. It becomes one with the form it directly perceives. This does not mean that the soul is made up of many parts. The forms it knows are present through the soul’s own being. As the soul becomes more complete, it gathers more things while becoming more simple.

[^V08-459]: This differs from the usual view of philosophers and natural scientists, who say that the vital spirit is made from the finest bodily fluids. The body’s organs, they say, are made from the thicker fluids. The author’s view is like his account of food: some things come from the soul itself, or from a natural power that obeys the soul. Other bodily powers may resist it and cause fatigue.

[^V08-460]: The soul is nourished by ideas and grows stronger through them. When its life appears in the body, bodily things nourish and strengthen that life. The soul rises toward the highest realm until it can stand before God. In the body, its power appears as growth, movement, and reproduction. Each soul helps produce another like itself, so life continues. In this way, all forms in the lower world are shadows of lights and qualities in the higher world.

[^V08-461]: The source of all things has three levels: God, the existence that flows from Him, and the limited forms that result. The source itself is beyond being a substance or a quality. Earlier thinkers may have meant that God is present in things through His action, not as a separate thing alongside them. Since His action is His command and light, they spoke as if He were nature, soul, or intellect in each thing. But care is needed: each level must be kept distinct and placed in its proper role.

[^V08-462]: Here “source” means the thing that gives a movement, not a second object of a verb.

[^V08-463]: The point is that every moving thing needs a source, but not always in the same way. A change in an outer feature needs something to cause that movement. A change in the thing’s very being needs something to give it existence. In this kind of movement, the changing being itself is its movement; the source does not first make it exist and then add movement to it. In outward movement, the source gives the thing existence and also causes the added movement. In either case, all movement needs a source, and the chain of moving things ends at a source that does not move.

[^V08-464]: The word “change” here is used broadly. In the usual terms, it means change in quality. Change in a thing’s substance is called coming-to-be, as explained earlier.

[^V08-465]: Substantial movement does not mean that a thing’s definition changes at every moment. Avicenna treats a changing essence as if it became an entirely different kind of thing at each moment. But the definition can remain fixed while the thing’s existence flows and changes. The whole natural world, from the heavens down to the earth, is renewed in its being. This is how the author joins the changing nature of the world with the claim that its definitions do not change.

[^V08-466]: The movement must be one individual existence unfolding through time; otherwise, there could be no gradual change. Its definition can remain one, like the definition of a human being, even though that definition is expressed through many distinct beings.

[^V08-467]: Separate intellects do not change. They are complete and do not wait to become something else. Change is possible in kinds of substance that depend on matter.

[^V08-468]: The highest general kind, such as substance, does not turn into another general kind. Nor does a specific kind of substance turn into another kind during ordinary gradual change. But many kinds of change can take a thing beyond its kind: a color may weaken until it is no longer that color, and a human soul may develop until it becomes angel-like or decline until it becomes devil-like or beast-like.

[^V08-469]: When the mind is only potential intellect, it receives ideas. When it connects with the active intellect, it can create and preserve them. Or “receiving” may simply mean that the soul is described as having an idea; on either reading, one thing does not have to be both receiver and creator in the same way.

[^V08-470]: The two parts can each be understood because the condition for understanding applies to them; the whole cannot be understood in that way once the condition is missing. The contrast may be between the whole before division and a part afterward, or between their identities and their accidental features. Either way, if understanding required division, it would require the opposite of what it needs.

[^V08-471]: If understanding depended on division, then every intelligible thing would have to be divisible. But some intelligible things are simple and cannot be divided.

[^V08-472]: A part of a magnitude is one particular portion of it, while a particular thing can carry its whole kind. The two objections here may be one or two arguments. Either way, if a smaller part were enough to contain the form, the supposed form would be present with features unrelated to what it is. It would always be understood together with something that does not help complete its meaning.

[^V08-473]: The questioner points to a mistaken reversal: every division into measured parts gives similar parts, but not every division into similar parts is a division of size. Dividing a universal into its particular kinds is an example.

[^V08-474]: The phrase about adding “rational” to “animal” may sound as if human and horse natures differ, even though the questioner says their animal nature is the same. The reply is that adding a different feature forms a new kind; it does not split one reality into physical pieces. A universal kind is never found outside its particular instances as one separate thing. Even in the mind, its divisions are made by considering it in different ways. If it were literally divided, its parts would have size.

[^V08-475]: This would also require an accident to move from one subject to another.

[^V08-476]: These souls may not yet understand in a fully intellectual way, but they have a capacity close to understanding. If divine care gives them a true intellectual form, they must be ready to receive it, since gifts follow the recipient’s capacity.

[^V08-477]: Most people cannot understand a universal as a purely intellectual existence, shared equally by many instances. They picture it as an extended thing covering its instances, much as they picture existence as a beam of light. This comes from the strength of imagination and the weakness of their developing intellect. People trained in theory or practice can correct these images with reason. For many, “immaterial” means only that something is not material; a few understand it as a nature stripped of all accompanying features.

[^V08-478]: The author’s account seems to distinguish three things: a particular idea perceived by sense or imagination; a universal in the strict philosophical sense; and an indeterminate idea that can be considered in different ways. Philosophers usually mean the second when they speak of universals. If minds genuinely understand a universal that is free of particular limits, this understanding is immaterial, though immateriality itself has degrees. The author’s account may deny ordinary people the highest degree of this understanding, but it cannot deny that universal ideas exist in our minds.

[^V08-479]: The argument moves from number to other realities and then to intellectual forms. The fact that ten can be divided does not prove that other things, especially intellectual forms, can be divided. Ten is divisible because it is a multitude of units; bodies are divisible because they have matter. The argument concerns universal ten as understood by the intellect, not an endless series of individual tens.

[^V08-480]: Ten is not identical with the many units that compose it, though it can be understood as made up of them. Its meaning as a number includes unity.

[^V08-481]: The problem with Avicenna’s claim about multitude and unity has already been discussed. In his examples, unity itself includes the multiplicity of parts. That is why some philosophers say that division belongs to the thing represented, not to the feature that represents it.

[^V08-482]: If “division” means actual separation, the body itself is not what is divided; matter is. If it means division in thought, magnitude is what is directly divisible, and the body is divisible through it.

[^V08-483]: Even if body and unity refer to one thing, they do not have the same features. A body exists and produces effects; its unity is what rules out division and generality. Different descriptions of one thing can carry different features. The better reply is that, if unity is only a way we consider a thing, it does not literally reside in the body.

[^V08-484]: Self-knowledge may be direct: the self is both what knows and what is known. Or it may be knowledge through an image. If so, the image is either a likeness, a mere trace, or a description made from features and effects. The respondent rejects some of these options, but the answer does not rule out knowing oneself through one’s features. The clearest answer is that we know our particular self directly, as the “I” we point to; any other image or description is different from that identity.

[^V08-485]: Here, “awareness is the effect itself” treats the word “awareness” as the form present in the mind. The wording is a technical point: if awareness is the same as the effect, the next phrase adds nothing.

[^V08-486]: This assumes that “by” means “because of.” The question is whether awareness is the self’s presence itself or a separate awareness caused by an effect.

[^V08-487]: Avicenna’s point is that our own self is present through its individual identity and nature. The active intellect, by contrast, is present to us only through its nature, not as that individual being. This is direct knowledge of ourselves, but knowledge of the active intellect through an idea.

[^V08-488]: I think the reply is sound, given its assumptions: separate intellects have distinct natures, and we can know them through concepts. Knowledge through a concept differs from direct awareness, which is the very presence of what is known. A possible being's nature remains recognisable across its different ways of existing. We may therefore understand and define that nature even when we cannot fully grasp the thing's particular existence.

    The intellect can understand not only general kinds but also immaterial individuals. Knowing a separate intellect's kind, however, is not the same as possessing that intellect's own individual existence. The same nature can be present in our minds as an intelligible form without becoming the very separate being we know. Its kind may have only one individual outside the mind; that does not prevent its nature from being understood by another mind.

    A similar distinction applies when we know God through His names and attributes. We can form ideas that truly point to Him—for example, that He is existence without limitation, simple and undivided, knowing all things through Himself, and fully powerful. Such ideas express what the names mean. They are not God's own existence present in our minds. Just as God's reality is existence itself, so the concept of existence can serve, in our thought, somewhat as an essence serves for a possible being. The divine names and attributes can also play that role. A concept may be general even if its real object cannot be multiplied: the mind can consider several instances of what in fact has only one.

    It is true that members of one material kind differ through matter and its features. But what receives a form need not always be a material body. In the case of intellects, the knowing mind is the receiver. Two minds can therefore share knowledge of one nature without becoming one individual intellect. That is a different question from whether what is understood is itself a substance.

    If, instead, we reject conceptual knowledge of the intellects and allow only direct awareness of them, the situation changes. Then many souls could be directly aware of one and the same intellect. Their awareness would not be many separate copies of it, because in direct awareness what is known is present rather than represented by another image. Yet direct knowledge itself comes in degrees: the soul's awareness of itself when its intellect is only a possibility is not the same as the awareness it reaches when its intellect is fully developed and, in knowing itself, it knows its Lord.

    Nor should we be satisfied with loose, general descriptions of the intellects. We can identify the first intellect as an immaterial substance, the first thing produced by God, the cause of the second intellect, or the object sought by the soul of the highest sphere. Someone might say these are only effects, not the intellect's nature. But the reply is that they point to the inner principle from which those effects follow, just as a human being's distinctive nature is the source of reasoning and speech rather than simply those acts themselves. — س ره

[^V08-489]: One might say that the self causes its powers and so can know them, while a power cannot fully know what causes it. The reply is that the questioner only asks for the kind of self-awareness that estimation could provide, and estimation can provide that much.

[^V08-490]: The example is the active intellect. This restates the earlier question more broadly: estimation, too, could serve as a mirror through which the self becomes aware of itself.

[^V08-491]: A third possibility is that you know yourself directly while knowing Zayd’s soul through an image.

[^V08-492]: The idea of Zayd’s humanity is joined to your own self, but does not appear twice in you. The one image is known directly as your own self and considered in another way in relation to Zayd. Humanity considered as universal is not an essential part of your self; what is essential is the particular self itself.

[^V08-493]: The objection concerns the original step in the proof: that self-understanding must mean the self itself is present to itself.

[^V08-494]: The phrase “exists in itself” does not clearly support the three meanings the questioner assigns to it. The text may instead mean “is not present in something else,” as whiteness is in a body. The later version of this argument lists only two meanings: existing without dependence on something else, and being related to itself.

[^V08-495]: Animal souls appear in two ways: as their own living presence, connected with the heart’s vital spirit, and as an image in the brain, where the power of estimation is located.

[^V08-496]: The strongest part of an animal’s soul is its power of desire, which includes appetite and anger and is connected with the heart’s vital spirit. In animals, the heart serves desire. A complete animal soul can survive in an imaginal form after death and, when perfected, its meaning can join the kind of soul to which it belongs.

[^V08-497]: The reply does not quite follow the first argument. That argument says universal ideas are indivisible, so their perceiver must be immaterial. Here, the reply distinguishes human self-awareness from animal self-awareness: human beings can understand their self apart from bodily features, while animals perceive themselves mixed with other things. But the author’s own view is that animals directly perceive themselves in an imaginal way. That is why he says the reply turns back to the first proof, even though it does not establish the same point in exactly the same way.

[^V08-498]: Avicenna may mean that understanding the self apart from bodily features is direct awareness of one’s own identity, not understanding one’s universal nature. On that reading, the reply does not change to a different argument.

[^V08-499]: The questioner is now arguing that animal souls are immaterial. The author agrees with the basic point, in keeping with his account of complete animals, and answers only the weakest part of the objection.

[^V08-500]: A living being has a soul, and the organ contains an image that corresponds to that soul. The reply compares the image in the organ to reflected green color; it does not itself make the organ alive. If the argument is that the soul and its organ depend on each other, the dependence is different: the soul prepares the organ, while the soul gives it life.

[^V08-501]: “Present in the organ” means that the soul is what makes the organ actually alive. The soul’s image in the organ is not merely an effect of that life.

[^V08-502]: The first argument says that universal ideas are indivisible, so their place must be immaterial. This argument says that a universal idea has no particular feature, so its place must have none either. The two arguments are closely related.

[^V08-503]: In standard philosophical terminology, an “accident” includes not only ordinary qualities but also the category of substance as it exists in the mind. The author, however, treats intellectual universals as immaterial lights or divine forms. A universal can be immaterial and still shared by material individuals: its nature is present in them in a weaker way. This is a different kind of sharing from several people having the same bodily object.

[^V08-504]: The author’s distinctive claim is that what makes an idea an individual also makes it universal. This is as striking as the claim attributed to Aristotle that what makes opposites alike can also keep them apart.

[^V08-505]: The idea’s wide intellectual existence lets it apply to many things. Its nature by itself is neither universal nor particular, but it can be applied to many. Applying a concept to many things is a way of thinking about it, not an extra object inside it.

[^V08-506]: By a “pure human being” the author does not mean the bare idea of human nature emptied of everything, even existence. He means the full intellectual reality of a human being, free from all that is alien to it: boundaries, defects, and limitations. It has none of what does not belong to human reality, and contains everything that does belong to it. As the verse says, “It is no strange thing for God to gather a world in one.”

    Although that reality embraces all the countless human individuals found outside the mind or considered within it, it remains one simple reality. Matter and its conditions—place, time, and the like—are what make the individuals many. Once those limiting conditions are removed, the general reality is not divided by them.

    Consider whiteness without referring it to this object or that object, this time or that time. Taken simply as whiteness, it is one. So it is with the pure intellectual reality of humanity: we cannot distinguish separate examples inside what is pure in this way.

    All these simple and undivided realities resemble, in their own lesser ways, the simple truth and unity of existence itself, which is the most complete of all. The author specifies *particular bodily position* because there is no problem with a more general concept of position being applied at the intellectual level, as he will argue later when responding to al-Ṭūsī.

[^V08-507]: The “parts” here are intellectual powers that fulfill the role of bodily organs. The eye’s essential power is sight; the hand’s is the power to grasp. At the highest level, all such powers are one. In lower forms, their differences reappear.

[^V08-508]: The author means that most people imagine commonality and likeness rather than grasping a universal intellectually.

[^V08-509]: The commentator disagrees with the examples: recognizing what individuals have in common is an act of intellect, not merely sense or imagination.

[^V08-510]: “Unlimited” here means that the intellect can keep understanding more without reaching a final limit. Bodily and imaginative powers cannot grasp this endlessness as such; they can picture only a bounded number of images. The intellect may even understand an infinite number of instances in one act, since the nature it considers is one, though the instances are many.

[^V08-511]: A thing’s ability to be affected cannot be separated from its ability to act: if it exists, it can act, just as it can be affected.

[^V08-512]: Calling prime matter “eternal” confuses what is true in the mind with what exists outside it. In the world, matter changes along with the forms it receives; it is connected when the form is connected and divided when the form is divided.

[^V08-513]: On the theory of substantial change, each bodily power is limited in what it can do and what it can undergo. The “new qualities” are changing states, such as successive acts of understanding.

[^V08-514]: This means the image imprinted in the organ of the intellect—the body that contains the intellectual power. This follows from the assumptions that perception through an organ requires the form in that organ, and that a bodily power uses its bodily subject as its tool.

[^V08-515]: The second part of the proof is a conditional argument: it joins one statement to an either-or claim, then rejects both alternatives.

[^V08-516]: The author has already addressed this point. Even so, his objection still works: on his account, the bodily instruments do not themselves perceive. His wording is brief, but the premise is rejected either way.

[^V08-517]: The objection may be that a form is present in the soul as its source, even if it is not imprinted in the bodily tool. But if bodily powers are imprinted in matter, as many philosophers claim, then the form should be present in their organ.

[^V08-518]: If mental images exist, then on the opponent’s view knowledge is the relation between the image and what it represents. If no mental images exist, knowledge is a relation without such an image. In either case, the author’s point is that knowledge of a thing and direct self-knowledge are not the same relation.

[^V08-519]: There is no relation in direct self-knowledge, because there are not two separate things. When the intellect knows its bodily subject, there are two things, but the particular relation proposed by the opponent still needs something real to ground it.

[^V08-520]: Different ways of existing do not necessarily mean different natures. The same kind may exist materially and immaterially, or the same thing may have these modes at different times or in different respects.

[^V08-521]: The commentator says the issue is simple: features considered universally differ from features considered as particular, sensed things. The author’s answer also treats existence as a feature, and the ways in which a nature exists can differ.

[^V08-522]: The wording is loose. The point may be that knowing a material thing means knowing an imaginal or intellectual form of it.

[^V08-523]: This answer depends on the second and third premises of the proof.

[^V08-524]: The form of the argument is this: a bodily power cannot act without the body, but the intellect is said to contain the form without the body. If the original premise is true, its reversal is true too.

[^V08-525]: Here “being present” means being joined with or paired with something. If the form is joined to the intellect, it is also joined to the intellect’s subject. Otherwise, the same form would exist in two subjects at once.

[^V08-526]: The objection assumes that the soul must always attend to every feature it has. But it may have a feature without thinking about it. Its knowledge is present while it is actively aware; when attention turns away, that knowledge is no longer present as an act. The same is true of bodily feelings such as anger or fear.

[^V08-527]: Someone may ask why sleep, which leaves the outer senses and movement quiet, does not always make understanding stronger. The reply is that inner senses and natural powers become more active in sleep, and imagination occupies the soul. While awake, the outer senses can correct imagination; in sleep, they cannot, so imagination and estimation gain influence over the intellect.

[^V08-528]: The claim is only that the soul acts without the body in some activities, such as the three listed here. This avoids the common claim that the soul depends on matter for its acts even though it exists without matter.

[^V08-529]: Understanding is usually described as being affected, not acting. The reply is that “action” is used broadly here for any operation. Direct self-awareness is not an added act separate from the self; it is the self’s own presence to itself. The argument reaches its conclusion by considering the idea of self-awareness, not by assuming that the self and its awareness are two separate things.

[^V08-530]: One may object that knowing that one knows is itself acquired knowledge, and therefore not always present. If it were present without any intermediary, it should always remain. The first claim about direct self-awareness also does not count as an added act, since it is identical with the self.

[^V08-531]: This note supports the broad use of “action” in the argument, including being acted upon.

[^V08-532]: If a quality depends on a subject for its existence, saying that it can act without the subject only strengthens the claim that it needs the subject. How could something exist independently of what it necessarily depends on?

[^V08-533]: If the opponent means that powers act independently within their subjects, that claim can be accepted only if the powers themselves are immaterial. The author’s own view is that a body also contributes to the effect, because the relation and position needed for an effect require a body. The body’s role is not independent, but it is still real. The opposing account would leave bodily powers with no work to do.

[^V08-534]: Some copies may have reversed the wording of the earlier claim about whether a power can act weakly after acting strongly, or strongly after acting weakly. The author’s reasoning and examples do not make the intended direction entirely clear. Practice may also matter: a power that has become used to a weak act may not manage a strong one.

[^V08-535]: The point is that a power suited to strong action cannot simply perform a weak action, and a weak power cannot simply perform a strong one. Each act requires a matching power.

[^V08-536]: The idea is that the intellect does not tire after many acts, unlike bodily powers. This appears to be the intended reading of the author’s argument.

[^V08-537]: Someone might deny that the strength of an imagined form matches the strength of the thing as it exists outside the mind. Even if so, mental images can still be stronger or weaker than one another. The mind can move from a powerful image to a weaker one without losing its ability to perceive. What matters is that imagination is not limited in the way bodily powers are. Becoming absorbed in an image is also different from being unable to imagine a weaker thing at all.

[^V08-538]: It is the imagined thing, not the act of imagining, that the opponent calls “small.”

[^V08-539]: The soul’s unity, and the intellect’s unity if it is immaterial, are unified in a way that gathers many powers into one. A body and powers imprinted in it have only numerical unity. Even if bodily powers can act, they cannot act in the immaterial way attributed to intellect; bodily action depends on position and relation, which cannot apply to nonexistent things. If several distinct powers were bodily, each would need its own subject, but the argument assumes only one body.

[^V08-540]: This point does not answer the objection; it is a separate argument.

[^V08-541]: The second case occurs when a cause remains but something blocks its effect. Once the obstacle is removed, the cause can act again.

[^V08-542]: An idea might return because a new inner cause replaces the original outside cause. The argument works only if the returning idea is numerically the same as the one that was forgotten. On that reading, it is really a further argument about memory.

[^V08-543]: Many copies say “ten arguments,” but the correct reading is “eleven arguments.”

[^V08-544]: Depending on reports alone is not enough; reports joined with reason are a light added to light. Reports can also awaken the intellect before it has reached this understanding. Souls with strong intellectual support are rare.

[^V08-545]: The verse about breathing God’s spirit applies to Adam and to human beings generally. Other verses describe the creation of the human being from clay and the command that the angels bow to Adam.

[^V08-546]: If “My spirit” is an honorific relation, the verse points to the spirit’s immaterial nature, since a created effect resembles its source. If the wording means that the spirit is distinct from God, it points to a still higher state, beyond matter and perhaps even beyond essence as ordinarily understood. The same applies to “His Word”: divine speech is immaterial, unlike bodily speech or an imagined voice.

[^V08-547]: The spirit’s relation to God is not just a way of describing it; God brings it into existence directly.

[^V08-548]: The one who perceives and what is perceived must have something in common. The body cannot perceive the immaterial kingdom of heaven.

[^V08-549]: The verse emphasizes the human being’s new creation, then praises God as the best creator.

[^V08-550]: The reference to pairs may include the body and soul of the next life, existence and essence, and God’s face and the soul’s face. “What they do not know” may include the unseen worlds and the “good word,” which the Imams sometimes use of themselves. True unity means that the human being becomes a state of being and returns to God by taking on divine qualities.

[^V08-551]: If the soul were imprinted in the body, then at death it would either perish with its subject, or move to another body. The first would rule out the soul’s survival; the second would require an accident or bodily power to move from one subject to another.

[^V08-552]: “Whoever knows himself knows his Lord” has several meanings. A person may see the soul as a meeting point of dependence and independence, or as a mirror for all divine names. The soul can also serve as a small-scale example of God’s being, qualities, and acts: it is not confined to a place, knows itself, acts by intention, and has a kind of power and love. Or a person may recognize that they own nothing and that every act belongs to God. Knowing that the self changes and comes to be also points to God as the lasting source of existence; knowing its weakness points to divine power. In each case, knowledge of the self leads to knowledge of God.

[^V08-553]: The Prophet’s private “time with God” is a state in which the limits that distinguish one being from another fall away. Gabriel said that coming any closer would burn him. The report does not mean a meeting in physical space.

[^V08-554]: “How You give life to the dead” may also refer to seeing how God gives life: consider bodies on their own, then see the souls and intellects that give them life as belonging to the divine realm.
