In that sense, bodies are dead in themselves. When viewed through the divine source, they are immaterial. A similar interpretation appears in Rumi’s *Masnavi*.

[^V08-555]: The two fingers may also mean God’s gentleness and severity, fear and hope, or contraction and expansion.

[^V08-556]: The spirits’ priority to bodies is essential or timeless, not a period of two thousand ordinary years. The numbers refer symbolically to the realms of soul and intellect, each of which displays a thousand divine names.

[^V08-557]: The phrase that Imam ‘Ali was “possessed by God” means he was overwhelmed by nearness to God, like the hadith in which God becomes the hearing and sight of the servant who draws near to Him. This explains how he could perform extraordinary acts through divine power.

[^V08-558]: The number seventy thousand may describe the soul’s many levels—soul, heart, intellect, spirit, secret, hidden, and most hidden—with ten powers at each level. It may also refer to the many powers and kinds of nature present in the soul. In either case, the number expresses abundance: the human spirit contains the powers found throughout the world.

[^V08-559]: Aristotle’s statement “without a body” avoids suggesting that the soul returns to a body through reincarnation. The author reads it as a description of spiritual ascent. Two points matter: a person is not identical with the visible body, and knowledge of God, the divine qualities and acts, and the next life must be sought for its own sake—not as a tool for worldly gain. Otherwise, the soul remains earthbound. Few people actually reach that destination.

[^V08-560]: The meaning of the soul’s “fault,” “fall,” and escape from this world will be discussed later, in the section on how the soul comes to be.

[^V08-561]: Souls differ in whether they are intellect only in potential or intellect in reality. The same soul can also differ over time: it begins with a bodily origin and may end as spiritual. Even at one time, it can act like an animal while absorbed in bodily life, and like an angel while absorbed in God or in knowledge of the first and last things.

[^V08-562]: The two wings are theoretical reason and practical reason.

[^V08-563]: If it is not necessary for the world to lack intellect, then it is possible for it to have one. In the realm of creation, a thing’s possibility is enough for it to be brought into being.

[^V08-564]: The argument also relies on the principle that a higher kind of existence cannot arise directly from a purely lower one. Here “intellect” can mean the source of a kind, while the soul of a simple element or compound may be its imaginal form. Intellectual forms are the intellectual life of a thing; its imaginal forms are its life and knowledge in the world of images. “Then He sent our souls” may repeat the general point for emphasis, or refer specifically to the souls of the heavenly spheres.

[^V08-565]: Some religious scholars, like the philosophers, used reasoning to discuss the spirit.

[^V08-566]: “The Throne” may mean the outermost sphere, with “what it contains” referring to the world of images. It may also mean the all-embracing existence or universal mercy, with everything limited contained within it. In that reading, the saying describes the soul’s absorption in God.

[^V08-567]: The statement that spirit is present in dense matter seems to make it a bodily quality. It can be understood instead to mean that the spirit gives life to things. The phrase “spirit is a name” may mean that the name is known while the spirit’s true nature is not. The living source present in things is the meaning behind the name. The wording is difficult, but it need not imply that the spirit is an accident in a body.

[^V08-568]: If the spirit is God’s word, then it exists in God, not in a physical body.

[^V08-569]: Scholars’ disagreement may come from how they read the word “from” in “the spirit is from the command of my Lord.” If it means “coming from,” the spirit was created. If it means “belonging to,” it may be eternal with God’s command. This resembles the debate over whether God’s speech is eternal or created, and a similar debate appears among philosophers.

[^V08-570]: “Made in their form” may mean that spirits have inward organs matching the human form, or that their outward form is human. In either case, the spirits are not bodily. The angels may be the sources of kinds, or the angels who govern rain and other natural events.

[^V08-571]: The “spirit” may mean Gabriel, with whom the human rational soul becomes united after changes and growth in its very being. On that reading, what is said of Gabriel also helps explain the perfected human soul. The Throne is greater than this spirit because it stands for all-embracing mercy and the divine gift of existence. Gabriel is one of the bearers of the Throne.

    The statement that the spirit could swallow the heavens means that the heavens are like a ring in a vast open country compared with the world of intellect. Gabriel also has a true form, in which the Prophet is said to have seen him twice, filling the two horizons, and an appearance suited to the human beings who see him—such as the form of Diḥya al-Kalbī in the Prophet's time.

    Gabriel is said to stand on the right of the Throne because the right stands for the stronger side, and immaterial beings belong to higher levels of existence. On this symbolic map, the holy intellects occupy the upper right, universal souls the lower right, independent imaginal forms the upper left, and universal natures the lower left. Gabriel's intercession for those who affirm God's oneness means bringing them from what they are capable of becoming to what they actually achieve. What is now an inward change will appear as intercession in the life to come.

    There is another possible reading. The spirit may be the human spirit itself, and the greater Throne may mean the full order of divine knowledge or the heart of a perfected human being, which is called God's Throne. The statement then resembles praise expressed in a form that sounds like a qualification. The perfected soul contains the higher heavens within its own existence, to say nothing of their imaginal and physical counterparts. As a Persian verse puts it, there are heavens within the realm of the soul that govern the heavens of this world.

    Another poet pictures a bird that takes in thorns with the whole rose garden and finds what is bitter sweet. The point is that the perfected soul can contain the signs of divine gentleness and divine power together. Its “form” is not merely an outward shape: it is what makes it fully what it is. Its “face” may indicate the imaginal appearance closest to its true being, a form corresponding to the human form. Its place on the right has the same symbolic meaning as before.

    The soul also intercedes by displaying a reflected form of divine unity. It gathers qualities that point to God's transcendence and qualities that point to His presence in creation, making the truth of oneness easier for others to understand. Its light burns with such force that ordinary bodily appearance veils it. As another line suggests, the sun appears in human form with its face covered. God knows best.

[^V08-572]: The speaker may mean that the spirit belongs to the world of command, not the natural world of things that come to be and pass away. “Be” can also mean existence itself, spread through spirits and bodies. The highest spirits do not need a separate command to exist; they are themselves a command and pure existence.

[^V08-573]: This refers to the spirits’ affirmation of God in the primordial covenant. The author explains it through several levels of existence, from the divine names and intellects down to the soul and world of images. At each level, existence belongs to God; created natures have only a dependent existence, like images that depend on a mirror. In this world, matter and time make things seem separate and self-sufficient, but some remain faithful to the covenant and know that all existence belongs to God.

[^V08-574]: “Hidden things” includes not only what is imagined, but also what is estimated and what is understood. All are hidden in relation to the senses.

[^V08-575]: Reports that spirits visit their homes on Friday nights can be explained by saying that the barriers to perception are lifted, while the soul’s powers of awareness remain. The worlds of this life and the next are therefore alike to the spirit. A soul that has reached a high divine station, however, longs for the life of union with luminous intellects and does not turn back toward lesser images. “The angels speak in heaven” should be read as something the spirits see. “Spirits beneath the Throne” means spirits from the higher realm. In “an intermediate place on earth,” the word “from” marks where the journey begins.

[^V08-576]: This means the sins for which people are punished while still alive. The wording suggests that the dead receive a reason to ask that the living be forgiven. A hardship that afflicts everyone can make an excuse for each person; the passage hints at a well-known saying by the poet ‘Umar Khayyam.

[^V08-577]: “They have fallen” means “they have fallen into the abyss.”

[^V08-578]: “The Hour” here means the great Resurrection and the final unveiling. A person’s death is also a resurrection, but on a smaller or intermediate scale.

[^V08-579]: If the world of images exists, descriptions of spirits with forms do not conflict with immateriality. They strengthen the point, because the spirit can exist in both an imaginal and an intellectual mode.

[^V08-580]: The claim that humanity and animal life are created in a person can refer to the natural, bodily human and animal forms, which death removes. Or it can refer to human and animal life across all worlds, which only complete spiritual passing away removes. At a deeper level, existence is the true reality of a person, while what we call the person’s essence is a feature drawn from a particular level of existence.

[^V08-581]: “Spirit” may mean the body’s vital spirit, which is interwoven with the body. Likewise, saying the soul is a subtle body flowing through the body like water through a flower or fire through coal may refer to the soul’s lowest level.

[^V08-582]: Ibn ‘Abbas was a respected student of Imam ‘Ali. His words might seem to say that spirits perish when bodies perish, just as a lamp’s light disappears when its oil is gone. The author says he meant the opposite: when a branch lamp goes out, its light returns to the lasting source from which it came. So souls, especially those perfected in life, return to the Holy Spirit or active intellect when their bodies decay. The comparison with flesh in sickness also suggests that the body’s true form can remain in the next life even when its material parts have changed.

[^V08-583]: These movements of thought may be intellectual or practical.

[^V08-584]: This answers the claim that the afterlife is only spiritual and cannot include particular bodily pleasures or pains. The author says the soul has particular, immaterial powers of perception of its own. These are subtler than intellect, but survive with the soul. That is why al-Ghazali says the soul carries its power of estimation after death.

[^V08-585]: “The spirit” here is the rational soul in its immaterial state; “animal spirit” is the soul’s capacity for sense and movement; its bodily home is the vital spirit, which carries these powers.

[^V08-586]: When the spirit from the world of command enters the animal spirit, the animal spirit becomes a new kind of thing. The animal spirit may be treated as a broad kind, while the human spirit gives it its essential distinguishing feature.

[^V08-587]: This follows the idea of a marriage that continues through all generations.

[^V08-588]: The lamp is the light of God's oneness. The comparison with the heart's tapering shape is fitting. The heart of a believer who understands is a world of intellect that mirrors the world outside. We can picture the order of existence as a cone of light: its narrow point is towards God, and its broad base towards matter. Nearer God, oneness and simplicity are clearer; nearer matter, multiplicity and composition increase.

    In the report, the unbeliever's heart is described as dark because the world is, for that person, like a cone of darkness. It is turned upside down because attention rests on the many things of the bodily world without turning towards the unity that gathers them, and on outward forms without reaching their deeper meaning.

    A commentator distinguishes several kinds of unbelief. Some people have not received the prophetic message, or have not understood parts of it. He calls this unbelief through ignorance and says that many of these people, because of their limited opportunity, may not be punished. Others hear the message but reject it through pride, inherited loyalties, or prejudice: this is deliberate rejection. Still others affirm the message outwardly, perhaps to protect their lives or property, while denying it inwardly: this is hypocrisy, which the commentator regards as more serious.

    A further case is someone who privately accepts the truth but refuses to admit it, out of envy, stubbornness, or deference to others. The commentator treats this as a different kind of concealment. Another person may affirm the message in speech and belief yet misunderstand it because of poor judgment or misleading teachers. This is error, and its seriousness varies with the extent of the error.

    Lastly, a person may understand and accept the message yet disobey its commands while recognising the wrong. Some texts also use “unbelief” for this kind of wrongdoing. The commentator notes that other schools disagree over whether a grave sinner should be called an unbeliever, a believer, or neither. He does not treat every case as deserving the same judgment or punishment.

    The image of the dark, inverted heart can cover these different cases. Hypocrisy receives a separate image—the heart bound as if to a stake—because the person keeps the inner denial concealed while presenting the opposite outwardly. Someone who knows the truth but rejects it openly is different in that respect. The report's heart with two faces corresponds to the self that reproaches itself, pulled between contrary tendencies. — س ره

[^V08-589]: The author has described four of the human being’s seven levels: soul, heart, intellect, and spirit. He leaves the final three—the secret, the hidden, and the most hidden—without explanation, since they are known through direct experience.

[^V08-590]: Some manuscript copies read “the body is understood” rather than “the body is an intellect.”

[^V08-591]: This kind of dependence is strongest because a thing’s nature and its existence depend on each other in meaning, both in the mind and in reality. The relation is present even in thought; it is not merely that we choose to consider one thing alongside another. Every nature that exists requires existence, though existence can also be without a nature, as in separate intellects. A thing with an essence therefore depends in two ways: in its essence and in its existence.

[^V08-592]: The example is the existence of a possible thing depending on the Necessary Being. One may also read this as the possibility or neediness of the possible thing itself, without supplying the word “existence.”

[^V08-593]: An image needs matter in order to be an individual image, but not to be the kind of image it is. By contrast, a quality such as blackness depends both for its individual presence and its kind of existence on a particular subject. The subject must be an actual individual, not just some possible subject in general.

[^V08-594]: This type of dependence lasts only as long as the thing exists, not in its nature as such. It differs from the third kind, which lasts from the thing’s coming to be through its continued existence, and from the fifth, where the dependence is needed only at the beginning. The list may not cover every kind of relation: for example, two related things may each depend on the other in meaning.

[^V08-595]: The fifth kind depends on the nature of the thing it relies on, as the soul depends on bodily matter at the beginning. The body is the soul’s matter, so the soul’s need is general rather than for one particular body.

[^V08-596]: The soul depends on the body for completion only after it has gained its bodily form and practical powers, and before its theoretical intellect becomes actual. Once it has reached intellectual completion, it no longer depends on the body in this way; it can seem to put the body off like a garment.

[^V08-597]: If the soul had to use sight in order to perceive what is seen, and hearing for what is heard, then the soul would not be one simple perceiver.

[^V08-598]: Losing a sense may mean losing knowledge of the sensible things that belong to it, or losing the general understanding drawn from those particular things.

[^V08-599]: On the usual account, the intellect abstracts general ideas from particular things and makes those forms. On the author’s account, the soul understands universals by witnessing their sources in the world of intellect, and the knower becomes one with what is known. “Making” an idea can then mean that the soul itself becomes an intellect, or that the universals are titles and reflections of those higher sources.

[^V08-600]: The claim that the soul has only one level means, on the opposing view, that it remains numerically the same with no inward movement or change in its very existence. The author also holds that one soul has many levels, but for him these are stages of one developing being. The soul can show the qualities of the natural world, even of basic matter, and can also take on the qualities associated with God. Between those extremes lie many stages.

    The Qur'anic verse speaks of a time that passed “over the human being.” This wording suggests that even the stage of bare matter, and not only the stages of plant or animal life, can belong to the history of a human soul. That is not surprising if we mean matter and nature as stages along a person's own path of development. It would be surprising if we meant a plant, animal, or piece of matter standing outside that path, with no such movement. Considered in themselves, those separate things are not stages of this human being.

    The author gives one possible meaning of the verse. A higher reading is that it refers to the human being's prior existence in the divine realm. There, the fixed reality of a human being belonged to the level of God's names and attributes and was known directly through His knowledge. The “time” mentioned in the verse is like the “time” in the Prophet's saying, “I have a time with God.” It does not mean an ordinary period measured by clocks. The person did not yet exist through a separate existence of their own. Rather, all these realities were present through God's existence. There was not yet the scattered multitude of limited, material beings we see here.

    Even in the lower worlds, light prevails over darkness, mercy comes before anger, and unity has precedence over multiplicity. But those signs are far from the undivided reality of that earlier divine level.

[^V08-601]: The distinction between these two kinds of form follows from eight differences discussed earlier, in the section on intellect and intelligible forms.

[^V08-602]: Views on the soul’s beginning can be sorted into two broad camps. Those who say it comes to be disagree about whether it existed before the body, began with the body, or was created through the body so that the body’s coming to be is also its own. Those who say it is eternal may mean that it is eternal by nature, as in the extreme view that the soul is God; eternal in time but created in its nature; eternal among many bodies, as those who believe in souls moving between bodies say; or eternal in a higher chain of existence as a universal intellect. The last view is Plato’s.

[^V08-603]: The heavens were thought by some philosophers to be eternal, though subject to weakness as their positions change; Sadra also says their substance changes. The point is: even if the soul were eternal, its immaterial nature would make it complete and free of weakness.

[^V08-604]: One could object that species reproduced over generations are eternal and have many individuals. The reply is that what matters is not eternity but being free of changing accidents that create many individuals. The kind survives by one individual following another; it is not one eternal individual.

[^V08-605]: The point is that this is not a feature found in every case. We should not object that the celestial bodies, too, each have a kind limited to one individual: having only one individual is not a feature reserved for immaterial beings.

    There is another answer, one that follows the author's philosophy and that of the leading sages. The heavenly spheres and the kinds of things that reproduce are coming into being and passing away in their very substance. Their existence changes at every moment. What looks like one lasting individual is, in reality, a continuous succession of stages, each coming after the disappearance of what preceded it and passing away before what follows.

    A natural kind, considered generally, has no existence apart from the individuals that embody it. Each individual comes to be; the kind therefore comes to be through them. The whole collection has no further existence in addition to the existence of its members. There are two different kinds of multiplicity: the many separate individuals of a kind, and the continuous succession of stages within each apparent individual.

    On this view, having many individuals that share one kind is a common feature of bodily and changing things. A newly occurring thing is one whose very being is renewed. What is eternal in the strict sense remains the same in itself and awaits no future development. That applies to God and to what belongs to His own reality. — س ره

[^V08-606]: “Perfect souls” may mean souls that reach the afterlife or souls that have a mode of existence before nature. In truth, a prior mode belongs to a soul that also has a later one. If all things have earlier modes in relation to their source, this is not enough to call them eternal; only the human soul can pass through to higher modes. Potentiality can be treated as the thing itself when it is ready to become actual.

[^V08-607]: The cause is the complete definition of its effect, and the effect is an incomplete expression of its cause. A lower reality is a weaker form of a higher one. The higher reality remains present in the lower without losing its own rank. This idea underlies the author’s language of eternity and coming to be, giving and receiving, descent and ascent.

[^V08-608]: This is the strongest argument for eternity. If a complete, unchanging intellect produces a soul, the soul cannot be material. But if it is immaterial, how can it begin at a particular time? If the soul’s connection or action begins at a particular time while the soul itself is eternal, then it is eternal in its nature—the very claim the argument set out to prove.

[^V08-609]: The argument assumes that whatever is eternal cannot perish, and reverses this to say that whatever does not perish must be eternal. The commentator says each claim needs its own proof; one cannot assume that a true statement stays true when reversed.

[^V08-610]: The earlier argument does not prove that an unlimited number is impossible in every sense. It proves that an unlimited number of things arranged together in an order is impossible. The objection about counting assumes more than that proof establishes.

[^V08-611]: The commentator says the argument does not need the universal claim that everything divisible has continuous unity. It concerns only something that is genuinely one and divides into real parts. Souls are not divided like a kind into its species, or a whole into units; those are only divisions made in thought.

[^V08-612]: This question is not unique to this argument. Distinctions do not have to lead to an endless chain or a circle. They can end in a basic difference that explains itself. In this case, saying souls differ by their nature means they differ in kind, which the argument rejects.

[^V08-613]: If the soul’s powers are material, then a soul with no further intellectual development would lose the features that made it distinct once its body perished. If imagination and estimation survive, however, the soul may still keep distinct mental images even without universal knowledge.

[^V08-614]: The “awareness of oneself” mentioned here may mean a mental awareness of one’s position and limits in the body. The argument assumes souls that have not gained intellectual knowledge.

[^V08-615]: This reply works only if every thing whose part is itself a part must have magnitude. The needed claim is that anything with parts of the same kind is extended.

[^V08-616]: The objection’s explanation does not match its claim of common knowledge. It also speaks of human souls, though the claim was meant to cover every kind of soul.

[^V08-617]: The commentator offers a further answer. A feature can be essential to the individual it distinguishes, while accidental to the broader kind it divides. “Rational” distinguishes a human being from other animals, though it is not part of the animal kind. This distinction exists in thought, not as an extra thing in reality. In reality, animal and rational form one living being. Likewise, existence makes individuals distinct before we think about their natures.

[^V08-618]: This is an argument from purpose. Material kinds have many individuals because matter cannot last forever in one individual. The kind is preserved through a succession of individuals. Separate intellects do not need this succession.

[^V08-619]: The soul changes in its substantial existence, yet something remains through the change. The stable part may be the underlying subject, the soul’s relation to God, or the identity formed by existence itself. In a changing being, continued existence may consist in continued change.

[^V08-620]: The image is of fresh water taking on the taste and smell of whatever vessel holds it. Souls receive different features from their bodies, and retain a trace of those differences.

[^V08-621]: Philosophers often call an accidental feature that is not part of a thing’s nature something that happens by chance. This does not mean there is no cause at all; it means the feature does not come from the thing’s nature.

[^V08-622]: On the view being discussed, particular perceptions depend on the body and disappear when bodily powers end. Universal knowledge can remain with the soul. Another possible answer is that souls that truly understand universals do not return to bodies; souls that move between bodies do not grasp universals without imagined images.

[^V08-623]: The objection assumes the earlier body was a natural body in this same world. If it was a body in another world, that objection still needs to be answered. The author had already argued against there being more than one physical world. If the earlier body was an imaginal body, its relation to the soul would be like a source’s relation to what it produces, not like a body the soul governs.

[^V08-624]: If this objection were decisive, it would also undermine the earlier argument offered by the opposing thinkers. The reply in both cases is to distinguish imaginal forms belonging to the soul's descent into bodily life from forms acquired during its later ascent. The later forms arise from actions, habits, and movement. The earlier forms come before the world of action and movement. They have no matter that could receive new capacities, so they cannot by themselves explain the soul's growth.

    In bodily life, matter helps explain why one soul takes on one particular shape and another soul a different one. But in the earlier realm there is no such matter. What would explain why one soul had a tall imaginal form and another a short one, or why one had a light or dark complexion, or a beautiful or different appearance? We must not treat forms at the earlier level as separate souls already tied to individual bodies. Rather, the forms depend on souls considered in their higher, unified intellectual existence. They are like shadows of that higher reality.

    Another objection asks how the primordial world can contain many individual imaginal forms of the same kind if differences between individuals normally depend on matter. The reply is that these forms do not each have an independent, particular existence. They exist through one divine knowledge, much as many shapes can be present within a single imagination. The rule about many individuals of one kind concerns things that exist separately, each through its own material conditions. That is the case in the physical world, not in the same way in the primordial world.

    The forms there can still be distinguished by what each represents, even without separate individual lives. Their life and awareness come through God. In the world between bodily life and final resurrection, the situation is different again: imaginal forms are sustained by the many souls whose habits and dispositions were shaped through their earlier bodies. Their differences still reflect those acquired conditions. Souls that have reached a deeper spiritual unity may be an exception. The note ends by recalling Rūmī's image of breaking down the battlements that keep people apart, so that what divides them may disappear.

[^V08-625]: Because the heart is subtle and not fixed in one bodily color or form, it can receive whichever form it turns toward. These may be forms that arise in sequence as a person develops through changes in their very being and in their outward qualities. They may also be images in the mind that do not follow a fixed natural sequence.

    This explains the poet's images of a “pasture for gazelles” and a “monastery for monks.” The pasture can stand for the soul's earlier, animal stage, while the monastery stands for its later, intellectual stage. Or the pasture can mean that imagination is busy with the possessions of the world—animals, crops, and the like—while the monastery means that it has turned toward God, the angels near Him, and the best of human beings.

    The poet uses wild gazelles because desires, until they have been trained and restrained, are like wild animals. Yet the heart can also be a pasture for tame creatures: bodily desires can come under the direction of reason and be turned toward God. One can go further and sacrifice animal desire, as an offering made for God's sake. The Qur'an says that God has bought from believers their lives and wealth in exchange for the Garden.

    There is another possible reading. The gazelles may be beloved people with beautiful eyes and faces, so that the heart is at times a pasture for human love. This too is one of the images that the heart can take.

[^V08-626]: The four objections listed here are weak arguments against the view that the soul is immaterial from its first moment. A better proof is needed for the author’s view that it comes to be through the body and survives as a spirit.

[^V08-627]: The “derived feature” is the real feature that produces the logical distinction. “Rational” is the logical feature; the soul is the source of that feature in reality.

[^V08-628]: The author is speaking of logical kind and difference. The real kind and its real difference are the same realities, considered in different ways.

[^V08-629]: If kind and difference correspond to matter and form, they come together in one existence. If the difference completes the kind, it also completes the kind’s existence. Without substantial change, this would make the soul bodily. With substantial change, the soul may become more complete and join an active intellect. The author accepts that it may be called bodily in an earlier state, but not in the same way as an ordinary body.

[^V08-630]: The usual view leaves several questions unanswered: how can an immaterial soul begin at one time, and what carries its readiness to begin? How can something that begins in time be immaterial? How can an immaterial thing govern one particular body? These puzzles led philosophers to disagree about the soul’s nature and survival.

[^V08-631]: The soul has several modes at once: it is natural, bodily, and intellectual, then can become free of these through God. The commentary uses a set of apparent contradictions: the soul can be both created and eternal, bodily and spiritual, changing and lasting, depending on which mode is meant. It is a “bird of many colors,” a structure that brings unity and multiplicity together.

[^V08-632]: The relation is explanatory: the cause contains the fullness of its effect.

[^V08-633]: Aristotle says that in many cases, knowing what a thing is and why it exists are one and the same. If the reader understands these principles, the soul’s existence before the body becomes clearer: the whole reality of a thing belongs to its cause, not merely its incomplete state.

[^V08-634]: The author’s comparison with animals and plants is not exact if they too undergo substantial change. They may also depend on a body for their full identity.

[^V08-635]: This seems to be the first argument from the previous chapter, stated more strongly.

[^V08-636]: The phrase “before bodies” means the souls’ mode of existence in the material world before they become joined to particular bodies. The argument first establishes that souls are many in this world, then tries to ask how they could have been one in the intellectual world.

[^V08-637]: In *The Wisdom of Illumination*, “barrier” means a body. It does not mean an intermediate being that shares the features of two worlds.

[^V08-638]: A commentator begins with this argument: there are infinitely many souls, yet their degree of light must be limited because more powerful lights stand above them. If degrees of light are limited while souls are infinite, infinitely many souls must share each degree. How, then, could the souls at any one degree be distinguished from one another?

    An objection to this argument is that a range bounded at both ends may still contain infinitely many levels. The possible balance of bodily qualities lies between two extremes, yet there can be endlessly many balances in between. Likewise, a finite line can contain infinitely many points, and a limited stretch of time infinitely many instants. So perhaps each distinct degree of the soul's light could belong to just one soul. Nothing impossible follows from that alone.

    The reply draws a distinction. On the view that infinitely many souls existed from eternity, their separate levels would all have to exist together in reality. Points on a line and possible bodily balances are different: although we can distinguish indefinitely many of them, they need not all exist as separate, actual things at once. The difficulty is therefore that infinitely many actually existing levels of soul would be confined between two fixed limits. That problem does not follow in the same way for the points and possible balances. — س ره

[^V08-639]: The point is that souls before bodies are like intellectual beings: they differ in strength and weakness.

[^V08-640]: An objection might be raised. Earlier, the souls were said to differ by degrees of strength and weakness—or, more exactly, by their own distinct realities, just as different existences and individual beings do. Strength and weakness, like being earlier or later in rank, are part of what each existence is. Here, however, the difference is said to arise from their causes. How can both accounts be right?

    They do not conflict. Souls differ through their own existence, but that existence comes from the powers that bring them forth. Their differences therefore reflect the differences in their sources. In their inward reality before their entry into bodies, distinct souls have the standing of intellectual substances. At the highest level the sources of their differences are God's names and attributes.

    “Causes” may also mean the souls themselves considered as causes of what is below them. Immaterial beings are channels through which God gives existence to their lesser reflections. Understood this way, saying that souls differ by their causes is much like saying that they differ through their own realities.

    When the receiving conditions also play a part, the difference is like the difference between the reflections of a person and a tree in the same mirror, or the reflections of one person in different mirrors—small and large, clear and cloudy, straight and curved.

[^V08-641]: The claim that all souls share self-knowledge may confuse knowing the idea of “a self” with directly knowing one’s own individual self. These are not the same.

[^V08-642]: The same claim appears in the commentary on Suhrawardi's *Philosophy of Illumination* and in the author's notes on it. Taken at face value, though, it seems to assume what it is trying to prove. A soul's direct knowledge of itself is its own existence. If souls are really distinct, their acts of self-knowledge are distinct too. If they are one, their self-knowledge is one. We cannot prove that they are one simply by assuming that they share self-knowledge.

    Perhaps the statement instead concerns the *idea* of the self, learned through concepts. Such knowledge can be shared like other basic truths. There is, however, a stronger answer. Each person is directly aware of an 'I' that is not forgotten and is not identical with the unfamiliar bodily features that surround it. The claim of unity concerns this immediately felt selfhood. What Zayd finds when he is aware of himself is, in this respect, also found by others of his kind.

    We can go further. At the level of intellect, souls may be one in what they understand, especially if we accept the union of the intellect with what it knows. A universal object of thought, such as whiteness itself, is one even when understood by a thousand minds. Pure universality contains no mark distinguishing one copy from another. The same holds for other true general concepts and judgments.

    This helps explain why those who genuinely know the truth, from Adam to Muhammad, are said to have one faith and one understanding. Their hearts resemble mirrors facing one another, each reflecting the same truth because all face the same direction. Scripture says, 'Those are the ones God has guided, so follow their guidance.' And the saying declares, 'The believer is the mirror of the believer.' Such people affirm God's unity, names, and highest attributes in one essential way. Apparent differences belong to words, sounds, and outward expressions, while our subject here is intellectual understanding. Anyone who does not share this knowledge has not yet reached that level; it is not that the same fully attained knowledge differs in its truth.

    Even qualities experienced through our bodily powers have both unity and difference. Their differences may arise from the person who receives them, or from their strength and weakness, while what allows them to differ also allows us to recognize them as the same kind of state. Joy and sadness, hunger and thirst, being full or having one's thirst quenched, are experiences shared by humans and, in some respects, other animals. This is one meaning of the saying that everything contains something of everything else.

[^V08-643]: The same point appears in the author's note on *The Philosophy of Illumination*. Suhrawardī's argument concerns souls while they are joined to bodies. He denies that they form one soul in that state and affirms their plurality. He then argues that, if they had been one before joining bodies and later became many, they would have had to split into parts. If you accept that souls are many while joined to bodies, his argument stands. Nothing has yet been said against it.

    Perhaps, however, the author intends a narrower claim. Although souls are not one during their ordinary bodily lives, they can become united in a higher sense while they still have some connection with bodily form. That happens when they attain an existence beyond bodily dependence, become spiritual in their lasting life, and join the intellect in their fully developed intellectual state. He could then read *before bodies* in Suhrawardī's statement as a priority belonging to a different order of being, not as an earlier date in time. The author's remark that, in the intellectual world, souls become united with the separate intellect when complete supports this reading. Even that limited unity is enough to challenge part of Suhrawardī's argument.

    Nor has the author accepted that souls are many in every sense before they become united at the intellectual level. Union in the natural world does not mean that each soul must know the particular experiences of every other. His phrases “unity according to each world” and “how can it be compared?” point toward this distinction.

    He then quotes Jalāl al-Dīn al-Dawānī's commentary on *The Temples of Light*. Dawānī asks what is meant when different souls are said to perceive through different bodily organs or instruments. One possibility is that the instrument is part of what defines each act of perceiving. But then the perceiver described as *using this instrument* differs from the perceiver described as *using that instrument*. The instrument becomes part of what is meant by the perceiver. Two such defined perceivers would be distinct in themselves, even though the souls were assumed to be one.

    The other possibility is that the instruments merely explain how the different acts occur. On that reading, many actions do not require many agents. A single soul perceives different things through different senses while remaining the one subject of all these perceptions.

    In his response, the author chooses the first understanding: the instruments help define the souls as they act through them. But he denies that this makes the souls merely imaginary combinations. Each soul, with its own instrument, is a real being united with that instrument and is distinct from another soul in that respect. Dawānī objects that the souls would therefore differ in themselves. The author answers: yes, in the bodily world, because each soul has its own essential connection with its bodily instruments. Dawānī then reminds him that the souls were supposed to be one. The author replies: yes, but not in this world. They are one in a prior intellectual world, which is also the world to which they return after bodily life. This supports the explanation given above. — S. R.

[^V08-644]: The author may be denying that souls exist before bodies as many individual souls, not that they exist as one intellect. His view of what is brighter and more complete supports this reading.

[^V08-645]: The soul learns the divine names that appear through likeness and manifestation, just as, when it existed at the intellectual level, it knew the names that express God's freedom from all likeness. It gathers the signs of both kinds of names within itself: animal life, angelic life, and other forms. By learning the names of things in the lower world, it becomes capable of gathering the whole order of existence. If it had never descended, it could not have become a complete image of divine unity, a meeting place for every perfection, or a manifestation of God's Greatest Name. Nor could every lesson reach it, or could it pass through every realm. This is why the Teacher said that the soul's descent into this world did it no harm. The great Arab master likewise said that the world's completeness includes the existence within it of a lower and less complete realm.

    Two further reasons for the soul's descent occur to me. First, it descends so that it may possess a kingdom of its own. At the higher level it had no separate kingdom: its existence was dependent, and its identity was absorbed into what was above it. There it did not have the sort of bounded individual nature to which matter or possessions could be added. Here, matter makes limitation and separate identity possible; it gives the soul a realm over which it can exercise rule.

    Second, the soul was once like a clear and transparent light, unable to hold reflections or images. It descended into this world so that, through its connection with a dark body, it could receive and retain them. A mirror similarly needs a coating on its back before an image can appear in it. Such purposes, among others, are what the author has in mind when he says that the descent happened “for a great wisdom.”

[^V08-646]: As noted in the preceding comment, the author denies that souls existed before their bodies *as separate, individual souls*. He does not deny their prior reality at the level of intellect. In the same way he denies that intellect, as intellect, descends or changes, but not that the soul has a descent. An intellect lacks nothing it is waiting to become, and so it cannot literally move from one condition to another.

    What, then, does descent mean? The less complete realities in our lower world have the same basic nature as the higher realities of which they are reflections or faces. A thing's face, in this sense, is the thing itself viewed in a weaker way. The lower reality can therefore exist here without the higher reality leaving its own level. Similarly, ascent means that the lower reality becomes connected with its higher truth. The higher being is present at its own level throughout.

    The mistake arises when people picture descent, fall, and return as though the higher being vacated one place in order to occupy another. In fact these words describe the giving of existence: something proceeds from its source without diminishing that source, and returns without adding a new perfection to it. In this sense, descent and return cannot reasonably be denied, least of all by the author. Religious teaching, spiritual experience, inward awareness, and philosophical demonstration all support them; the language of the tradition is full of these themes.

[^V08-647]: All things, not only souls, have a prior mode of existence. Only souls can become aware of it and understand it directly.

[^V08-648]: The human soul mirrors God’s nature, attributes, and acts. It is outside the body and its powers, yet not outside the world in the way a separate object would be. The soul can help us understand divine attributes and actions by looking at our own powers, especially when the intellect is fully developed.

[^V08-649]: The soul learns the nature of things that the angels do not know in the same bodily way. By entering nature and using its powers, it may come to know what it could not know in its intellectual state.

[^V08-650]: The soul has genuine inward acts and powers that become visible through what it does in the body. The levels of existence correspond to one another. Physical reproduction, for example, is the outward shadow of a deeper kind of bringing forth within the soul. Every soul seeks in some way to become like what it loves and to take on that beloved one's qualities, just as a flame takes on the appearance of what it sets alight.

    Feeding the body reflects the soul's nourishment through understanding, when the mind takes knowledge into itself and becomes one with what it knows. Bodily formation reflects the mind's power to form things through their truths. Similar correspondences can be found in the other powers associated with plant life.

    There are also correspondences for animal powers. Seeing physical light is a shadow of beholding the higher, immaterial lights. Hearing good words reflects an inward hearing of spiritual words and inspired thoughts. Smelling a pleasant fragrance reflects the reception of what spiritual writers call a breath of divine kindness. The same pattern extends to the other senses and capacities.

    If these bodily powers never appeared in a way we could sense, we would not recognise the inward acts and qualities that lie hidden at the intellectual level.

    Another interpretation takes the passage to refer to the perfections of the Active Intellect. Then the soul has descended so that it can develop a connection with the Active Intellect and, in its ascent, come to share in its activity. What appears through it may receive something of the reality of what appears. Without such an act, that perfection would not be achieved, and the Active Intellect could not rightly be called the end toward which the activity is directed.

[^V08-651]: The story is told in detail in the ninth section of Avicenna’s *Pointers and Reminders*, with a commentary by al-Tusi.

[^V08-652]: The poem continues:

    > When the *hāʾ* of her descent met the *mīm* of her center
    > in the land of Ajraʿ,
    > she fastened upon the *thāʾ* of heaviness
    > and found herself among earthly landmarks and ruins.

    Ajraʿ is a broad tract of uneven land, part sandy and part rocky. Here it points symbolically to the wide, lofty world of intellect. The poet's letters carry several meanings.

    First, the numerical values of *hāʾ* and *mīm* together equal the value of the name Adam. The suggestion is that by descending, the soul became human after having been angelic. The verse thus points to the human being's ability to gather hidden and visible levels of reality. The *hāʾ* is pronounced in the throat and the *mīm* at the lips; together they span the beginning and end of speech, its inner and outer sides. The complete human reality gathers these different levels as a word gathers its letters.

    The *mīm* also points to sovereignty, while *hāʾ* points to relation and belonging. In a treatise associated with the earlier philosopher, this letter marks a divine relation displayed through five forms. The *thāʾ* of heaviness suggests the increase of multiplicity as the soul descends. In this symbolic arithmetic, five becomes five hundred. The letters are treated as different levels of an underlying reality. The soul's multiplicity is slight in the highest spiritual world, greater in the intermediate world, and greatest in bodily life. — س ره

[^V08-653]: The “state” from which something comes means a feature belonging to its cause. Different schools describe those features differently. Spiritual teachers speak of the divine names and qualities. The philosophers of illumination speak of the ways one intellect sheds light on another and directly sees it, and of the way those intellects serve as sources for their counterparts in the worlds of image and matter.

    Followers of the school of Aristotle speak of three aspects within each of the ten intellects: its necessity, existence, and possibility; or light, shadow, and darkness; or its understanding of its source, its own existence, and its own possibility. These are different ways of expressing the same kind of distinction.

    Since features of an acting cause are usually counted as perfections and goods, the author mentions an apparent difficulty. Even God's most complete servants follow His example, yet Adam's descent is said to have resulted from an error. The author therefore pauses to examine the point in the words that follow.

[^V08-654]: The limitations meant here belong to the *causes* and what follows from them, not to the effects considered on their own. Since a thing's possibility of existence is what makes it dependent, the relevant limitation must already be present on the side of the cause. Otherwise we could not say that the 'sin' led to the fall.

    A lower effect comes from a lower or more limited aspect of its cause, while a higher effect comes from a higher aspect. Thus the second intellect is related to what is necessary in the first intellect, the soul of the outermost heaven to its actual existence, and the heavenly body, as a lower effect, to its possible and limited existence.

    'Sin' here means a limitation in the order of creation, not a breach of a religious command. There are commands and prohibitions governing how things come into being, just as there are commands and prohibitions governing our actions. No moral duty had been imposed at the higher level being described. Without a limitation in the causal source, a lower body would not have appeared. Likewise, without the aspect of possibility in the human being's higher intellectual reality, the natural human being of this world would not have appeared.

    Because the different worlds correspond and retain a common underlying truth, the human being of nature may be said to have existed in a higher manner at the level of intellect, and then to have descended through limitation to this lower world, even 'the lowest of the low.' The author's note elsewhere states the same rule: lower effects proceed from the limited and possible aspects of their causes.

    There is another possible interpretation. The 'sin' may mean the connection of existence with limited natures, while the tree mentioned in scripture may mean the branching multiplicity of those natures. Without distinct natures and determinations, existence would not appear through them, and the distance and separation of this world would not arise.

[^V08-655]: The “flight from God’s anger” means the lower soul flees from the overwhelming light of God, much as starlight disappears in sunlight. It seeks a darker material world where it can become visible, even though that state is less complete.

[^V08-656]: Some works say that God has seventy thousand veils of light and seventy thousand veils of darkness. The numbers may be symbolic: the soul has seven levels, and the worlds may be counted in groups of ten. The veils of light may mean spiritual beings; the veils of darkness may mean material beings.

[^V08-657]: This means the cause of the soul’s descent as a soul that governs bodies. Its descent is part of its mode of existence here. When its wings of knowledge and action grow strong, the soul becomes an intellect.

[^V08-658]: “Nature” can mean a thing’s kind, so each natural kind may have one intellectual form and many bodily forms. It can also mean the source of a thing’s movement and rest, while “intellectual nature” refers to its separate intellectual form.

[^V08-659]: The descent is by the soul’s own power and choice, not by force. In the highest state it is power and choice itself; the same remains true in the intellectual world.

[^V08-660]: This points to other reasons for the soul’s descent discussed earlier.

[^V08-661]: The claim concerns those who have returned to the higher world and reached its highest rank, absorbed in the vision of God’s beauty and overwhelmed by the light of His majesty.

[^V08-662]: “Imagine” is used by comparison with the previous wording. It may mean directly knowing a whole class of things, such as all kinds of fire, without one thing blocking knowledge of another.

[^V08-663]: You may object that Aristotle's words do not show this. They could mean simply that a soul in a body is not divided in itself, although it is divided indirectly through the bodily instruments it uses. If he had said that the soul was indivisible without qualification, the words would support the stronger claim.

    The reply is that his condition, “when it is in a body,” implies another way of existing, outside a body. In that state the soul is not divided at all, either in itself or indirectly through a body. That is precisely what the stronger, unqualified statement means.

    His further words, “if it is indivisible, it is so in itself,” can also be understood this way. The question is whether it is indivisible only in itself or also free from indirect division. Aristotle's point is that in its earlier intellectual state, before its connection with the body, it was indivisible in both senses. Indivisibility without qualification belongs to its nature, whereas division through bodily organs comes only from its circumstances. The author indicates this with the words “in itself, not by itself or by accident.”

[^V08-664]: The words suggest that intellect in its own world is the soul’s prior mode, while the soul’s life in the natural world comes later. The author uses this to explain how the soul can be eternal in one mode and created in another.

[^V08-665]: This means that the soul is still subject to changing states; it has not reached a settled state. This is why the Qur'an commands, ‘Remain steadfast as you have been commanded.’ The reference to the body means that the body has become a place where the light of intellect reaches down through the soul. The soul joins the body's powers to the intellect, much as a link joins what begins in time to what is eternal. When bodily nature still dominates the soul, it turns its gaze outward and forgets its own reality: ‘They forgot God, so He made them forget themselves.’ This is the opposite of the saying, ‘Whoever knows himself knows his Lord.’ Scripture speaks of the record of wrongdoers in Sijjīn and the record of the righteous in ʿIlliyyīn.

    As the Persian verse puts it: ‘One thing entered the earth and became earth; salt entered it and made it pure.’ When the soul ‘turns its gaze upon itself,’ it looks toward its own reality and toward the existence that makes it what it is, rather than merely toward the things it observes.

[^V08-666]: An objection may be raised. We understand why the soul's 'sin' could be called its possibility of existence. Earlier, the author explained that the descent of a soul, like the coming forth of other lower effects, depends on the limited possibilities present in its causes. But he also calls the soul's incompleteness, its being an intellect only in possibility, and its inability to receive everything at once a 'sin.' Are those not simply features of its descent from paradise? How can the descent be caused by the very thing it is supposed to explain? Moreover, these are conditions of our present world, while religious accounts and symbols place the soul's sin before entry into this world.

    Two answers are possible. First, those limitations can be understood as features of the soul's forms of knowledge in the higher levels that precede material life. What appears there as a known reality becomes a natural limitation when it is expressed in material things. The natures, qualities, and acts of created beings are already present in higher forms of knowledge. Those forms are real, not mere unrealized possibilities.

    Second, the limitations of souls in this life can explain their distance, on the path of return, from the intellects and beautiful forms that lie above them. There are levels of paradise associated with the divine essence, attributes, and acts, including the acts by which things are brought forth. The paradises from which Adam is said to have departed correspond to these levels, though they are not simply identical with the levels souls reach in their ascent. The descending and ascending intermediate worlds differ, as do the ways in which possibilities and impossibilities are hidden from us; yet corresponding levels in the two orders reflect one another.

    The many worlds share a lasting underlying reality. What distinguishes their ways of existing is also what links them. This is especially true at the level of intellect, where division, bodily structure, size, motion, and even the distinction between one thing and another no longer apply in the ordinary way. The intellects reached by perfected souls in their ascent thus correspond deeply to the intellects through which divine generosity descends, even if they differ from one point of view.

    The limitation that distances a soul from the higher paradises on its return can therefore also be called the source of its departure from the corresponding paradises in descent. We may speak of being 'sent out' or 'falling' because the soul has a natural capacity for a higher life. Most of the author's next sentences repeat this account. They make clear that the 'sin' here is natural and belongs to the order of creation, not an offence against a command. It may also be understood in a religious sense by the second reading. The words 'being an intellect in possibility' add another name for the soul's incompleteness.

[^V08-667]: An objection may be raised. If infinitely many souls existed before bodies as separate souls, they would need infinitely many bodies on which to act. That would contradict the assumption that they existed before their bodies. If they were first attached to other bodies and later entered these, the result would be the movement of souls from body to body.

    But the author is not saying that countless souls already existed as separate agents acting on separate bodies. He means that their reality existed as one intelligible whole. What later acts through bodies was present there in its underlying reality, source, and perfection—not yet under the description of acting through a body. In other words, the higher intellect contains the powers and grounds of existence from which these later activities come.

    The opposing philosopher may deny that souls existed before bodies as many distinct souls, without denying their prior existence as a single intelligible reality. Arguments directed against the first claim do not by themselves defeat the second.

[^V08-668]: The intellectual source has unlimited power.

[^V08-669]: A thing’s existence has three relations: to its own nature, which makes it possible; to its matter, which makes it possible to receive form; and to its cause, which gives it necessary existence. The cause gives existence and is nearer to the thing than the thing is to itself.

[^V08-670]: The confusion comes from treating existence as if it were just an idea, and from overlooking what stays the same as existence grows stronger. The cause, the receiving matter, and the connection through change let us say “it is still the same thing” without claiming an impossible change of nature.

[^V08-671]: If these three features let us say that the thing remains itself, its different natures follow from it. One reality can give rise to many ideas.

[^V08-672]: The commentator says the author’s sentence should be shortened so that it does not seem to assume what it is trying to prove.

[^V08-673]: On this view, material forms are reflections of intellectual forms, reversing the usual way of speaking. This is the meaning of the saying, “In you the greater world is folded up.”

[^V08-674]: These are thinkers who deny that one thing can have higher and lower levels while remaining one thing through change. They also deny substantial motion. Because they see only separate concepts and images, they cannot explain how we know a thing’s reality or how it exists in the mind.

[^V08-675]: The forms that exist in matter.

[^V08-676]: The soul once pointed to its body and said “I,” because it was absorbed in the body’s life. As it rises, it sees more inward levels of its own existence and can point to them as “I.” The folktale is about a man who tied a gourd to his leg so he would not lose himself in a crowd. Someone moved it to another man, and when he woke up, he pointed at the other man and said, “You are me—so who am I?”

[^V08-677]: The manuscript reads “not in possibility,” while the printed text reads “in possibility.” The manuscript reading seems more likely.

[^V08-678]: The commentator asks why the author did not interpret the philosopher’s arguments as denying the soul’s existence specifically as a soul, rather than denying its existence as an intellect. This would have avoided the objections that follow.

[^V08-679]: The commentary says all lights, whether sunlight, moonlight, lamp light, or spiritual light, belong to one kind and differ by greater or lesser strength. Just as faster and slower motion are different levels of one continuous movement, light and shadow are different levels of one kind of existence.

[^V08-680]: Souls may exist before bodies in three ways: as knowledge in the descending chain of intellects; as one intellectual reality; or through souls moving from one body to another. The first two are accepted here; the third is rejected.

[^V08-681]: “Two thousand years” is only an approximate way of speaking. The two periods may mean the intellectual and imaginal worlds, each showing a thousand divine names.

[^V08-682]: If a limited number of souls moved through an unlimited succession of bodies, they would eventually have to stop, unless new souls began to exist. That would contradict the view that souls never begin. If the number of souls were limited, they could still all have bodies at any one time, so they would not be inactive. The argument also raises other problems about many immaterial individuals of one kind.

[^V08-683]: The soul’s earlier modes may be divine, intellectual, or imaginal. They can be one and simple, with no size or direction. The imaginal world is made of forms without matter; each form there corresponds to something in this world.

[^V08-684]: The soul’s cause at the beginning need not be the same as its cause as it continues to exist. A vessel may be made by a potter but remain because its clay has hardened. Likewise, the body’s condition brings the soul into being, while the active intellect sustains it after bodily life.

[^V08-685]: A Persian poem says: “I seize existence from nonexistence; in nonexistence I am a king and a knower.” The prayer says, “O You who created things from nothing.”

[^V08-686]: “Dependence” and “action” here name actual modes of existence, not just relations between things. The author’s terms for relation, need, and dependence refer to real ways that beings exist through God.

[^V08-687]: “Soul” here means the source of perception, movement, and action in a living being. The natural form that comes before it is a lower stage in the soul’s development; the animal form that comes after it is not, by itself, a stage of the complete human soul.

[^V08-688]: An objection: if the aim is merely to show an endless chain of causes, why mention that each cause is simple? The same argument could be applied to each new event: the first needs a cause, the second needs a cause, and so on. Would there not be an infinite series even if the cause were made up of parts?

    The answer is no. A cause made up of parts need not lead to that impossible infinite series. The philosopher whose proof is being examined explains the difference in his discussion of general principles. A new event requires some new condition; otherwise there would be no reason why it occurred at one time rather than earlier. If its cause is simple and must itself be new, its own cause must again be both simple and new. The same reasoning then repeats without end.

    But a cause with parts might contain something eternal and a new condition. A lasting cause could produce its effect when the new condition arose or when an obstacle ceased to exist. Since the condition could even be the *absence* of something, this does not require infinitely many existing causes, all ordered one before another. The supposed endless chain does not follow.

    Still, the original proof takes a longer route than necessary. Its author later says that the soul cannot be composed of parts. He could simply have argued: if the soul began to exist and is simple, it would need another new cause because it began and a simple cause because it is simple. Instead he first raises the choice between a simple and a composite cause. His further treatment of that choice also includes a step that is not needed for the proof.

[^V08-689]: The argument also allows the possibility that the soul and body depend on one another. Some theologians say an effect can be delayed after its cause.

[^V08-690]: A missing thing cannot have a position. A separate intellect also has no position, even though it exists.

[^V08-691]: The body may be needed only to make the soul’s relation to it possible, not to give the soul existence itself. Coming to be may mean the boundary between nonexistence and existence, not a new quality added to a thing. The body may also be a condition for the soul’s completion rather than its existence.

[^V08-692]: The condition in question is the body. It is both the condition for the soul’s beginning and the place where the soul acts, unlike the motion of a heavenly sphere, where the condition for motion is not the place where it acts.

[^V08-693]: “The soul in general” may mean the soul’s intellectual reality, or the enduring reality that remains through its stages of substantial change. It does not mean one individual soul or a general idea shared by many souls.

[^V08-694]: Some philosophers say bodily powers act only through bodily position, and therefore cannot act on their own matter. Illuminationist thinkers instead assign actions such as feeding and growth to separate forms that govern each kind.

[^V08-695]: The commentator argues that coming to be is not simply a thing’s existence; it is the boundary where nonexistence becomes existence. A condition for this boundary need not be a condition for the existence that follows it.

[^V08-696]: The cause of something beginning to exist can differ from the cause that keeps it in existence. The body may cause the soul to begin, while the active intellect sustains it.

[^V08-697]: The difficulty has two parts. First, how can an immaterial substance begin to exist only when a particular body is ready and at a particular time? Being free of matter, it should have the same relation to every time. Second, what a thing is prepared to receive ought to become one of its own states and complete it. How can a substance wholly separate from matter become a state of matter?

    This is the difficult objection that the author says he will later quote from Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Ṭūsī in *The Eastern Return*. That is why he tells the reader to watch for it. The objection has force. But part of it also applies to the opposing view. Both sides must explain how a soul that eventually survives as an immaterial being can have a beginning.

    One reply is that only a new relationship between body and soul begins. But on the opposing view this seems to leave the soul itself eternal: the newly arising thing is merely a relation, not the soul. That is very different from the author's account, in which the soul begins with the body and survives as spirit. At first it has a changing, bodily mode of existence. Its relation to the body is real and begins with it, but in the end the soul leaves time behind. Past, present, and future no longer apply to it in the ordinary way. Times are then present to it as though in a single moment, and places as though at a single point. Something similar is already true of its grasp of general truths, even before we consider the soul's own immaterial reality.

    On the author's account the soul also passes through stages that belong to its very being. On the opposing account it has just one level, that of immaterial existence. The author will soon examine what carries the capacity for its later, enduring immaterial life. — س ره

[^V08-698]: The earlier argument was that the soul cannot be destroyed when the body dies. Here the claim is that the soul cannot perish at all.

[^V08-699]: This applies even to the agent's power to act. But an obvious difficulty arises here. Mīr Dāmād reports it from Naṣīr al-Dīn al-Tusi in *Al-Qabasāt*, calling it one of the hardest problems to untangle.

    If anything in the world ceases to exist, the objection goes, God's existence would also have to cease. A thing can fail to exist because one of its conditions is missing, because a condition of its cause is missing, or because part of that cause is missing. We can then ask why *that* condition or part is missing. The same question repeats until we reach God, since every existing thing ultimately depends on Him. Does the disappearance of a thing, then, imply a failure at the first source of everything?

    The reply is that a passing event ends when its particular stretch of an ever-renewing reality ends. The author understands this as the flowing existence of material nature; other philosophers have explained it through the changing positions of the heavens. Each stage of such a changing reality is necessarily absent when a different stage is present. Friday is necessarily absent on Thursday and Saturday. No further cause is needed to explain why Friday is absent on those days.

    Once the ending of an event is traced to the ending of its own stage in that flow, its absence follows from what that stage is. There is no need to explain that absence through another cause, and the chain never leads to God's being absent. The creator brings the changing reality itself into existence; He does not first create a fixed thing and then add the feature of constant passing away.

    Mīr Dāmād first points toward this reply. He then offers another argument, in these terms:

    “We must cut this difficulty off at its root. We have shown that coming into being and passing away in time can be understood only by tracing them to a nature whose very being is constantly renewed and spent. The continuity of that renewal and passing is what remains stable in it. It flows through successive beginnings and endings without needing an outside cause to make each stage disappear. That insight offers a way out of these difficulties. But I will now explain the matter from another direction.

    “The problem becomes hard only for someone who imagines that the absence of a perishable thing is itself a new event in the world. On that view, when a thing ends, a new destruction occurs after it has existed. Its earlier existence is supposedly taken away, even from the time in which its cause brought it about. Nonexistence becomes something an agent produces, as though the agent performed an act of destroying. The thing's not being present at a later time is treated as a fact that first comes into being at that later time. And the removal of an obstacle is treated as a cause that must come before the effect it makes possible.

    “None of these assumptions has a sound basis. Some who call themselves philosophers are satisfied with them, but a careful thinker knows that whatever comes into being, changes, acts, or receives an action must be *something*. Absolute nonexistence is not something that acts or receives action. It is pure denial: saying that a thing is not there. The word points to no new thing in reality. To say that something which exists at one time is absent later is simply to deny its presence at that later time.

    “Could its existence during its own time be removed? That would amount to saying that it both existed and did not exist then. Could it be removed from a later time? That makes no sense, since it never existed at that later time in the first place.

    “So passing away is properly understood as the end of the continuing gift of existence: the further sustaining of the thing is not given. The Qur'an speaks of this sustaining as *preserving*—‘Their preservation does not tire Him’—and as *holding*—‘He holds the heavens and the earth lest they cease.’ There is no absence that first comes into being. The absence of a temporal thing means that the time allotted to its existence has an ending, just as it has a beginning. Its existence, bounded at both ends, is not erased from its proper time or from the wider order in which that time has its place.

    “It follows that the absence which comes after a perishable thing is not a new event needing a new cause. From the outset, the complete cause for its existing during the later time never exists. The same is true of its absence before the time when it actually begins. Neither absence is a fresh event produced when part of the complete cause of its *actual* existence is removed. The complete cause of an existence that really took place has not been undone. Rather, the supposed complete cause of an existence that never takes place is itself never realized.

    “Our minds can imagine an endless series of such absences before and after a thing's actual life. We distinguish them by their relation to possible states of being, not because one absence physically causes another, or because the series must arrive at a last, independent absence. This should now be clear.”

    That ends his illuminating account. I have quoted it at length, even though it goes beyond the usual limits of a marginal note, because it contains valuable explanations for this question and for others. The reader should think carefully about its depth and recognize the strength of the approach. — S. R.

[^V08-700]: The second argument treats perishing as a change that must have something to receive it. If the soul itself cannot be that receiver, it would need matter. But that matter would need another basis if it could perish, leading to an endless chain. The argument concludes that the final, enduring receiver is the soul itself.

[^V08-701]: The previous arguments concern a soul that exists separately from matter. They do not explain how a soul connected to the body can come into being or survive the body's loss.

[^V08-702]: The Active Intellect does not itself begin at a certain time or have a readiness to exist. It becomes the soul's storehouse of remembered ideas through a new relation to the soul.

[^V08-703]: “Receiver” can mean something ready to take on a form, or something that is acted on and completed by a form. These are different meanings. The body is a receiver of the soul in the second sense, not the first.

[^V08-704]: “Matter” can mean the source from which a thing comes, or what receives the form of the thing. The body may receive the soul-form without being the source from which the soul comes. The same difference applies to what comes to be and what can perish.

[^V08-705]: Tusi's question is whether the body can be the basis for the soul's coming into being but not for its perishing.

[^V08-706]: The author refers to an earlier answer that he had accepted and refined before he considered the question again.

[^V08-707]: The point is not that the body mistakenly demanded a power bound to matter when it actually needed a spiritual power to govern it. Natural processes do not make errors of judgment: bodily readiness does not 'choose wrongly.' Nor should we say that the Giver noticed what would suit the body and granted it a separate thinking power, for then the body would already be ready for that separate power, which is the very difficulty under discussion.

    A third explanation would say that the body rightly received the bodily power it asked for, and that the Giver then added a separate spiritual reality out of sheer generosity, making the bodily power its servant or its shadow. But that would conflict with the rule that gifts are given according to readiness. If readiness were unnecessary, the same generosity could bestow a rational human soul on an ant.

    The intended answer, as the author will explain, is that bodily matter is ready for a spiritual power *indirectly*. Divine gifts match what a thing is ready to receive, even where the readiness is of this indirect kind.
