    An objection follows. If the separate soul has no material subject carrying the possibility of its existence, then, by the usual rule, what has no such subject cannot begin after being absent. The soul would not come into being. If it does have a material subject, the objection seems to return. Could we say the body carries the soul's possibility only indirectly? Then it might also carry, indirectly, the possibility of the soul's passing away.

    The reply accepts the force of that distinction, as the author's later account of the soul being preceded by the body's readiness suggests. But 'indirectly' must not be understood as a fiction or a merely verbal connection. Nor is this like saying that someone seated in a moving ship moves only because the ship moves. It is more like the way a natural kind exists through its particular members, or the way a general kind is present in a simple reality through what distinguishes that reality. The indirect connection is real.

[^V08-708]: The body is said to rise from the earth in the life to come because the body of that life grows out of what was formed in this earthly body and soul. Its beautiful or ugly appearance, and the forms taken by its deeds—gardens, dwellings, bitter cold, fierce heat, and other experiences—come from the person's movements, lasting habits, and character.

    The forms arise through deeds and habits, as the argument that deeds take bodily form requires and as revelation describes. Yet those habits were formed through the earthly body. In another sense, the bringing forth of this body from earth is already the bringing forth of the bodies belonging to the world between death and resurrection and to the final life. Those bodies are fuller and more complete expressions of this one.

    Think of a picture painted on a surface and the same picture existing without needing the surface. What makes a thing what it is lies in its form. Moreover, all three bodies have their identity through the one soul. This will be examined in the discussion of resurrection.

[^V08-709]: The author reads the quoted verse as describing stages: the body is formed, actions take shape, and the lower powers are gathered into the human being's higher life.

[^V08-710]: This note points to the three reasons given here for the soul's survival after the body's death.

[^V08-711]: A preparatory cause helps bring about an effect but does not sustain it in the way a necessary cause does. The rule about what can or cannot begin or end does not show that something cannot have a new stage of existence after an earlier one.

[^V08-712]: The qualification refers to the thing's later existence. The point is that its earlier existence, which depended on something carrying it, can be enough to make its later existence more likely even when that later existence no longer needs such a carrier.

    Think of something that prepares the water of a river to flow downhill. The river's water then joins the sea. Its earlier course helped bring about its later condition, but the water no longer depends on the same setting. The first answer can also be understood in this way: this is its deeper meaning, while the earlier wording gives its outward form. There is one condition, however. In that first answer, “spiritual” must refer to the thing's continued existence, not to the way it first comes into being. Think carefully about this distinction.

[^V08-713]: The soul's separate existence follows its bodily existence as a stronger stage of the same continuing life. This is change and persistence, not a second beginning from nothing.

[^V08-714]: The body supports the soul's existence while the soul exists for and through the body. After its deep change, the soul exists in itself. The thing that holds the possibility for this change need not be the same as what holds the completed state.

[^V08-715]: The soul loses its bodily potential but continues by joining the Active Intellect. It remains through God.

[^V08-716]: The argument concerns a human soul separate from matter, not a soul-form that remains within a heavenly body.

[^V08-717]: A body's measurable size is what makes it capable of being divided in thought, and of being divided in fact. That capacity does not arise only after the body has come into existence. It belongs to what the body is, rather than being an extra condition added to its existence later.

    To put it differently, the body's position, understood through its spatial relations, is part of the way it exists. It is not a later feature attached to an otherwise complete body. This establishes the point at issue.

    When the author speaks of something “that has no position,” there are two reasons. First, the thing has not yet come into existence, and what does not exist has no position in relation to a body. Second, even if it were to exist, it would be free from matter; what is free from matter has no position of this kind.

[^V08-718]: “Active” can mean that something produces effects; it does not always mean that it acts with greater force.

[^V08-719]: This explanation works under the position of the philosophers who follow Aristotle. They say that the source which produces souls and brings them from possibility into actuality is the Active Intellect, identified with the tenth intellect. With God's permission, it also produces the natural world. They speak of its being entrusted with the management of the world of the elements.

    But the followers of Suhrawardī, and the author himself, take a different view. They say that human souls are brought into actuality by the higher reality belonging specifically to the human kind, just as other kinds of beings have their own higher sources. It is not the higher source belonging to another species, and not simply the tenth intellect.

    These philosophers accept both a series of intellects arranged above and below one another and another order of intellects standing alongside one another. But they do not limit the first series to ten, twenty, or any fixed number. They say that the higher lights descend through different levels until they reach a light too weak to give rise to any further light. This resembles visible light: each successive reflection is weaker, until no further light can be passed on. The different kinds of beings in the natural world, whether souls or bodies, depend on the parallel order, not simply on the descending series.

    Moreover, in his discussion of spiritual resurrection, the author says that each kind of created thing must have a suitable link with a separate form or intellectual being. These are the angels brought near to God, called the *lords of the kinds* by earlier philosophers and the Platonic Forms or divine forms by those who follow Plato. That statement appears to conflict with the one made here.

    Perhaps the two can be reconciled. By *the Active Intellect that produces and develops souls*, he might mean the higher source belonging to humankind. He could call it the source of this whole world because its visible expression contains the expressions of all the other kinds, and its higher reality brings together the powers present in their higher sources. Its relation to those sources is like the relation of its visible expression to their visible expressions.

    On that reading it produces the world's forms as they contribute to the human path, not as separate kinds considered in complete isolation. The essential identities of those kinds remain. Their forms of existence differ, yet the very existence in which they differ is also what they share. All are, in this account, made from what remains of the clay of the human being. They are like mirrors reflecting that higher human reality. — S. R.

[^V08-720]: Animal-like, predatory, and demonic souls are below rational souls and cannot cause them. An angelic soul could be a cause only as actual intellect; in that role, it is not acting as a human soul but as the Active Intellect.
